  • web.dev Google's guides on performance, Core Web Vitals and modern web practice
  • RFC 9110: HTTP Semantics Methods, status codes and headers, authoritatively
  • WCAG 2.2 Accessibility guidelines
  • OWASP Cheat Sheet Series XSS, CSRF, session management and more

Docker

Build small, reproducible images, run and debug containers, and compose a local stack. See Cloud Run and Kubernetes for where the images go.

Mental model

  • Image: a read-only stack of layers plus metadata (entrypoint, env, user). Built from a Dockerfile, named registry/repo:tag, pinned exactly by @sha256:digest.
  • Container: a running process from an image, with its own filesystem layer, network and process namespace. Not a VM: it shares the host kernel.
  • Layer cache: each instruction is a layer. A change invalidates that layer and every one after it, so put what changes least first.
  • Volume: storage that outlives the container. Anything written elsewhere disappears with it.
  • Tag vs digest: a tag can be moved to a new image; a digest cannot. Deploy by digest or by an immutable tag such as the commit SHA.

Everyday commands

docker build -t api:dev .
docker run --rm -p 8080:8080 -e APP_PROFILE=local api:dev
docker run -d --name db -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=pw -p 5433:5432 postgres:16
docker ps -a
docker logs -f --tail=100 db
docker exec -it db psql -U postgres
docker stop db && docker rm db

docker images
docker image inspect api:dev --format '{{.Config.User}} {{.Size}}'
docker history api:dev                   # layer sizes
docker inspect db --format '{{.State.ExitCode}} {{.State.OOMKilled}}'
docker stats                             # live CPU and memory

docker tag api:dev asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev/my-proj/apps/api:3f2a9c1
gcloud auth configure-docker asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev
docker push asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev/my-proj/apps/api:3f2a9c1

docker system df                         # what uses disk
docker system prune                      # stopped containers, dangling images
docker builder prune                     # build cache

A good Dockerfile

# syntax=docker/dockerfile:1
FROM python:3.12-slim AS base
ENV PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1
WORKDIR /app

# dependencies first: cached until requirements change
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/pip pip install -r requirements.txt

# then the code, which changes often
COPY src/ ./src/

RUN useradd --uid 1001 --no-create-home app
USER 1001
EXPOSE 8080
ENTRYPOINT ["python", "-m", "src.main"]
# .dockerignore: keeps the build context small and secrets out
.git
.venv
__pycache__/
*.pyc
.env
node_modules/
target/
build/

Multi-stage builds

# Build with the full toolchain, ship only the result
FROM eclipse-temurin:21-jdk AS build
WORKDIR /src
COPY gradle/ gradle/
COPY gradlew settings.gradle.kts build.gradle.kts ./
RUN ./gradlew dependencies --no-daemon          # cache dependencies
COPY src/ src/
RUN ./gradlew bootJar --no-daemon -x test

FROM gcr.io/distroless/java21-debian12          # no shell, no package manager
COPY --from=build /src/build/libs/app.jar /app.jar
USER nonroot
ENTRYPOINT ["java", "-XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75", "-jar", "/app.jar"]

# build one stage only
docker build --target build -t api:build .

Distroless images have no shell, which makes them smaller and safer but harder to debug. Keep a -debug variant or use an ephemeral debug container.

Instructions that trip people up

  • ENTRYPOINT vs CMD: ENTRYPOINT is the program; CMD supplies default arguments that docker run image … replaces.
  • Exec form ["java","-jar","app.jar"] runs your process as PID 1 so it receives SIGTERM. Shell form java -jar app.jar wraps it in /bin/sh, which may not forward signals.
  • COPY over ADD: ADD also unpacks archives and fetches URLs, which surprises people.
  • ARG vs ENV: ARG exists only at build time; ENV persists into the running container. Neither is a safe place for secrets: use RUN --mount=type=secret.
  • One RUN per logical step, cleaning up in the same command: apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends curl && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*.
  • Pin base images to a version (python:3.12-slim) or a digest, never latest.

Docker Compose for local stacks

# compose.yaml
services:
  db:
    image: postgres:16
    environment:
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: pw
      POSTGRES_DB: corpus
    ports: ["${DB_PORT:-5433}:5432"]
    volumes: [dbdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data]
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U postgres"]
      interval: 5s
      retries: 10
  api:
    build: .
    ports: ["${APP_PORT:-8080}:8080"]
    environment:
      DB_HOST: db                      # service name is the hostname
    depends_on:
      db: { condition: service_healthy }
volumes:
  dbdata:
docker compose up -d --build
docker compose logs -f api
docker compose exec db psql -U postgres corpus
docker compose down            # keep volumes
docker compose down -v         # also delete volumes (data gone)
docker compose -p wt2 up -d    # second copy with its own project name

Multi-platform & build secrets

# Apple Silicon builds arm64 by default; Cloud Run and most servers need amd64
docker buildx build --platform linux/amd64 -t REGISTRY/api:3f2a9c1 --push .
docker buildx build --platform linux/amd64,linux/arm64 -t REGISTRY/api:3f2a9c1 --push .
docker image inspect api:dev --format '{{.Os}}/{{.Architecture}}'

# secret available during one RUN, never stored in a layer
docker build --secret id=pip_token,env=PIP_TOKEN .
RUN --mount=type=secret,id=pip_token \
    PIP_INDEX_URL="https://__token__:$(cat /run/secrets/pip_token)@pypi.example.com/simple" \
    pip install -r requirements.txt

Debugging containers

SymptomCheck
Exits immediatelydocker logs; docker inspect … .State.ExitCode. 137 = killed (often OOM), 139 = segfault, 1 = app error, 127 = command not found.
"exec format error"Wrong CPU architecture: rebuild with --platform linux/amd64.
Port works inside, not from hostApp binds to 127.0.0.1; bind 0.0.0.0. Or a missing -p host:container.
Cannot reach another containerUse the service name on a shared network, not localhost.
Permission denied on a volumeContainer user's UID does not own the host directory.
Image is hugedocker history; move build tools to a build stage; add a .dockerignore.
Build ignores code changeCached layer: check COPY order, or --no-cache once.
docker run --rm -it --entrypoint sh api:dev        # look inside the image
docker run --rm -it --network container:db nicolaka/netshoot   # debug its network

Image security

  • Run as a non-root user (USER 1001).
  • Start from a minimal base (slim, distroless, or Alpine where musl is fine). Fewer packages, fewer CVEs.
  • Scan images in CI: trivy image REGISTRY/api:tag, or Artifact Registry's built-in scanning.
  • Never bake secrets into an image or a layer; anyone who can pull it can read them with docker history or by unpacking layers.
  • Rebuild regularly so base-image security fixes reach you.
  • Generate an SBOM (docker buildx build --sbom=true or syft) so you can answer "are we affected?" quickly.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Build, run, stop and inspect containers
  • Read a Dockerfile and explain each instruction
  • Use docker compose for a local stack

2 Practitioner

  • Write small multi-stage Dockerfiles with good layer caching
  • Run as non-root; keep secrets out of layers
  • Build for linux/amd64; debug exit codes and OOM kills

3 Senior

  • Set image standards: base images, scanning, SBOMs, signing
  • Optimise image size and startup time for Cloud Run or Kubernetes
  • Design local development environments for a team

4 Expert

  • Own the organisation's container supply chain
  • Understand the OCI image and runtime specs
  • Choose and govern registries, build systems and policies

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Getting better at SQL and BigQuery

How SQL actually evaluates a query, the patterns that solve most real problems, the mistakes that give wrong answers quietly, and exercises with solutions. Written in BigQuery GoogleSQL. The BigQuery tab is the syntax reference.

Practice schema used on this tab

judgment(judgment_key STRING, cnr STRING, court_code STRING, decision_date DATE,
         data_year INT64, judges ARRAY<STRING>, raw_date STRING, _landed_at TIMESTAMP)
court(court_code STRING, name STRING, state STRING)

Practise for free in the BigQuery sandbox (no billing account needed) against bigquery-public-data, for example bigquery-public-data.stackoverflow.posts_questions or bigquery-public-data.new_york_taxi_trips. Always dry-run first and select only the columns you need.

The order SQL really runs in

  1. FROM and JOIN: build the row set
  2. WHERE: filter rows
  3. GROUP BY: form groups
  4. aggregates computed
  5. HAVING: filter groups
  6. window functions computed
  7. QUALIFY: filter on window results
  8. SELECT expressions, then DISTINCT
  9. ORDER BY
  10. LIMIT

This explains most errors: you cannot use a window function in WHERE (it does not exist yet; use QUALIFY), and WHERE cannot filter on an aggregate (use HAVING).

Write it so it reads top to bottom

WITH
landed AS (                         -- 1. narrow the input early
  SELECT judgment_key, court_code, decision_date
  FROM `p.fdp.judgment`
  WHERE decision_date BETWEEN '2024-01-01' AND '2024-12-31'
),
per_court AS (                      -- 2. aggregate at one grain
  SELECT court_code, COUNT(*) AS n
  FROM landed
  GROUP BY court_code
)
SELECT c.name, COALESCE(p.n, 0) AS n  -- 3. join for labels last
FROM `p.ref.court` AS c
LEFT JOIN per_court AS p USING (court_code)
ORDER BY n DESC;
  • One CTE per step, named for what it holds.
  • State each CTE's grain in a comment when it is not obvious.
  • Filter and aggregate before joining wide tables.

NULL: the source of quiet bugs

  • NULL = NULL is NULL, not TRUE. Use IS NULL, or a IS NOT DISTINCT FROM b to compare nullable values.
  • COUNT(*) counts rows; COUNT(col) skips NULLs. AVG, SUM, MIN ignore NULLs too.
  • x NOT IN (subquery) returns no rows if the subquery yields a single NULL. Use NOT EXISTS.
  • WHERE status != 'X' drops rows where status is NULL.
  • SUM of no rows is NULL, not 0: wrap in COALESCE(…, 0).
  • SAFE_CAST and SAFE.PARSE_DATE turn bad input into NULL. Count those NULLs, or the failure is invisible.
SELECT
  COUNT(*)                                        AS total_rows,
  COUNT(decision_date)                            AS with_date,
  COUNTIF(raw_date IS NOT NULL AND decision_date IS NULL) AS failed_parse
FROM `p.fdp.judgment`;

Joins without surprises

  • Know the grain of both sides before joining. Joining one-to-many multiplies rows; a SUM afterwards is inflated (fan-out).
  • Aggregate first, then join when you need totals from two child tables.
  • A filter on the right table in WHERE turns a LEFT JOIN into an inner join. Put it in ON.
  • Check row counts before and after each join while developing.
-- WRONG: courts with no 2024 judgments disappear
SELECT c.court_code, COUNT(j.judgment_key) n
FROM court c LEFT JOIN judgment j USING (court_code)
WHERE j.data_year = 2024
GROUP BY 1;

-- RIGHT: the filter belongs to the join
SELECT c.court_code, COUNT(j.judgment_key) n
FROM court c
LEFT JOIN judgment j
  ON j.court_code = c.court_code AND j.data_year = 2024
GROUP BY 1;

-- did my join fan out? keys should be unique on the "one" side
SELECT court_code, COUNT(*) FROM court GROUP BY 1 HAVING COUNT(*) > 1;

Semi-joins and anti-joins

-- semi-join: courts that HAVE at least one judgment (no duplicates)
SELECT * FROM court c
WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM judgment j WHERE j.court_code = c.court_code);

-- anti-join: courts with NO judgment
SELECT * FROM court c
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM judgment j WHERE j.court_code = c.court_code);

-- same anti-join with LEFT JOIN
SELECT c.* FROM court c
LEFT JOIN judgment j USING (court_code)
WHERE j.court_code IS NULL;

-- set operations (columns by position)
SELECT cnr FROM a EXCEPT DISTINCT SELECT cnr FROM b;   -- in a, not in b
SELECT cnr FROM a INTERSECT DISTINCT SELECT cnr FROM b;
SELECT cnr FROM a UNION ALL SELECT cnr FROM b;          -- keeps duplicates, cheaper

Aggregation toolkit

SELECT
  court_code,
  COUNT(*)                                  AS n,
  COUNT(DISTINCT cnr)                       AS cases,
  COUNTIF(decision_date IS NULL)            AS undated,
  SAFE_DIVIDE(COUNTIF(decision_date IS NULL), COUNT(*)) AS undated_share,
  MIN(decision_date) AS first_d, MAX(decision_date) AS last_d,
  ANY_VALUE(cnr HAVING MAX decision_date)   AS latest_cnr,   -- value at the max
  STRING_AGG(DISTINCT CAST(data_year AS STRING), ',' ORDER BY CAST(data_year AS STRING)) AS years,
  APPROX_QUANTILES(DATE_DIFF(DATE(_landed_at), decision_date, DAY), 100)[OFFSET(50)] AS median_lag_days,
  LOGICAL_AND(decision_date IS NOT NULL)    AS all_dated
FROM `p.fdp.judgment`
GROUP BY ALL;            -- groups by every non-aggregated column

-- subtotals in one pass
SELECT state, court_code, COUNT(*) n
FROM judgment JOIN court USING (court_code)
GROUP BY ROLLUP (state, court_code);       -- per court, per state, grand total

Window functions, properly

A window function computes a value for each row from a set of related rows, without collapsing them. Three parts: PARTITION BY (which rows are related), ORDER BY (their sequence), and the frame (which of them count for this row).

SELECT
  court_code, d, n,
  ROW_NUMBER() OVER w_date                            AS rn,          -- 1,2,3 no ties
  RANK()       OVER (PARTITION BY court_code ORDER BY n DESC) AS rnk, -- 1,1,3
  DENSE_RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY court_code ORDER BY n DESC) AS drnk,-- 1,1,2
  LAG(n)  OVER w_date                                 AS prev_day,
  LEAD(n) OVER w_date                                 AS next_day,
  SUM(n)  OVER (PARTITION BY court_code ORDER BY d
                ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW) AS running_total,
  AVG(n)  OVER (PARTITION BY court_code ORDER BY d
                ROWS BETWEEN 6 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW)         AS avg_7_rows,
  AVG(n)  OVER (PARTITION BY court_code ORDER BY UNIX_DATE(d)
                RANGE BETWEEN 6 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW)        AS avg_7_days,
  n / SUM(n) OVER (PARTITION BY court_code)           AS share_of_court,
  FIRST_VALUE(d) OVER w_date                          AS first_day,
  LAST_VALUE(d)  OVER (w_date ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING) AS last_day
FROM daily
WINDOW w_date AS (PARTITION BY court_code ORDER BY d);
  • ROWS vs RANGE: ROWS 6 PRECEDING means six rows back, even if days are missing. RANGE on a numeric day number means six calendar days back. Fill gaps with a date spine or use RANGE.
  • Default frame trap: with ORDER BY and no frame, the frame ends at the current row, so LAST_VALUE returns the current row. Give an explicit frame.
  • Ties: ROW_NUMBER picks arbitrarily among ties. Add a tie-breaker column to ORDER BY so results are repeatable.

Pattern: top N per group

-- 3 most recent judgments per court
SELECT court_code, cnr, decision_date
FROM `p.fdp.judgment`
WHERE decision_date IS NOT NULL
QUALIFY ROW_NUMBER() OVER (
  PARTITION BY court_code
  ORDER BY decision_date DESC, judgment_key      -- tie-breaker
) <= 3;

Use RANK instead of ROW_NUMBER if ties should all be included. If BigQuery complains that QUALIFY needs a WHERE, GROUP BY or HAVING clause, add WHERE TRUE.

Pattern: deduplicate

-- find duplicates first: never dedupe blind
SELECT cnr, COUNT(*) AS copies, COUNT(DISTINCT _landed_at) AS loads
FROM `p.odp.hc_judgment`
GROUP BY cnr HAVING COUNT(*) > 1
ORDER BY copies DESC LIMIT 20;

-- keep the latest copy of each key
SELECT * FROM `p.odp.hc_judgment`
QUALIFY ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY cnr ORDER BY _landed_at DESC) = 1;

-- exact duplicate rows only
SELECT DISTINCT * EXCEPT (_run_id) FROM t;   -- DISTINCT * fails on ARRAY columns

Pattern: date spine and missing days

WITH days AS (
  SELECT d FROM UNNEST(GENERATE_DATE_ARRAY('2025-01-01', '2025-03-31')) AS d
),
grid AS (                                  -- every court × every day
  SELECT c.court_code, days.d FROM `p.ref.court` c CROSS JOIN days
),
actual AS (
  SELECT court_code, decision_date AS d, COUNT(*) AS n
  FROM `p.fdp.judgment`
  WHERE decision_date BETWEEN '2025-01-01' AND '2025-03-31'
  GROUP BY 1, 2
)
SELECT g.court_code, g.d, COALESCE(a.n, 0) AS n
FROM grid g LEFT JOIN actual a USING (court_code, d)
ORDER BY 1, 2;

Without the spine, days with zero rows simply do not appear, and averages and LAGs are computed over the wrong set.

Pattern: gaps and islands

-- streaks of consecutive days on which a court published decisions
WITH d AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT court_code, decision_date
  FROM `p.fdp.judgment` WHERE decision_date IS NOT NULL
),
tagged AS (
  SELECT *,
    DATE_SUB(decision_date,
      INTERVAL ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY court_code ORDER BY decision_date) DAY
    ) AS island                            -- constant within a run of consecutive days
  FROM d
)
SELECT court_code, MIN(decision_date) AS start_d, MAX(decision_date) AS end_d,
       COUNT(*) AS days
FROM tagged
GROUP BY court_code, island
ORDER BY days DESC;

The trick: a date minus its row number stays the same while dates are consecutive and jumps at each gap.

Pattern: change vs previous period

WITH yearly AS (
  SELECT court_code, data_year, COUNT(*) AS n
  FROM `p.fdp.judgment` GROUP BY ALL
)
SELECT * FROM (
  SELECT *,
    LAG(n) OVER (PARTITION BY court_code ORDER BY data_year) AS prev_n
  FROM yearly
)
WHERE SAFE_DIVIDE(n - prev_n, prev_n) < -0.2;   -- fell by more than 20%

LAG takes the previous row. If a year is missing for a court, "previous" is two years back. Use a spine when that matters.

Pattern: arrays (one-to-many in one row)

-- top 3 judges per court by number of judgments
WITH per_judge AS (
  SELECT court_code, TRIM(judge) AS judge, COUNT(*) AS n
  FROM `p.fdp.judgment`, UNNEST(judges) AS judge
  GROUP BY ALL
)
SELECT * FROM per_judge
QUALIFY ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY court_code ORDER BY n DESC, judge) <= 3;

-- filter rows by array content without exploding
SELECT cnr FROM `p.fdp.judgment`
WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM UNNEST(judges) j WHERE j LIKE '%SHARMA%');

-- array size, and rows whose array is empty or NULL
SELECT ARRAY_LENGTH(judges) AS n_judges FROM t;
SELECT COUNTIF(ARRAY_LENGTH(judges) = 0 OR judges IS NULL) FROM t;

The comma before UNNEST is a CROSS JOIN: rows with an empty array vanish. Use LEFT JOIN UNNEST(judges) AS judge to keep them.

Pattern: pivot and unpivot

-- one row per court, a column per year
SELECT * FROM (
  SELECT court_code, data_year, judgment_key FROM `p.fdp.judgment`
  WHERE data_year BETWEEN 2023 AND 2025
)
PIVOT (COUNT(judgment_key) FOR data_year IN (2023 AS y2023, 2024 AS y2024, 2025 AS y2025));

-- the portable way, works in any SQL
SELECT court_code,
  COUNTIF(data_year = 2023) AS y2023,
  COUNTIF(data_year = 2024) AS y2024,
  COUNTIF(data_year = 2025) AS y2025
FROM `p.fdp.judgment` GROUP BY court_code;

-- columns back to rows
SELECT * FROM wide UNPIVOT (n FOR year IN (y2023, y2024, y2025));

Pattern: recursive hierarchy

-- every judgment that cites X, directly or through a chain (max depth 5)
WITH RECURSIVE chain AS (
  SELECT citing_key, cited_key, 1 AS depth
  FROM `p.fdp.citation`
  WHERE cited_key = 'X'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT c.citing_key, c.cited_key, chain.depth + 1
  FROM `p.fdp.citation` c
  JOIN chain ON c.cited_key = chain.citing_key
  WHERE chain.depth < 5                    -- always bound the recursion
)
SELECT DISTINCT citing_key, MIN(depth) AS depth FROM chain GROUP BY citing_key;

Pattern: point-in-time join (SCD type 2)

-- which court name was valid on the decision date?
SELECT j.cnr, j.decision_date, s.name
FROM `p.fdp.judgment` j
JOIN `p.snapshots.court_snapshot` s
  ON s.court_code = j.court_code
 AND j.decision_date >= DATE(s.dbt_valid_from)
 AND (s.dbt_valid_to IS NULL OR j.decision_date < DATE(s.dbt_valid_to));

Half-open intervals (>= from AND < to) never match two versions for one date.

Reusable logic

-- temporary SQL function for this script
CREATE TEMP FUNCTION parse_any_date(s STRING) AS (
  COALESCE(SAFE.PARSE_DATE('%Y-%m-%d', s), SAFE.PARSE_DATE('%d-%m-%Y', s))
);
SELECT raw_date, parse_any_date(raw_date) FROM t;

-- persistent function in a dataset
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION `p.util.safe_ratio`(a FLOAT64, b FLOAT64) AS (
  IF(b = 0 OR b IS NULL, NULL, a / b)
);

-- table function: a parameterised view
CREATE OR REPLACE TABLE FUNCTION `p.util.court_year`(c STRING, y INT64) AS
SELECT * FROM `p.fdp.judgment` WHERE court_code = c AND data_year = y;
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM `p.util.court_year`('2_5', 2024);

Making BigQuery fast and cheap

HabitWhy
Select named columnsBytes billed = bytes of the columns you read. One wide text column can be most of a table.
Filter on the partition column with literals or parametersLets BigQuery skip partitions before reading. Wrapping the column in a function can prevent it.
Filter on clustered columnsSkips blocks inside partitions; benefit shows in "bytes processed" after the run.
Dry-run before runningShows bytes to be scanned for free. Set a maximum bytes billed on risky jobs.
Aggregate early, join lateShuffling fewer rows between stages is the main cost in slot time.
Avoid ORDER BY without LIMIT on huge resultsA global sort runs on one worker at the end.
Use APPROX_COUNT_DISTINCT for explorationExact distinct counts on billions of rows are expensive; the approximate version has a small, documented error and is far cheaper.
Materialise reused intermediate resultsA CTE referenced several times may be computed several times. Write it to a table (or temp table in a script) when it is big.
Read the execution graphIn the console's Execution details: look for a stage with skewed time (one huge key), large shuffle, or spill to disk.
Avoid self-joins for running totalsA window function does in one pass what a self-join does quadratically.

Mistakes that give wrong answers quietly

  • Fan-out from a one-to-many join before a SUM.
  • A right-table filter in WHERE after a LEFT JOIN.
  • NOT IN against a column that contains NULL.
  • Integer division in other engines (1/2 = 0); BigQuery's / returns FLOAT64, but DIV() is integer.
  • BETWEEN on timestamps: BETWEEN '2025-01-01' AND '2025-01-31' misses most of the 31st. Use >= start AND < next_day.
  • Time zones: DATE(ts) is UTC unless you pass a zone (DATE(ts, 'Asia/Kolkata')).
  • ROW_NUMBER without a tie-breaker: a different row "wins" on each run.
  • LIMIT without ORDER BY: an arbitrary sample, not the top rows.
  • Averaging averages, or averaging percentages, instead of recomputing from totals.
  • Safe-parsing into NULL and never counting the NULLs.

Check your own query

  • Grain: what does one output row mean? Is the key unique? (COUNT(*) vs COUNT(DISTINCT key))
  • Totals: does the sum across groups equal the ungrouped total?
  • Rows in vs out: did a join drop or multiply rows you did not expect?
  • Edges: empty groups, NULLs, the first and last day, ties.
  • One example by hand: pick one key and check its row against the source.
  • Cost: dry-run bytes reasonable for the question?

Exercises (open each for a solution)

Try each on the practice schema, or adapt it to a public dataset. Solutions are one good answer, not the only one.

1. Judgments per court in 2024, including courts with none.
SELECT c.court_code, c.name, COUNT(j.judgment_key) AS n
FROM `p.ref.court` c
LEFT JOIN `p.fdp.judgment` j
  ON j.court_code = c.court_code AND j.data_year = 2024
GROUP BY ALL
ORDER BY n DESC;

Filter in ON, and count a column from the right table so empty courts give 0.

2. The most recent judgment per court, with ties broken consistently.
SELECT court_code, cnr, decision_date
FROM `p.fdp.judgment`
WHERE decision_date IS NOT NULL
QUALIFY ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY court_code
                           ORDER BY decision_date DESC, judgment_key) = 1;
3. What share of each court's rows failed to parse their date? Only courts above 1%.
SELECT court_code,
       COUNT(*) AS n,
       COUNTIF(raw_date IS NOT NULL AND decision_date IS NULL) AS failed,
       SAFE_DIVIDE(COUNTIF(raw_date IS NOT NULL AND decision_date IS NULL), COUNT(*)) AS failed_share
FROM `p.fdp.judgment`
GROUP BY court_code
HAVING SAFE_DIVIDE(COUNTIF(raw_date IS NOT NULL AND decision_date IS NULL), COUNT(*)) > 0.01
ORDER BY failed_share DESC;
4. Daily counts for one court with a 7-day moving average, including days with zero.
WITH days AS (
  SELECT d FROM UNNEST(GENERATE_DATE_ARRAY('2025-01-01', '2025-06-30')) d
),
daily AS (
  SELECT decision_date AS d, COUNT(*) n FROM `p.fdp.judgment`
  WHERE court_code = '2_5' AND decision_date BETWEEN '2025-01-01' AND '2025-06-30'
  GROUP BY 1
)
SELECT days.d, COALESCE(n, 0) AS n,
       AVG(COALESCE(n, 0)) OVER (ORDER BY days.d ROWS BETWEEN 6 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW) AS ma7
FROM days LEFT JOIN daily USING (d)
ORDER BY d;

The spine makes ROWS equal to days, so the moving average is over seven calendar days.

5. Courts whose yearly count fell by more than 20% compared with the previous year.
WITH y AS (
  SELECT court_code, data_year, COUNT(*) n FROM `p.fdp.judgment` GROUP BY ALL
),
c AS (
  SELECT *, LAG(n) OVER (PARTITION BY court_code ORDER BY data_year) AS prev_n,
            LAG(data_year) OVER (PARTITION BY court_code ORDER BY data_year) AS prev_year
  FROM y
)
SELECT *, SAFE_DIVIDE(n - prev_n, prev_n) AS change
FROM c
WHERE prev_year = data_year - 1               -- only compare adjacent years
  AND SAFE_DIVIDE(n - prev_n, prev_n) < -0.2;
6. Find duplicate keys, then build the deduplicated table keeping the latest load.
SELECT cnr, COUNT(*) copies FROM `p.odp.hc_judgment`
GROUP BY cnr HAVING COUNT(*) > 1;

CREATE OR REPLACE TABLE `p.scratch.hc_dedup` AS
SELECT * FROM `p.odp.hc_judgment`
QUALIFY ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY cnr ORDER BY _landed_at DESC) = 1;

-- verify: should return 0
SELECT COUNT(*) - COUNT(DISTINCT cnr) FROM `p.scratch.hc_dedup`;
7. The longest run of consecutive publishing days for each court.
WITH d AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT court_code, decision_date FROM `p.fdp.judgment`
  WHERE decision_date IS NOT NULL
),
t AS (
  SELECT *, DATE_SUB(decision_date, INTERVAL
           ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY court_code ORDER BY decision_date) DAY) AS grp
  FROM d
),
runs AS (
  SELECT court_code, MIN(decision_date) s, MAX(decision_date) e, COUNT(*) len
  FROM t GROUP BY court_code, grp
)
SELECT * FROM runs
QUALIFY ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY court_code ORDER BY len DESC, s) = 1;
8. Each court's share of all 2024 judgments, and its rank within its state.
WITH n AS (
  SELECT c.state, j.court_code, COUNT(*) n
  FROM `p.fdp.judgment` j JOIN `p.ref.court` c USING (court_code)
  WHERE j.data_year = 2024
  GROUP BY ALL
)
SELECT *,
  n / SUM(n) OVER ()                                  AS share_all,
  RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY state ORDER BY n DESC)     AS rank_in_state
FROM n
ORDER BY state, rank_in_state;
9. Courts present in the reference table but absent from the landed data, and the reverse.
(SELECT 'missing_data' AS issue, court_code FROM `p.ref.court`
 EXCEPT DISTINCT
 SELECT 'missing_data', court_code FROM `p.fdp.judgment`)
UNION ALL
(SELECT 'unknown_court', court_code FROM `p.fdp.judgment`
 EXCEPT DISTINCT
 SELECT 'unknown_court', court_code FROM `p.ref.court`);

Parenthesise when mixing different set operators; BigQuery requires it.

10. Median, 90th percentile and maximum lag (days) between decision and landing, per court.
SELECT court_code,
  APPROX_QUANTILES(lag_d, 100)[OFFSET(50)] AS p50,
  APPROX_QUANTILES(lag_d, 100)[OFFSET(90)] AS p90,
  MAX(lag_d) AS max_lag
FROM (
  SELECT court_code, DATE_DIFF(DATE(_landed_at), decision_date, DAY) AS lag_d
  FROM `p.fdp.judgment` WHERE decision_date IS NOT NULL
)
GROUP BY court_code
ORDER BY p90 DESC;
11. Upsert a day's new rows into a target table without creating duplicates.
MERGE `p.cdp.serving` T
USING (
  SELECT * FROM `p.fdp.judgment`
  WHERE DATE(_landed_at) = @run_date
  QUALIFY ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY judgment_key ORDER BY _landed_at DESC) = 1
) S
ON T.judgment_key = S.judgment_key
WHEN MATCHED THEN UPDATE SET decision_date = S.decision_date, cnr = S.cnr
WHEN NOT MATCHED THEN INSERT ROW;

Deduplicate the source first: MERGE fails if two source rows match one target row.

12. Explain why this returns no rows: SELECT * FROM court WHERE court_code NOT IN (SELECT court_code FROM judgment).

If any judgment.court_code is NULL, x NOT IN (…, NULL) evaluates to NULL for every x, never TRUE, so no row passes. Use NOT EXISTS, or add WHERE court_code IS NOT NULL inside the subquery.

A plan to get better

  1. Week 1: the evaluation order, NULLs and joins. Redo exercises 1, 9 and 12 until they feel obvious.
  2. Week 2: window functions. Exercises 2, 4, 5 and 8; then rewrite one self-join you have seen as a window.
  3. Week 3: patterns. Gaps and islands, date spines, dedup, pivots (6, 7).
  4. Week 4: BigQuery cost and plans. Dry-run everything, read the execution graph of your slowest query, and cut its bytes in half.
  5. Ongoing: before sharing any number, run the self-check list on this tab.

Read other people's SQL: dbt models in your repo, and the queries behind dashboards you use. Ask "what is the grain?" of each.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • SELECT, WHERE, GROUP BY, ORDER BY and simple joins
  • Know how NULL behaves in comparisons and aggregates
  • Dry-run before running in BigQuery

2 Practitioner

  • Window functions, QUALIFY, CTEs, semi- and anti-joins
  • Check grain and fan-out before trusting a number
  • Write readable, tested SQL in dbt

3 Senior

  • Patterns: gaps and islands, spines, SCD joins, recursion
  • Read execution plans and cut cost and slot time
  • Review others' SQL for correctness and cost

4 Expert

  • Design query patterns and models for a whole platform
  • Teach SQL; set standards and linting
  • Know engine internals well enough to predict plans

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Database design

Relational modelling, indexes, partitioning, sharding, transactions and migrations. Examples in PostgreSQL; the ideas carry to other engines. Warehouse design is on the BigQuery tab.

Modelling rules

  • Start normalised (third normal form): each fact stored once, in the table it describes. Denormalise later, on purpose, for a measured read need.
  • Every table has a primary key. Surrogate (bigint identity or UUID) for internal identity; natural keys (CNR, email) get a UNIQUE constraint.
  • Never reuse or renumber a key that others store: URLs, foreign systems, legal records.
  • Constraints are free correctness: NOT NULL, UNIQUE, CHECK, FOREIGN KEY. The database enforces them for every writer, including the one you forgot.
  • Right types: date / timestamptz not strings; numeric for money, never float; text over arbitrary varchar(n); jsonb for truly variable attributes, not as a substitute for columns.
  • Name things plainly and consistently: snake_case, singular or plural but one choice, _at for timestamps, _id for keys.

A schema example

CREATE TABLE court (
  court_code text PRIMARY KEY,
  name       text NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE judgment (
  id            bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  judgment_key  text NOT NULL UNIQUE,             -- stable natural key
  cnr           text NOT NULL,
  court_code    text NOT NULL REFERENCES court,
  decision_date date,
  status        text NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('DISPOSED','PENDING')),
  full_text     text,
  created_at    timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  updated_at    timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);

-- many-to-many through a join table
CREATE TABLE judgment_citation (
  citing_id bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES judgment ON DELETE CASCADE,
  cited_id  bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES judgment,
  PRIMARY KEY (citing_id, cited_id)
);

Index types (PostgreSQL)

TypeGood forExample
B-tree (default)Equality, ranges, sorting, prefix LIKE 'abc%' (with C collation, or an index using text_pattern_ops)CREATE INDEX ON judgment (decision_date);
CompositeFilters on several columns; column order matters(court_code, decision_date DESC)
Covering (INCLUDE)Answering a query from the index alone (index-only scan)(court_code) INCLUDE (cnr, decision_date)
PartialIndexing only the rows you query; small and fast(id) WHERE full_text IS NULL
ExpressionQueries on a computed value(lower(email))
UniqueEnforcing uniqueness, and lookupsUNIQUE (cnr, order_number)
GINFull-text search (tsvector), jsonb containment, arraysUSING gin (to_tsvector('english', full_text))
BRINHuge tables physically ordered by a column (append-only by time); tiny indexUSING brin (created_at)
HNSW / IVFFlat (pgvector)Approximate nearest-neighbour search on embeddingsUSING hnsw (embedding vector_cosine_ops)

Index rules of thumb

  • Index for your queries, not your columns. Start from the slow query's WHERE, JOIN and ORDER BY.
  • Composite order: equality columns first, then the range or sort column. (court_code, decision_date) serves court_code = ? AND decision_date > ?; the reverse order serves it poorly.
  • Leftmost prefix: an index on (a, b, c) helps queries on a, a,b, a,b,c, not on b alone.
  • Foreign keys usually need an index on the referencing column, or deletes and joins scan.
  • Every index slows writes and uses disk. Drop unused ones (pg_stat_user_indexes.idx_scan = 0).
  • Low-selectivity columns (a boolean, a status with 3 values) rarely help alone; use a partial index instead.
  • Functions on columns (WHERE date(created_at) = …) skip a plain index; write a range or index the expression.
  • Build on live tables with CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY, then confirm it is valid: an interrupted build leaves an INVALID index.

Reading a query plan

EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT cnr, decision_date FROM judgment
WHERE court_code = '2_5' AND decision_date >= '2024-01-01'
ORDER BY decision_date DESC LIMIT 20;

-- look for:
--   Seq Scan on a big table          → missing or unusable index
--   rows=10 (estimated) vs actual rows=500000 → stale stats: ANALYZE
--   Sort … external merge Disk       → work_mem too low, or index the sort
--   Nested Loop with huge inner loops → join order or missing index
--   Index Only Scan, Heap Fetches: 0 → ideal

-- check index validity after a concurrent build
SELECT indexrelid::regclass, indisvalid FROM pg_index WHERE NOT indisvalid;
-- slowest queries (pg_stat_statements extension)
SELECT query, calls, mean_exec_time FROM pg_stat_statements
ORDER BY total_exec_time DESC LIMIT 10;

Partitioning (one database)

CREATE TABLE event (
  id         bigserial,                -- identity columns on partitioned tables need PostgreSQL 17+
  event_date date NOT NULL,
  payload    jsonb,
  PRIMARY KEY (id, event_date)          -- must include the partition key
) PARTITION BY RANGE (event_date);

CREATE TABLE event_2025 PARTITION OF event
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2025-01-01') TO ('2026-01-01');
CREATE TABLE event_2026 PARTITION OF event
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-01-01') TO ('2027-01-01');
CREATE TABLE event_default PARTITION OF event DEFAULT;

-- retire old data instantly instead of a slow DELETE
ALTER TABLE event DETACH PARTITION event_2025;
DROP TABLE event_2025;

-- other kinds:  PARTITION BY LIST (region)   PARTITION BY HASH (customer_id)
  • Why: queries that filter on the key skip other partitions (pruning); old data drops in one statement; maintenance runs per partition.
  • When: very large tables (tens of millions of rows and up) with a natural time or category filter. Small tables only get slower.
  • Cost: unique constraints must include the partition key; queries without it scan every partition.

Sharding (many databases)

  • What: split rows across separate database servers by a shard key, so each holds part of the data and load.
  • Shard key choice is the whole design: high cardinality, evenly spread, present in almost every query (tenant ID, user ID).
  • Hash sharding spreads load evenly but makes range queries hit every shard. Range sharding keeps ranges together but creates hot shards (all of today's writes on one).
  • Directory / lookup sharding maps keys to shards in a table: flexible, but the directory becomes critical.
  • Costs: cross-shard joins and transactions become application problems; resharding is a migration project; operations multiply.
  • Try first: better indexes, read replicas, caching, partitioning, a bigger instance, archiving cold data. Shard when one primary truly cannot take the writes.
  • Managed options do it for you: Spanner, AlloyDB with read pools, Citus, Vitess, CockroachDB.

Replication & scaling reads

  • Read replicas copy the primary asynchronously. Scale reads; accept replica lag (a user may not see their own write).
  • Synchronous replicas confirm writes before commit: no data loss on failover, at the cost of write latency.
  • High availability (Cloud SQL HA, Patroni) promotes a standby when the primary fails.
  • Connection pooling (PgBouncer, HikariCP, R2DBC pool): Postgres connections are expensive. Size pools so instances × pool size stays under max_connections.
  • Caching (Redis) for hot, read-heavy data, with a deliberate invalidation rule.

Transactions, isolation, locking

IsolationPrevents
Read committed (Postgres default)Dirty reads. Each statement sees data committed before it began.
Repeatable readNon-repeatable reads: the transaction sees one snapshot throughout.
SerializableAll anomalies; transactions behave as if run one by one. Retry on serialization failure.
-- pessimistic: lock the row you will change
SELECT * FROM account WHERE id = 7 FOR UPDATE;

-- job queue: each worker claims different rows
SELECT id FROM job WHERE state = 'READY'
ORDER BY created_at LIMIT 10 FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED;

-- optimistic: version column, fail if someone else changed it
UPDATE doc SET body = $1, version = version + 1
WHERE id = $2 AND version = $3;     -- 0 rows → conflict, reload and retry

Keep transactions short. A long one holds locks and stops vacuum cleaning up dead rows.

Safe schema changes on live data

  • Additive first: add a nullable column, a new table, a new index (concurrently). Safe and reversible.
  • Expand / contract for renames and type changes: add the new column, write to both, backfill in batches, switch reads, stop writing the old, drop it in a later release.
  • Avoid long locks: some ALTER TABLE forms rewrite the whole table. Set lock_timeout so a migration fails fast instead of blocking traffic.
  • Backfill in batches (by ID range), committing as you go, not one giant UPDATE.
  • Version every change with a migration tool (Liquibase, Flyway, Alembic), and never edit a migration that has already run in production.
  • Test on a production-sized copy; a change that takes 30 s on 10k rows can take hours on 20M.

Anti-patterns

  • Entity-attribute-value tables (key, value rows for everything): no types, no constraints, painful queries.
  • Comma-separated lists in a column: use a join table or an array with a GIN index.
  • Floats for money and strings for dates.
  • No foreign keys "for speed": orphans and silent corruption cost more.
  • N+1 queries from an ORM loop: one query per row instead of one join.
  • SELECT * in application code: breaks when columns change, fetches what you do not need.
  • OFFSET pagination on deep pages: use keyset pagination (WHERE (date, id) < ($1, $2) ORDER BY date DESC, id DESC LIMIT 20).
  • Unbounded growth with no retention or archiving plan.

OLTP vs OLAP design

OLTP (Postgres)OLAP (BigQuery)
WorkloadMany small reads/writes by keyFew large scans and aggregates
StorageRow-orientedColumn-oriented
SchemaNormalised, constraintsDenormalised, nested, wide
Speed comes fromIndexesPartitioning, clustering, reading fewer columns
UpdatesRow by row, transactionalBatch loads and MERGE; avoid row-by-row DML

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Design a normalised table with a primary key and constraints
  • Add an index for a slow lookup
  • Understand what a transaction guarantees

2 Practitioner

  • Read EXPLAIN ANALYZE and fix the plan
  • Choose composite, partial and covering indexes
  • Write safe, additive migrations

3 Senior

  • Partition large tables; plan retention and archiving
  • Choose isolation levels and locking strategies
  • Plan replicas, pooling and capacity

4 Expert

  • Decide between scaling up, partitioning, sharding or a distributed database
  • Run zero-downtime migrations on very large tables
  • Set database standards and review all schema changes

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Modern syntax 14151617

// Switch expression (14)
int quarter = switch (month) {
  case 1, 2, 3 -> 1;
  case 4, 5, 6 -> 2;
  default -> { log.warn("odd"); yield 0; }
};

// Text block (15)
String sql = """
    SELECT cnr, decision_date
    FROM `proj.fdp.judgment`
    WHERE data_year = @year
    """;

// Record (16): immutable carrier, equals/hashCode/toString free
record Judgment(String cnr, LocalDate date) {
  Judgment { Objects.requireNonNull(cnr); }   // compact constructor
}

// instanceof pattern (16)
if (obj instanceof String s && !s.isBlank()) use(s);

// Sealed hierarchy (17)
sealed interface Outcome permits Ok, Failed {}
record Ok(long rows) implements Outcome {}
record Failed(String reason) implements Outcome {}

Java 21 21

// Pattern matching switch + record patterns: exhaustive over a sealed type
String describe(Outcome o) {
  return switch (o) {
    case Ok(long rows) when rows == 0 -> "empty";
    case Ok(long rows)                -> rows + " rows";
    case Failed(String why)           -> "failed: " + why;
  };   // no default needed: compiler checks exhaustiveness
}

// Virtual threads: one cheap thread per blocking task
try (var exec = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) {
  List<Future<Result>> fs = uris.stream()
      .map(u -> exec.submit(() -> fetch(u))).toList();
  for (var f : fs) handle(f.get());
}   // ExecutorService is AutoCloseable since 19: close() waits
Thread.ofVirtual().start(() -> work());

// Sequenced collections
list.getFirst(); list.getLast(); list.reversed();
linkedHashMap.firstEntry(); linkedHashMap.pollLastEntry();

Virtual threads suit blocking I/O (HTTP, JDBC, GCS). Do not pool them. CPU-bound work still wants a fixed pool.

class PositiveAmount extends Amount {
  PositiveAmount(long v) {
    if (v <= 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("must be > 0");
    super(v);            // statements before super() may not touch `this`
  }
}

Collectors cookbook

// group → list / count / sum / max
groupingBy(Row::court)
groupingBy(Row::court, counting())
groupingBy(Row::court, summingLong(Row::bytes))
groupingBy(Row::court, maxBy(comparing(Row::date)))
groupingBy(Row::court, TreeMap::new, mapping(Row::cnr, toSet()))

// toMap with a merge rule (otherwise duplicate keys throw)
toMap(Row::cnr, r -> r, (a, b) -> a.date().isAfter(b.date()) ? a : b)

partitioningBy(r -> r.date() == null)      // Map<Boolean, List<Row>>
joining(", ", "[", "]")

// two results in one pass (12)
var minMax = nums.stream().collect(teeing(
    minBy(naturalOrder()), maxBy(naturalOrder()),
    (mn, mx) -> List.of(mn.orElseThrow(), mx.orElseThrow())));

stream.toList();          // 16+, unmodifiable
IntStream.rangeClosed(1950, 2025).boxed().toList();

CompletableFuture<Long> a = supplyAsync(() -> countHc(), exec);
CompletableFuture<Long> b = supplyAsync(() -> countSc(), exec);
long total = a.thenCombine(b, Long::sum)
              .orTimeout(30, TimeUnit.SECONDS)
              .exceptionally(e -> { log.error("count", e); return -1L; })
              .join();

CompletableFuture.allOf(fs.toArray(CompletableFuture[]::new)).join();

// try-with-resources: closed in reverse order
try (var in = Files.newBufferedReader(p);
     var out = Files.newBufferedWriter(q)) {
  in.lines().map(String::strip).forEach(l -> write(out, l));
}

BigQuery Java client

// com.google.cloud:google-cloud-bigquery (use libraries-bom)
BigQuery bq = BigQueryOptions.getDefaultInstance().getService();

QueryJobConfiguration q = QueryJobConfiguration.newBuilder("""
    SELECT court, COUNT(*) n FROM `proj.fdp.judgment`
    WHERE decision_date >= @from GROUP BY court""")
  .addNamedParameter("from", QueryParameterValue.date("2024-01-01"))
  .setUseLegacySql(false)
  .build();

TableResult rs = bq.query(q);
for (FieldValueList row : rs.iterateAll()) {
  String court = row.get("court").getStringValue();
  long n = row.get("n").getLongValue();
}

// Load parquet from GCS
LoadJobConfiguration load = LoadJobConfiguration
  .newBuilder(TableId.of("odp", "hc_judgment"), "gs://bkt/path/*.parquet",
              FormatOptions.parquet())
  .setWriteDisposition(JobInfo.WriteDisposition.WRITE_APPEND)
  .build();
Job job = bq.create(JobInfo.of(load)).waitFor();
if (job.getStatus().getError() != null) throw new IllegalStateException(
    job.getStatus().getError().toString());

// Dry run: bytes before you pay
JobStatistics.QueryStatistics s = bq.create(JobInfo.of(
    q.toBuilder().setDryRun(true).setUseQueryCache(false).build()))
  .getStatistics();
long bytes = s.getTotalBytesProcessed();

For high-volume streaming writes use the Storage Write API (JsonStreamWriter / BigQueryWriteClient), not legacy insertAll.

Apache Beam / Dataflow (Java)

PipelineOptions opts = PipelineOptionsFactory.fromArgs(args).withValidation().create();
Pipeline p = Pipeline.create(opts);

p.apply("Read", BigQueryIO.readTableRows()
        .fromQuery("SELECT cnr, court FROM `proj.odp.hc_judgment`")
        .usingStandardSql())
 .apply("Key", MapElements.into(TypeDescriptors.kvs(
         TypeDescriptors.strings(), TypeDescriptors.longs()))
       .via(r -> KV.of((String) r.get("court"), 1L)))
 .apply(Sum.longsPerKey())
 .apply("ToRow", MapElements.into(TypeDescriptor.of(TableRow.class))
       .via(kv -> new TableRow().set("court", kv.getKey()).set("n", kv.getValue())))
 .apply(BigQueryIO.writeTableRows().to("proj:cdp.court_counts")
       .withSchema(schema)
       .withWriteDisposition(BigQueryIO.Write.WriteDisposition.WRITE_TRUNCATE)
       .withCreateDisposition(BigQueryIO.Write.CreateDisposition.CREATE_IF_NEEDED));
p.run().waitUntilFinish();
// --runner=DataflowRunner --project=… --region=… --tempLocation=gs://…

java --enable-preview Foo

// Containers: size heap from cgroup limits
java -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75 -XX:+UseZGC -jar app.jar

// AOT cache (24/25): faster startup
java -XX:AOTCacheOutput=app.aot -jar app.jar   # training run (25)
java -XX:AOTCache=app.aot -jar app.jar

"Unsupported class file major version" means the build JDK is newer than the tool (often Gradle) supports. Set JAVA_HOME to the version the build expects.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Write classes, records, collections and streams
  • Use Optional and java.time correctly
  • Run and write JUnit tests

2 Practitioner

  • Use modern features (records, sealed types, pattern switch, virtual threads)
  • Handle concurrency safely with executors and CompletableFuture
  • Build with Gradle or Maven and manage dependencies

3 Senior

  • Tune the JVM for containers (memory, GC, startup)
  • Design APIs and module boundaries
  • Profile and fix performance problems

4 Expert

  • Lead platform upgrades across LTS releases
  • Understand JVM internals (JIT, GC, class loading)
  • Set the organisation's Java standards and libraries

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Idioms for pipeline code, the Google Cloud clients, testing and packaging.

Core idioms

from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from pathlib import Path
from datetime import date, datetime

@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class Judgment:
    cnr: str
    decision_date: date | None
    tags: list[str] = field(default_factory=list)

rows = [r for r in rows if r.decision_date]           # filter
by_cnr = {r.cnr: r for r in rows}                     # index
years = {r.decision_date.year for r in rows}          # set

if (n := len(rows)) > 1000: print(f"{n=}")           # walrus + debug f-string

for p in Path("data").glob("**/*.parquet"): ...
text = Path("q.sql").read_text()

d = datetime.strptime("05-05-1959", "%d-%m-%Y").date()
cfg = defaults | overrides                            # 3.9 merge

match (structural pattern matching)

match event:
    case {"state": "SUCCEEDED", "rows": int(n)} if n == 0:
        warn("succeeded with zero rows")
    case {"state": "SUCCEEDED", "rows": int(n)}:
        ok(n)
    case {"state": "FAILED", "error": str(msg)}:
        fail(msg)
    case {"state": state}:
        unknown(state)
    case _:
        raise ValueError(f"bad event: {event!r}")

match path.split("/"):
    case ["data", y, *rest] if y.startswith("year="): ...

Patterns match structure and types; they do not do string prefix matching. Use a guard (if) for that.

Batching & counting

from itertools import batched, groupby, chain, islice
from collections import Counter, defaultdict

for chunk in batched(rows, 500):        # 3.12; tuples of ≤500
    client.insert_rows_json(table, chunk)

counts = Counter(r.court for r in rows)
counts.most_common(5)

by_court = defaultdict(list)
for r in rows: by_court[r.court].append(r)

# groupby needs sorted input
rows.sort(key=lambda r: r.court)
for court, grp in groupby(rows, key=lambda r: r.court): ...

flat = list(chain.from_iterable(nested))
first10 = list(islice(gen, 10))

Errors, logging, context managers

import logging, contextlib, time
log = logging.getLogger(__name__)
logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO,
    format="%(asctime)s %(levelname)s %(name)s %(message)s")

try:
    load(uri)
except NotFound:
    log.warning("missing %s", uri)          # lazy formatting
except Exception:
    log.exception("load failed for %s", uri) # includes traceback
    raise
else:
    log.info("loaded %s", uri)

@contextlib.contextmanager
def timed(label):
    t = time.perf_counter()
    try: yield
    finally: log.info("%s took %.2fs", label, time.perf_counter() - t)

with timed("odp load"): run()

Concurrency

# Threads for blocking I/O (GCS, BQ, HTTP)
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor, as_completed
with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=16) as ex:
    futs = {ex.submit(copy, uri): uri for uri in uris}
    for f in as_completed(futs):
        try: f.result()
        except Exception as e: log.error("%s: %s", futs[f], e)

# asyncio with structured cancellation (3.11)
import asyncio
async def main():
    async with asyncio.TaskGroup() as tg:
        tasks = [tg.create_task(fetch(u)) for u in urls]
    return [t.result() for t in tasks]
asyncio.run(main())

# Processes for CPU-bound work
from concurrent.futures import ProcessPoolExecutor

BigQuery Python client

from google.cloud import bigquery
client = bigquery.Client(project="my-proj", location="asia-south1")

job_cfg = bigquery.QueryJobConfig(query_parameters=[
    bigquery.ScalarQueryParameter("from_d", "DATE", "2024-01-01"),
    bigquery.ArrayQueryParameter("courts", "STRING", ["2_5", "14_25"]),
])
sql = """SELECT court, COUNT(*) n FROM `my-proj.fdp.judgment`
         WHERE decision_date >= @from_d AND court IN UNNEST(@courts)
         GROUP BY court"""
for row in client.query(sql, job_config=job_cfg).result():
    print(row.court, row["n"])

df = client.query(sql, job_config=job_cfg).to_dataframe()   # needs db-dtypes

# Dry run
dry = client.query(sql, job_config=bigquery.QueryJobConfig(
    query_parameters=job_cfg.query_parameters,   # same parameters as the real query
    dry_run=True, use_query_cache=False))
print(dry.total_bytes_processed / 1e9, "GB")

# Load parquet from GCS
load = client.load_table_from_uri(
    "gs://bkt/data/year=2024/*.parquet", "my-proj.odp.hc_judgment",
    job_config=bigquery.LoadJobConfig(
        source_format=bigquery.SourceFormat.PARQUET,
        write_disposition=bigquery.WriteDisposition.WRITE_APPEND))
load.result()                     # raises on failure
print(load.output_rows)

client.insert_rows_json("my-proj.ops.events", [{"k": "v"}])  # returns errors list

pytest

import pytest

@pytest.fixture
def client(monkeypatch):
    fake = FakeBq()
    monkeypatch.setattr("pipeline.bq.client", fake)
    return fake

@pytest.mark.parametrize("raw,expected", [
    ("05-05-1959", date(1959, 5, 5)),
    ("2024-01-31", date(2024, 1, 31)),
])
def test_parse_date(raw, expected):
    assert parse_date(raw) == expected

def test_rejects_garbage():
    with pytest.raises(ValueError, match="does not parse"):
        parse_date("31/31/31")

def test_writes(tmp_path):
    out = tmp_path / "o.json"; write(out); assert out.exists()

Beam / Dataflow (Python)

import apache_beam as beam
from apache_beam.options.pipeline_options import PipelineOptions

with beam.Pipeline(options=PipelineOptions()) as p:
    (p
     | beam.io.ReadFromBigQuery(query="SELECT court FROM `p.odp.hc`",
                                use_standard_sql=True)
     | beam.Map(lambda r: (r["court"], 1))
     | beam.CombinePerKey(sum)
     | beam.Map(lambda kv: {"court": kv[0], "n": kv[1]})
     | beam.io.WriteToBigQuery("p:cdp.court_counts",
           schema="court:STRING,n:INTEGER",
           write_disposition=beam.io.BigQueryDisposition.WRITE_TRUNCATE))

Testing

What to test, at which level, and how, in Java, Python, SQL and pipelines. A test is worth having when it would fail if the code were wrong, and only then.

Levels of testing

LevelChecksSpeedTools
UnitOne function or class in isolation; logic, edge cases, errorsmsJUnit 5 + AssertJ, pytest, dbt unit tests
IntegrationYour code with a real database, queue or HTTP layersecondsTestcontainers, WireMock, @SpringBootTest, @QuarkusTest, BigQuery emulator
ContractProducer and consumer agree on an API or schemasecondsPact, Spring Cloud Contract, schema tests, dbt contracts
End to endA user journey through the deployed systemminutesPlaywright, Selenium, scripted API checks
Data testsThe data itself: keys, nulls, ranges, freshness, row countsper rundbt data tests, assertions in pipelines
Non-functionalLoad, latency, resilience, securityminutes–hoursk6, Gatling, Locust, chaos tests, ZAP

Most tests should be fast unit and integration tests; a few end-to-end tests cover the critical journeys. A suite nobody runs because it is slow protects nothing.

What makes a good test

  • Fails for the right reason: write it first, or break the code on purpose, and watch it go red. A test only ever seen green is unproven.
  • Tests behaviour, not implementation: inputs and outputs, not which private method was called. Refactors should not break it.
  • One reason to fail, with a name that says what it checks: rejectsUnparseableDate.
  • Arrange, act, assert: set up, do one thing, check the result.
  • Independent: no shared state or order between tests; unique test data per test.
  • Deterministic: fixed clock, fixed seed, no real network, no sleeps.
  • Covers the edges: empty, null, one, many, boundary values, bad input, the dependency being down.

TDD in one loop

  1. Red: write a small failing test for the next behaviour.
  2. Green: write the least code that makes it pass.
  3. Refactor: clean up with the tests as a safety net.

For a bug: first write a test that reproduces it and fails, then fix. The test stays, so the bug cannot return.

This is also the best way to brief an AI assistant: give it the failing test as the definition of done.

Java: JUnit 5 + AssertJ + Mockito

class DecisionDateParserTest {

  @ParameterizedTest(name = "{0} → {1}")
  @CsvSource({
      "05-05-1959, 1959-05-05",   // SC format
      "2024-01-31, 2024-01-31",   // HC format
  })
  void parsesBothFormats(String raw, LocalDate expected) {
    assertThat(DecisionDateParser.parse(raw)).isEqualTo(expected);
  }

  @ParameterizedTest
  @ValueSource(strings = {"", "31-31-2024", "yesterday"})
  void rejectsUnparseable(String raw) {
    assertThatThrownBy(() -> DecisionDateParser.parse(raw))
        .isInstanceOf(IllegalArgumentException.class)
        .hasMessageContaining("does not parse");
  }
}

@ExtendWith(MockitoExtension.class)
class LoaderTest {
  @Mock BlobStore store;
  @InjectMocks Loader loader;

  @Test
  void reportsUnknownWhenListingFails() {
    when(store.list(any())).thenThrow(new RuntimeException("403 Forbidden"));
    assertThat(loader.run().state()).isEqualTo(State.UNKNOWN);   // not "0 objects"
    verify(store, never()).copy(any(), any());
  }
}

Python: pytest

import pytest
from datetime import date
from pipeline.dates import parse_decision_date

@pytest.mark.parametrize("raw, expected", [
    ("05-05-1959", date(1959, 5, 5)),
    ("2024-01-31", date(2024, 1, 31)),
])
def test_parses_both_formats(raw, expected):
    assert parse_decision_date(raw) == expected

@pytest.mark.parametrize("raw", ["", "31-31-2024", None])
def test_rejects_unparseable(raw):
    with pytest.raises(ValueError, match="does not parse"):
        parse_decision_date(raw)

@pytest.fixture
def fake_store(monkeypatch):
    store = FakeStore()                 # a small in-memory fake beats a deep mock
    monkeypatch.setattr("pipeline.land.store", store)
    return store

def test_copies_only_new_objects(fake_store):
    fake_store.seed(["a", "b"])
    result = land(source=["a", "b", "c"])
    assert result.copied == 1 and result.already_current == 2

Test doubles: pick the lightest

DoubleIs
DummyPassed but never used
StubReturns canned answers
FakeA working lightweight version (in-memory store)
SpyRecords how it was called
MockPre-programmed with expectations it verifies
  • Mock what you do not own only at the boundary; prefer a fake or a real container.
  • Build stubs from captured real responses, never from a guess. A stub shaped from the docs confirms the guess.
  • Too many mocks means the test checks wiring, not behaviour.

Integration tests with real dependencies

// Java: real Postgres in Docker for the test
@Testcontainers
@SpringBootTest
class JudgmentRepositoryIT {
  @Container @ServiceConnection
  static PostgreSQLContainer<?> pg = new PostgreSQLContainer<>("postgres:16");

  @Autowired JudgmentRepository repo;

  @Test
  void upsertKeepsTheExistingId() {
    long id = repo.upsert(sample("K1")).id();
    long again = repo.upsert(sample("K1").withCnr("changed")).id();
    assertThat(again).isEqualTo(id);          // ids are never renumbered
  }
}

// HTTP dependency: WireMock serving a captured response
stubFor(get(urlPathEqualTo("/objects"))
    .willReturn(aResponse().withStatus(200)
        .withBodyFile("mirror/listing-page-2.xml")));
# Python: testcontainers
from testcontainers.postgres import PostgresContainer
def test_load_roundtrip():
    with PostgresContainer("postgres:16") as pg:
        dsn = pg.get_connection_url()
        load_rows(dsn, rows); assert count(dsn) == len(rows)

Testing data pipelines

  • Idempotency: run the same input twice; the output must be identical (row count and content).
  • Incremental vs full: an incremental run on top of yesterday equals a full rebuild.
  • Edge data: empty partition, one row, duplicates, late rows, a new source column, a malformed date.
  • Reconciliation: rows in = rows loaded + rows rejected + rows skipped, every run.
  • Run records: assert the counters written to the ledger, not only the log line.
  • "Source down": the run must end FAILED or UNKNOWN, never SUCCEEDED with zero.
  • Real dialect: a SQL string checked against a mock proves its shape, not that BigQuery accepts it. Run it on the emulator or a dry run.
# dbt
dbt build -s my_model+            # models + their tests, in order
dbt test  -s test_type:unit       # logic on fixed inputs
dbt test  -s test_type:data       # assertions on built tables

Property-based testing

Instead of hand-picked examples, state a property that must hold for all inputs and let the tool generate hundreds, shrinking any failure to the smallest case.

# Python: hypothesis
from hypothesis import given, strategies as st

@given(st.dates(min_value=date(1950,1,1), max_value=date(2030,12,31)))
def test_format_then_parse_roundtrips(d):
    assert parse_decision_date(d.strftime("%d-%m-%Y")) == d

@given(st.lists(st.text()))
def test_dedupe_is_idempotent(xs):
    assert dedupe(dedupe(xs)) == dedupe(xs)

Java: jqwik (@Property, @ForAll). Good properties: round trips, idempotency, invariants (sorted output stays sorted), and "never throws".

Load and performance tests

// k6: ramp to 60 searches/s and check p95 latency
import http from 'k6/http';
import { check } from 'k6';
export const options = {
  scenarios: { search: { executor: 'ramping-arrival-rate',
    startRate: 5, timeUnit: '1s', preAllocatedVUs: 50,
    stages: [{ target: 60, duration: '5m' }, { target: 60, duration: '10m' }] } },
  thresholds: { http_req_duration: ['p(95)<500'], http_req_failed: ['rate<0.01'] },
};
export default function () {
  const r = http.get(`${__ENV.BASE}/api/search?q=land+acquisition`);
  check(r, { '200': (x) => x.status === 200 });
}
// k6 run -e BASE=https://staging.example.com search.js
  • Test against production-sized data in a production-like environment.
  • Measure percentiles (p95, p99), not averages.
  • Find the breaking point, then decide the alert threshold below it.

Flaky tests, coverage, CI

  • Flaky test: passes and fails on the same code. Causes: time, ordering, shared state, real network, async waits. Fix or delete it; never retry it into green or quarantine it forever.
  • Coverage shows what was executed, not what was checked. 100% coverage with weak assertions proves little. Use it to find untested code, not as a target.
  • Mutation testing (PIT for Java, mutmut for Python) changes your code and checks that some test fails. It measures test strength.
  • CI runs every test on every PR, on a clean checkout. A local green and a CI red on the same commit usually mean untracked files or a different environment.
  • Never skip, disable or weaken a test to get green. If a test is wrong, fix it in its own reviewed change.

Testing AI features

  • Unit-test everything around the model: prompt building, parsing, retries, fallbacks.
  • Validate structured outputs against the schema, and the values against your data.
  • Run an eval set in CI and track the score; fail the build on a regression beyond a threshold.
  • Include adversarial cases: prompt injection in documents, empty retrieval, the model refusing.
  • Mock the model in unit tests with recorded real responses; run the eval against the real model.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Write unit tests with clear arrange-act-assert
  • Run the suite locally and read CI failures
  • Reproduce a bug with a failing test before fixing it

2 Practitioner

  • Use Testcontainers and WireMock for integration tests
  • Test edge cases, errors and dependency failures
  • Keep tests fast, independent and deterministic

3 Senior

  • Design a test strategy across unit, integration, contract and end to end
  • Add property-based, load and mutation testing where they pay off
  • Eliminate flaky tests and slow suites

4 Expert

  • Set testing standards and quality gates for the organisation
  • Build test infrastructure: environments, data, emulators
  • Measure test effectiveness, not just coverage

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Explore further

  • Contract testing with Pact
  • Mutation testing with PIT or mutmut
  • Test data management and synthetic data
  • Testing in production: canaries and feature flags
  • Chaos engineering

Security

The risks every engineer must recognise, and the habits that prevent most of them: in application code, APIs, data, cloud and the supply chain.

Principles

  • Least privilege: every person, service and token gets only the access it needs, for only as long as it needs it.
  • Defence in depth: several independent layers (network, identity, application, data), so one failure is not a breach.
  • Secure by default: private unless opened, denied unless allowed, encrypted unless there is a reason not to be.
  • Never trust input: anything from a user, another service, a file, a web page or a model's output is validated before use.
  • No secrets in code, images, logs or chat. They live in a secret manager and are injected at runtime.
  • Fail closed: when an auth or permission check cannot run, deny. A gate that opens on error is not a gate.
  • Assume breach: log and alert so that a compromise is noticed and contained quickly.

OWASP Top 10 (2021 edition)

A 2025 edition of the list has been published; compare it at owasp.org. The categories below remain a sound checklist.

RiskLooks likePrevent with
A01 Broken access controlGET /api/cases/123 returns another user's caseCheck ownership on every request, server side; deny by default; test it
A02 Cryptographic failuresPlain HTTP, passwords hashed with MD5, keys in the repoTLS everywhere, argon2id/bcrypt for passwords, managed keys
A03 InjectionSQL built by string concatenation; shell commands with user inputParameterised queries, no shell with input, allow-lists
A04 Insecure designNo rate limit on login; reset tokens that never expireThreat model at design time; abuse cases as requirements
A05 Security misconfigurationPublic bucket, debug endpoints on, default passwords, open CORSInfrastructure as code, reviewed; secure defaults; scanners
A06 Vulnerable componentsAn old library with a known CVEDependency updates, SCA scanning, SBOMs
A07 Authentication failuresWeak passwords allowed, no MFA, session never expiresUse a proven identity provider; MFA; short-lived tokens
A08 Integrity failuresUnsigned build artefacts; deserialising untrusted dataSigned images, pinned dependencies, protected CI
A09 Logging & monitoring failuresA breach discovered months laterAudit logs, alerts on suspicious patterns, retained logs
A10 Server-side request forgeryServer fetches a user-supplied URL that points to an internal service or metadata endpointAllow-list destinations; block internal ranges; separate egress

OWASP revises this list every few years; check owasp.org for the latest edition. The API Security Top 10 is the companion list for APIs.

Injection: always parameterise

// Java JDBC
// WRONG: "SELECT * FROM judgment WHERE cnr = '" + cnr + "'"
PreparedStatement ps = conn.prepareStatement(
    "SELECT * FROM judgment WHERE cnr = ?");
ps.setString(1, cnr);

// Spring JdbcClient
jdbc.sql("SELECT * FROM judgment WHERE cnr = :cnr").param("cnr", cnr)...

# Python psycopg
cur.execute("SELECT * FROM judgment WHERE cnr = %s", (cnr,))   # never f-strings

# BigQuery
bigquery.QueryJobConfig(query_parameters=[
    bigquery.ScalarQueryParameter("cnr", "STRING", cnr)])      # WHERE cnr = @cnr
  • Identifiers (table and column names) cannot be parameters: pick them from an allow-list.
  • Never pass user input to a shell. Use the library call, or an argument list without a shell.
  • Escape output for its context (HTML, JSON, URLs) to prevent XSS. Use the framework's templating, not string building.

Authentication & authorisation

  • Authentication is who you are; authorisation is what you may do. Most breaches are authorisation bugs.
  • Use OIDC / OAuth 2.0 with a proven provider rather than building login yourself. For browser apps, the authorisation code flow with PKCE.
  • JWTs: verify the signature, issuer, audience and expiry on every request; keep them short-lived; never accept alg: none; never put secrets in them (they are only encoded).
  • Refresh tokens rotate on use and are revocable; in browsers keep them in HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite cookies.
  • Check permissions on the server for every request and every object: "is this user allowed this case?", not just "is the user logged in?".
  • Load roles from the source of truth on each authentication; do not trust roles a client sends.
  • Passwords: argon2id or bcrypt, rate-limited login, breached-password checks, MFA for admins.

Spring Security: a stateless API

@Bean
SecurityFilterChain api(HttpSecurity http) throws Exception {
  return http
    .authorizeHttpRequests(a -> a
        .requestMatchers("/actuator/health/**").permitAll()
        .requestMatchers("/api/admin/**").hasRole("ADMIN")
        .anyRequest().authenticated())            // deny by default
    .oauth2ResourceServer(o -> o.jwt(Customizer.withDefaults()))
    .sessionManagement(s -> s.sessionCreationPolicy(SessionCreationPolicy.STATELESS))
    .csrf(c -> c.disable())   // only safe when auth is a bearer header, not a cookie
    .build();
}

// object-level check in the service, not only the URL rule
if (!caseFile.ownerId().equals(currentUserId)) throw new AccessDeniedException("not yours");

Web & API hardening

  • HTTPS only, with HSTS (Strict-Transport-Security).
  • Content-Security-Policy to limit where scripts load from; X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff; frame-ancestors against clickjacking.
  • CORS: list exact origins; never reflect any origin with credentials allowed.
  • CSRF: needed when the browser sends cookies automatically; use tokens or SameSite cookies.
  • Rate limits on login, signup, password reset and expensive endpoints.
  • Validate request bodies (types, lengths, ranges) and reject unknown fields where it matters.
  • Errors: generic messages to the client, details to the logs. No stack traces in responses.
  • Uploads: check type and size, store outside the web root, scan if users share them.

Secrets

# store
echo -n "$VALUE" | gcloud secrets create db-password --data-file=-
# grant one workload access to one secret
gcloud secrets add-iam-policy-binding db-password \
  --member=serviceAccount:api@p.iam.gserviceaccount.com \
  --role=roles/secretmanager.secretAccessor
# rotate: add a version, roll the workload, then disable and destroy the old one
gcloud secrets versions add db-password --data-file=new.txt
gcloud secrets versions destroy 1 --secret=db-password
  • No service account JSON keys: Workload Identity Federation for CI, attached service accounts for workloads.
  • A rotation is not finished until old versions are destroyed.
  • Scan commits for secrets (gitleaks, GitHub secret scanning). A leaked secret is rotated, not just deleted from Git: history and forks keep it.
  • Never print secrets in logs, CI output, error messages or AI prompts.

Cloud security on GCP

  • IAM: one service account per workload; predefined narrow roles; no Owner or Editor for services; grant to groups, not individuals.
  • Organisation policies: block public buckets, service account key creation, and external IPs where not needed.
  • Network: private IPs for databases; ingress limited to the load balancer; Cloud Armor in front of public apps.
  • Data: encryption at rest is on by default; CMEK when you must control keys; VPC Service Controls around sensitive datasets.
  • Audit logs: Admin Activity is always on; enable Data Access logs for sensitive services so reads are attributable.
  • Security Command Center surfaces misconfigurations and threats across the project.
  • Separate projects for dev and prod; nobody's laptop has standing prod write access.

Threat modelling in 30 minutes

  1. Draw the data flow: users, services, stores, and the trust boundaries between them.
  2. For each boundary, ask STRIDE questions.
  3. List the threats, rate likelihood and impact, and pick mitigations.
  4. Turn mitigations into tickets and tests.
STRIDEQuestion
SpoofingCan someone pretend to be another user or service?
TamperingCan data be changed in transit or at rest?
RepudiationCan someone deny an action because nothing recorded it?
Information disclosureCan data leak to someone not entitled to it?
Denial of serviceCan someone exhaust a resource or the budget?
Elevation of privilegeCan someone gain rights they should not have?

Data protection & privacy

  • Know where personal data is: tag columns and datasets; keep an inventory.
  • Collect less, keep less: only what the purpose needs, with a retention period.
  • Mask or tokenise personal data in non-production environments and analytics.
  • Honour legal obligations: deletion requests, court suppression orders, consent. Apply them on every read path, not only in one table.
  • Know the law that applies: for India, the Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023; GDPR for EU residents.
  • Logs are data too: no personal data or tokens in log lines unless necessary and protected.

Supply chain & CI

  • Pin dependencies with lockfiles; review what new dependencies bring.
  • Automated updates (Dependabot, Renovate) gated by tests.
  • Scan: SAST (CodeQL, Semgrep), SCA (Dependabot alerts, OSV), images (Trivy), secrets (gitleaks).
  • Pin third-party GitHub Actions to a commit SHA; give workflows the minimum permissions:.
  • Protect main and deploy tags; require reviews and checks; restrict who can push release tags.
  • Sign images and generate SBOMs so you can prove what is running.

Security testing

  • Unit and integration tests for access control: user A cannot read user B's object; an expired token is refused; a missing role gets 403.
  • SAST reads code for dangerous patterns; DAST (OWASP ZAP) probes the running app.
  • Dependency and image scans on every build.
  • Fuzzing parsers and file handlers with malformed input.
  • Penetration test before launch and after major changes, by someone independent.
  • Prove each guard red: try the forbidden action in a test and watch it fail.

Security checklist for a pull request

  • Is every new endpoint authenticated, and does it check ownership of each object?
  • Is all input validated, and every query parameterised?
  • Any new secret? Is it in the secret manager, not in code or config?
  • Any new permission or IAM role? Is it the narrowest that works?
  • Does anything new get logged that should not be (tokens, personal data)?
  • New dependency: maintained, licensed acceptably, no known CVEs?
  • What happens on error: does it fail closed?
  • Is there a test that proves the forbidden case is refused?

When something goes wrong

  1. Contain: revoke the leaked key, disable the account, block the IP, take the endpoint down.
  2. Preserve evidence: keep logs and snapshots before cleaning up.
  3. Assess: what was accessed, since when, whose data.
  4. Notify: security lead, then customers and regulators as the law requires and within its deadlines.
  5. Fix the cause and add a guard (test, policy, alert) so it cannot recur.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Parameterise every query; never commit secrets
  • Check that users can only reach their own data
  • Apply the PR security checklist

2 Practitioner

  • Implement OAuth 2.0 / OIDC with a provider and validate tokens properly
  • Use least-privilege IAM and a secret manager
  • Fix findings from SAST, SCA and image scans

3 Senior

  • Threat-model a system and turn threats into tests
  • Design authentication, authorisation and audit for a product
  • Respond to and contain a security incident

4 Expert

  • Own the security architecture and policies across teams
  • Lead compliance (DPDP, GDPR, SOC 2) and external testing
  • Build a security culture and secure-by-default platforms

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

  • Supply-chain security with SLSA
