OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect

How delegated authorisation and login work, which flow to use for which client, how to validate tokens, and the mistakes that cause breaches. Broader security is on Security.

The roles and the two standards

RoleIsExample
Resource ownerThe user who owns the dataA lawyer
ClientThe app that wants accessThe web app, a mobile app, a partner's server
Authorisation serverAuthenticates the user and issues tokensGoogle Identity, Okta, Auth0, Keycloak, Entra ID
Resource serverThe API that accepts tokensThe search API, or Apigee in front of it
  • OAuth 2.0 is about authorisation: giving a client an access token to call an API on someone's behalf, limited by scopes.
  • OpenID Connect (OIDC) adds authentication on top: an ID token (a JWT) that says who the user is. "Sign in with Google" is OIDC.
  • Access token goes to APIs. ID token is for the client to learn who logged in; never send it to APIs as an access token. Refresh token gets new access tokens without a new login.

Which flow for which client

ClientFlowNotes
Web app with a serverAuthorisation code + PKCEConfidential client: the server holds the secret and tokens
Single-page app (browser)Authorisation code + PKCE, ideally through a backend-for-frontendThe BFF keeps tokens server-side and gives the browser a secure cookie
Mobile / desktop appAuthorisation code + PKCE via the system browserPublic client: no secret can be kept safe in the app
Service to serviceClient credentialsNo user; the service authenticates as itself
TV, CLI, device without a browserDevice authorisation grantUser approves on another device with a code
Workloads on GCPService account identity (Google-signed tokens)No secrets at all; Workload Identity Federation from outside GCP
Implicit, passwordDo not useDeprecated by current best practice (RFC 9700, OAuth 2.1 draft)

Authorisation code + PKCE, step by step

1. Client makes a random code_verifier and its hash:
   code_challenge = BASE64URL(SHA256(code_verifier))

2. Browser is sent to the authorisation server:
   GET /authorize?response_type=code
       &client_id=legal-web
       &redirect_uri=https://legal.example.com/callback
       &scope=openid%20profile%20email%20search.read
       &state=RANDOM            (CSRF protection)
       &nonce=RANDOM            (ID-token replay protection)
       &code_challenge=…&code_challenge_method=S256

3. User logs in and consents; server redirects back:
   https://legal.example.com/callback?code=AUTH_CODE&state=RANDOM
   (client checks state matches)

4. Client exchanges the code (server to server):
   POST /token
   grant_type=authorization_code&code=AUTH_CODE
   &redirect_uri=…&client_id=legal-web&code_verifier=ORIGINAL

5. Response: access_token, id_token, refresh_token, expires_in

6. Client calls the API:
   Authorization: Bearer ACCESS_TOKEN

PKCE proves the party redeeming the code is the one that started the flow, so a stolen code is useless.

Client credentials (machine to machine)

curl -s https://auth.example.com/oauth2/token \
  -u "$CLIENT_ID:$CLIENT_SECRET" \
  -d grant_type=client_credentials \
  -d scope=judgments.read

# → {"access_token":"…","token_type":"Bearer","expires_in":3600}
curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" https://api.example.com/v1/judgments/123
  • Cache the token until shortly before it expires; do not fetch one per call.
  • Prefer private-key JWT or mTLS client authentication over shared secrets where supported.
  • On GCP, prefer service account identity over client secrets.

Validating an access token (JWT)

  1. Fetch the issuer's keys from its JWKS URL (from /.well-known/openid-configuration); cache them.
  2. Verify the signature with the key matching the token's kid; accept only the expected algorithm (e.g. RS256 or ES256). Never none.
  3. Check iss equals your issuer exactly.
  4. Check aud contains your API's identifier.
  5. Check exp (and nbf) with small clock skew.
  6. Check required scopes for the operation.
  7. Then check object-level permission in your own code.
# Python (PyJWT)
jwks = jwt.PyJWKClient("https://auth.example.com/.well-known/jwks.json")
key = jwks.get_signing_key_from_jwt(token)
claims = jwt.decode(token, key.key, algorithms=["RS256"],
                    audience="https://api.example.com", issuer="https://auth.example.com/")
# Spring Boot resource server
spring.security.oauth2.resourceserver.jwt.issuer-uri: https://auth.example.com/
spring.security.oauth2.resourceserver.jwt.audiences: https://api.example.com

# Quarkus
quarkus.oidc.auth-server-url=https://auth.example.com/
quarkus.oidc.token.audience=https://api.example.com

Opaque (non-JWT) tokens are validated by calling the server's introspection endpoint (RFC 7662).

Scopes, claims and roles

  • Scopes limit what a client may do on the user's behalf (judgments.read). They are about the app, not the user's own rights.
  • Claims are facts in the token: sub (stable user ID), email, groups, custom claims.
  • Roles and permissions are your application's rules. Load them from your source of truth; do not trust a role the client could have influenced.
  • Key users by sub plus issuer, not by email, which can change or be reused.
  • Keep tokens small; large claim sets slow every request.

Tokens in browsers and apps

  • Best for SPAs: a backend-for-frontend does the OAuth flow and keeps tokens; the browser gets an HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite session cookie.
  • Avoid localStorage for tokens: any XSS can read it.
  • Short access tokens (minutes) and rotating refresh tokens that are revoked if reused.
  • Mobile: store refresh tokens in the platform keychain / keystore; use the system browser, not an embedded web view, for login.
  • Logout revokes refresh tokens and ends the provider session if needed.

Common mistakes

  • Accepting a token without checking audience: a token for another API works on yours.
  • Using the ID token to call APIs.
  • Loose redirect URIs (wildcards, prefix matching): codes get sent to an attacker.
  • Missing or unchecked state: login CSRF.
  • Implicit flow or tokens in URLs, where they leak through logs and referrers.
  • Long-lived access tokens with no revocation.
  • Trusting scopes alone and skipping object-level checks.
  • Rolling your own authorisation server instead of using a maintained one.

Beyond the basics

  • Sender-constrained tokens (DPoP or mutual TLS) make a stolen token useless to anyone else.
  • Pushed authorisation requests (PAR) send the request parameters server to server first.
  • Token exchange (RFC 8693) for a service calling another on the user's behalf.
  • OAuth 2.1 (draft) consolidates current best practice: PKCE everywhere, no implicit or password grants.
  • OAuth for AI agents and MCP servers: agents act with delegated, scoped, revocable tokens, never the user's password.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain access, ID and refresh tokens and scopes
  • Protect an API with a JWT resource server configuration
  • Use the authorisation code flow with PKCE through a library

2 Practitioner

  • Validate tokens fully: signature, issuer, audience, expiry, scopes
  • Choose the right flow per client type
  • Store tokens safely in web and mobile apps

3 Senior

  • Design identity for a product: providers, BFF, service-to-service, token lifetimes
  • Review OAuth implementations for the common mistakes
  • Integrate gateways (Apigee) and workload identity

4 Expert

  • Own identity architecture across the organisation
  • Apply advanced specs (DPoP, PAR, token exchange)
  • Lead identity incidents and provider migrations

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Explore further

Study next

  • RFC 9700 security best current practice
  • OpenID Connect discovery and dynamic registration
  • Token exchange (RFC 8693)
  • Sender-constrained tokens (DPoP, mTLS)
  • Workload Identity Federation

Official docs and good reading

  • OAuth 2.0 (oauth.net) Overview and links to every OAuth spec
  • RFC 6749: OAuth 2.0 The core framework
  • RFC 7636: PKCE Proof Key for Code Exchange
  • RFC 9700: OAuth 2.0 Security Best Current Practice Current security guidance
  • OpenID Connect Core The OIDC specification

What data engineering is

The concepts behind the tools: what data engineers build, the shape of every pipeline, and the properties that make data trustworthy. Read this before the dbt, DAG and BigQuery tabs.

In one paragraph

Data engineering is building and running the systems that move data from where it is produced to where it is used, and making it correct, current, documented and affordable on the way. Source systems (apps, files, APIs, sensors, public datasets) produce data in their own shapes and at their own pace. Analysts, dashboards, machine-learning models, AI features and products need it in a shape they can query and trust. The data engineer owns the path between them: getting it in, keeping a faithful copy, reshaping it, checking it, delivering it, and knowing at any moment whether it is right.

A pipeline that runs is not the goal. The goal is data that people can rely on without asking the data engineer first.

Who does what

RoleFocusTypical output
Data engineerIngestion, storage, pipelines, platform, reliabilityLanded raw data, loaded tables, orchestrated jobs, a working platform
Analytics engineerModelling data for use, mostly in SQL and dbtClean, tested, documented models and metrics
Data analystAnswering business questionsQueries, dashboards, reports
Data scientistStatistics, experiments, predictive modelsAnalyses, models, experiment results
ML / AI engineerPutting models into products and running themFeatures, training and inference pipelines, evals, AI features
Backend engineerApplications that create and serve dataAPIs and services, the source systems data engineers read from

In small teams one person covers several rows. The boundaries matter less than knowing who owns each table.

The lifecycle every pipeline follows

StageQuestion it answersOn GCP, for exampleTab
GenerationWhere does the data come from, how often, in what shape, under what licence?App databases, partner files, public mirrors, event streams—
IngestionHow do we get a faithful copy without losing or duplicating anything?Storage Transfer Service, Cloud Run jobs, Pub/Sub, Datastream (CDC)Cloud Run
StorageWhere does each version live, for how long, at what cost?GCS (files), BigQuery (tables), Cloud SQL (serving)BigQuery
TransformationHow do raw records become clean, conformed, useful tables?dbt on BigQuery, Dataflow (Beam), Dataproc (Spark)dbt
ServingHow do consumers read it, fast enough and safely?BigQuery views, Postgres for an API, Looker, exports, vector indexes for AISpring

Running underneath every stage: orchestration (DAGs), data quality and observability, security and access, data management (catalog, lineage, retention), infrastructure as code (Terraform), and ordinary software engineering (tests, CI, reviews).

This stage model follows Fundamentals of Data Engineering by Joe Reis and Matt Housley, a good first book on the subject.

Batch vs streaming

  • Batch: process a bounded chunk (yesterday, one file, one partition) on a schedule. Simple, cheap, easy to rerun. Latency of minutes to a day.
  • Streaming: process events continuously as they arrive. Latency of seconds, but harder: ordering, late events, state, exactly-once.
  • Micro-batch: small batches every few minutes. Often the practical middle.
  • Choose by the consumer's need, not by fashion. Most reporting and legal research is fine with daily or hourly batch.

ETL vs ELT

  • ETL: extract, transform in a separate engine, then load the result. Common when the warehouse was expensive or small.
  • ELT: extract, load the raw data into the warehouse, transform there with SQL (dbt). The modern default on BigQuery and similar.
  • Why ELT wins today: you keep the raw data, so a bug in a transform is fixed by rerunning it, not by re-ingesting from a source that may have changed or gone.
  • Heavy non-SQL work (parsing PDFs, images, large joins outside the warehouse) still goes to an engine built for it, such as Dataflow or Spark.

Where data lives

StoreGood at
OLTP database (Postgres)Many small reads and writes by key; serving an app
Warehouse / OLAP (BigQuery)Scanning and aggregating billions of rows; analytics
Data lake (GCS)Cheap storage of any file, raw and forever
Lakehouse (Iceberg / Delta on object storage)Warehouse-style tables over lake files, readable by several engines
Vector indexSimilarity search for AI retrieval

File formats: CSV and JSON are easy but untyped and slow to scan. Parquet and ORC are columnar, compressed and typed, best for analytics. Avro suits row-by-row streams with schema evolution.

Layers: each one has one job

LayerAlso calledRule
Raw / landingbronze, RAWByte-for-byte copy of what the source published. Never edited. Lets you replay everything.
Operational / stagingODP, stagingThe raw data as typed tables, as published. Renamed and cast, not reinterpreted.
Foundation / conformedsilver, FDP, intermediateCleaned, deduplicated, keyed, versioned, joined to reference data. Structure added once, reused by many.
Consumable / martsgold, CDP, martsShaped for one named use: a dashboard, an API, a model's features.

Derivation runs one way: each layer reads only the layer below. A product that reads from a downstream serving database creates a cycle and should be refused.

Modelling basics

  • Grain: what one row means ("one judgment", "one order line per day"). State it for every table; most bugs are grain confusion.
  • Keys: a natural key from the source and, where needed, a surrogate key you generate. Never reuse or renumber a key that others store.
  • Normalised (many narrow tables, no repetition) suits OLTP. Dimensional (facts plus dimensions, a star schema) suits analytics. One big table is fine on BigQuery for a single use.
  • Facts are events with measures (a decision, a payment). Dimensions describe them (court, judge, date).
  • Slowly changing dimensions: type 1 overwrites; type 2 keeps history with valid-from/valid-to rows (dbt snapshots).

Properties of a good pipeline

  • Idempotent: running it twice for the same input gives the same result. Overwrite a partition or MERGE on a key; never blindly append.
  • Incremental: processes only what is new or changed, decided from evidence (a watermark, ETag or ledger), not "we visited it".
  • Backfillable: any past period can be rerun on purpose.
  • Late data aware: records can arrive after their period; reprocess a lookback window.
  • Schema-change safe: a new or renamed source column fails loudly or is handled deliberately, never silently dropped.
  • Observable: every run records what it read, wrote, skipped and failed, with counts.
  • Resumable: a failure midway restarts from where it stopped, not from zero.

Data quality

DimensionExample check
CompletenessRow count matches the source; no unexpected NULLs
ValidityDates parse; codes are in the allowed set
UniquenessOne row per key at the stated grain
ConsistencyEvery court code exists in the court table
FreshnessNewest record is within the expected age
AccuracyValues match the source on a sample

The worst quality failure is a silent one: a parse that turns bad dates into NULL, or a monitor that reports 0 when its source was unreachable. Reject or count what you cannot read, and report "unknown" rather than a confident zero.

Ingestion patterns

  • Full snapshot: copy everything each time. Simple; fine for small tables.
  • Incremental by watermark: fetch rows changed since the last updated_at or ID.
  • Change data capture (CDC): read the source database's change log for inserts, updates and deletes (Datastream, Debezium).
  • File drops: compare source and destination listings (names, sizes, ETags) and copy only the difference.
  • Events: producers publish to a queue (Pub/Sub, Kafka); consumers process at-least-once, so handle duplicates.
  • APIs: paginate, respect rate limits, keep the raw response, and record the error body when a call fails.

Governance & responsibility

  • Ownership: every table has a named owner and a description.
  • Lineage: you can say which sources feed a column and which products a change will affect.
  • Access: least privilege per dataset; serving systems get read-only access.
  • Personal and sensitive data: know where it is, mask or restrict it, honour deletion and suppression orders.
  • Licences: respect source terms; carry attribution (for example CC-BY) into every published output.
  • Retention and cost: decide how long each layer is kept and who pays for scans.
  • Contracts: agree the schema and meaning of anything another team reads, and change it on purpose.

A worked example, end to end

Indian court judgments published as open data, served to lawyers through a search API.

  1. Source: two public mirrors publish judgment metadata as Parquet and JSON archives, partitioned by year, under CC-BY-4.0. Partitions are sometimes republished in place.
  2. Ingest (LAND): a Cloud Run job compares the mirror's listing with the GCS landing bucket and copies only new or changed objects. It records examined, copied, already-current and failed counts.
  3. Raw: the bucket keeps the originals byte for byte, so anything later can be replayed.
  4. Load (ODP): each landed file becomes rows in BigQuery tables, with run ID and load time, exactly as published.
  5. Transform (FDP): dbt cleans and conforms: parses two date formats (and rejects what does not parse), computes a stable judgment key, deduplicates republished rows, joins court reference data, and runs tests.
  6. Consumable (CDP): a dbt model shapes exactly what the search API needs, with a contract on its columns.
  7. Serve: a loader upserts that table into Postgres on the stable key, without renumbering existing IDs that links and legal records depend on. The API reads Postgres read-only.
  8. Orchestrate and observe: a scheduler ticks daily; an orchestrator runs each stage when its input is ready and appends every state change to a ledger, so "what happened on 2026-09-19?" has an answer.

Every tab in this guide appears somewhere in that path. That is why a data engineer needs breadth.

Questions a data engineer always asks

  • What does one row mean, and what is its key?
  • What happens if this runs twice? If it fails halfway?
  • What does this return when the source is down, and can anyone tell that apart from "no data"?
  • How will I know it is late or wrong before a user does?
  • Who reads this table, and what breaks for them if I change it?
  • How much does a run cost, and what makes it grow?
  • Can I rebuild it from raw if I have to?

Glossary

  • Partition: a slice of a table (by date or year) that queries can skip.
  • Watermark: the point up to which data is known to be processed.
  • Backfill: deliberately reprocessing a past period.
  • Upsert / MERGE: insert new rows, update existing ones, by key.
  • Lineage: the graph of which data feeds which.
  • Data contract: an agreed schema and meaning between producer and consumer.
  • Freshness: how old the newest data is.
  • SCD: slowly changing dimension, how history of descriptive attributes is kept.
  • CDC: change data capture, streaming a database's changes.
  • DAG: directed acyclic graph, the dependency order of tasks.
  • Ledger: an append-only record of every run and state change.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain the lifecycle and the layers
  • Load a file into a warehouse and query it
  • Write a simple, idempotent transformation

2 Practitioner

  • Build incremental, tested pipelines end to end
  • Choose batch vs streaming and ingestion patterns
  • Model data at a stated grain with keys and tests

3 Senior

  • Design a data platform: layers, contracts, orchestration, observability
  • Own data quality and reconciliation across sources
  • Plan cost, retention and governance

4 Expert

  • Set data architecture and standards across the organisation
  • Lead migrations between platforms without losing trust
  • Shape data products and their contracts with consumers

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Data processing libraries

The major libraries and engines for processing data in Python and Java, what scale each suits, the same task written in several of them, and the habits that make any of them fast. Frameworks that sit around these engines, such as Culvert, are on their own tab.

The landscape

Library / engineLanguageRuns onModelBest for
pandasPythonOne machine, mostly one coreEager, in memoryAnalysis and small-to-medium data that fits in RAM; huge ecosystem
PolarsPython (Rust core)One machine, all coresLazy or eager, Arrow-basedFast DataFrame work on tens of GB; query optimisation
DuckDBSQL from Python, Java, CLIIn-process, all coresColumnar SQL engineSQL over Parquet/CSV/Arrow files, local or in object storage
Apache Arrow / PyArrowManyOne machineColumnar memory format and Parquet IOReading and writing Parquet, zero-copy exchange between tools
NumPyPythonOne machineN-dimensional arraysNumerical computation underneath most Python data tools
DaskPythonMany cores or a clusterParallel pandas/NumPy-like collectionsScaling existing pandas code beyond one core
Ray (Ray Data)PythonClusterDistributed tasks and actorsPython-heavy distributed work, ML preprocessing
Apache Spark / PySparkScala, Java, Python, SQLCluster (Dataproc, EMR, Databricks)Distributed DataFrames, lazyLarge batch processing, existing Spark estates
Apache BeamJava, Python, GoRunners: Dataflow, Flink, Spark, directUnified batch and streaming pipelinesPortable pipelines; streaming on Dataflow
Apache FlinkJava, SQL, PythonClusterStateful stream processingLow-latency streaming with large state
Kafka StreamsJavaInside your serviceStream processing libraryStateful processing of Kafka topics without a cluster
BigQuery DataFrames (bigframes)PythonBigQuerypandas-like API compiled to SQLpandas-style code on data that stays in BigQuery
dbtSQL + JinjaYour warehouseModels compiled to SQLTransformations inside the warehouse (dbt tab)
Spring BatchJavaOne JVM or partitionedChunked read-process-write jobs with restartBusiness batch jobs in Spring applications

Choosing by situation

  • Data already in BigQuery: SQL or dbt first; bigframes if you need a DataFrame API. Do not pull it out to process it.
  • Files up to a few GB on one machine: Polars or DuckDB; pandas if a library you need expects it.
  • Tens to low hundreds of GB on one large machine: Polars (lazy, streaming) or DuckDB.
  • Beyond one machine, batch: BigQuery, Dataflow (Beam) or Spark on Dataproc.
  • Streaming: Dataflow (Beam), Flink, or Kafka Streams inside a service.
  • Inside a Java service: plain Java streams for small sets; Beam for pipelines; Spring Batch for restartable business jobs.
  • Reading and writing Parquet in Python: PyArrow underneath, whatever DataFrame you use on top.

Validation libraries

  • pandera: schemas for pandas and Polars DataFrames (types, ranges, nullability) checked in code and tests.
  • Great Expectations: expectation suites and data docs for pipelines.
  • Pydantic: validating individual records and API payloads in Python.
  • dbt tests: keys, relationships and accepted values in the warehouse.
  • Jakarta Bean Validation: record validation in Java.

Whatever the tool: reject or quarantine what does not parse, and count it. Never coerce failures to NULL silently.

The same task in five libraries

Count judgments per court since 2020 from Parquet files partitioned by year.

# pandas: reads into memory, eager
import pandas as pd
df = pd.read_parquet("data/", columns=["court", "year"],
                     filters=[("year", ">=", 2020)])   # pushdown; partition columns arrive as category
out = (df.groupby("court", observed=True).size()
         .sort_values(ascending=False))
# Polars: lazy; filters and column selection pushed into the scan
import polars as pl
out = (pl.scan_parquet("data/year=*/*.parquet", hive_partitioning=True)
         .filter(pl.col("year") >= 2020)
         .group_by("court").agg(pl.len().alias("n"))
         .sort("n", descending=True)
         .collect())
# DuckDB: SQL straight over the files
import duckdb
out = duckdb.sql("""
  SELECT court, count(*) AS n
  FROM read_parquet('data/year=*/*.parquet', hive_partitioning = true)
  WHERE year >= 2020
  GROUP BY court ORDER BY n DESC
""").df()
# PySpark: distributed, writes results back to GCS
from pyspark.sql import SparkSession, functions as F
spark = SparkSession.builder.getOrCreate()
(spark.read.parquet("gs://landing/data/")
   .where(F.col("year") >= 2020)
   .groupBy("court").count()
   .orderBy(F.desc("count"))
   .write.mode("overwrite").parquet("gs://out/court_counts/"))
# BigQuery DataFrames: pandas-like code, executed inside BigQuery
import bigframes.pandas as bpd
df = bpd.read_gbq("my-proj.dp_fdp.fdp_judgment")
out = df[df["data_year"] >= 2020].groupby("court_code").size()

Arrow and Parquet in Python

import pyarrow.dataset as ds
import pyarrow.parquet as pq

dataset = ds.dataset("data/", format="parquet", partitioning="hive")
table = dataset.to_table(columns=["cnr", "court", "decision_date"],
                         filter=ds.field("year") >= 2020)   # pushdown

print(table.schema)          # check types before trusting them
pq.write_table(table, "out.parquet",
               compression="zstd",
               coerce_timestamps="us",           # many readers expect microseconds
               allow_truncated_timestamps=True)

Timestamp units are a classic trap: Parquet written with nanosecond timestamps can be read wrongly or refused by some engines, especially for dates before 1970. Check the schema and write the unit the reader expects.

Java options

  • Java streams and collectors for in-memory work (Java tab).
  • Apache Beam (Java) for pipelines on Dataflow, Flink or Spark.
  • Spark (Java/Scala) on Dataproc or Databricks.
  • Spring Batch for chunked, restartable jobs with skip and retry policies.
  • parquet-java and Arrow Java for columnar file IO.
  • Jackson (JSON), Apache Commons CSV or FastCSV for text formats; stream rather than loading whole files.
  • DuckDB JDBC for SQL over local or remote Parquet from Java.

Habits that make any library fast

  • Columnar formats (Parquet) over CSV and JSON for anything read more than once.
  • Read only the columns and rows you need (projection and predicate pushdown).
  • Lazy evaluation (Polars, Spark, Beam) lets the engine optimise the whole plan.
  • No row-by-row loops (apply, iterrows); use vectorised expressions.
  • Right types: categoricals for repeated strings, Arrow-backed strings, proper dates.
  • Partition by the common filter (date, year); avoid thousands of tiny files.
  • Stream or chunk data larger than memory.
  • Push work to the warehouse when the data is already there.

Correctness traps

  • Silent type coercion: integers with nulls become floats in classic pandas; IDs with leading zeros become numbers. Set dtypes explicitly.
  • Date parsing: mixed formats (dd-mm-yyyy and yyyy-mm-dd) parse wrongly or to null; specify the format and count failures.
  • Time zones: naive vs aware timestamps compared silently.
  • Joins: duplicate keys multiply rows, just as in SQL.
  • Floating-point money: use decimals or integer minor units.
  • Schema drift: a new or renamed source column must fail loudly or be mapped on purpose.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Load, filter and aggregate a file with pandas or Polars
  • Read Parquet and inspect its schema
  • Know which library suits which data size

2 Practitioner

  • Use lazy Polars, DuckDB or PyArrow with pushdown on large files
  • Write a PySpark or Beam job for data beyond one machine
  • Validate data with pandera or dbt tests and count rejects

3 Senior

  • Choose engines per workload: warehouse SQL, single-node, distributed, streaming
  • Tune memory, partitioning and file layout
  • Design portable pipelines with open formats

4 Expert

  • Set the organisation's processing stack and standards
  • Lead migrations between engines (pandas to Polars, Spark to BigQuery)
  • Understand engine internals: query optimisers, vectorised execution

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

  • Polars lazy query optimisation
  • DuckDB extensions (httpfs, Iceberg)
  • Spark adaptive query execution
  • Beam windowing and triggers

Culvert

enrichme.ai's open-source, cloud-agnostic data pipeline framework for Java and Python. Pipeline code depends on a set of contracts, and adapters implement them for each cloud. Every fact on this tab is taken from the Culvert page on enrichmeai.com, which is checked daily against Maven Central and PyPI; read it there for the current release.

The idea

Pipelines are defined once against a language-neutral contract set (sixteen contracts plus a metrics record: BlobStore, Warehouse, Source, Sink, JobControlRepository and eleven more), realised as Java interfaces and Python Protocols. Adapters implement the contracts for each cloud, and every adapter must pass the same conformance suite, or it is not an adapter. Swap the cloud, keep the logic.

LayerIs
Your business logicWritten once; depends only on the contracts, never on a cloud SDK
The contractsJava interfaces and Python Protocols from one spec
Cloud adaptersSwappable implementations that pass the shared conformance suite

This is the "framework, not generated boilerplate" argument from the Frameworks vs AI tab made concrete: one contract and one test suite instead of a hand-written cloud integration in every pipeline.

Design choices

  • Java owns Beam: the Dataflow/Beam execution layer is Java; legacy Python Beam is not carried forward. The two languages deliberately do different jobs.
  • dbt, reused: transformation is SQL and macros, packaged once; there is deliberately no Java transform module.
  • Local-first: the whole stack runs against emulators, so you can develop and test without a cloud account.
  • No silent defaults: Warehouse.loadFromUri requires a LoadOptions, because a defaulted write disposition let a re-run silently double its data.
  • Honest failure: reconciliation runs before the target is written, so a failed load cannot be reported as succeeded.
  • Not a replacement for Beam, Airflow or dbt: they are engines, and Culvert is a framework around them.

Install (Python)

# core contracts only, no cloud SDKs
pip install culvert
# + BigQuery, GCS, Pub/Sub, Secret Manager, observability
pip install 'culvert[gcp]'
# + Airflow-side DAG factory / operators / sensors, or dbt
pip install 'culvert[orchestration]'
pip install 'culvert[transform]'
# + StageClaim on PostgreSQL
pip install 'culvert[postgres]'
# test fixtures, and the conformance suite for your own adapters
pip install 'culvert[tester]'
pip install 'culvert[contract-tests]'
# gcp + orchestration + transform
pip install 'culvert[all]'
# adapters are discovered through entry points
from data_pipeline_core import autoconfig
blob_store = autoconfig.discover().first("blob_store")
# -> GcsBlobStore, when culvert[gcp] is installed

Python 3.10 or newer.

Java artifacts (Maven Central)

Separate artifacts under com.enrichmeai.culvert, all at the same version:

AreaArtifacts
Coredata-pipeline-core, -contract-tests, -tester, -it-support, -orchestration, -console
PostgreSQLdata-pipeline-postgres (job control, StageClaim, InputReadiness)
GCP-gcp-gcs, -gcp-bigquery, -gcp-pubsub, -gcp-secrets, -gcp-observability, -gcp-dataflow
AWS-aws-s3, -aws-athena, -aws-dynamodb, -aws-sqs, -aws-secrets, -aws-cloudwatch
Azuredata-pipeline-azure-blob (a skeleton: its methods throw until built out)
Documentsdata-pipeline-pdf-text (PDF to text with PDFBox; returns null rather than throwing for a scanned or unreadable PDF)
// Gradle
implementation("com.enrichmeai.culvert:data-pipeline-core:<version>")
implementation("com.enrichmeai.culvert:data-pipeline-gcp-bigquery:<version>")

The current version of every artifact is on the Culvert page.

What runs where

ContractGCPAWSAzure
BlobStoreGCSS3skeleton
WarehouseBigQueryAthenaroadmap
Source · SinkPub/SubSQSroadmap
JobControlRepositoryBigQueryDynamoDB · Athenaroadmap
ObservabilityCloud MonitoringCloudWatchroadmap
SecretsSecret ManagerSecrets Managerroadmap

GCP is the full implementation; AWS has the Java adapter family; Azure has a Blob Storage skeleton and is otherwise roadmap. JobControlRepository also has a PostgreSQL implementation.

Features worth knowing

  • Job control: run state through JobControlRepository on BigQuery, DynamoDB, Athena or PostgreSQL; a read-only culvert command line (runs, run, entities, failures, adapters).
  • StageClaim (PostgreSQL): one stage of one unit for one period cannot start twice.
  • InputReadiness (PostgreSQL): names each expected input that is missing, failed or still running, rather than counting rows.
  • Orchestration: a cloud-neutral DAG model rendered to Airflow/Composer or Cloud Run jobs, with stage gates and a concurrency ceiling.
  • Schema files: an entity's columns, types, required flags, classification, masking, ranges and allowed values in YAML or JSON, loaded with the same rules in Java and Python.
  • BlobStore.head(uri): size, version token and metadata without reading the object, for loaders that must be idempotent by object version.
  • BigQueryWarehouse.merge(): upserts with one MERGE and refuses a key that is not a target column.

Status, stated plainly

  • Released in both languages on Maven Central and PyPI, under Apache 2.0 (the earliest releases were MIT).
  • Ran end to end on a real GCP project (Cloud Run to BigQuery, event-driven) before release; that run caught eight production-only bugs every local test had passed.
  • Culvert is pre-1.0: read the changelog's upgrade notes before moving between versions.
  • A book on its design is in progress.

Pin a version in your build, and verify any API against that version's sources, not against main.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain contracts, adapters and the conformance suite
  • Install culvert with the right extras or add the Maven artifacts
  • Use an adapter through auto-discovery instead of a cloud SDK

2 Practitioner

  • Write pipeline code against BlobStore, Warehouse and job control
  • Use LoadOptions, head() and merge() for idempotent loads
  • Run and test against emulators locally

3 Senior

  • Design pipelines whose logic is cloud-neutral
  • Add an adapter that passes the conformance suite
  • Use StageClaim, InputReadiness and orchestration gates

4 Expert

  • Shape the contract spec and its evolution
  • Lead adoption across teams and clouds
  • Contribute adapters for new clouds (Azure)

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

  • Orchestration renderers

dbt on BigQuery

dbt-core 1.8+ with dbt-bigquery. Unit tests arrived in 1.8; microbatch and YAML snapshots in 1.9.

CLI

dbt deps                         # install packages.yml
dbt debug                        # check profile + connection
dbt build                        # seed + run + snapshot + test, in DAG order
dbt run   -s my_model
dbt test  -s my_model
dbt build -s my_model --full-refresh
dbt compile -s my_model          # see SQL in target/compiled/
dbt show  -s my_model --limit 20 # preview rows
dbt ls    -s tag:daily --resource-type model
dbt seed  && dbt snapshot
dbt source freshness
dbt docs generate && dbt docs serve

dbt run --target prod --vars '{run_date: "2025-01-01"}'
dbt retry                        # re-run only what failed last time

Node selection

-s my_model          # just it
-s +my_model         # it and all parents
-s my_model+         # it and all children
-s 2+my_model+1      # 2 levels up, 1 down
-s @my_model         # children + their parents (safe rebuild)
-s tag:daily
-s path:models/fdp
-s config.materialized:incremental
-s source:raw+       # everything downstream of a source
-s "fdp_judgment,tag:daily"     # intersection (comma)
-s a b               # union (space)
--exclude tag:slow

# Slim CI: only what changed vs prod's manifest
dbt build -s state:modified+ --defer --state prod-artifacts/
dbt build -s result:error+ --state target/

profiles.yml (BigQuery)

my_project:
  target: dev
  outputs:
    dev:
      type: bigquery
      method: oauth                 # gcloud auth application-default login
      project: my-proj-dev
      dataset: dbt_jo               # personal dev dataset
      location: asia-south1
      threads: 8
      job_execution_timeout_seconds: 600
      maximum_bytes_billed: 50000000000   # hard cost cap per query
    prod:
      type: bigquery
      method: service-account
      keyfile: /secrets/sa.json     # or oauth on Workload Identity
      project: my-proj-prod
      dataset: fdp
      location: asia-south1
      threads: 16

Project layout & dbt_project.yml

models/
  staging/   stg_*.sql   1:1 with sources, rename + cast only (view)
  intermediate/ int_*.sql joins, dedupe (ephemeral or view)
  marts/     fct_* dim_*  what consumers read (table/incremental)
seeds/  snapshots/  macros/  tests/  analyses/

# dbt_project.yml
name: fdp_judgments
profile: my_project
models:
  fdp_judgments:
    +persist_docs: {relation: true, columns: true}
    staging:
      +materialized: view
    marts:
      +materialized: table
      +labels: {owner: data, layer: fdp}
vars:
  start_year: 2023

Model config for BigQuery

{{ config(
    materialized = 'table',
    partition_by = {
      'field': 'decision_date',
      'data_type': 'date',          # date | timestamp | datetime | int64
      'granularity': 'month'        # day | hour | month | year
    },
    cluster_by = ['court_code', 'cnr'],   # up to 4 columns
    require_partition_filter = true,
    partition_expiration_days = none,
    labels = {'layer': 'fdp'},
    tags = ['daily']
) }}

-- integer-range partition
partition_by = {'field': 'data_year', 'data_type': 'int64',
                'range': {'start': 1950, 'end': 2030, 'interval': 1}}

Incremental: merge

{{ config(
    materialized = 'incremental',
    incremental_strategy = 'merge',
    unique_key = 'judgment_key',            # or a list
    on_schema_change = 'append_new_columns',# ignore | fail | sync_all_columns
    partition_by = {'field': 'decision_date', 'data_type': 'date'},
    incremental_predicates = [
      "DBT_INTERNAL_DEST.decision_date >= date_sub(current_date(), interval 30 day)"
    ]
) }}

select * from {{ ref('int_judgment') }}
{% if is_incremental() %}
  where _landed_at > (select max(_landed_at) from {{ this }})
{% endif %}

incremental_predicates prunes the target side of the MERGE. Without it BigQuery scans the whole destination on every run.

Incremental: insert_overwrite

{% set partitions_to_replace = [
  'current_date()',
  'date_sub(current_date(), interval 1 day)'
] %}

{{ config(
    materialized = 'incremental',
    incremental_strategy = 'insert_overwrite',
    partition_by = {'field': 'event_date', 'data_type': 'date'},
    partitions = partitions_to_replace       # static: cheapest
) }}

select * from {{ ref('stg_events') }}
{% if is_incremental() %}
  where event_date in ({{ partitions_to_replace | join(',') }})
{% endif %}

Replaces whole partitions, no row matching. Leave out partitions for "dynamic" mode: dbt finds the partitions in the new data, at the cost of an extra query.

Incremental: microbatch 1.9

{{ config(
    materialized = 'incremental',
    incremental_strategy = 'microbatch',
    event_time = 'decision_date',
    batch_size = 'day',            # hour | day | month | year
    begin = '2023-01-01',
    lookback = 2,
    partition_by = {'field': 'decision_date', 'data_type': 'date',
                    'granularity': 'day'}
) }}
-- no is_incremental() block: dbt filters each batch for you,
-- and filters upstream refs that declare their own event_time
select * from {{ ref('stg_judgment') }}
dbt run -s fct_judgment --event-time-start 2024-01-01 --event-time-end 2024-02-01
dbt retry      # re-runs only failed batches

Sources & freshness

# models/staging/_sources.yml
sources:
  - name: odp
    database: my-proj            # = GCP project
    schema: dp_odp               # = dataset
    loaded_at_field: _landed_at
    freshness:
      warn_after:  {count: 1, period: day}
      error_after: {count: 3, period: day}
    tables:
      - name: hc_judgment
      - name: sc_judgment
        freshness: null          # opt out

-- usage
select * from {{ source('odp', 'hc_judgment') }}

Data tests

models:
  - name: fdp_judgment
    columns:
      - name: judgment_key
        data_tests: [unique, not_null]
      - name: court_code
        data_tests:
          - relationships: {to: ref('fdp_court'), field: court_code}
      - name: status
        data_tests:
          - accepted_values: {values: ['DISPOSED', 'PENDING']}
      - name: decision_date
        data_tests:
          - not_null:
              config:
                severity: warn
                where: "data_year >= 2000"
                error_if: ">100"
                warn_if: ">0"
-- tests/assert_no_future_dates.sql  (singular: fails if it returns rows)
select * from {{ ref('fdp_judgment') }}
where decision_date > current_date()

data_tests: replaced tests: in 1.8; the old key still works. Packages: dbt-labs/dbt_utils, metaplane/dbt_expectations.

Unit tests 1.8

unit_tests:
  - name: parses_sc_dd_mm_yyyy
    model: int_judgment_normalised
    given:
      - input: ref('stg_sc_judgment')
        rows:
          - {cnr: "SC1", raw_date: "05-05-1959"}
          - {cnr: "SC2", raw_date: "bad"}
    expect:
      rows:
        - {cnr: "SC1", decision_date: 1959-05-05}
        - {cnr: "SC2", decision_date: null}

  # format: csv | dict | sql ; overrides: {macros: {is_incremental: true}}

Unit tests run SQL logic on fixed inputs before the model is built. Run with dbt test -s test_type:unit; exclude them in prod.

Jinja & macros

{{ ref('model') }}   {{ ref('other_pkg', 'model') }}
{{ source('odp', 'hc_judgment') }}
{{ this }}                      -- current relation
{{ var('start_year', 2020) }}
{{ env_var('DBT_ENV', 'dev') }}
{{ target.name }} {{ target.project }} {{ target.dataset }}

{% set courts = ['2_5', '14_25', '16_20'] %}
where court in ({% for c in courts %}'{{ c }}'{% if not loop.last %},{% endif %}{% endfor %})

-- macros/safe_date.sql
{% macro safe_date(col, fmt='%Y-%m-%d') -%}
  safe.parse_date('{{ fmt }}', {{ col }})
{%- endmacro %}

-- run a query at compile time
{% set rs = run_query("select distinct court from " ~ ref('fdp_court')) %}
{% if execute %}{% set courts = rs.columns[0].values() %}{% endif %}

-- dev sampling
{% if target.name == 'dev' %} limit 1000 {% endif %}

Custom generic test

-- tests/generic/null_ratio_below.sql
{% test null_ratio_below(model, column_name, max_ratio=0.01) %}
select *
from (
  select countif({{ column_name }} is null) / count(*) as ratio
  from {{ model }}
)
where ratio > {{ max_ratio }}
{% endtest %}

# use it
columns:
  - name: decision_date
    data_tests:
      - null_ratio_below: {max_ratio: 0.0}

Contracts & snapshots

# Model contract: build fails if shape drifts
models:
  - name: serving_judgments
    config:
      contract: {enforced: true}
    columns:
      - name: judgment_key
        data_type: string
        constraints: [{type: not_null}]
      - name: decision_date
        data_type: date

# Snapshot, YAML form (1.9): SCD type 2
snapshots:
  - name: court_snapshot
    relation: source('odp', 'court')
    config:
      schema: snapshots
      unique_key: court_code
      strategy: check          # or timestamp + updated_at
      check_cols: all
      hard_deletes: invalidate # ignore | invalidate | new_record

    post_hook = [
      "alter table {{ this }} set options(description='built {{ run_started_at }}')"
    ],
    grants = {'roles/bigquery.dataViewer': ['serviceAccount:api@p.iam.gserviceaccount.com']}
) }}

# dbt_project.yml
on-run-end:
  - "{{ log_results(results) }}"

# ad-hoc macro
dbt run-operation drop_dev_datasets --args '{prefix: dbt_jo}'

Airflow DAGs

Airflow 3 syntax (Task SDK), with 2.x differences noted. Cloud Composer runs either; check its image version first.

TaskFlow DAG (Airflow 3)

from datetime import datetime, timedelta
from airflow.sdk import dag, task

@dag(
    schedule="15 6 * * *",          # cron, "@daily", timedelta, Asset, None
    start_date=datetime(2025, 1, 1),
    catchup=False,                  # default False in 3.x (True in 2.x)
    max_active_runs=1,
    default_args={"retries": 2, "retry_delay": timedelta(minutes=5)},
    tags=["corpus"],
)
def corpus_daily():

    @task
    def list_new(ds=None) -> list[str]:
        return find_objects(since=ds)

    @task(retries=3)
    def load(uri: str) -> int:
        return load_to_bq(uri)

    @task
    def report(counts: list[int]):
        print(sum(counts))

    report(load.expand(uri=list_new()))   # dynamic task mapping

corpus_daily()

BigQuery & GCS operators

from airflow.providers.google.cloud.operators.bigquery import (
    BigQueryInsertJobOperator, BigQueryCheckOperator)
from airflow.providers.google.cloud.transfers.gcs_to_bigquery import (
    GCSToBigQueryOperator)
from airflow.providers.google.cloud.sensors.gcs import GCSObjectExistenceSensor

wait = GCSObjectExistenceSensor(
    task_id="wait_manifest", bucket="landing",
    object="manifests/{{ ds }}.json",
    deferrable=True, timeout=6 * 3600)

load = GCSToBigQueryOperator(
    task_id="load_parquet", bucket="landing",
    source_objects=["data/year={{ logical_date.year }}/*.parquet"],
    destination_project_dataset_table="proj.odp.hc_judgment",
    source_format="PARQUET", write_disposition="WRITE_APPEND")

build = BigQueryInsertJobOperator(
    task_id="build_fdp", location="asia-south1", deferrable=True,
    configuration={"query": {
        "query": "{% include 'sql/fdp_judgment.sql' %}",
        "useLegacySql": False}})

check = BigQueryCheckOperator(
    task_id="rows_today", use_legacy_sql=False,
    sql="SELECT COUNT(*) > 0 FROM `proj.fdp.judgment` WHERE _run_date = '{{ ds }}'")

wait >> load >> build >> check

Templates & context

{{ ds }}                     2025-01-31     (logical date, YYYY-MM-DD)
{{ ds_nodash }}              20250131
{{ logical_date }}           pendulum DateTime
{{ data_interval_start }}    start of the period this run covers
{{ data_interval_end }}      end (exclusive)
{{ macros.ds_add(ds, -1) }}  2025-01-30
{{ params.window }}          from dag(params={...})
{{ var.value.my_var }}       Airflow Variable
{{ conn.my_conn.host }}      Connection field
{{ run_id }}  {{ dag.dag_id }}  {{ task_instance.try_number }}

# in TaskFlow, ask for context by name
@task
def t(data_interval_start=None, ti=None):
    ti.xcom_push(key="n", value=1)
# or: from airflow.sdk import get_current_context

A daily run for 2025-01-31 starts after that interval ends. Use data_interval_* for "which data", never now(). execution_date is removed in Airflow 3: use logical_date.

Running dbt from a DAG

from airflow.providers.standard.operators.bash import BashOperator
# (2.x: from airflow.operators.bash import BashOperator)

dbt_build = BashOperator(
    task_id="dbt_build",
    bash_command=(
        "cd /home/airflow/gcs/dags/dbt/fdp && "
        "dbt build --target prod -s tag:daily "
        "--vars '{run_date: \"{{ ds }}\"}'"),
    env={"DBT_PROFILES_DIR": "/home/airflow/gcs/dags/dbt"},
    append_env=True)

# Or run dbt in its own image, which keeps it off the worker:

Branching, trigger rules, task groups

from airflow.sdk import task, task_group

@task.branch
def choose(**ctx):
    return "full_load" if ctx["logical_date"].day == 1 else "delta_load"

@task(trigger_rule="none_failed_min_one_success")
def join(): ...

@task(trigger_rule="all_done")   # always runs: cleanup, alerts
def cleanup(): ...

@task.short_circuit
def has_work(n: int) -> bool:
    return n > 0       # False skips everything downstream

@task_group
def per_court(court: str): ...

Data-aware scheduling (Assets)

from airflow.sdk import Asset, dag, task
odp = Asset("bigquery://proj/dp_odp/hc_judgment")

@dag(schedule="@daily", start_date=datetime(2025,1,1))
def producer():
    @task(outlets=[odp])
    def load(): ...
    load()

@dag(schedule=[odp], start_date=datetime(2025,1,1))   # runs when odp updates
def consumer():
    @task
    def build(): ...
    build()

producer(); consumer()

Airflow 2.4–2.10 call these Dataset (from airflow.datasets import Dataset).

Reliability checklist

  • Idempotent tasks: a rerun of the same interval gives the same result. Use WRITE_TRUNCATE on a partition (table$20250131) or a MERGE, never a blind append.
  • No heavy work at parse time: top-level code runs on every scheduler parse. No API calls or DB queries outside tasks.
  • Small XComs: pass URIs and counts, not dataframes.
  • Deferrable operators and sensors (deferrable=True) free worker slots while waiting.
  • Pools cap concurrency against a shared resource: pool="bq_slots".
  • Alert on failure: on_failure_callback in default_args.
  • Pin provider versions in your Composer/requirements; operator args change between majors.

CLI & local testing

airflow dags list
airflow dags list-import-errors
airflow dags test corpus_daily 2025-01-31          # full run, no scheduler
airflow tasks test corpus_daily load 2025-01-31    # one task, no state saved
airflow dags backfill corpus_daily -s 2025-01-01 -e 2025-01-07   # 2.x
airflow backfill create --dag-id corpus_daily \
  --from-date 2025-01-01 --to-date 2025-01-07                    # 3.x
airflow dags trigger corpus_daily -c '{"window": 3}'

# pytest: every DAG imports cleanly
from airflow.models import DagBag
def test_dags_load():
    bag = DagBag(dag_folder="dags", include_examples=False)
    assert not bag.import_errors

Airflow 2 → 3 gotchas

  • Authoring imports move to airflow.sdk (dag, task, Asset, Variable).
  • schedule_interval and timetable args are removed: use schedule.
  • catchup now defaults to False.
  • Context keys execution_date, next_ds, prev_ds, tomorrow_ds are removed.
  • Bash, Python and Empty operators live in the apache-airflow-providers-standard package.
  • Tasks no longer get direct metadata-DB access; use the REST API or the SDK.
  • SubDagOperator and SLAs are gone; use task groups and deadline alerts.
  • Run ruff check --select AIR3 dags/ to find most of these.

2 Practitioner

  • Use provider operators and deferrable sensors
  • Test DAGs locally and in CI

3 Senior

  • Design orchestration across many pipelines (assets, pools, SLAs)
  • Run Composer or Airflow reliably; plan upgrades
  • Decide what belongs in Airflow vs inside the job

4 Expert

  • Choose the orchestration platform for the organisation
  • Run large Airflow estates: scaling, multi-tenancy, security
  • Set standards for scheduling and data-aware workflows

Systems, APIs and integration

The kinds of systems you will meet, how they talk to each other, and what REST, APIs, gateways, gRPC, GraphQL, webhooks and messaging each mean. Then Kafka, Spring Boot and Quarkus for building them.

Kinds of systems

TypeWhat it doesExampleBuilt with
Request/response serviceAnswers calls synchronouslySearch API, loginSpring Boot, Quarkus, FastAPI on Cloud Run or GKE
Event-driven serviceReacts to events; publishes its ownIndex a judgment when it landsKafka or Pub/Sub consumers
Batch jobProcesses a bounded set, then exitsNightly loadCloud Run jobs, Dataflow, Spark
Stream processorProcesses an unbounded stream continuouslyReal-time fraud scoreDataflow, Kafka Streams, Flink
OLTP database systemMany small transactional reads and writesUser accounts, ordersPostgres, Cloud SQL, Spanner
OLAP / analytics systemLarge scans and aggregatesReporting, dashboardsBigQuery
Workflow / orchestration systemCoordinates multi-step processesPipeline stages, sagasAirflow, Workflows, Temporal
Integration / API platformExposes, secures and manages APIs for othersPartner APIApigee, API Gateway
Front endUser interfaceWeb or mobile appReact, native apps
AI-powered systemUses models to understand or generateResearch assistantModel APIs, RAG, agents

What an API is

An API (application programming interface) is a contract: the operations a system offers, the inputs they take, the outputs and errors they return, and the rules for calling them. The contract is what other teams build against; the implementation behind it can change freely as long as the contract holds.

  • Public / partner / internal: who may call it, and so how strict versioning, security and documentation must be.
  • Synchronous: the caller waits (REST, gRPC, GraphQL).
  • Asynchronous: the caller sends and moves on; the result arrives later (queues, events, webhooks).
  • Spec first: write the contract (OpenAPI, protobuf, AsyncAPI) before the code; generate clients and docs from it.

REST in practice

  • Resources are nouns with URLs: /judgments, /judgments/{id}, /courts/{code}/judgments.
  • HTTP methods are the verbs: GET reads, POST creates, PUT replaces, PATCH updates part, DELETE removes.
  • Stateless: each request carries everything needed (including auth); no server session required.
  • Status codes carry meaning: 200, 201 Created (+ Location), 204, 400, 401, 403, 404, 409 Conflict, 422, 429, 500, 503.
  • Errors as Problem Details (RFC 9457): type, title, status, detail.
  • Pagination with a cursor (?cursor=…&limit=50) rather than deep offsets.
  • Filtering and sorting by query parameters: ?court=2_5&from=2024-01-01&sort=-decision_date.
  • Caching with ETag/If-None-Match and Cache-Control.
MethodSafeIdempotent
GET, HEADyesyes
PUT, DELETEnoyes
POSTnono: use an Idempotency-Key header
PATCHnonot guaranteed

An OpenAPI contract

openapi: 3.1.0
info: { title: Legal Search API, version: 1.2.0 }
paths:
  /v1/judgments/{id}:
    get:
      operationId: getJudgment
      parameters:
        - { name: id, in: path, required: true, schema: { type: string } }
      responses:
        "200":
          description: The judgment
          content:
            application/json:
              schema: { $ref: "#/components/schemas/Judgment" }
        "404":
          description: Not found
          content:
            application/problem+json:
              schema: { $ref: "#/components/schemas/Problem" }
components:
  schemas:
    Judgment:
      type: object
      required: [id, cnr, court]
      properties:
        id: { type: string }
        cnr: { type: string }
        court: { type: string }
        decisionDate: { type: [string, "null"], format: date }
  securitySchemes:
    bearer: { type: http, scheme: bearer, bearerFormat: JWT }
security: [ { bearer: [] } ]

API styles compared

StyleShapeStrengthsWeaknessesUse for
REST / JSON over HTTPResources + HTTP verbsUniversal, cacheable, easy to debugOver- or under-fetching; loose typing unless specifiedPublic and partner APIs, most web services
gRPCProtobuf messages over HTTP/2, generated clientsFast, strongly typed, streaming both waysNot browser-native; harder to inspectInternal service-to-service calls
GraphQLOne endpoint; client asks for exactly the fields it needsFlexible for UIs; one round trip for nested dataCaching, query cost control, authorisation per fieldFront ends combining many sources
WebhooksYour server calls the client's URL when something happensNo pollingDelivery retries, signature checks, client downtimeNotifying partners (payments, Git events)
Messaging / eventsPublish to a topic; subscribers consumeDecoupling, buffering, fan-outEventual consistency, duplicates, debuggingIntegration between domains, pipelines
WebSockets / SSELong-lived connection for pushesReal-time updates, streaming responsesConnection management at scaleLive dashboards, chat, streaming AI output
SOAP / XMLEnvelope with WSDL contractStrict contracts, WS-* standardsVerbose, legacy toolingIntegrating older enterprise systems
File exchangeCSV/Parquet drops on SFTP or bucketsSimple, bulkLatency, partial files, schema driftBulk data between organisations

gRPC contract

syntax = "proto3";
package legal.v1;

service JudgmentService {
  rpc GetJudgment(GetJudgmentRequest) returns (Judgment);
  rpc StreamNew(StreamNewRequest) returns (stream Judgment);   // server streaming
}

message GetJudgmentRequest { string id = 1; }
message StreamNewRequest  { string court = 1; }
message Judgment {
  string id = 1;
  string cnr = 2;
  string court = 3;
  string decision_date = 4;      // ISO date; field numbers never reused
}

Compatibility rules: add fields with new numbers; never change or reuse a field number; reserve removed ones.

Gateway, load balancer, proxy, mesh: who does what

ComponentJobKnows aboutGCP examples
Load balancerSpread traffic across healthy instances; terminate TLSIPs, ports, hosts, pathsCloud Load Balancing
Reverse proxyFront a server: TLS, compression, routing, cachingHTTP requestsEnvoy, nginx
API gatewaySingle entry for APIs: authentication, rate limits, routing, request transformationAPI keys, tokens, routes, quotasAPI Gateway, Apigee
API management platformGateway plus API products, developer portal, analytics, monetisation, lifecycleConsumers, apps, products, plansApigee
Service meshService-to-service mTLS, retries, traffic splitting, telemetry inside the platformEvery internal callCloud Service Mesh (Istio)
Backend for frontendAn API shaped for one client typeThat client's screensA small service on Cloud Run

North-south traffic (clients into your platform) goes through the gateway; east-west traffic (services to each other) is the mesh's job. Keep business logic out of both.

API versioning and evolution

  • Additive changes are safe: new endpoints, new optional fields, new enum values clients are told to tolerate.
  • Breaking changes (removing or renaming fields, changing types or meaning) need a new version.
  • Where to version: in the path (/v1/) is the most visible and the easiest to route at a gateway; headers are cleaner but harder to test.
  • Deprecate politely: announce, add Deprecation and Sunset headers, measure who still calls, then remove.
  • Meaning can break without a schema change: a field whose values change meaning is a breaking change even if its type is the same.
  • Contract tests in CI catch accidental breaks.

Reliable integration

  • Timeouts on every call; retries only for idempotent operations, with backoff and jitter.
  • Idempotency keys for POSTs that create or charge.
  • Long-running work: return 202 Accepted with a status URL, or emit an event when done.
  • Webhooks you send: sign the payload (HMAC), retry with backoff, let clients replay.
  • Webhooks you receive: verify the signature, respond fast, process asynchronously, dedupe by event ID.
  • Rate limits with 429 and Retry-After.
  • Correlation IDs (traceparent) passed through every hop.

Integration styles (oldest to newest)

  1. File transfer: one system writes files, another reads them. Simple and still common for bulk data.
  2. Shared database: several systems read and write the same tables. Avoid: no system owns the schema.
  3. Remote procedure call: one system calls another's API (REST, gRPC). Simple; couples availability.
  4. Messaging: systems exchange messages through a broker. Decoupled in time; more moving parts.

From Enterprise Integration Patterns (Hohpe and Woolf), still the best catalogue of messaging patterns.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain REST resources, methods and status codes
  • Call an API with curl and read its OpenAPI spec
  • Tell sync from async integration

2 Practitioner

  • Design a REST API with pagination, errors and versioning
  • Write and publish an OpenAPI contract
  • Add timeouts, retries and idempotency keys

3 Senior

  • Choose REST, gRPC, GraphQL or events for a context
  • Design gateway, mesh and BFF responsibilities
  • Run API evolution: deprecation and contract tests

4 Expert

  • Set API standards and governance across the organisation
  • Design a partner and public API programme
  • Lead integration architecture across many systems

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

GraphQL

A query language for APIs: one typed schema, and clients ask for exactly the fields they need. How it works, when it beats REST, the performance and security traps, and servers in Spring, Quarkus and Python. API styles in general are on APIs & Integration.

The idea in one paragraph

A GraphQL server publishes a schema: the types, their fields and how they connect. Clients send a query to a single endpoint (usually POST /graphql) describing the shape of data they want, and get back JSON in exactly that shape. Reads are queries, writes are mutations, live updates are subscriptions. Each field is filled by a resolver function on the server, which can read from a database, another service or a cache.

RESTGraphQL
EndpointsMany, one per resourceOne
Response shapeFixed by the serverChosen by the client
Nested dataSeveral round trips or custom endpointsOne request
ContractOpenAPI (optional)The schema (built in, introspectable)
HTTP cachingNatural for GETHarder: needs persisted queries or client caches
ErrorsHTTP status codesUsually 200 with an errors array; partial data possible
Versioning/v1, /v2Evolve one schema; deprecate fields
Best forPublic APIs, simple resources, cachingUIs combining many related entities; many client types

Schema (SDL)

type Query {
  judgment(id: ID!): Judgment
  searchJudgments(q: String!, court: String, first: Int = 20, after: String): JudgmentConnection!
}

type Mutation {
  saveToCase(caseId: ID!, judgmentId: ID!): CaseFile!
}

type Subscription {
  judgmentAdded(court: String!): Judgment!
}

type Judgment {
  id: ID!
  cnr: String!
  decisionDate: Date          # custom scalar
  court: Court!
  judges: [String!]!
  citedBy(first: Int = 10): [Judgment!]!
}

type Court { code: ID!  name: String!  state: String }

scalar Date

! means non-null. [String!]! is a non-null list of non-null strings.

Queries, variables, fragments

query Search($q: String!, $court: String, $after: String) {
  searchJudgments(q: $q, court: $court, first: 10, after: $after) {
    edges {
      node { ...JudgmentCard }
    }
    pageInfo { hasNextPage endCursor }
  }
}

fragment JudgmentCard on Judgment {
  id
  cnr
  decisionDate
  court { name }
}

# variables (sent separately as JSON):
# { "q": "land acquisition", "court": "2_5" }
curl -s https://api.example.com/graphql \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"query":"query($id:ID!){judgment(id:$id){cnr court{name}}}","variables":{"id":"123"}}'

Always use variables, never string interpolation into the query: it enables caching of the parsed query and avoids injection.

Resolvers and the N+1 problem

Asking for 20 judgments, each with its court, naively runs 1 query for the list and 20 more for courts: N+1 queries.

  • Batch loading (DataLoader pattern): collect all the court IDs requested in one execution step, fetch them in one query, and hand each judgment its court.
  • Per-request cache: the same court requested twice in one query is loaded once.
  • Spring: @BatchMapping. Quarkus: a resolver taking @Source List<…>. Python Strawberry: DataLoader.
  • Measure: log query counts per GraphQL request in tests.

Spring for GraphQL

// schema files: src/main/resources/graphql/*.graphqls
@Controller
class JudgmentGraphQl {
  private final JudgmentService judgments;
  private final CourtService courts;
  JudgmentGraphQl(JudgmentService j, CourtService c) { judgments = j; courts = c; }

  @QueryMapping
  Judgment judgment(@Argument String id) {
    return judgments.find(id).orElse(null);       // null → field is null, no error
  }

  @MutationMapping
  CaseFile saveToCase(@Argument String caseId, @Argument String judgmentId) {
    return judgments.saveToCase(caseId, judgmentId);   // check ownership inside
  }

  @BatchMapping                                    // one query for all courts
  Map<Judgment, Court> court(List<Judgment> list) {
    Map<String, Court> byCode = courts.byCodes(
        list.stream().map(Judgment::courtCode).collect(Collectors.toSet()));
    return list.stream().collect(Collectors.toMap(j -> j, j -> byCode.get(j.courtCode())));
  }
}

// test
@GraphQlTest(JudgmentGraphQl.class)
class JudgmentGraphQlTest {
  @Autowired GraphQlTester tester;
  @MockitoBean JudgmentService judgments;  @MockitoBean CourtService courts;
  @Test void returnsNullForUnknownId() {
    tester.document("{ judgment(id: \"x\") { cnr } }")
          .execute().path("judgment").valueIsNull();
  }
}

Quarkus (SmallRye GraphQL)

// quarkus ext add smallrye-graphql   → schema generated from code
@GraphQLApi
public class JudgmentApi {
  @Inject JudgmentService judgments;
  @Inject CourtService courts;

  @Query
  public Judgment judgment(@NonNull String id) { return judgments.find(id).orElse(null); }

  @Mutation
  public CaseFile saveToCase(@NonNull String caseId, @NonNull String judgmentId) {
    return judgments.saveToCase(caseId, judgmentId);
  }

  // batched field resolver: adds Judgment.court, one call for all judgments
  public List<Court> court(@Source List<Judgment> list) {
    return courts.inOrderFor(list);
  }
}
// GraphQL UI in dev mode: http://localhost:8080/q/graphql-ui

Spring for GraphQL is schema-first (you write SDL); SmallRye GraphQL is code-first (the schema comes from your classes).

Python (Strawberry)

import strawberry
from strawberry.dataloader import DataLoader

async def load_courts(codes: list[str]) -> list["Court"]:
    rows = await db.courts_by_codes(codes)              # one query
    by = {r.code: r for r in rows}
    return [by.get(c) for c in codes]                   # same order as input

@strawberry.type
class Court:
    code: strawberry.ID
    name: str

@strawberry.type
class Judgment:
    id: strawberry.ID
    cnr: str
    court_code: strawberry.Private[str]
    @strawberry.field
    async def court(self, info: strawberry.Info) -> Court:
        return await info.context["court_loader"].load(self.court_code)

@strawberry.type
class Query:
    @strawberry.field
    async def judgment(self, id: strawberry.ID) -> Judgment | None:
        return await db.judgment(id)

schema = strawberry.Schema(query=Query)
# context per request: {"court_loader": DataLoader(load_fn=load_courts)}

Pagination: connections

type JudgmentConnection {
  edges: [JudgmentEdge!]!
  pageInfo: PageInfo!
  totalCount: Int            # optional; can be expensive
}
type JudgmentEdge { cursor: String!  node: Judgment! }
type PageInfo { hasNextPage: Boolean!  endCursor: String }
  • Cursor-based (the Relay connection convention): stable under inserts, works with keyset queries in the database.
  • Cap first on the server (e.g. max 100).
  • Make cursors opaque (base64 of the sort key and ID).

Errors

{
  "data": { "judgment": null },
  "errors": [{
    "message": "Not authorised to view this judgment",
    "path": ["judgment"],
    "extensions": { "code": "FORBIDDEN" }
  }]
}
  • A response can hold partial data plus errors; clients must check errors even on HTTP 200.
  • Put a stable machine-readable extensions.code on every error.
  • Expected business outcomes (validation failed, not found) can also be modelled in the schema as result types (union SaveResult = CaseFile | ValidationError).
  • Never leak stack traces or SQL in message.

Security

  • Authorise per field and per object in resolvers or the service layer; one endpoint means URL-based rules cannot protect anything.
  • Limit query depth and complexity so a client cannot request citedBy { citedBy { citedBy … } } a thousand levels deep.
  • Cap list sizes and set timeouts per request.
  • Persisted queries (allow-list of known query hashes) for first-party clients: blocks arbitrary queries and enables GET caching.
  • Introspection: consider disabling it in production for private APIs; it is not a security boundary, just less help to attackers.
  • Rate limit by cost, not by request count, since one request can be cheap or enormous.
  • Batching attacks: limit the number of operations or aliases per request (aliases can repeat a login mutation hundreds of times).

Performance and caching

  • Batch loaders everywhere a field fetches by key.
  • Select only needed columns: look at the requested fields (selection set) before querying.
  • Client caches (Apollo Client, Relay, urql) normalise results by type and ID; give every object a stable id.
  • HTTP caching needs GET with persisted queries; otherwise cache in resolvers or the data layer.
  • Trace per resolver (OpenTelemetry) to find slow fields.

Schema evolution and scale

  • Add freely: new types, new fields, new optional arguments.
  • Deprecate before removing: oldField: String @deprecated(reason: "Use newField"), then watch usage and remove.
  • Never change a field's type or meaning in place.
  • Schema checks in CI compare against the published schema and fail on breaking changes.
  • Federation: several teams own subgraphs (judgments, users, billing) composed by a router into one supergraph (Apollo Federation, GraphQL Mesh).
  • Behind a gateway: Apigee or another gateway can still handle authentication and rate limits in front of /graphql.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Read a schema and write queries with variables and fragments
  • Explain queries, mutations, subscriptions and resolvers
  • Tell when GraphQL fits better than REST

2 Practitioner

  • Build a server with Spring for GraphQL, SmallRye or Strawberry
  • Avoid N+1 with batch loaders; paginate with connections
  • Return useful errors and test resolvers

3 Senior

  • Secure a GraphQL API: field authorisation, depth and cost limits, persisted queries
  • Evolve a schema without breaking clients; schema checks in CI
  • Tune performance with tracing and caching

4 Expert

  • Design a federated graph across teams
  • Set GraphQL governance and standards for the organisation
  • Decide GraphQL vs REST vs gRPC strategy across products

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

  • DataLoader and batching
  • Relay connection specification
  • Apollo Federation and supergraphs

Apache Kafka

A distributed, durable, replayable log for events. The core model, the settings that decide durability and ordering, clients in Java, Spring, Quarkus and Python, and the ecosystem around it.

The model

ConceptMeaning
TopicA named stream of records, e.g. judgment.landed
PartitionAn ordered, append-only log; a topic has many. Ordering is guaranteed only within a partition.
RecordKey, value, headers, timestamp. The key decides the partition (same key → same partition → ordered).
OffsetA record's position in its partition. Consumers track how far they have read.
BrokerA server storing partitions. A cluster has several.
Replication factorCopies of each partition on different brokers (3 is typical).
Leader / ISROne replica takes reads and writes; in-sync replicas are caught-up followers.
Consumer groupConsumers sharing the work: each partition is read by one consumer in the group. Max parallelism = partition count.
RetentionRecords kept for a time or size, then deleted, regardless of whether they were read.
CompactionKeep only the latest record per key; a table-like topic.
KRaftKafka's built-in metadata quorum, replacing ZooKeeper (removed in Kafka 4.0).

Unlike a queue, reading does not remove a record. Many groups can read the same topic independently, and a group can rewind to replay.

Durability and delivery settings

  • Producer acks=all with topic min.insync.replicas=2 and replication factor 3: a write survives one broker failure.
  • enable.idempotence=true (the default in modern clients): retries do not create duplicates within a partition.
  • At-least-once consuming: process, then commit the offset. A crash between the two replays the record, so consumers must be idempotent.
  • At-most-once: commit before processing. Rarely what you want.
  • Exactly-once inside Kafka (read-process-write between topics) with transactions and isolation.level=read_committed. Side effects outside Kafka still need idempotency.
  • Ordering: choose the key for the entity whose events must stay in order (case ID, account ID).

CLI essentials

B=localhost:9092
kafka-topics.sh --bootstrap-server $B --create --topic judgment.landed \
  --partitions 6 --replication-factor 3 --config retention.ms=604800000
kafka-topics.sh --bootstrap-server $B --describe --topic judgment.landed

kafka-console-producer.sh --bootstrap-server $B --topic judgment.landed \
  --property parse.key=true --property key.separator=:
# type:  CNR123:{"court":"2_5"}

kafka-console-consumer.sh --bootstrap-server $B --topic judgment.landed \
  --group debug --from-beginning --property print.key=true

kafka-consumer-groups.sh --bootstrap-server $B --describe --group indexer   # lag per partition
kafka-consumer-groups.sh --bootstrap-server $B --group indexer \
  --reset-offsets --to-datetime 2026-11-01T00:00:00.000 --topic judgment.landed --execute

Consumer lag (latest offset minus committed offset) is the key health metric: growing lag means consumers cannot keep up.

Spring for Apache Kafka

# application.yml
spring:
  kafka:
    bootstrap-servers: ${KAFKA_BOOTSTRAP}
    producer:
      acks: all
      key-serializer: org.apache.kafka.common.serialization.StringSerializer
      value-serializer: org.springframework.kafka.support.serializer.JsonSerializer
    consumer:
      group-id: indexer
      auto-offset-reset: earliest
      properties:
        spring.json.trusted.packages: "com.example.events"
@Service
class Publisher {
  private final KafkaTemplate<String, JudgmentLanded> kafka;
  Publisher(KafkaTemplate<String, JudgmentLanded> kafka) { this.kafka = kafka; }
  void publish(JudgmentLanded e) { kafka.send("judgment.landed", e.cnr(), e); }  // key = cnr
}

@Component
class Indexer {
  @KafkaListener(topics = "judgment.landed", groupId = "indexer")
  void on(JudgmentLanded e) {
    index.upsert(e);           // idempotent: same event twice is harmless
  }
}

// retries and a dead-letter topic for poison messages
@Bean DefaultErrorHandler errorHandler(KafkaTemplate<Object, Object> t) {
  return new DefaultErrorHandler(new DeadLetterPublishingRecoverer(t),
                                 new FixedBackOff(1000L, 3));
}

Quarkus with Kafka

# application.properties (quarkus-messaging-kafka)
kafka.bootstrap.servers=${KAFKA_BOOTSTRAP}
mp.messaging.incoming.landed.connector=smallrye-kafka
mp.messaging.incoming.landed.topic=judgment.landed
mp.messaging.incoming.landed.group.id=indexer
mp.messaging.outgoing.indexed.connector=smallrye-kafka
mp.messaging.outgoing.indexed.topic=judgment.indexed
@ApplicationScoped
public class Indexer {
  @Incoming("landed")
  @Outgoing("indexed")
  public JudgmentIndexed process(JudgmentLanded e) {
    return index.upsert(e);
  }
}

@ApplicationScoped
public class Publisher {
  @Inject @Channel("indexed") Emitter<JudgmentIndexed> out;
  void send(JudgmentIndexed e) { out.send(e); }
}

In dev and test, Dev Services starts a Kafka-compatible broker for you.

Python (confluent-kafka)

from confluent_kafka import Producer, Consumer

p = Producer({"bootstrap.servers": BOOTSTRAP, "acks": "all",
              "enable.idempotence": True})
p.produce("judgment.landed", key=cnr, value=json.dumps(evt),
          callback=lambda err, msg: err and log.error("send failed: %s", err))
p.flush()

c = Consumer({"bootstrap.servers": BOOTSTRAP, "group.id": "indexer",
              "auto.offset.reset": "earliest", "enable.auto.commit": False})
c.subscribe(["judgment.landed"])
while True:
    msg = c.poll(1.0)
    if msg is None: continue
    if msg.error(): log.error(msg.error()); continue
    handle(json.loads(msg.value()))           # idempotent
    c.commit(message=msg, asynchronous=False) # commit after processing

The ecosystem

  • Schema Registry with Avro, Protobuf or JSON Schema: producers and consumers agree on schemas; compatibility rules block breaking changes.
  • Kafka Connect: ready-made source and sink connectors (databases, GCS, BigQuery) without custom code.
  • Debezium: change data capture from databases into Kafka topics.
  • Kafka Streams: a Java library for stateful stream processing (joins, windows, aggregates) inside your service.
  • Flink: a separate engine for large-scale stream processing.
  • On GCP: Managed Service for Apache Kafka runs clusters for you; Dataflow and BigQuery have Kafka connectors.

Kafka vs Pub/Sub

KafkaPub/Sub
ModelPartitioned log; consumers track offsetsTopics and subscriptions; per-message ack
OrderingPer partition (by key)Per ordering key, when enabled
ReplayRewind offsets within retentionSeek to a time or snapshot within retention
CapacityYou size brokers and partitions (managed or self-run)Serverless, scales automatically
EcosystemConnect, Streams, Schema Registry, DebeziumNative GCP integrations (BigQuery, GCS, Dataflow, Eventarc)
Choose whenKafka skills or tools exist; log semantics, compaction, very high throughputGCP-native, simple operations, global delivery

Designing topics

  • Name by event, past tense: judgment.landed, order.paid.
  • Partitions: enough for peak consumer parallelism with headroom; adding later changes key-to-partition mapping.
  • Keys: the entity that needs ordering; avoid hot keys.
  • Payload: include an event ID, type, version and timestamp; keep events self-describing.
  • Retention: long enough to recover from a consumer outage and replay.
  • Dead-letter topic for records that keep failing, with alerting on it.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain topics, partitions, offsets and consumer groups
  • Produce and consume with the CLI
  • Read consumer lag

2 Practitioner

  • Build producers and consumers in Spring, Quarkus or Python with correct acks and commits
  • Make consumers idempotent; use dead-letter topics
  • Use a schema registry

3 Senior

  • Design topics, keys and partition counts for scale and ordering
  • Run Kafka Connect and CDC pipelines
  • Tune throughput, latency and durability

4 Expert

  • Operate Kafka platforms (or choose managed) for the organisation
  • Design event-driven architectures across domains
  • Lead stream processing with Kafka Streams or Flink

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.
