Payment gateways

How online payments work, the parties and flows (cards, UPI, subscriptions), how to integrate a gateway such as Razorpay or Stripe safely, and the engineering that keeps money correct: idempotency, webhooks, state machines, ledgers and reconciliation.

Who is involved

PartyRole
CustomerPays with a card, UPI, net banking or wallet
MerchantYou: the business being paid
Payment gateway / aggregatorCollects payment details securely, routes the transaction, gives you APIs, checkout and webhooks (Razorpay, Stripe, PayU, Cashfree)
AcquirerThe merchant's bank, which receives the funds
Card networkVisa, Mastercard, RuPay: routes card transactions between banks
IssuerThe customer's bank, which approves or declines
UPI (NPCI)India's real-time account-to-account network; apps such as PhonePe, Google Pay, Paytm act as front ends
RegulatorIn India the RBI: rules on recurring payments, tokenisation, data storage and aggregators

Card payment lifecycle

  1. Authorise: the issuer checks funds and fraud and places a hold. With 3-D Secure (OTP or app approval) the customer authenticates.
  2. Capture: the merchant confirms the charge (automatically or later, e.g. after shipping). An uncaptured authorisation expires.
  3. Settle: funds move to the gateway, then to your bank account, usually T+1 or T+2, minus fees.
  4. Refund: full or partial, returned through the same route; takes days to reach the customer.
  5. Chargeback / dispute: the customer disputes with their bank; you submit evidence or lose the funds plus a fee.

UPI in brief

  • Intent / QR flow: your checkout opens the customer's UPI app (or shows a QR); they approve with their UPI PIN.
  • Collect flow: you send a payment request to a UPI ID; the customer approves in their app. (Being phased down for many use cases; check current NPCI rules.)
  • Real-time and irrevocable from the customer's side; refunds are separate transactions.
  • UPI AutoPay: recurring mandates the customer approves once, for subscriptions.
  • Pending is normal: a payment can sit in a pending state for minutes; your system must wait for the final status rather than assume failure.

Standard integration flow (server-verified)

  1. Create an order on your server with the amount in the smallest unit (paise) and your own receipt ID. Store it as CREATED.
  2. Open the gateway's checkout in the browser or app with that order ID. Card and UPI details go to the gateway, never to your servers.
  3. Checkout returns payment ID, order ID and a signature to your client, which sends them to your server.
  4. Verify the signature on the server and fetch the payment from the gateway API to confirm amount, currency and status.
  5. Webhooks (payment.captured, payment.failed, refunds, disputes) arrive at your endpoint; verify their signature and update state idempotently. Treat webhooks and API fetches as the source of truth, never the browser.
  6. Fulfil only when your order is PAID according to the server.
  7. Reconcile daily against the gateway's settlement reports.

Razorpay: order and verification

# create an order (amount in paise: 50000 = ₹500.00)
curl -u "$RZP_KEY_ID:$RZP_KEY_SECRET" \
  -X POST https://api.razorpay.com/v1/orders \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"amount":50000,"currency":"INR","receipt":"order_8421"}'
// verify checkout signature: HMAC-SHA256(order_id + "|" + payment_id, key_secret)
static boolean validCheckout(String orderId, String paymentId, String signature, String keySecret)
    throws Exception {
  Mac mac = Mac.getInstance("HmacSHA256");
  mac.init(new SecretKeySpec(keySecret.getBytes(UTF_8), "HmacSHA256"));
  String expected = HexFormat.of().formatHex(
      mac.doFinal((orderId + "|" + paymentId).getBytes(UTF_8)));
  return MessageDigest.isEqual(expected.getBytes(UTF_8), signature.getBytes(UTF_8)); // constant time
}

// webhook: HMAC-SHA256 of the RAW request body with the webhook secret,
// compared with the X-Razorpay-Signature header. Never re-serialise the JSON first.

Stripe: PaymentIntent and webhook

import stripe
stripe.api_key = STRIPE_SECRET_KEY

intent = stripe.PaymentIntent.create(
    amount=50000, currency="inr",
    metadata={"order_id": "order_8421"},
    idempotency_key="order_8421-create",         # safe to retry
)
# return intent.client_secret to the front end, which confirms the payment

# webhook endpoint
def stripe_webhook(request):
    payload = request.body                        # raw bytes
    sig = request.headers["Stripe-Signature"]
    try:
        event = stripe.Webhook.construct_event(payload, sig, WEBHOOK_SECRET)
    except (ValueError, stripe.SignatureVerificationError):   # older SDKs: stripe.error.SignatureVerificationError
        return 400
    if not seen_before(event["id"]):              # dedupe: webhooks repeat
        handle(event)
    return 200

Payment state machine

CREATED ──checkout──▶ PENDING ──captured──▶ PAID ──refund──▶ PARTIALLY_REFUNDED / REFUNDED
   │                     │                    │
   │                     ├──failed──▶ FAILED  └──dispute──▶ DISPUTED ──▶ WON / LOST
   └──expired──▶ EXPIRED └──timeout──▶ (stay PENDING; poll the gateway; never assume)
  • Allow only valid transitions; reject or log anything else.
  • Events can arrive late or out of order (a webhook before the browser callback, a failure after a success for a different attempt). Decide by the gateway's fetched status.
  • Keep every transition as an append-only event with its source (webhook, API fetch, user).

Money-handling rules

  • Integers in the smallest unit (paise, cents) or decimal types; never floats.
  • Store the currency with every amount.
  • Compute prices and totals on the server. Never trust an amount sent by the client.
  • Idempotency keys on every create, capture and refund call, derived from your own IDs, so a retry cannot charge twice.
  • Webhook handlers are idempotent: dedupe by event ID; applying the same event twice changes nothing.
  • Unknown is not failed: a timeout means "we do not know yet". Poll the gateway before telling the customer it failed or retrying the charge.
  • Round once, at a defined step, with a documented rule (taxes, discounts, splits).

Double-entry ledger

CREATE TABLE ledger_entry (
  id           bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  txn_id       uuid    NOT NULL,          -- groups the entries of one transaction
  account      text    NOT NULL,          -- 'customer_receivable', 'gateway_clearing', 'revenue', 'gst_payable'
  amount_paise bigint  NOT NULL,          -- positive = debit, negative = credit
  currency     char(3) NOT NULL,
  created_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);
-- every txn_id must sum to zero
SELECT txn_id FROM ledger_entry GROUP BY txn_id HAVING SUM(amount_paise) <> 0;  -- must return nothing

Append only: corrections are new entries, never edits. Balances are sums. This makes every rupee traceable and reconciliation possible.

Subscriptions and recurring payments

  • Gateways model plans (price, interval) and subscriptions (a customer on a plan) with a mandate or saved token for charging.
  • India: RBI e-mandate rules govern recurring card and UPI payments: customer registration with authentication, pre-debit notifications, and extra authentication above a limit. Check the current limits and rules; they have changed over time.
  • Handle the lifecycle via webhooks: activated, charged, payment failed, halted, cancelled, completed.
  • Dunning: retries on failed renewals, customer notices, grace periods, then downgrade or cancel.
  • Entitlements (what the user may use) are derived from subscription state in your system, updated only from verified events.
  • Proration rules for upgrades and downgrades mid-period, decided and written down.

Security and compliance

  • PCI DSS: use hosted checkout or the gateway's elements so card data never reaches your servers; your compliance scope stays small.
  • Card tokenisation (India): merchants may not store card numbers; saved cards use network or issuer tokens through the gateway.
  • Data localisation: RBI requires payment system data to be stored in India; confirm your gateway and your own storage comply.
  • Secrets: API keys and webhook secrets in a secret manager; separate test and live keys; rotate on staff changes.
  • Webhook endpoint: verify signatures on the raw body, reject old timestamps where provided, respond quickly, process asynchronously.
  • Fraud: use the gateway's risk tools, 3-D Secure, velocity limits, and watch for card testing (many small failed attempts).
  • Tax and invoices: GST-compliant invoices with the correct rates and place of supply.

Reconciliation

  1. Download settlement and transaction reports from the gateway daily (API or dashboard).
  2. Match each gateway transaction to your order by order ID and amount.
  3. Match settlements to bank credits, accounting for fees and taxes.
  4. Flag mismatches: paid at gateway but not in your system (missed webhook), paid in your system but not settled, amount differences, unexpected refunds or disputes.
  5. Resolve each one and record why. A rising mismatch count is an incident.

Run reconciliation as a scheduled job with run records, like any pipeline.

Testing payments

  • Use the gateway's test mode keys, test cards and test UPI IDs for success, failure, 3-D Secure and pending cases.
  • Replay recorded webhook payloads in automated tests; build fixtures from real test-mode events, not guesses.
  • Test duplicates, out-of-order events, timeouts and partial refunds explicitly.
  • Keep a test-mode end-to-end checkout in your release checks.
  • Never test with live keys or real cards in non-production environments.

Operating payments

  • Metrics: payment success rate by method and bank, latency, webhook delay, pending count and age, refund rate, dispute rate.
  • Alerts: success rate drops, webhooks stop arriving, pending payments older than a threshold, reconciliation mismatches.
  • Support tools: look up an order's full event history; never let support edit payment state by hand.
  • Multiple gateways: routing or failover between gateways improves success rates, at the cost of more reconciliation.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain authorisation, capture, settlement, refunds and chargebacks
  • Explain the order → checkout → verify → webhook flow
  • Know why card data must never touch your servers

2 Practitioner

  • Integrate a gateway with server-side verification and signed webhooks
  • Make payment calls and webhook handling idempotent
  • Model payments as a state machine; test with test-mode fixtures

3 Senior

  • Design subscriptions, dunning and entitlements
  • Build a double-entry ledger and daily reconciliation
  • Own payment security, PCI scope and regulatory rules (RBI)

4 Expert

  • Design multi-gateway routing and payment platforms
  • Lead payments compliance and audits
  • Set money-handling standards across the organisation

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Explore further

  • UPI AutoPay and e-mandates
  • Card network tokenisation
  • Double-entry accounting for engineers
  • Fraud detection and chargeback management

  • Stripe documentation PaymentIntents, webhooks, billing

Spring Boot

Spring Boot 3.x on Java 17+, with what 3.2–3.5 added and what changes in 4.0. Servlet (MVC) and reactive (WebFlux) shown side by side.

Version map

VerYearWhat changed
2.72022Last 2.x line. Java 8+, javax.* packages
3.02022Java 17 baseline, Jakarta EE 9+ (javax.* → jakarta.*), Micrometer Observation, GraalVM native images, ProblemDetail
3.12023Testcontainers @ServiceConnection, Docker Compose support
3.22023Virtual threads (spring.threads.virtual.enabled), RestClient, JdbcClient
3.32024Class Data Sharing support, SBOM actuator endpoint
3.42024Structured logging (ECS, Logstash, GELF), @MockitoBean replaces @MockBean, graceful shutdown by default
3.52025The last 3.x feature release; upgrade here before moving to 4.0
4.02025Spring Framework 7, Jakarta EE 11, Jackson 3, smaller modular starters, API versioning, JSpecify null-safety; deprecated 3.x APIs removed

Moving to 4.0: upgrade to the latest 3.5 first, clear every deprecation warning, then follow the official migration guide.

@SpringBootApplication

  JudgmentController(JudgmentService svc) { this.svc = svc; }   // constructor injection

  @GetMapping("/{id}")
  JudgmentDto get(@PathVariable UUID id) { return svc.get(id); }

  @PostMapping("/search")
  List<JudgmentDto> search(@Valid @RequestBody SearchRequest req) {
    return svc.search(req);
  }

  @GetMapping
  Page<JudgmentDto> list(@RequestParam(defaultValue = "0") int page) { ... }
}

Data access

// JdbcClient (3.2): plain SQL, records out
@Repository
class JudgmentDao {
  private final JdbcClient jdbc;
  JudgmentDao(JdbcClient jdbc) { this.jdbc = jdbc; }

  Optional<JudgmentDto> find(String cnr) {
    return jdbc.sql("SELECT cnr, decision_date FROM court_judgments WHERE cnr = :cnr")
               .param("cnr", cnr)
               .query(JudgmentDto.class)
               .optional();
  }
  int upsert(JudgmentDto j) {
    return jdbc.sql("""
        INSERT INTO court_judgments (cnr, decision_date) VALUES (:cnr, :d)
        ON CONFLICT (cnr) DO UPDATE SET decision_date = EXCLUDED.decision_date""")
      .param("cnr", j.cnr()).param("d", j.decisionDate()).update();
  }
}

// Spring Data JPA
interface JudgmentRepo extends JpaRepository<Judgment, Long> {
  List<Judgment> findByCourtCodeAndDecisionDateAfter(String court, LocalDate d);
  @Query("select j from Judgment j where j.cnr in :cnrs")
  List<Judgment> byCnrs(Collection<String> cnrs);
}

@Transactional            // on a public method of a Spring bean, called from outside it
public void reload() { ... }
@Transactional(readOnly = true)

@Transactional works through a proxy: a call from another method in the same class skips it.

HTTP clients

// RestClient (3.2): synchronous, fluent
@Bean RestClient mirror(RestClient.Builder b) {
  return b.baseUrl("https://api.example.org").build();
}

Listing l = mirror.get()
    .uri("/objects?prefix={p}", prefix)
    .retrieve()
    .onStatus(HttpStatusCode::is4xxClientError, (req, res) -> {
      throw new IllegalStateException("listing failed: " +
          new String(res.getBody().readAllBytes()));   // keep the error body
    })
    .body(Listing.class);

// Declarative interface client
interface MirrorApi {
  @GetExchange("/objects") Listing list(@RequestParam String prefix);
}
@Bean MirrorApi api(RestClient mirror) {
  return HttpServiceProxyFactory.builderFor(RestClientAdapter.create(mirror))
      .build().createClient(MirrorApi.class);
}

WebFlux (reactive)

@RestController
class SearchController {
  @GetMapping("/api/search")
  Flux<JudgmentDto> search(@RequestParam String q) { return svc.search(q); }

  @GetMapping("/api/judgments/{id}")
  Mono<ResponseEntity<JudgmentDto>> get(@PathVariable UUID id) {
    return svc.get(id).map(ResponseEntity::ok)
              .defaultIfEmpty(ResponseEntity.notFound().build());
  }
}

WebClient wc = WebClient.builder().baseUrl(base).build();
Mono<Listing> m = wc.get().uri("/objects").retrieve()
    .bodyToMono(Listing.class)
    .timeout(Duration.ofSeconds(10))
    .retryWhen(Retry.backoff(3, Duration.ofMillis(200)));

// combine
Mono.zip(countHc(), countSc()).map(t -> t.getT1() + t.getT2());
Flux.fromIterable(uris).flatMap(this::fetch, 8);     // concurrency 8

Never call .block() on a request thread. Wrap blocking calls with Mono.fromCallable(...).subscribeOn(Schedulers.boundedElastic()).

Errors as ProblemDetail

@RestControllerAdvice
class ApiErrors {
  @ExceptionHandler(NotFoundException.class)
  ProblemDetail notFound(NotFoundException e) {
    ProblemDetail pd = ProblemDetail.forStatusAndDetail(HttpStatus.NOT_FOUND, e.getMessage());
    pd.setTitle("Judgment not found");
    pd.setProperty("cnr", e.cnr());
    return pd;
  }
}
// application.yml: spring.mvc.problemdetails.enabled: true
//                  (spring.webflux.problemdetails.enabled for WebFlux)
// Response: application/problem+json {type,title,status,detail,instance,cnr}

Batch job / Cloud Run job

// No web server; run once and exit with a code
@SpringBootApplication
class LoaderApp {
  public static void main(String[] args) {
    System.exit(SpringApplication.exit(SpringApplication.run(LoaderApp.class, args)));
  }
  @Bean ApplicationRunner run(Loader loader) {
    return args -> loader.load(args.getOptionValues("year"));   // --year=2024
  }
  @Bean ExitCodeGenerator exitCode(Loader loader) {
    return () -> loader.failed() ? 1 : 0;
  }
}
# spring.main.web-application-type: none

// In-app schedule (only for an always-on service)
@EnableScheduling
@Scheduled(cron = "0 15 6 * * *", zone = "UTC")
void tick() { ... }

Google Cloud (Spring Cloud GCP)

// com.google.cloud:spring-cloud-gcp-starter-bigquery / -storage
// (import spring-cloud-gcp-dependencies BOM for versions)
spring.cloud.gcp.project-id: my-proj
spring.cloud.gcp.bigquery.dataset-name: fdp

@Service
class Counts {
  private final BigQuery bq;                  // auto-configured client
  Counts(BigQuery bq) { this.bq = bq; }
  long total() throws InterruptedException {
    return bq.query(QueryJobConfiguration.of(
        "SELECT COUNT(*) n FROM `my-proj.fdp.judgment`"))
      .iterateAll().iterator().next().get("n").getLongValue();
  }
}

Testing

@WebMvcTest(JudgmentController.class)          // web slice only
class JudgmentControllerTest {
  @Autowired MockMvc mvc;
  @MockitoBean JudgmentService svc;            // 3.4+ (was @MockBean)

  @Test void returns404() throws Exception {
    when(svc.get(any())).thenThrow(new NotFoundException("x"));
    mvc.perform(get("/api/judgments/{id}", UUID.randomUUID()))
       .andExpect(status().isNotFound())
       .andExpect(jsonPath("$.title").value("Judgment not found"));
  }
}

Actuator & observability

management:
  endpoints.web.exposure.include: health,info,metrics,prometheus
  endpoint.health.probes.enabled: true    # /actuator/health/liveness, /readiness
  tracing.sampling.probability: 0.1

// custom metric
@Component
class LoadMetrics {
  private final Counter loaded;
  LoadMetrics(MeterRegistry r) { loaded = r.counter("corpus.rows.loaded"); }
  void add(long n) { loaded.increment(n); }
}

// custom health
@Component
class MirrorHealth implements HealthIndicator {
  public Health health() {
    return reachable() ? Health.up().build()
                       : Health.down().withDetail("mirror", "unreachable").build();
  }
}

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Build a REST endpoint with validation and a test
  • Configure an app with application.yml and profiles
  • Use Spring Data or JdbcClient for simple queries

2 Practitioner

  • Handle errors with ProblemDetail; write slice and integration tests
  • Use transactions correctly; understand proxies
  • Add Actuator, metrics and structured logging

3 Senior

  • Choose servlet vs reactive and design for throughput
  • Secure APIs with Spring Security and OAuth 2.0
  • Tune startup, memory and connection pools for the cloud

4 Expert

  • Lead major upgrades (3.x to 4.x) across services
  • Build internal starters and platform libraries
  • Set architecture and module boundaries (Spring Modulith)

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Quarkus 3.x: build-time dependency injection, fast startup, optional native images. Each card maps to its Spring Boot equivalent where one exists.

CLI & dev mode

quarkus create app org.acme:corpus-api \
  --extension='rest-jackson,hibernate-orm-panache,jdbc-postgresql,hibernate-validator'

quarkus ext add smallrye-health micrometer-registry-prometheus

./mvnw quarkus:dev           # same with Maven
./gradlew quarkusDev         # same with Gradle

./mvnw package -Dnative      # native binary (GraalVM / Mandrel)
./mvnw package -Dnative -Dquarkus.native.container-build=true   # no local GraalVM

Dev UI: http://localhost:8080/q/dev-ui. Ship target/quarkus-app/ as a directory; the -run.jar alone is not runnable.

REST endpoint

@Path("/api/judgments")
@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)
public class JudgmentResource {
  private final JudgmentService svc;
  JudgmentResource(JudgmentService svc) { this.svc = svc; }   // constructor injection

  @GET @Path("/{id}")
  public JudgmentDto get(@RestPath UUID id) { return svc.get(id); }

  @GET
  public List<JudgmentDto> list(@RestQuery @DefaultValue("0") int page) { ... }

  @POST @Path("/search")
  public List<JudgmentDto> search(@Valid SearchRequest req) { return svc.search(req); }

  @GET @Path("/{id}/raw")
  public RestResponse<String> raw(@RestPath UUID id) {
    return svc.raw(id).map(RestResponse::ok)
              .orElse(RestResponse.notFound());
  }
}

Data with Panache

// Active record
@Entity
public class Judgment extends PanacheEntity {     // gives Long id
  public String cnr;
  public LocalDate decisionDate;

  public static Optional<Judgment> byCnr(String cnr) {
    return find("cnr", cnr).firstResultOptional();
  }
}

// Repository style
@ApplicationScoped
public class JudgmentRepo implements PanacheRepository<Judgment> {
  public List<Judgment> recent(String court) {
    return list("courtCode = ?1 and decisionDate > ?2 order by decisionDate desc",
                court, LocalDate.now().minusDays(30));
  }
}

@Transactional
public void save(Judgment j) { j.persist(); }

Judgment.find("courtCode", "2_5").page(Page.of(0, 50)).list();
Judgment.count("decisionDate is null");

Dev Services: in dev and test, with no JDBC URL set, Quarkus starts a Postgres container for you (needs Docker or Podman).

REST client

@RegisterRestClient(configKey = "mirror")
@Path("/objects")
public interface MirrorClient {
  @GET Listing list(@RestQuery String prefix);
  @GET @Path("/{key}") Uni<byte[]> fetch(@RestPath String key);   // reactive
}

# application.properties
quarkus.rest-client.mirror.url=https://api.example.org
quarkus.rest-client.mirror.read-timeout=10000

@Inject @RestClient MirrorClient mirror;

// fault tolerance (quarkus-smallrye-fault-tolerance)
@Retry(maxRetries = 3, delay = 200)
@Timeout(10_000)
@CircuitBreaker(requestVolumeThreshold = 10)
public Listing safeList(String p) { return mirror.list(p); }

Threading: blocking, reactive, virtual

// Blocking signature → worker thread (default for non-Uni returns)
@GET public List<JudgmentDto> list() { ... }

// Reactive signature → event loop: never block inside it
@GET public Uni<JudgmentDto> get(@RestPath UUID id) {
  return client.fetch(id)
      .onItem().transform(this::toDto)
      .onFailure().retry().atMost(3)
      .ifNoItem().after(Duration.ofSeconds(5)).fail();
}

Multi.createFrom().iterable(uris)
     .onItem().transformToUniAndMerge(this::fetch);   // concurrent

// Virtual threads (Java 21+)
@GET @RunOnVirtualThread
public List<JudgmentDto> slowList() { return blockingDao.all(); }

// Force a reactive-typed method onto a worker
@Blocking

Errors, health, metrics

public class ErrorMappers {
  @ServerExceptionMapper
  public RestResponse<Map<String, String>> notFound(NotFoundException e) {
    return RestResponse.status(Response.Status.NOT_FOUND,
        Map.of("error", "not_found", "cnr", e.cnr()));
  }
}

@Readiness @ApplicationScoped
public class MirrorCheck implements HealthCheck {
  public HealthCheckResponse call() {
    return HealthCheckResponse.named("mirror").status(reachable()).build();
  }
}

  @Test void returns404() {
    when(svc.get(any())).thenThrow(new NotFoundException("x"));
    given().when().get("/api/judgments/{id}", UUID.randomUUID())
           .then().statusCode(404)
           .body("error", is("not_found"));
  }
}

@QuarkusIntegrationTest                    // runs against the built jar or native binary
class JudgmentResourceIT extends JudgmentResourceTest {}

@TestProfile(NoSchedulerProfile.class)     // per-test config overrides
public class NoSchedulerProfile implements QuarkusTestProfile {
  public Map<String, String> getConfigOverrides() {
    return Map.of("quarkus.scheduler.enabled", "false");
  }
}

Architecture patterns

Event-driven systems, choreography vs orchestration, sagas, the outbox, and a full catalogue of architectural, data, cloud and integration patterns, each with when to use it and what it costs. Ends with how to choose a style and record the decision.

Event-driven systems

Services communicate by publishing events (facts about something that happened: JudgmentLanded, OrderPaid) to a broker. Other services subscribe and react. The producer does not know or wait for its consumers.

TermMeaning
EventSomething that happened, past tense, immutable. Many consumers.
CommandA request to do something (LoadPartition). One handler, may be refused.
BrokerPub/Sub, Kafka, Eventarc: stores and delivers messages.
Topic / subscriptionWhere events are published / one consumer group's view of them.
At-least-onceThe usual guarantee: a message may arrive twice. Consumers must be idempotent.
OrderingUsually not global. Use an ordering key (per order, per entity) when sequence matters.
Dead-letter queueWhere messages go after repeated failures, so one bad message does not block the rest.

Gains: loose coupling, independent scaling, new consumers added without touching producers. Costs: eventual consistency, harder debugging (no single call stack), duplicates and reordering to handle, schema changes that ripple to unknown consumers.

Choreography vs orchestration

ChoreographyOrchestration
HowEach service reacts to events and emits its own. No one is in charge.A central orchestrator tells each service what to do next and tracks state.
AnalogyDancers who each know their cueA conductor with the score
Good forFew steps, independent teams, fan-out reactions ("on landing, also index and notify")Many steps, strict order, retries, compensation, a need to answer "where is this run?"
RiskThe overall flow exists only in people's heads; cycles and lost steps are hard to seeThe orchestrator is a central dependency and can grow into a god service
ToolsPub/Sub, Kafka, EventarcAirflow, Cloud Workflows, Temporal, Step Functions, a custom orchestrator with a ledger

Many systems mix them: orchestration inside a bounded process (a data pipeline's stages), choreography between domains (the pipeline publishes "ready"; the search team reacts).

Saga: transactions across services

A distributed transaction across several databases cannot use one ACID commit. A saga splits it into local transactions, each with a compensating action that undoes it if a later step fails.

Book a paid subscription:
  1. Billing:  create pending invoice        ↺ compensate: void invoice
  2. Payments: charge card                   ↺ compensate: refund charge
  3. Accounts: activate plan                 ↺ compensate: deactivate plan
  4. Email:    send welcome                  (no compensation needed; last, pivot-free)

Step 3 fails → run compensations in reverse: refund (2), void invoice (1).
  • Orchestrated saga: a coordinator runs the steps and the compensations, and stores the saga's state. Easier to follow and test.
  • Choreographed saga: each service listens for the previous step's event and emits its own, including failure events that trigger compensation.
  • Compensation is a new action, not a rollback: a refund is not "un-charging". Some steps (an email sent) cannot be undone, so put them last.
  • Every step and compensation must be idempotent, because messages repeat.
  • Expose intermediate states (PENDING, ACTIVE, CANCELLED): other readers will see them.

Transactional outbox

Problem: you update your database and publish an event. If one succeeds and the other fails, they disagree.

BEGIN;
  UPDATE orders SET status = 'PAID' WHERE id = 42;
  INSERT INTO outbox (id, topic, payload, created_at)
  VALUES (gen_random_uuid(), 'order.paid', '{"order_id":42}', now());
COMMIT;

-- relay process (or CDC on the outbox table)
SELECT * FROM outbox WHERE published_at IS NULL
ORDER BY created_at LIMIT 100 FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED;
-- publish each, then
UPDATE outbox SET published_at = now() WHERE id = ANY($1);

One local transaction makes the state change and the intent to publish atomic. The relay may publish twice; consumers dedupe by event ID.

Idempotent consumers

-- remember what you processed, in the same transaction as the effect
CREATE TABLE processed_events (event_id uuid PRIMARY KEY, processed_at timestamptz);

BEGIN;
  INSERT INTO processed_events VALUES ($event_id, now())
  ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING;
  -- if 0 rows inserted: duplicate, skip the effect
  UPDATE accounts SET plan = 'pro' WHERE id = $account;
COMMIT;
  • Prefer naturally idempotent operations: SET status = 'PAID' rather than balance = balance + 10.
  • APIs take an idempotency key header so a retried POST does not charge twice.
  • Batch jobs overwrite a partition or MERGE on a key.

CQRS & event sourcing

  • CQRS (command query responsibility segregation): writes go to a model shaped for updates; reads come from separate models shaped for queries (a search index, a denormalised table). Reads are eventually consistent.
  • Event sourcing: store the sequence of events as the source of truth; current state is derived by replaying them. Full audit history and time travel, at the cost of complexity, schema versioning of old events, and snapshots for speed.
  • Append-only ledger is the lightweight version many data platforms use: one row per state change, current state from a "latest event" view. Never UPDATE, never DELETE.
  • Use them where history and audit are the product. Most CRUD services do not need either.

Common architectural patterns

PatternWhat it isUse when / watch out
Layered (n-tier)Controller → service → repository → databaseDefault for a service. Keep controllers thin and rules in services.
Hexagonal (ports & adapters)Domain logic in the centre; databases, APIs and queues are adapters behind interfacesLogic you want to test without infrastructure, or swap providers.
Modular monolithOne deployable with enforced module boundariesMost teams' best starting point. Split later along boundaries that proved real.
MicroservicesIndependently deployed services owning their dataMany teams, different scaling needs. Costs: network failures, distributed data, ops overhead.
API gatewaySingle entry point: routing, auth, rate limitsMany backends behind one public surface.
Backend for frontend (BFF)A thin API per client type (web, mobile)Clients with very different data needs.
Strangler figRoute features one by one from the old system to the newReplacing a legacy system without a big-bang rewrite.
Cache-asideRead the cache; on a miss read the DB and fill the cacheRead-heavy data that tolerates brief staleness. Decide invalidation up front.
Circuit breakerStop calling a failing dependency for a while; fail fastPrevents one slow service taking down its callers.
Retry with backoff + jitterRetry transient failures with growing, randomised waitsOnly for idempotent calls. Cap attempts; never retry 4xx validation errors.
BulkheadSeparate pools for separate dependenciesOne slow dependency cannot exhaust all threads or connections.
Queue-based load levellingPut work on a queue; workers drain it at their paceSpiky load in front of a resource with fixed capacity.
SidecarA helper container next to the app (proxy, log shipper)Cross-cutting concerns in Kubernetes and service meshes.

Consistency & trade-offs

  • CAP: during a network partition a distributed store chooses consistency (refuse some requests) or availability (answer, possibly stale).
  • PACELC: and when there is no partition, it trades latency against consistency.
  • Strong consistency: every read sees the latest write. Eventual: replicas converge, reads may lag. Read-your-writes: a user always sees their own changes.
  • Exactly-once delivery is rarely real end to end; build at-least-once delivery plus idempotent processing.
  • Fallacies of distributed computing: the network is not reliable, latency is not zero, bandwidth is not infinite, and the topology changes.

Design review checklist

  • Who owns each piece of data, and who may write it?
  • What happens when each dependency is slow, down, or returns garbage?
  • Which operations must be atomic, and how is that achieved across boundaries?
  • What is retried, and is it idempotent?
  • How do we see a request or run end to end (IDs, traces, ledger)?
  • What is the expected load, and what breaks first at 10×?
  • How do we deploy, migrate and roll back without downtime?
  • What is the simplest design that meets today's need?

Pattern catalogue: architectural styles

StyleShapeUse whenCosts
Layered (n-tier)Presentation → business → persistenceMost CRUD services; easy to understandLogic leaks across layers; changes cut through every layer
Hexagonal / ports and adaptersDomain core; databases, queues, APIs plug in through interfacesLogic worth testing without infrastructure; swappable providersMore interfaces and mapping code
Clean / onionConcentric rings; dependencies point inward to the domainLong-lived, complex domainsCeremony for simple services
Modular monolithOne deployable, enforced module boundariesDefault starting point for most productsNeeds discipline and tooling to keep boundaries
MicroservicesIndependently deployed services owning dataMany teams, different scaling or release needs (tab)Distributed-systems complexity
Service-oriented (SOA)Shared enterprise services, often via an ESBIntegrating large legacy estatesCentral bus becomes a bottleneck
Event-drivenProducers emit events; consumers reactDecoupled reactions, fan-out, integration across domainsEventual consistency; harder to trace
Microkernel / plug-inSmall core plus plug-insProducts extended by customers or partners (IDEs, rule engines)Plug-in contract must stay stable
Pipes and filtersData flows through independent processing stepsData pipelines, ETL, media processingError handling across steps; overhead between them
Space-basedProcessing units with replicated in-memory data gridsExtreme, spiky load with low latencyComplex; data consistency is hard
ServerlessFunctions and managed services, scale to zeroSpiky or low traffic, event handlers, small teamsCold starts, limits, vendor coupling
CQRSSeparate write and read modelsReads and writes with very different shapes or scaleTwo models; eventual consistency
Event sourcingEvents as the source of truthAudit and history are the product (tab)Event versioning, replay, learning curve

Pattern catalogue: data architecture

PatternIdeaUse when
Layered / medallion (raw → foundation → consumable)Each layer has one job; derivation runs one wayAny analytics platform (Data Engineering)
Lambda architectureA batch layer for accuracy plus a speed layer for freshness, merged at query timeLegacy; most teams now prefer kappa or streaming warehouses
Kappa architectureOne streaming path; reprocess by replaying the logEvent streams with replayable history (Kafka)
LakehouseWarehouse tables over open files in object storageSeveral engines sharing the same data
Data meshDomains own and publish data products; a platform supports themLarge organisations where a central team is the bottleneck
Database per serviceEach service owns its schemaMicroservices
Change data captureStream a database's changes to other systemsReplication to warehouses, caches, search
Materialised view / read modelPrecomputed query results kept up to dateExpensive queries read often
Claim checkStore a large payload in object storage; send only a reference in the messageMessages larger than the broker's limit

Pattern catalogue: cloud and integration

PatternIdea
Anti-corruption layerA translation layer that keeps a legacy or external model from leaking into your domain
Gateway routing / aggregation / offloadingOne entry point routes requests, combines backend calls, or handles TLS, auth and rate limits
Backend for frontendAn API tailored to one client type
Strangler figReplace a system piece by piece behind a router
Competing consumersSeveral workers read one queue to share load
Priority queueSeparate queues so urgent work is not stuck behind bulk work
Queue-based load levellingA queue absorbs spikes in front of a fixed-capacity service
Throttling / rate limitingCap use per client to protect the system
Valet keyGive clients a short-lived signed URL to upload or download directly from storage
Static content hostingServe static files from object storage and a CDN
Leader electionOne instance coordinates; others take over if it fails
Scheduler agent supervisorCoordinate steps, watch them, retry or compensate failures
Health endpoint monitoringEndpoints that report liveness and readiness
Sidecar / ambassador / adapterHelper containers beside the app for proxying, telemetry or protocol translation
Compensating transactionUndo completed steps when a later one fails (sagas)

Choosing a style

  1. List the quality attributes that matter most, in order: e.g. correctness and auditability, then cost, then latency.
  2. Note the constraints: team size and skills, budget, regulations, existing systems.
  3. Pick the simplest style that satisfies the top attributes; resist adding patterns "for later".
  4. Identify the riskiest assumption and test it with a spike or prototype.
  5. Record the decision and its trade-offs in an ADR.

Architecture decision record

# ADR 0042: Serve corpus search from Postgres, not a search engine
Status:   Accepted (2026-11-12)
Context:  20 M judgments, ~60 searches/s peak, p95 < 500 ms;
          team already runs Postgres; no search-engine skills.
Decision: Postgres full-text (GIN) + B-tree filters on a read replica;
          revisit if p95 > 500 ms or relevance complaints exceed X/month.
Alternatives: Elasticsearch (more ops, better relevance);
              BigQuery (latency too high for interactive search).
Consequences: + one database to run  − relevance tuning limited
Links:    load test results, issue #…

One decision per record, numbered, never edited after acceptance; supersede it with a new one. Keep them in the repo.

  • Explain layered and hexagonal architecture
  • Tell events from commands; choreography from orchestration
  • Recognise retries that need idempotency

2 Practitioner

  • Implement outbox, idempotent consumers and retries with backoff
  • Design a saga with compensations
  • Apply circuit breakers, bulkheads and timeouts

3 Senior

  • Choose monolith, modular monolith or services for a context
  • Design event-driven systems with contracts and evolution
  • Run design reviews and write decision records

4 Expert

  • Set architecture direction across many teams
  • Balance consistency, availability, cost and team structure
  • Lead large migrations (strangler fig) safely

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Microservices

Building a system as small, independently deployable services that each own their data. When it pays off, how to split, how services talk and fail, and the traps that turn microservices into a distributed monolith. Patterns referenced here are on Architecture.

Monolith, modular monolith, microservices

MonolithModular monolithMicroservices
DeployablesOneOne, with enforced internal modulesMany, deployed independently
DataOne shared databaseOne database, schema per moduleEach service owns its data
Calls between partsIn-processIn-process through module APIsOver the network
ScalingWhole appWhole appPer service
Team fitOne small teamOne to a few teamsMany teams owning services end to end
Main costTangling as it growsDiscipline to keep boundariesDistributed-systems complexity and operations

Most systems should start as a modular monolith and extract a service only when a boundary has proved stable and there is a concrete reason: independent scaling, a different release cadence, a separate team, or different technology or security needs.

What microservices buy, and cost

Gains

  • Teams deploy their service without coordinating a big release.
  • Scale and tune each service for its own load.
  • A failure can be contained to one service.
  • Different services can use different technologies where it helps.

Costs

  • Network calls fail, time out and add latency.
  • No cross-service transactions: sagas and eventual consistency instead.
  • Debugging needs distributed tracing; testing needs contracts.
  • Many pipelines, deployments, dashboards and on-call rotations.

How to split services

  • By business capability or bounded context (domain-driven design): search, case files, billing, identity. Not by technical layer (a "database service" is an anti-pattern).
  • Each service owns its data and is the only one that writes it. Others ask through its API or react to its events.
  • High cohesion inside, loose coupling between: a typical change should touch one service.
  • Align with teams (Conway's law): one team owns a service end to end.
  • Size by responsibility, not lines of code. Too many tiny services ("nano-services") multiply network calls and operations for no gain.
  • Test the split: if two services must always deploy together, they are one service.

Communication

StyleUse forWatch out for
Synchronous REST / gRPCQueries that need an answer nowChains of calls: latency adds up and availability multiplies down
Asynchronous events (Kafka, Pub/Sub)Telling others something happenedEventual consistency, duplicates, ordering
Asynchronous commands (queues)Work another service should doRetries, dead letters, idempotency
  • Prefer events between domains; keep synchronous calls shallow (ideally one hop).
  • Cache or replicate the data you need from other services (from their events) rather than calling them on every request.
  • See APIs & Integration, Kafka and GraphQL.

Infrastructure around services

  • Service discovery: Kubernetes DNS names, Cloud Run URLs, or a registry. Never hard-code instance IPs.
  • API gateway at the edge for authentication, rate limits and routing (Apigee); a backend for frontend per client type when needs differ.
  • Service mesh (Istio / Cloud Service Mesh) for mTLS, retries and traffic splitting between services, without code in each one.
  • Configuration and secrets injected at runtime per environment.
  • Platform blueprint: a service template with logging, tracing, health checks, security and CI already wired, so each new service starts correct.

Resilience in code (Spring + Resilience4j)

@Service
class MirrorClient {
  private final RestClient http;
  MirrorClient(RestClient http) { this.http = http; }

  @CircuitBreaker(name = "mirror", fallbackMethod = "unavailable")
  @Retry(name = "mirror")
  Listing list(String prefix) {
    return http.get().uri("/objects?prefix={p}", prefix).retrieve().body(Listing.class);
  }

  // honest degradation: "cannot see", never "nothing there"
  Listing unavailable(String prefix, Throwable t) {
    return Listing.unknown("mirror unavailable: " + t.getMessage());
  }
}
resilience4j:
  circuitbreaker:
    instances:
      mirror:
        slidingWindowSize: 20
        failureRateThreshold: 50
        waitDurationInOpenState: 30s
  retry:
    instances:
      mirror:
        maxAttempts: 3
        waitDuration: 200ms
        enableExponentialBackoff: true

Quarkus offers the same through MicroProfile Fault Tolerance (@Retry, @CircuitBreaker, @Timeout, @Fallback). Always set a client timeout too.

Data across services

  • Database per service: separate schemas or instances; no other service reads its tables.
  • Cross-service transactions become sagas with compensating steps.
  • Reliable events with the transactional outbox, so a state change and its event never disagree.
  • Queries across services: API composition (call and combine) for small cases; a read model built from events (CQRS) for heavy ones.
  • Reporting goes to the warehouse via events or CDC, not by joining services' databases.
  • Details on Architecture and Event Sourcing.

Observability is not optional

  • Distributed tracing (OpenTelemetry): one trace ID follows a request through every service, queue and database call.
  • Structured logs with trace ID, service, version and request ID on every line.
  • Metrics per service: rate, errors, duration (RED) and saturation; SLOs on user-facing ones.
  • Health endpoints: liveness (restart me) separate from readiness (send me traffic).
  • Dependency maps from traces show who calls whom, and where latency comes from.

Testing microservices

  • Unit and component tests inside each service, with its own database in a container.
  • Contract tests (Pact, Spring Cloud Contract) between consumers and providers, run in both pipelines, so a provider cannot break a consumer unnoticed.
  • A few end-to-end tests of critical journeys in a shared environment.
  • Event schema checks with a schema registry and compatibility rules.
  • Resilience tests: kill or slow a dependency and check the caller degrades as designed.

Security between services

  • Zero trust: authenticate every call, even inside the network.
  • Service identity: mTLS from a mesh, or Google-signed ID tokens between Cloud Run services.
  • User context: propagate the user's token or a signed internal token; each service still checks its own permissions.
  • Least privilege per service: its own service account, database user and secrets.

Anti-patterns

  • Distributed monolith: services that must be deployed together or share a database.
  • Shared database written by several services.
  • Chatty synchronous chains: A calls B calls C calls D for one request.
  • Nano-services too small to justify their operational cost.
  • Shared libraries with business logic that force lock-step upgrades (shared infrastructure blueprints are fine).
  • No ownership: services nobody is on call for.
  • Splitting before understanding the domain: boundaries drawn too early are expensive to move.

Migrating from a monolith

  1. Make the monolith modular first: enforce module boundaries and separate each module's data.
  2. Pick one capability with a clear boundary and a real reason to move.
  3. Put a routing layer (gateway or proxy) in front, and route that capability's traffic to the new service (strangler fig).
  4. Move its data, with a period of syncing or dual reads, and make the new service the only writer.
  5. Remove the old code. Repeat only if the first extraction paid off.

Readiness checklist

  • Can you deploy a service with one automated pipeline, in minutes?
  • Do you have centralised logs, metrics and tracing?
  • Can you create a new service from a template in under an hour?
  • Do teams own services end to end, including on-call?
  • Are the domain boundaries understood and stable?

If several answers are no, a modular monolith will serve you better for now.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain monolith vs modular monolith vs microservices
  • Work in one service: build, test, deploy, read its logs and traces
  • Know why services own their data

2 Practitioner

  • Build a service with timeouts, retries, circuit breakers and health checks
  • Use events and the outbox to integrate with other services
  • Write contract tests with consumers

3 Senior

  • Draw service boundaries from the domain; decide when not to split
  • Design sagas, read models and observability across services
  • Lead a strangler-fig extraction from a monolith

4 Expert

  • Set the organisation's service architecture and platform blueprint
  • Align team structure with service ownership
  • Decide when to consolidate services as well as split them

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

  • Contract testing with Pact
  • Service mesh traffic management
  • Team Topologies
  • Platform engineering and service templates

  • microservices.io Pattern language: decomposition, data, communication, observability

Event sourcing and CQRS

Storing every change as an immutable event and deriving current state from them. How the event store, aggregates and projections work, how to evolve events, and when the extra complexity is worth it.

The idea

Most systems store current state and overwrite it: a subscription row says plan = 'pro'. Event sourcing stores the sequence of facts instead: SubscriptionStarted(basic), PlanUpgraded(pro), PaymentFailed, PaymentSucceeded. Current state is computed by replaying (folding) those events. The events are the source of truth; every other view is derived from them and can be rebuilt.

TermMeaning
EventAn immutable fact in the past tense, with data and metadata (who, when, causation)
StreamThe ordered events of one entity, e.g. subscription-42
AggregateThe entity that enforces business rules; rebuilt from its stream; decides which new events a command produces
CommandA request to change something (UpgradePlan); may be rejected
Event storeAppend-only storage of streams with per-stream versions
Projection / read modelA view built from events for querying (a table, a search index, a cache)
SnapshotSaved aggregate state at a version, to avoid replaying long streams
CQRSSeparate models for writes (commands) and reads (queries); event sourcing usually comes with it

When it is worth it

Good fits

  • History is the product: audit trails, finance, legal records, compliance.
  • You need to answer "what was the state on date X, and why?"
  • Several different read models from the same changes.
  • Complex domains where business events are first-class (orders, claims, subscriptions).

Poor fits

  • Simple CRUD with no interest in history.
  • Teams new to the pattern on a deadline.
  • Data that must be truly deleted on request with no workaround.

Apply it to the one part of the system that needs it, not the whole system.

The flow

  1. A command arrives: UpgradePlan(subscription-42, pro).
  2. Load the aggregate's events (from the latest snapshot) and fold them into current state.
  3. The aggregate checks business rules and decides zero or more new events.
  4. Append them with the expected version (optimistic concurrency). If someone else appended first, reload and retry.
  5. Projections consume new events and update read models.
  6. Queries read from the read models, which may lag slightly behind.

An aggregate in Java

sealed interface SubEvent permits Started, Upgraded, Cancelled {}
record Started(String id, String plan, Instant at)   implements SubEvent {}
record Upgraded(String id, String plan, Instant at)  implements SubEvent {}
record Cancelled(String id, String reason, Instant at) implements SubEvent {}

enum Status { ACTIVE, CANCELLED }

record Subscription(String id, String plan, Status status, int version) {

  static Subscription replay(List<SubEvent> events) {
    Subscription s = null;
    for (SubEvent e : events) s = apply(s, e);
    return s;
  }

  static Subscription apply(Subscription s, SubEvent e) {     // pure: no side effects
    return switch (e) {
      case Started st   -> new Subscription(st.id(), st.plan(), Status.ACTIVE, 1);
      case Upgraded u   -> new Subscription(s.id(), u.plan(), s.status(), s.version() + 1);
      case Cancelled c  -> new Subscription(s.id(), s.plan(), Status.CANCELLED, s.version() + 1);
    };
  }

  List<SubEvent> upgrade(String newPlan, Instant now) {        // decide
    if (status != Status.ACTIVE) throw new IllegalStateException("subscription not active");
    if (plan.equals(newPlan)) return List.of();                 // idempotent: nothing to do
    return List.of(new Upgraded(id, newPlan, now));
  }
}

Separate decide (rules, may reject) from apply (pure state change). Apply must never fail for a stored event: it already happened.

An event store in Postgres

CREATE TABLE events (
  global_position bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  stream_id   text        NOT NULL,
  version     int         NOT NULL,
  type        text        NOT NULL,
  data        jsonb       NOT NULL,
  metadata    jsonb       NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}',   -- user, causation_id, correlation_id
  recorded_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  UNIQUE (stream_id, version)                      -- optimistic concurrency
);

-- load a stream
SELECT type, data, version FROM events
WHERE stream_id = 'subscription-42' ORDER BY version;

-- append, expecting the stream to be at version 3
INSERT INTO events (stream_id, version, type, data)
VALUES ('subscription-42', 4, 'PlanUpgraded', '{"plan":"pro"}');
-- unique violation → another writer got version 4 first → reload, re-decide, retry

Caution: identity values are assigned at insert, not commit, so with concurrent writers a projection reading by global_position can miss a row committed late. Dedicated event stores (KurrentDB, formerly EventStoreDB) or a transactional outbox with careful checkpointing handle this.

Projections and read models

// a projection keeps a checkpoint and is idempotent
void handle(StoredEvent e) {
  tx.run(() -> {
    switch (e.payload()) {
      case Started s   -> db.upsert("subscription_view", s.id(), Map.of("plan", s.plan(), "status", "ACTIVE"));
      case Upgraded u  -> db.update("subscription_view", u.id(), Map.of("plan", u.plan()));
      case Cancelled c -> db.update("subscription_view", c.id(), Map.of("status", "CANCELLED"));
    }
    db.saveCheckpoint("subscription_view", e.globalPosition());  // same transaction
  });
}
  • Store the checkpoint in the same transaction as the update, so a crash never applies an event twice or skips one.
  • To change a read model, build a new one by replaying all events, then switch reads to it.
  • Read models are eventually consistent. Show users "updating…" or read their own writes from the aggregate when it matters.

Evolving events

  • Events are immutable and permanent. You cannot fix old ones in place; plan their shape carefully.
  • Additive changes (new optional fields with defaults) are safe.
  • Version the event type (PlanUpgraded.v2) when the meaning changes.
  • Upcasting: convert old event versions to the current shape when reading, so the code handles one version.
  • Never rename or reuse an event type for a different meaning.
  • Name events in business language (PaymentFailed), not technical (RowUpdated).

Snapshots, size and deletion

  • Snapshots every N events (or when replay gets slow) store state plus version; load the snapshot, then only later events.
  • Keep streams short-lived where the domain allows (one stream per billing period, not per customer forever).
  • Personal data and deletion: keep personal data out of events where possible (reference an ID), or encrypt it per person and destroy the key to "forget" them (crypto-shredding).
  • Archiving: move closed streams to cold storage after their retention period.

Testing: given, when, then

@Test
void upgradeEmitsEventWhenActive() {
  var given = List.<SubEvent>of(new Started("s1", "basic", T0));
  var events = Subscription.replay(given).upgrade("pro", T1);          // when
  assertThat(events).containsExactly(new Upgraded("s1", "pro", T1));   // then
}

@Test
void cannotUpgradeCancelled() {
  var given = List.<SubEvent>of(new Started("s1", "basic", T0), new Cancelled("s1", "user", T1));
  assertThatThrownBy(() -> Subscription.replay(given).upgrade("pro", T2))
      .hasMessageContaining("not active");
}

Aggregates tested this way need no database or mocks: given past events, when a command, then expected events or a rejection.

The lightweight version: an append-only ledger

Many systems get most of the benefit without full event sourcing: an append-only table with one row per state change, and current state from a "latest event" view. Never UPDATE, never DELETE.

-- BigQuery: current state of each pipeline stage from its event log
CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW `p.dp_control.v_pipeline_run_latest` AS
SELECT * FROM `p.dp_control.pipeline_run_events`
WHERE TRUE
QUALIFY ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY run_date, stage ORDER BY event_ts DESC) = 1;

You get history, auditability and "what happened on date X" with ordinary SQL. Add aggregates, projections and replay only if the domain needs them.

Tools

  • KurrentDB (formerly EventStoreDB): a database built for event streams, subscriptions and projections.
  • Axon Framework / Axon Server (Java): aggregates, sagas, projections and an event store.
  • Postgres with an events table: simple and often enough, with the caveats above.
  • Kafka is a good event log for distribution, but a weak event store: no efficient per-entity stream reads and no per-stream optimistic concurrency. Many systems store events in a database and publish them to Kafka through an outbox.

Common mistakes

  • Event sourcing the whole system instead of the part that needs history.
  • Events that mirror table changes (CustomerUpdated with every field) instead of business facts.
  • Side effects (emails, payments) inside apply, so a replay sends them again.
  • Querying the event store for lists and searches instead of building read models.
  • No plan for event versioning, personal data or replay time.
  • Treating eventual consistency of read models as a bug rather than designing for it.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain events, streams, aggregates, projections and CQRS
  • Read an event stream and follow how state was built
  • Tell an append-only ledger from full event sourcing

2 Practitioner

  • Implement an aggregate with decide/apply and given-when-then tests
  • Build idempotent projections with checkpoints
  • Append with optimistic concurrency

3 Senior

  • Decide where event sourcing fits in a system and where it does not
  • Design event schemas, versioning and upcasting
  • Plan snapshots, replay, rebuilds and personal-data handling

4 Expert

  • Lead event-sourced architectures across domains and teams
  • Choose and operate event stores at scale
  • Set standards for event modelling and governance

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

  • CQRS (Martin Fowler) When separate read and write models help
  • Event sourcing pattern (microservices.io) In a microservices context

System design

How to design a system from a blank page: a repeatable method, the numbers to estimate with, the building blocks, how each scales, and worked examples. The Architecture and Database Design tabs go deeper on patterns and storage.

The method, in order

  1. Clarify requirements. Functional: what users can do. Non-functional: scale, latency, availability, consistency, durability, cost, security, compliance. Write down what is out of scope.
  2. Estimate. Users, requests per second (average and peak), read/write ratio, data size per item, storage per year, bandwidth. Numbers decide the design.
  3. Define the API. The few endpoints or events that deliver the functional requirements, with their inputs and outputs.
  4. Model the data. Entities, keys, access patterns ("fetch by X, list by Y sorted by Z"), and which store fits each pattern.
  5. Draw the high-level design. Clients, load balancer, services, stores, caches, queues. One request path end to end.
  6. Deep-dive the hard parts. The component that is hottest, biggest or most consistency-sensitive. Show how it scales and fails.
  7. Find bottlenecks and failure modes. What breaks first at 10×? What happens when each dependency is down?
  8. State trade-offs. What you chose, what you gave up, and what would make you change it.

The same steps make a good design document. Start simple, and add a component only when a requirement or a number demands it.

Back-of-the-envelope numbers

QuantityRule of thumb
Seconds per day86,400 ≈ 105
1 M requests/day≈ 12 per second on average
Peak2–10× average, depending on the traffic shape
1 KB × 1 M items1 GB
1 KB × 1 B items1 TB
One Postgres instancethousands of simple queries/s; much less for heavy ones
One app instancehundreds to a few thousand requests/s, workload-dependent
Example: search API for 50k lawyers
  50k users × 20 searches/day = 1 M/day ≈ 12/s avg, ~60/s peak
  20M judgments × 2 KB metadata ≈ 40 GB  (fits one Postgres with indexes)
  full text: 20M × ~7 KB avg ≈ 140 GB    (store separately; index for search)
→ one primary + read replica + cache is plenty; no sharding needed

Latency, order of magnitude

OperationRoughly
Main memory read100 ns
SSD random read~100 µs
Round trip within a datacenter / zone~0.5 ms
Indexed database lookup~1 ms
Cache (Redis) get over the network~1 ms
Round trip between regions on one continent10–50 ms
Round trip across continents~150 ms
LLM responsehundreds of ms to tens of seconds

Network calls dominate. Ten sequential service calls cost far more than ten database rows. Parallelise independent calls and keep hot paths short.

Availability in downtime

AvailabilityDowntime / year/ month
99%3.65 days7.3 h
99.9%8.8 h43.8 min
99.95%4.4 h21.9 min
99.99%52.6 min4.4 min
99.999%5.3 min26 s

Dependencies in series multiply: three services at 99.9% each give about 99.7%. Redundancy in parallel raises it. Each extra nine costs a lot more; pick the target the users actually need.

Building blocks

BlockDoesOn GCPWatch out for
DNSName to address; can route by region or weightCloud DNSTTLs slow down changes
CDNCaches static and cacheable responses near usersCloud CDNInvalidation; never cache personalised responses by mistake
Load balancerSpreads traffic, health checks, TLS terminationCloud Load BalancingSticky sessions hide stateful services
API gatewayAuth, rate limits, routing for many backendsAPI Gateway, ApigeeBecoming a place for business logic
Stateless serviceBusiness logic, scales horizontallyCloud Run, GKEHidden state in memory or local disk
Relational DBTransactions, constraints, flexible queriesCloud SQL, AlloyDB, SpannerWrite scaling, connection limits
Key-value / document storeMassive scale lookups by keyFirestore, Bigtable, MemorystoreQueries outside the key design are expensive
CacheServes hot reads from memoryMemorystore (Redis)Staleness, invalidation, stampedes
Object storageFiles and blobs, cheap and durableCloud StorageListing huge prefixes is slow
Queue / logDecouples producers and consumers, absorbs spikesPub/Sub, Kafka, Cloud TasksDuplicates, ordering, poison messages
Search indexFull-text and faceted search, rankingPostgres FTS, Elasticsearch/OpenSearch, Vertex AI SearchKeeping it in sync with the source of truth
WarehouseAnalytics over everythingBigQueryNot for low-latency per-user reads
Batch / stream processingHeavy transforms off the request pathDataflow, Cloud Run jobs, DataprocLate data, reprocessing, cost
Scheduler / workflowRecurring and multi-step workCloud Scheduler, Workflows, ComposerA laptop or a person standing in for it

Scaling playbook, cheapest first

  1. Measure. Find the actual bottleneck: CPU, memory, DB queries, a slow dependency.
  2. Fix the query. Indexes, fewer round trips, no N+1. Often 10–100× for free.
  3. Scale up the instance. Boring and effective.
  4. Make services stateless and scale out behind a load balancer.
  5. Cache hot reads; add a CDN for public content.
  6. Read replicas for read-heavy databases.
  7. Move work off the request path to queues and workers.
  8. Partition big tables; archive cold data.
  9. Shard or move to a distributed database only when one primary cannot take the writes.

Caching strategies

  • Cache-aside: app reads cache, on a miss reads the DB and fills the cache. Most common.
  • Read-through: the cache library loads from the DB itself on a miss.
  • Write-through: write to cache and DB together; reads always warm, writes slower.
  • Write-behind: write to cache, flush to DB later; fast, but data can be lost.
  • TTL bounds staleness; explicit invalidation on write keeps it fresher but is easy to miss.
  • Stampede: a popular key expires and thousands of requests hit the DB. Use request coalescing, a lock, or early random refresh.
  • What to key on: include everything that changes the answer (user, locale, version). A missing part of the key serves one user's data to another.

Rate limiting

  • Token bucket: tokens refill at a steady rate up to a burst size; each request takes one. Allows short bursts. The usual choice.
  • Leaky bucket: requests drain at a fixed rate; smooths traffic, no bursts.
  • Fixed window: N per minute; simple, but allows 2N across a window boundary.
  • Sliding window: counts over the last 60 s; fairer, slightly more state.
  • Where: at the gateway per API key or user; inside services to protect a fragile dependency.
  • Response: HTTP 429 with Retry-After, so well-behaved clients back off.
  • Distributed: keep counters in Redis with atomic increments, or accept per-instance limits.

Distributing data

  • Replication copies data for availability and read scale. Leader-follower is the common model; multi-leader and leaderless trade consistency for write availability.
  • Partitioning / sharding splits data for write scale and size. Choose a key that spreads load and matches the main access pattern.
  • Consistent hashing places keys and nodes on a ring, so adding or removing a node moves only about 1/N of the keys. Used by caches and distributed stores.
  • Hot keys (one celebrity, one big tenant, today's date) defeat even spreading. Split them, cache them, or add a random suffix.
  • Quorums: with N replicas, reading R and writing W where R + W > N guarantees a read sees the latest write.

Designing for failure

  • Timeouts on every network call, shorter than the caller's own timeout.
  • Retries only for idempotent operations, with backoff and jitter, and a cap.
  • Circuit breakers and bulkheads so one sick dependency cannot take down the rest.
  • Graceful degradation: serve cached or partial results, and say so. Never present "could not check" as "nothing found".
  • Redundancy: multiple instances across zones; regional failover only if the requirement justifies the cost.
  • Backpressure: queues with limits; shed load early (429/503) instead of collapsing.
  • Backups you have restored, with a known recovery point (RPO: how much data you can lose) and recovery time (RTO: how long you can be down).

Worked example: a URL shortener

  • Requirements: create a short link; redirect fast; links never change; 100 M new links/year; reads 100× writes; 99.9% available.
  • Estimates: writes ≈ 3/s average; reads ≈ 300/s, peak ~1,500/s. 100 M × 500 B ≈ 50 GB/year.
  • API: POST /links {url} → {code}; GET /{code} → 301/302 redirect.
  • Data: link(code PK, url, created_at, owner). Access pattern is lookup by code only: any key-value store or Postgres works.
  • Code generation: base62 of a unique ID (7 chars ≈ 3.5 trillion codes). Use a sequence or ID service to avoid collisions; random codes need a uniqueness check.
  • High level: load balancer → stateless service → cache (hot codes) → database. CDN can cache redirects for popular links.
  • Deep dive: reads are the load: cache-aside with long TTL works because links are immutable. Writes are tiny.
  • Trade-offs: 301 is cached by browsers (fewer hits, no click counting); 302 keeps analytics. Click analytics go to a queue and a warehouse, not the redirect path.

Worked example: legal search over 20 M judgments

  • Requirements: keyword and filter search (court, date, judge), open a judgment, cite-check a reference. Results within 500 ms at p95. Data refreshed daily. Court-ordered suppressions must apply everywhere immediately.
  • Estimates: ~60 searches/s at peak; 20 M records, ~40 GB metadata, ~140 GB text.
  • Data flow: open-data mirrors → landing bucket → warehouse layers (raw → foundation → consumable) → serving Postgres. The warehouse is the record; Postgres is a read-optimised copy. See Data Engineering.
  • Serving: stateless API on Cloud Run; Postgres with full-text (GIN) and B-tree indexes on filter columns; read replica for search; cache for hot queries and judgment pages.
  • Stable identity: IDs never renumber, because URLs, citations and suppression orders refer to them. Loads upsert on a stable key.
  • Suppressions: checked on every read path (search, page, API, exports) from the application database, not baked into the copy, so a new order takes effect without a reload.
  • Freshness and honesty: each result carries its source and date; when a source is stale or unreachable, the API says so rather than returning a confident empty result.
  • Scaling later: move search to a dedicated index (or add semantic search with embeddings) when relevance or load outgrows Postgres; keep Postgres for lookups.

More practice problems

  • Rate limiter for a public API
  • Notification service (email, push, SMS) with retries and user preferences
  • News feed or timeline
  • Chat with presence and message history
  • File upload and processing pipeline
  • Job scheduler that runs millions of tasks reliably
  • Metrics and logging pipeline
  • RAG question-answering over company documents
  • Payment flow with a saga and idempotency keys

For each: run the method above, and write the trade-offs down. The written reasoning is the skill.

Design doc template

# Title
## Context        why now, who asked, links
## Goals          measurable; and non-goals
## Requirements   functional / non-functional (with numbers)
## Estimates      traffic, data, cost
## Design         diagram, API, data model, request flow
## Alternatives   what else was considered and why not
## Failure modes  each dependency down / slow / wrong
## Rollout        migration, flags, rollback plan
## Observability  metrics, alerts, SLOs, run records
## Security       data, access, threats
## Open questions owner and due date for each

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Follow the method: requirements, estimates, API, data, diagram
  • Name the building blocks and what each is for
  • Do back-of-the-envelope estimates

2 Practitioner

  • Design a small service end to end with caching and a queue
  • Identify bottlenecks and failure modes
  • Write a design document others can build from

3 Senior

  • Design for scale, availability targets and cost together
  • Choose storage and consistency models with clear trade-offs
  • Lead design reviews and challenge assumptions with numbers

4 Expert

  • Mentor others in design and trade-off thinking

Performance engineering

Making systems fast enough at a cost you can afford, by measurement rather than guesswork. The method, the laws that explain slowness, load testing, profiling for the JVM and Python, and where time usually goes in services, databases and pipelines.

The method

  1. Set a goal in numbers: "p95 search latency under 500 ms at 60 requests/s", "nightly load finishes in 40 minutes", "cost under ₹X per 1,000 requests". No goal, no finish line.
  2. Measure the current state in a production-like environment with realistic data and traffic.
  3. Find the bottleneck: the one resource or step limiting everything (CPU, memory, I/O, a lock, a query, a dependency). Use traces and profiles, not intuition.
  4. Change one thing that addresses it.
  5. Measure again the same way. Keep the change only if the numbers improved and nothing else got worse.
  6. Repeat until the goal is met, then stop. Then guard it with a performance test in CI.

Most of the gain usually comes from one or two fixes: a missing index, an N+1 query, a synchronous chain, an oversized payload. Find those before tuning anything clever.

Laws worth knowing

  • Little's law: items in the system = arrival rate × time in system (L = λW). 100 requests/s × 0.2 s = 20 requests in flight, so you need about 20 concurrent workers or connections.
  • Amdahl's law: speeding up a part only helps by that part's share. Making a step that takes 10% of the time infinitely fast saves 10%.
  • Queueing: as utilisation approaches 100%, waiting time grows sharply, not linearly. Plan for 60–70% utilisation at peak.
  • Tail amplification: if one call is slow 1% of the time, a request that fans out to 100 such calls is slow 1 − 0.99100 ≈ 63% of the time.
  • Latency vs throughput: batching raises throughput and usually raises latency; tune for whichever the goal names.

Percentiles, not averages

  • An average hides the users having a bad time. Report p50 (typical), p95 and p99 (the tail), and max.
  • You cannot average percentiles across instances; compute them from histograms.
  • Measure at the edge the user sees (load balancer, client), and also per dependency.
  • Watch for coordinated omission in load tests: a tool that waits for slow responses before sending the next request under-reports latency. Use open-model (arrival-rate) tests.

Kinds of performance test

TestQuestion
LoadDo we meet the goal at expected peak?
StressWhere does it break, and how does it fail?
SpikeWhat happens on a sudden 10× burst? Does autoscaling keep up?
SoakDoes it degrade over hours (leaks, growing queues)?
CapacityHow much traffic per instance, and what will growth cost?
Micro-benchmarkIs this function faster than that one? (JMH, pytest-benchmark)

Load-test example with k6 is on the Testing tab. Test against production-sized data; a query fast on 10,000 rows can be slow on 20 million.

Profiling the JVM

# Java Flight Recorder: low overhead, safe in production
jcmd <pid> JFR.start duration=60s filename=/tmp/rec.jfr
# or at startup
java -XX:StartFlightRecording=duration=60s,filename=rec.jfr -jar app.jar
# open rec.jfr in JDK Mission Control: hot methods, allocations, locks, GC

# async-profiler: flame graphs of CPU, allocation or wall-clock time
asprof -d 30 -e cpu   -f cpu.html   <pid>
asprof -d 30 -e alloc -f alloc.html <pid>

jcmd <pid> GC.heap_info
jcmd <pid> Thread.print            # thread dump: what is everyone waiting on?
  • Flame graph: width = time spent; look for wide plateaus near the top.
  • Containers: set -XX:MaxRAMPercentage; watch GC pause time and allocation rate.
  • Startup: CDS/AOT caches, lazy initialisation, or native images for scale-to-zero.
  • Blocking I/O: virtual threads (21+) raise concurrency cheaply; CPU-bound work still needs cores.

Micro-benchmarks done right (JMH)

@State(Scope.Benchmark)
@BenchmarkMode(Mode.AverageTime)
@OutputTimeUnit(TimeUnit.MICROSECONDS)
@Warmup(iterations = 3) @Measurement(iterations = 5) @Fork(1)
public class ParseBench {
  String raw = "05-05-1959";

  @Benchmark
  public LocalDate parse() {
    return DecisionDateParser.parse(raw);    // return the result so it is not optimised away
  }
}

Hand-written timing loops on the JVM lie: no warm-up, dead-code elimination, JIT effects. Use JMH, and only after a profile shows the code matters.

Profiling Python

# sampling profiler, attach to a running process, no code changes
py-spy top --pid 1234
py-spy record -o profile.svg --pid 1234          # flame graph

# deterministic profiler for a script
python -m cProfile -o out.prof -m loader
python -c "import pstats; pstats.Stats('out.prof').sort_stats('cumtime').print_stats(20)"

# memory
python -X importtime -m loader 2> imports.txt  # slow imports
pip install memray && memray run -m loader     # allocations
  • Replace Python loops over rows with vectorised work: SQL in the warehouse, Polars, PyArrow, NumPy.
  • Threads or asyncio for I/O-bound work; processes for CPU-bound work.
  • Stream large files instead of reading them whole into memory.

Where time usually goes

AreaCommon causeFix
DatabaseMissing index, N+1 queries, fetching unused columns, lock waitsEXPLAIN ANALYZE, indexes, joins or batch loads, select only needed columns (Database Design)
Connection poolsPool too small (queueing) or too large (database overload)Size from Little's law; watch pool wait time
Service callsSequential calls, no timeouts, new connection per callParallelise independent calls, keep-alive, HTTP/2, timeouts
PayloadsHuge JSON responses, no compression, no paginationPaginate, compress, return only needed fields
SerialisationReflection-heavy mapping in hot pathsProfile; precompiled serialisers; smaller objects
Memory and GCHigh allocation rate, heap too small, large object churnProfile allocations; reuse buffers; tune heap and collector
Cold startsLarge images, eager initialisation, JVM warm-upSmaller images, CPU boost, min instances, AOT/native (Cloud Run)
Warehouse queriesFull scans, skewed joins, repeated subqueriesPartition filters, clustering, pre-aggregation (SQL Skills)
PipelinesOne object at a time through one container; small filesParallel tasks, bulk loads, purpose-built engines (Dataflow), compact files
LLM callsLong prompts, sequential calls, large models for simple tasksPrompt caching, parallel calls, streaming, smaller models where evals allow

Concurrency and backpressure

  • Bound every queue and pool; an unbounded queue turns overload into an out-of-memory crash.
  • Shed load early (429/503) when saturated rather than accepting work you cannot finish in time.
  • Set timeouts shorter than the caller's, so work is not done for a caller that has given up.
  • Cloud Run concurrency and max instances, and database pool size, must agree: instances × pool size ≤ database connection limit.
  • Locks and synchronised blocks serialise work; look for them in thread dumps under load.

Performance as a habit

  • Performance budgets in CI: fail the build if a key benchmark or bundle size regresses beyond a threshold.
  • Track p95/p99 per endpoint after every deploy, with deploy markers on dashboards.
  • Cost per unit of work (per request, per row loaded) next to latency: faster that costs 5× may be the wrong trade.
  • Capacity reviews before expected growth or launches.
  • Write down the numbers of each investigation, so the next one starts from evidence.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Read p50/p95/p99 latency and throughput on a dashboard
  • Spot an obvious N+1 query or missing index
  • Run an existing load test and report the result

2 Practitioner

  • Profile a JVM or Python service and fix the hot spot
  • Write load tests with goals and thresholds
  • Size pools and concurrency with Little's law

3 Senior

  • Lead performance investigations end to end with evidence
  • Set performance goals, budgets and capacity plans
  • Balance latency, throughput and cost in design

4 Expert

  • Own performance engineering practice across the organisation
  • Understand runtime, OS and hardware effects deeply
  • Design systems for extreme scale and tail latency

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.
