Caching

Keeping copies of expensive results closer to where they are needed. Where caches live, the patterns for reading and writing them, invalidation and consistency, stampedes, key design, and working code for Spring, Python, Redis, HTTP and BigQuery.

Where caches live

LayerExampleGood forWatch out for
BrowserHTTP cacheStatic assets, per-user responsesWrong Cache-Control serving stale pages
CDNCloud CDNPublic content near usersCaching personalised responses by mistake
GatewayApigee ResponseCacheIdentical API responsesKeys that ignore the caller's identity
In-processCaffeine, functools.lru_cacheHot reference data, nanosecond readsEach instance has its own copy; memory per instance
DistributedRedis / MemorystoreShared across instances, sessions, rate limitsNetwork hop; one more system to run
DatabaseBuffer cache, materialised viewsRepeated queries and aggregatesRefresh schedules
WarehouseBigQuery cached results, BI EngineRepeated dashboard queriesCache is skipped for some queries (non-deterministic functions, changed tables)
Model APIPrompt cachingLong, repeated prompt prefixesAny change in the prefix invalidates it

A cache is a copy, so every cache is a consistency decision. Add one when measurements show the need, and decide up front how stale its data may be.

Read and write patterns

  • Cache-aside (lazy loading): read the cache; on a miss, read the source and fill the cache. Simple and the most common.
  • Read-through: the cache library loads from the source itself on a miss (Caffeine LoadingCache).
  • Write-through: writes go to the cache and the source together; reads are always warm; writes are slower.
  • Write-behind: write to the cache, persist later. Fast, but a crash loses writes. Rarely worth it.
  • Refresh-ahead: refresh popular entries before they expire so users never wait for a miss.

Invalidation

  • TTL: entries expire after a set time. Bounds staleness; simplest and most robust. Every cache entry should have one.
  • Delete on write: after updating the source, delete the cache key (not update it), so the next read reloads fresh data.
  • Versioned keys: include a version in the key (judgment:v3:123, or a data version number); bump it to invalidate everything at once.
  • Event-driven: consume change events (CDC, domain events) and evict affected keys.
  • Immutable data (a published judgment version, a fingerprinted file) can be cached forever.

The race to know: reader A misses and reads the old value; writer B updates the source and deletes the key; A then writes the old value into the cache. A TTL bounds the damage; versioned keys avoid it.

Stampedes (thundering herd)

A popular key expires and thousands of requests miss at once and hit the database together.

  • Single flight / request coalescing: only one caller loads; the others wait for its result (Caffeine does this per key in-process).
  • Distributed lock: SET lock:key 1 NX EX 10 in Redis; the winner loads, others briefly serve stale or wait.
  • Stale-while-revalidate: serve the expired value while one background refresh runs.
  • Jittered TTLs: add randomness so many keys do not expire at the same moment.
  • Probabilistic early refresh: refresh slightly before expiry with rising probability.

Key design

  • Include every input that changes the answer: IDs, filters, page, locale, user or tenant, and the API or data version.
  • Normalise inputs (sort filters, lower-case where meaning allows) so equal requests share a key.
  • Namespace keys: service:entity:version:id.
  • Never share a cache entry across users when the response depends on permissions; include the permission scope in the key, or cache only the permission-independent part.
  • Keep values small; large values make the cache slow and evict everything else.

What not to cache, or not that way

  • Errors and outages as "not found": caching an empty result because the source was down turns "cannot see" into a confident "nothing there" for the whole TTL.
  • Negative results (genuinely not found) only briefly, if at all.
  • Secrets and tokens in shared caches.
  • Personalised data in public caches (CDN, gateway) without per-user keys.
  • Data with legal effect (a suppression, a deletion) behind a long TTL: it must take effect immediately, so check it outside the cache or evict on change.
  • Cheap queries: if the source answers in 1 ms, a cache adds complexity for little gain.

Spring Cache with Caffeine or Redis

@Configuration
@EnableCaching
class CacheConfig {
  @Bean
  CacheManager cacheManager() {
    var m = new CaffeineCacheManager("judgments", "courts");
    m.setCaffeine(Caffeine.newBuilder()
        .maximumSize(10_000)
        .expireAfterWrite(Duration.ofMinutes(10))
        .recordStats());                     // hit ratio for metrics
    return m;
  }
}

@Service
class JudgmentService {
  @Cacheable(cacheNames = "judgments", key = "#id", unless = "#result == null")
  public Judgment get(String id) { return repo.find(id).orElse(null); }

  @CacheEvict(cacheNames = "judgments", key = "#j.id()")
  public void update(Judgment j) { repo.save(j); }
}
# switch to Redis shared across instances
spring:
  cache:
    type: redis
    redis:
      time-to-live: 10m
      key-prefix: "legal:v3:"
  data:
    redis:
      host: ${REDIS_HOST}
      port: 6379

@Cacheable works through a proxy, like @Transactional: calling the method from inside the same class skips the cache.

Python: in-process and Redis

from functools import lru_cache
from cachetools import TTLCache, cached

@lru_cache(maxsize=1024)                     # immutable inputs, never expires
def court_name(code: str) -> str: ...

@cached(TTLCache(maxsize=10_000, ttl=600))   # expires after 10 minutes
def judgment_summary(jid: str) -> dict: ...
import json, random, redis
r = redis.Redis(host=REDIS_HOST, port=6379, decode_responses=True)

def get_judgment(jid: str) -> dict | None:
    key = f"legal:judgment:v3:{jid}"
    if (hit := r.get(key)) is not None:
        return json.loads(hit)
    j = db.load_judgment(jid)                # raises if the DB is down: do not cache that
    if j is not None:
        r.set(key, json.dumps(j), ex=600 + random.randint(0, 60))   # jittered TTL
    return j

def update_judgment(j: dict) -> None:
    db.save(j)
    r.delete(f"legal:judgment:v3:{j['id']}")  # delete after write

Redis essentials

SET judgment:v3:123 '{"cnr":"…"}' EX 600     # with TTL
SET lock:judgment:123 1 NX EX 10             # only if absent: a simple lock
GET judgment:v3:123
DEL judgment:v3:123
TTL judgment:v3:123
INCR ratelimit:app42:202611121015            # counters, rate limits
EXPIRE ratelimit:app42:202611121015 60
INFO stats                                   # keyspace_hits, keyspace_misses, evicted_keys
  • Set maxmemory and an eviction policy (allkeys-lru or allkeys-lfu for a pure cache).
  • Avoid KEYS * in production; use SCAN.
  • Design so the system still works (slower) if Redis is down; a cache should not be a single point of failure.
  • On GCP: Memorystore for Redis or Valkey, private IP, AUTH and in-transit encryption on.

HTTP and CDN caching

# fingerprinted static assets
Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable

# public API response, shared caches allowed, short freshness
Cache-Control: public, max-age=60, stale-while-revalidate=30
ETag: "v3-123-a1b2"

# per-user data
Cache-Control: private, max-age=30

# sensitive
Cache-Control: no-store

More on headers on Web Fundamentals. Configure the CDN to vary on, or bypass for, the Authorization header and cookies.

Caching in the data stack

  • BigQuery cached results: identical queries reuse results for free for about 24 hours, unless tables changed or the query is non-deterministic.
  • Materialised views: BigQuery keeps aggregates up to date incrementally and can rewrite queries to use them.
  • BI Engine: in-memory acceleration for dashboards.
  • Serving tables: a consumable table in Postgres is itself a cache of the warehouse; it needs a refresh rule and freshness monitoring like any other cache.
  • Pipeline-level: skip work whose inputs have not changed (compare ETags or content hashes) instead of recomputing.

Measuring a cache

  • Hit ratio (hits ÷ requests): low ratios mean wrong keys, short TTLs or data that is not reused.
  • Latency on hit and on miss.
  • Evictions and memory: constant evictions mean the cache is too small for the working set.
  • Staleness: how old served data is, against the agreed maximum.
  • Source load with and without the cache, to prove it earns its keep.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain cache-aside, TTL and hit ratio
  • Use an in-process cache for reference data
  • Read HTTP caching headers

2 Practitioner

  • Add Spring or Python caching with keys, TTLs and eviction on write
  • Use Redis safely with TTLs, eviction policy and a fallback
  • Measure hit ratio and staleness

3 Senior

  • Design multi-layer caching for a system with explicit staleness rules
  • Prevent stampedes and invalidation races
  • Decide what must never be cached (outages, permissions, legal effect)

4 Expert

  • Set caching standards and shared cache platforms
  • Design caching for global, multi-region systems
  • Lead consistency and cost trade-offs across products

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

GCP vs AWS vs Azure

The same building blocks under three names: a service-by-service map, how the providers differ in structure, identity, networking and pricing, each one's strengths, and how to stay portable. Service names change often; check each provider's own comparison page before deciding.

Where each tends to stand out

  • Google Cloud: data and analytics (BigQuery is serverless with nothing to size), global networking with a global VPC and single-IP global load balancing, Kubernetes (GKE), developer-friendly serverless (Cloud Run), and AI with Vertex AI. Projects make isolation simple.
  • AWS: the broadest and deepest service catalogue, the largest ecosystem and talent pool, many regions, and mature options for almost every niche. More configuration choices, and more to learn.
  • Azure: enterprises standardised on Microsoft (Entra ID, Microsoft 365, Windows, SQL Server, .NET), hybrid setups (Azure Arc), and the Microsoft data and AI stack (Fabric, Power BI, Azure OpenAI).

All three are capable for almost any workload. The deciding factors are usually existing skills and contracts, specific services you depend on, data location, and price after discounts.

Differences that bite

  • IAM thinking: GCP binds roles to principals on resources; AWS writes JSON policies with explicit allow/deny and resource ARNs; Azure assigns roles at a scope in a strict hierarchy.
  • Networking: a GCP VPC spans regions; AWS and Azure networks are regional and are joined with peering or transit gateways/hubs.
  • Isolation unit: GCP teams often use many projects; AWS uses many accounts; Azure uses subscriptions plus resource groups.
  • Warehouses: BigQuery bills by bytes scanned or by slots with no cluster to run; Redshift has provisioned clusters or serverless capacity units; Fabric uses capacity units shared across workloads.
  • Discounts: GCP committed and sustained use discounts; AWS Savings Plans and Reserved Instances; Azure reservations and savings plans. Spot or preemptible capacity everywhere for interruptible work.
  • Egress: data leaving a cloud costs money on all three; cross-region and cross-cloud traffic adds up. Design data placement before choosing multi-cloud.

Regions in India (data residency)

ProviderRegions
Google CloudMumbai (asia-south1), Delhi (asia-south2)
AWSMumbai (ap-south-1), Hyderabad (ap-south-2)
AzureCentral India (Pune), South India (Chennai), West India (Mumbai), plus newer regions

Not every service or model is available in every region. Check availability per service, and for AI models check which regions actually process the data.

CLI equivalents

# who am I / which project-account-subscription
gcloud config list            aws sts get-caller-identity     az account show

# list VMs
gcloud compute instances list aws ec2 describe-instances      az vm list -o table

# copy a file to object storage
gcloud storage cp f gs://b/   aws s3 cp f s3://b/             az storage blob upload -f f -c b -n f --account-name acct

# Kubernetes credentials
gcloud container clusters get-credentials c --region r
aws eks update-kubeconfig --name c --region r
az aks get-credentials -g rg -n c

# read a secret
gcloud secrets versions access latest --secret=s
aws secretsmanager get-secret-value --secret-id s
az keyvault secret show --vault-name v -n s

Staying portable (where it matters)

  • Containers and Kubernetes run the same on GKE, EKS and AKS; Cloud Run-style serverless is less portable but simpler.
  • Terraform for all infrastructure, with provider-specific modules behind your own interfaces.
  • Open data formats (Parquet, Iceberg, Delta) in object storage keep data readable by any engine.
  • Open protocols: Postgres, Kafka, OpenTelemetry, OIDC, S3-compatible APIs.
  • dbt and SQL transfer between warehouses with dialect adjustments.
  • Accept lock-in deliberately where a managed service saves real effort (BigQuery, Spanner, DynamoDB); write down the exit cost.

Multi-cloud for its own sake doubles skills, tooling and egress. Use it for a concrete reason: a customer requirement, a unique service, resilience against a provider-wide failure, or negotiation.

Choosing for a new workload

  1. Where is the data now, and where must it legally live?
  2. Which managed services would save the most work (warehouse, Kubernetes, AI models)?
  3. What does the team already know, and what do hiring markets offer?
  4. What identity and productivity stack does the organisation use?
  5. Price a realistic workload on each calculator, including egress and support.
  6. Prototype the riskiest piece on the leading candidate before committing.

Data engineering on Google Cloud

Which Google Cloud service does each job in the data lifecycle, how they fit together in common architectures, and how to choose between similar ones. Concepts are on Data Engineering; the warehouse itself is on BigQuery.

Transform: Spark/HadoopDataproc / Serverless for Apache Spark (formerly Dataproc Serverless)Existing Spark jobs, Spark ML, lift-and-shift Hadoop
OrchestrateCloud Composer (Airflow)Many dependent pipelines, sensors, backfills (DAGs tab)
Orchestrate (light)Workflows + Cloud SchedulerA few steps calling APIs and jobs, no Airflow to run
GovernDataplexCatalog, data quality scans, lineage, profiling, policy across lakes and warehouses
ProtectIAM, policy tags, Sensitive Data ProtectionColumn-level access, masking, finding personal data
ShareBigQuery sharing (formerly Analytics Hub)Publish datasets to other projects or organisations without copying
Serve: BILooker, Looker StudioGoverned metrics and dashboards
Serve: ML/AIBigQuery ML, Vertex AIModels and embeddings close to the data (AI tab)

Reference architectures

PatternFlowFits
Batch ELTSource → Storage Transfer / Cloud Run job → GCS (raw) → BigQuery load → dbt layers → marts → Looker / APIDaily or hourly data, files and exports, most analytics
StreamingApps → Pub/Sub → Dataflow (parse, enrich, window) → BigQuery (+ GCS archive)Seconds-level freshness, clickstreams, IoT, fraud signals
Simple streamingApps → Pub/Sub → BigQuery subscriptionEvents that need no transformation before landing
CDC replicationPostgres/MySQL → Datastream → BigQuery (merged or append) → dbtAnalytics on an operational database without load on it
LakehouseGCS (Parquet/Iceberg) ← Dataproc Spark / Dataflow; BigLake tables → BigQuery SQLOpen formats shared by several engines
Reverse ETL / servingBigQuery mart → export or loader → Cloud SQL / AlloyDB → APIServing curated data to an application with low latency
RAG over documentsGCS docs → parse (Dataflow / Cloud Run) → chunks + embeddings (BigQuery ML / Vertex AI) → vector search → modelAI question answering over your corpus

Choosing between similar services

  • SQL in BigQuery vs Dataflow: if it can be expressed in SQL over data already in BigQuery, use SQL (dbt). Use Dataflow for streaming, non-SQL parsing, or work before data reaches BigQuery.
  • Dataflow vs Dataproc: Dataflow for new pipelines (serverless, Beam, streaming). Dataproc for existing Spark code or Spark libraries.
  • Cloud Run job vs Dataflow: a job for simple, bounded work (copy files, call an API). Dataflow when data volume needs many workers and autoscaling.
  • Composer vs Workflows: Composer for many pipelines with dependencies, backfills and sensors. Workflows plus Scheduler for a handful of steps; much cheaper to run.
  • dbt vs Dataform: both do SQL ELT with tests. Dataform is built into BigQuery; dbt has the larger ecosystem and runs on any warehouse.
  • Pub/Sub vs Kafka: Pub/Sub is serverless and global with nothing to size. Kafka gives log semantics, replay by offset, compaction and the Kafka ecosystem.
  • Cloud SQL vs AlloyDB vs Spanner: Cloud SQL for most Postgres needs; AlloyDB for heavier Postgres workloads and analytics; Spanner for global scale with strong consistency.

Handy commands

# Pub/Sub straight into BigQuery
gcloud pubsub topics create judgment-events
gcloud pubsub subscriptions create judgment-to-bq \
  --topic=judgment-events \
  --bigquery-table=my-proj:dp_odp.judgment_events \
  --use-table-schema

# Storage Transfer: one-off copy between buckets
gcloud transfer jobs create gs://source-bucket gs://my-landing

# Serverless for Apache Spark (Dataproc batches): run a PySpark job
gcloud dataproc batches submit pyspark job.py \
  --region=asia-south1 --deps-bucket=gs://my-deps

# Dataflow: list and inspect jobs
gcloud dataflow jobs list --region=asia-south1 --status=active
gcloud dataflow jobs describe JOB_ID --region=asia-south1

# Datastream streams
gcloud datastream streams list --location=asia-south1

IAM for data, in practice

  • One service account per pipeline component (landing, load, transform, serve), each with only its roles.
  • Dataset-level grants rather than project-wide: bigquery.dataEditor on the datasets it writes, dataViewer on those it reads.
  • bigquery.jobUser on the project that pays for queries; this is separate from data access.
  • Bucket-level roles with uniform bucket-level access; objectViewer for readers, objectAdmin only for the writer.
  • Policy tags for sensitive columns; authorised views to share a subset.
  • Serving databases get a read-only user for the API.

Cost controls

  • Partition and cluster; require partition filters on large tables.
  • Set per-user and per-project custom query quotas, and maximum_bytes_billed on jobs.
  • Export billing to BigQuery and build a cost dashboard by label.
  • Label every resource with team, component and environment.
  • GCS lifecycle rules to move old raw data to Nearline/Coldline/Archive.
  • Stop or scale down non-production Cloud SQL, Dataproc and Composer when unused.
  • Dataflow: right-size workers, use streaming engine, and set max workers.
  • Keep development windows small: a few partitions, not the full history.

Data quality and governance on GCP

  • dbt tests in the transformation layer for keys, relationships and accepted values.
  • Dataplex data quality scans for rules on tables, scheduled, with results in BigQuery.
  • Dataplex data profiling to see distributions, nulls and distinct counts.
  • Lineage from BigQuery jobs and Dataflow appears automatically in Dataplex.
  • Run records of your own: every pipeline run writes what it read, wrote and rejected.
  • Sensitive Data Protection scans to find personal data you did not know you had.

Certification path

  • Cloud Digital Leader: broad, non-technical overview.
  • Associate Cloud Engineer: hands-on GCP basics: IAM, compute, networking, gcloud.
  • Professional Data Engineer: design, build and operate data systems on GCP. The exam tests choosing services for a scenario, which is exactly this tab.
  • Professional Cloud Architect next, if you design whole platforms.

Certifications test breadth and judgement. Pair each with a real project built end to end.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Name the GCP service for each lifecycle stage
  • Load files from GCS into BigQuery and query them
  • Navigate IAM roles for data access

2 Practitioner

  • Build a batch ELT pipeline: landing, BigQuery layers, dbt, scheduling
  • Set up Pub/Sub to BigQuery, or Datastream CDC
  • Apply dataset-level IAM and cost controls

3 Senior

  • Choose between Dataflow, Dataproc, SQL and Cloud Run for a workload
  • Design a platform with governance (Dataplex), lineage and quality
  • Plan cost, regions and data residency

4 Expert

  • Own the organisation's GCP data architecture
  • Lead migrations from other clouds or on-premises
  • Hold the Professional Data Engineer or Architect level of judgement, and teach it

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

BigQuery SQL

GoogleSQL patterns, cost controls and the bq CLI. On-demand pricing bills bytes scanned, so most tips are about scanning less.

Partitioned, clustered table

CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS `proj.fdp.judgment` (
  judgment_key STRING NOT NULL,
  cnr          STRING,
  court_code   STRING,
  decision_date DATE,
  parties      ARRAY<STRUCT<role STRING, name STRING>>,
  _landed_at   TIMESTAMP
)
PARTITION BY DATE_TRUNC(decision_date, MONTH)
CLUSTER BY court_code, cnr
OPTIONS (
  require_partition_filter = TRUE,
  description = 'FDP judgments, one row per judgment_key',
  labels = [('layer', 'fdp')]
);

-- integer range partition
PARTITION BY RANGE_BUCKET(data_year, GENERATE_ARRAY(1950, 2031, 1))
-- ingestion-time partition
PARTITION BY _PARTITIONDATE

Limit: 10,000 partitions per table. Daily partitions over 76 years overflow it; use MONTH or YEAR.

Deduplicate with QUALIFY

-- latest version of each key
SELECT *
FROM `proj.odp.hc_judgment`
WHERE data_year = 2024
QUALIFY ROW_NUMBER() OVER (
  PARTITION BY cnr, order_number
  ORDER BY _landed_at DESC
) = 1;

-- latest event per entity (append-only ledger → current state)
SELECT * EXCEPT(rn) FROM (
  SELECT *, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY run_id, stage
                               ORDER BY event_ts DESC) rn
  FROM `proj.dp_control.pipeline_run_events`)
WHERE rn = 1;

-- whole row of the max, no window
SELECT ARRAY_AGG(t ORDER BY decision_date DESC LIMIT 1)[OFFSET(0)].*
FROM `proj.fdp.judgment` t GROUP BY court_code;

MERGE (upsert)

MERGE `proj.cdp.serving` T
USING (
  SELECT * FROM `proj.fdp.judgment`
  WHERE decision_date >= DATE_SUB(CURRENT_DATE(), INTERVAL 7 DAY)
) S
ON T.judgment_key = S.judgment_key
   AND T.decision_date >= DATE_SUB(CURRENT_DATE(), INTERVAL 7 DAY)  -- prune T
WHEN MATCHED AND T.row_hash != S.row_hash THEN
  UPDATE SET cnr = S.cnr, decision_date = S.decision_date, row_hash = S.row_hash
WHEN NOT MATCHED BY TARGET THEN
  INSERT ROW
WHEN NOT MATCHED BY SOURCE AND T.decision_date >= DATE_SUB(CURRENT_DATE(), INTERVAL 7 DAY) THEN
  DELETE;

-- change detection hash
TO_HEX(SHA256(TO_JSON_STRING(STRUCT(cnr, court_code, decision_date)))) AS row_hash
FARM_FINGERPRINT(CONCAT(cnr, '|', order_number))   -- INT64 key

Dates & safe parsing

PARSE_DATE('%d-%m-%Y', '05-05-1959')          -- errors on bad input
SAFE.PARSE_DATE('%d-%m-%Y', raw)               -- NULL on bad input
SAFE_CAST(order_seq AS INT64)

-- never let a parse failure hide as a NULL: count it
SELECT COUNTIF(raw IS NOT NULL AND SAFE.PARSE_DATE('%d-%m-%Y', raw) IS NULL) AS unparsed
FROM t;

DATE_TRUNC(d, MONTH)   DATE_ADD(d, INTERVAL 1 DAY)   DATE_DIFF(a, b, DAY)
LAST_DAY(d, MONTH)     EXTRACT(YEAR FROM d)          FORMAT_DATE('%Y%m%d', d)
TIMESTAMP_TRUNC(ts, HOUR)  DATETIME(ts, 'Asia/Kolkata')
CURRENT_DATE('Asia/Kolkata')
UNIX_MILLIS(ts)  TIMESTAMP_MICROS(n)

-- calendar spine for gap finding
SELECT d FROM UNNEST(GENERATE_DATE_ARRAY('2025-01-01', CURRENT_DATE())) d
LEFT JOIN facts f ON f.dt = d WHERE f.dt IS NULL;

ARRAY, STRUCT, UNNEST, JSON

-- explode
SELECT j.cnr, p.role, p.name
FROM `proj.fdp.judgment` j, UNNEST(j.parties) p;      -- comma = CROSS JOIN
-- keep rows with empty arrays
LEFT JOIN UNNEST(j.parties) p

-- build
SELECT cnr, ARRAY_AGG(STRUCT(role, name) ORDER BY role) parties
FROM parties GROUP BY cnr;

ARRAY_LENGTH(a)  a[SAFE_OFFSET(0)]  ARRAY_TO_STRING(a, ',')
'x' IN UNNEST(a)  EXISTS(SELECT 1 FROM UNNEST(a) v WHERE v > 3)

-- JSON
JSON_VALUE(payload, '$.court.code')            -- scalar as STRING
JSON_QUERY(payload, '$.parties')               -- JSON fragment
JSON_QUERY_ARRAY(payload, '$.parties')         -- ARRAY<JSON> on a JSON column, ARRAY<STRING> on a STRING
LAX_INT64(payload.count)                       -- on a JSON column
TO_JSON_STRING(STRUCT(a, b))
PARSE_JSON(s)

Window functions

SELECT
  court, decision_date, n,
  SUM(n) OVER (PARTITION BY court ORDER BY decision_date)          AS running,
  AVG(n) OVER (PARTITION BY court ORDER BY decision_date
               ROWS BETWEEN 6 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW)           AS avg_7d,
  LAG(n) OVER (PARTITION BY court ORDER BY decision_date)          AS prev,
  n / SUM(n) OVER (PARTITION BY court)                             AS share,
  RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY court ORDER BY n DESC)                 AS rnk,
  NTILE(4) OVER (ORDER BY n)                                       AS quartile
FROM daily;

-- named window
SELECT SUM(n) OVER w, MAX(n) OVER w FROM daily
WINDOW w AS (PARTITION BY court ORDER BY decision_date);

APPROX_COUNT_DISTINCT(cnr)   APPROX_QUANTILES(n, 100)[OFFSET(50)]

Load, export, external

LOAD DATA INTO `proj.odp.hc_judgment`
FROM FILES (format = 'PARQUET',
            uris = ['gs://landing/data/year=2024/*.parquet']);

EXPORT DATA OPTIONS (
  uri = 'gs://exports/serving/2025-01-31/*.parquet',   -- one * required
  format = 'PARQUET', overwrite = TRUE, compression = 'SNAPPY'
) AS SELECT * FROM `proj.cdp.serving` WHERE _run_date = '2025-01-31';

CREATE OR REPLACE EXTERNAL TABLE `proj.raw.hc_ext`
WITH PARTITION COLUMNS
OPTIONS (format = 'PARQUET',
         uris = ['gs://landing/data/*'],
         hive_partition_uri_prefix = 'gs://landing/data');

Scripting & procedures

DECLARE run_date DATE DEFAULT CURRENT_DATE();
DECLARE n INT64;

SET n = (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM `proj.fdp.judgment` WHERE _run_date = run_date);
IF n = 0 THEN
  RAISE USING MESSAGE = FORMAT('no rows for %t', run_date);
END IF;

BEGIN TRANSACTION;
  DELETE FROM `proj.cdp.serving` WHERE _run_date = run_date;
  INSERT INTO `proj.cdp.serving` SELECT * FROM staging;
COMMIT TRANSACTION;

EXECUTE IMMEDIATE FORMAT('SELECT COUNT(*) FROM `proj.%s.t`', ds);

CREATE OR REPLACE PROCEDURE `proj.ops.rebuild`(d DATE)
BEGIN ... END;
CALL `proj.ops.rebuild`(DATE '2025-01-31');

Pipe syntax

FROM `proj.fdp.judgment`
|> WHERE decision_date >= '2024-01-01'
|> AGGREGATE COUNT(*) AS n GROUP BY court_code
|> ORDER BY n DESC
|> LIMIT 10;

-- other operators: SELECT, EXTEND (add columns), SET, DROP, RENAME,
-- JOIN, AS (alias), PIVOT, UNPIVOT, TABLESAMPLE, CALL

Reads top to bottom in execution order. Mixes freely with standard SQL in the same project.

Metadata & cost queries

-- most expensive queries in the last day
SELECT user_email, job_id,
       ROUND(total_bytes_billed / POW(1024, 4), 3) AS tib_billed,
       total_slot_ms, LEFT(query, 120) AS q
FROM `region-asia-south1`.INFORMATION_SCHEMA.JOBS
WHERE creation_time > TIMESTAMP_SUB(CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(), INTERVAL 1 DAY)
  AND job_type = 'QUERY'
ORDER BY total_bytes_billed DESC LIMIT 20;

-- partitions and their row counts
SELECT partition_id, total_rows, total_logical_bytes
FROM `proj.fdp.INFORMATION_SCHEMA.PARTITIONS`
WHERE table_name = 'judgment' ORDER BY partition_id;

-- columns of a table
SELECT column_name, data_type, is_nullable
FROM `proj.fdp.INFORMATION_SCHEMA.COLUMNS` WHERE table_name = 'judgment';

-- table sizes
SELECT table_id, row_count, size_bytes / 1e9 AS gb
FROM `proj.fdp.__TABLES__` ORDER BY size_bytes DESC;

Time travel, snapshots, wildcards

-- read the table as it was (up to 7 days back by default)
SELECT * FROM `proj.fdp.judgment`
FOR SYSTEM_TIME AS OF TIMESTAMP_SUB(CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(), INTERVAL 1 HOUR);

-- undo a bad write
CREATE OR REPLACE TABLE `proj.fdp.judgment_restore` AS
SELECT * FROM `proj.fdp.judgment` FOR SYSTEM_TIME AS OF '2025-01-31 06:00:00+00';

-- zero-copy snapshot / clone
CREATE SNAPSHOT TABLE `proj.backup.judgment_20250131` CLONE `proj.fdp.judgment`;
CREATE TABLE `proj.dev.judgment` CLONE `proj.fdp.judgment`;

-- sharded tables
SELECT * FROM `proj.logs.events_*` WHERE _TABLE_SUFFIX BETWEEN '20250101' AND '20250131';

Cost & performance rules

  • Name columns. Storage is columnar; SELECT * bills every column. SELECT * EXCEPT(raw_html) drops wide ones.
  • Filter on the partition column with a constant or parameter. A filter through a function of another table's subquery may not prune.
  • LIMIT does not reduce bytes on a regular table. Use table preview or TABLESAMPLE SYSTEM (1 PERCENT).
  • Dry-run first and set maximum_bytes_billed on jobs that could run away.
  • Cluster on high-cardinality filter/join columns; order matters, first column filters best.
  • Join big to small after filtering; pre-aggregate before joining.
  • Avoid row-by-row DML. Batch into one MERGE; DML quotas and costs are per statement.
  • Materialised views for repeated aggregates; BI Engine for dashboards.
  • Execution graph in the console shows skew, spills and which stage costs the slots.

bq CLI

bq query --use_legacy_sql=false --dry_run 'SELECT ...'
bq query --use_legacy_sql=false --format=prettyjson --max_rows=20 \
  --parameter=from_d:DATE:2024-01-01 \
  'SELECT COUNT(*) FROM `proj.fdp.judgment` WHERE decision_date >= @from_d'
bq query --nouse_legacy_sql --maximum_bytes_billed=10000000000 < q.sql

bq ls proj:fdp
bq show --schema --format=prettyjson proj:fdp.judgment
bq head -n 10 proj:fdp.judgment

bq load --source_format=PARQUET --replace \
  'proj:odp.events$20250131' 'gs://landing/events/dt=2025-01-31/*.parquet'   # one partition
bq extract --destination_format=PARQUET proj:cdp.serving 'gs://exp/serving-*.parquet'

bq cp proj:fdp.judgment proj:backup.judgment_20250131
bq mk --table --time_partitioning_field=decision_date --clustering_fields=court_code \
  proj:fdp.t schema.json
bq ls -j -a -n 20          # recent jobs
bq show -j <job_id>

Cloud Run

Serverless containers on GCP: services that answer requests, and jobs that run to completion. Deploy, scale, secure, connect and debug.

Services, jobs, worker pools

KindRuns whenUse it for
ServiceA request arrives (HTTP, gRPC, WebSocket, Pub/Sub push, Eventarc)APIs, web apps, webhooks. Scales on traffic, down to zero.
JobSomeone executes it (CLI, API, Cloud Scheduler, Workflows)Batch loads, migrations, nightly exports. Runs N tasks in parallel, then exits.
Worker poolAlways, with no HTTP endpointPull-based consumers (Kafka, Pub/Sub pull). Newer resource: check it is available in your region.

Container contract

  • Listen on $PORT (default 8080) on 0.0.0.0, not 127.0.0.1. Jobs do not listen at all.
  • Stateless. Any instance can serve any request; instances come and go.
  • The filesystem is in memory and counts against the memory limit. Write big files to GCS or a mounted volume.
  • Shutdown: the container gets SIGTERM, then about 10 seconds before SIGKILL. Flush and close there.
  • Logs: anything written to stdout/stderr goes to Cloud Logging.
  • Jobs: exit code 0 means the task succeeded; anything else fails it (and retries it if max-retries > 0).
  • Platform: build images for linux/amd64. An arm64 image from a Mac will not start.

Deploy a service

gcloud run deploy api \
  --image=asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev/my-proj/apps/api:3f2a9c1 \
  --region=asia-south1 \
  --service-account=api@my-proj.iam.gserviceaccount.com \
  --no-allow-unauthenticated \
  --cpu=1 --memory=1Gi \
  --concurrency=80 --min-instances=0 --max-instances=10 \
  --timeout=300 \
  --cpu-boost \
  --set-env-vars=APP_PROFILE=prod,DATASET=fdp \
  --set-secrets=DB_PASSWORD=db-password:3 \
  --labels=env=prod,team=data

# build from source with Cloud Build + buildpacks / Dockerfile
gcloud run deploy api --source . --region=asia-south1

gcloud run services describe api --region=asia-south1 --format='value(status.url)'
gcloud run services list

--set-env-vars replaces all env vars; --update-env-vars adds or changes only the ones named.

Revisions & traffic

# deploy without sending traffic; reach it at a tagged URL
gcloud run deploy api --image=…:4b1c2d0 --no-traffic --tag=canary
# → https://canary---api-xxxxx.a.run.app

# shift 10% to the new revision
gcloud run services update-traffic api \
  --to-revisions=api-00042-xyz=10,api-00041-abc=90

gcloud run services update-traffic api --to-latest     # 100% to newest
gcloud run services update-traffic api --to-tags=canary=100

# roll back = send traffic to the previous revision
gcloud run revisions list --service=api --region=asia-south1
gcloud run services update-traffic api --to-revisions=api-00041-abc=100

Every deploy or config change creates a new immutable revision. Settings live on the revision, so rolling back traffic also rolls back env vars and secrets.

Jobs

gcloud run jobs deploy loader \
  --image=asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev/my-proj/apps/loader:3f2a9c1 \
  --region=asia-south1 \
  --service-account=loader@my-proj.iam.gserviceaccount.com \
  --tasks=10 --parallelism=5 \
  --task-timeout=3600s --max-retries=0 \
  --cpu=2 --memory=4Gi \
  --args=--year=2024

gcloud run jobs execute loader --region=asia-south1 --wait
gcloud run jobs execute loader --wait \
  --args=--year=2023 --update-env-vars=DRY_RUN=true   # overrides for this run only

gcloud run jobs executions list --job=loader --region=asia-south1
gcloud run jobs executions describe loader-abc12 --region=asia-south1
gcloud run jobs executions cancel loader-abc12 --region=asia-south1

An execution's status is Cloud Run's word on whether the container exited 0. Whether it did the right work is your job to record, e.g. a summary row or log line per run.

Shard work across job tasks

Every task gets these environment variables:

  • CLOUD_RUN_TASK_INDEX: 0 … tasks − 1
  • CLOUD_RUN_TASK_COUNT: the --tasks value
  • CLOUD_RUN_TASK_ATTEMPT: 0 on the first try
  • CLOUD_RUN_EXECUTION, CLOUD_RUN_JOB: names for logs and run records
# Python
import os
i = int(os.environ.get("CLOUD_RUN_TASK_INDEX", 0))
n = int(os.environ.get("CLOUD_RUN_TASK_COUNT", 1))
mine = [u for k, u in enumerate(sorted(all_uris)) if k % n == i]

// Java
int i = Integer.parseInt(System.getenv().getOrDefault("CLOUD_RUN_TASK_INDEX", "0"));
int n = Integer.parseInt(System.getenv().getOrDefault("CLOUD_RUN_TASK_COUNT", "1"));
List<String> mine = IntStream.range(0, uris.size())
    .filter(k -> k % n == i).mapToObj(uris::get).toList();

Sort the input first so every task sees the same order. Make each task idempotent: a retry repeats the whole shard.

Scaling & cost

  • Concurrency (default 80, max 1000): requests one instance handles at once. Set 1 only for code that is not thread-safe.
  • Min instances keep warm instances to avoid cold starts; they bill while idle.
  • Max instances cap spend and protect downstream databases (connections = instances × pool size).
  • CPU allocation: by default CPU is only allocated during requests, so background threads stall after the response. --no-cpu-throttling keeps CPU on (instance-based billing).
  • Startup CPU boost (--cpu-boost) gives extra CPU while the container starts: helps JVM cold starts.
  • Cold start cuts: smaller images, lazy initialisation, CDS/AOT for Java, Quarkus or Spring native images.
  • Jobs bill for the time each task runs, at the CPU and memory you asked for.

Calling private services

# grant the caller the invoker role
gcloud run services add-iam-policy-binding api --region=asia-south1 \
  --member=serviceAccount:caller@my-proj.iam.gserviceaccount.com \
  --role=roles/run.invoker

# from your terminal
curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $(gcloud auth print-identity-token)" https://api-xxxxx.a.run.app/health
gcloud run services proxy api --region=asia-south1 --port=8080   # local tunnel
# Python: ID token whose audience is the service URL
import google.auth.transport.requests, google.oauth2.id_token, requests
aud = "https://api-xxxxx.a.run.app"
tok = google.oauth2.id_token.fetch_id_token(google.auth.transport.requests.Request(), aud)
requests.get(f"{aud}/health", headers={"Authorization": f"Bearer {tok}"})

// Java (google-auth-library)
IdTokenCredentials creds = IdTokenCredentials.newBuilder()
    .setIdTokenProvider((IdTokenProvider) GoogleCredentials.getApplicationDefault())
    .setTargetAudience(aud).build();
creds.refresh();
String token = creds.getIdToken().getTokenValue();

Services need an ID token (audience = URL). Google APIs, including the Cloud Run Admin API that executes a job, need an access token.

Networking

# who may reach the service
--ingress=all                                  # internet (still IAM-checked)
--ingress=internal                             # VPC and some Google sources only
--ingress=internal-and-cloud-load-balancing    # put a load balancer / Cloud Armor in front

# reach private IPs (Memorystore, internal services) with Direct VPC egress
--network=main --subnet=run-egress --vpc-egress=private-ranges-only
--vpc-egress=all-traffic        # everything via VPC → Cloud NAT gives a static egress IP

# Cloud SQL via the built-in connector: unix socket in the container
--add-cloudsql-instances=my-proj:asia-south1:corpus
# JDBC: jdbc:postgresql:///corpus?cloudSqlInstance=my-proj:asia-south1:corpus&socketFactory=com.google.cloud.sql.postgres.SocketFactory
# psycopg: host=/cloudsql/my-proj:asia-south1:corpus

A third party that allowlists IPs needs all-traffic egress through Cloud NAT with a reserved address. Without it, egress comes from shared Google ranges.

Secrets, config, volumes

# secret as env var: read once, when the instance starts
--set-secrets=DB_PASSWORD=db-password:3

# secret as a file: read when your code opens it
--set-secrets=/secrets/sa.json=sa-key:latest

# the runtime service account needs to read it
gcloud secrets add-iam-policy-binding db-password \
  --member=serviceAccount:api@my-proj.iam.gserviceaccount.com \
  --role=roles/secretmanager.secretAccessor

# GCS bucket or in-memory volume
--add-volume=name=data,type=cloud-storage,bucket=my-landing \
--add-volume-mount=volume=data,mount-path=/data
--add-volume=name=scratch,type=in-memory,size-limit=1Gi

Pin secret versions (:3) in prod so a rotation is a deliberate deploy. With :latest in an env var, running instances keep the old value until they restart.

Triggers

  • Cloud Scheduler → job: POST to https://run.googleapis.com/v2/projects/P/locations/R/jobs/J:run with an OAuth token (see the Terraform tab).
  • Cloud Scheduler → service: HTTP target with an OIDC token whose audience is the service URL.
  • Pub/Sub push: subscription pushes to the service URL with an OIDC token; return 2xx to ack, anything else retries.
  • Eventarc: route events (GCS object finalised, BigQuery job done, audit log entries) to a service.
  • Cloud Tasks: queued HTTP calls with rate limits and retries.
  • Workflows: run jobs in sequence, wait for each, branch on results.
gcloud eventarc triggers create on-landing --location=asia-south1 \
  --destination-run-service=ingest --destination-run-region=asia-south1 \
  --event-filters=type=google.cloud.storage.object.v1.finalized \
  --event-filters=bucket=my-landing \
  --service-account=eventarc@my-proj.iam.gserviceaccount.com

Dockerfiles that start fast

# Java (Spring Boot / Quarkus jar)
FROM eclipse-temurin:21-jdk AS build
WORKDIR /src
COPY . .
RUN ./gradlew bootJar -x test

FROM eclipse-temurin:21-jre
RUN useradd -u 1001 app
USER 1001
COPY --from=build /src/build/libs/app.jar /app.jar
ENV JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS="-XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75 -XX:+UseSerialGC"
ENTRYPOINT ["java", "-jar", "/app.jar"]
# Python
FROM python:3.12-slim
ENV PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 PIP_NO_CACHE_DIR=1
WORKDIR /app
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install -r requirements.txt
COPY . .
USER 1001
CMD exec gunicorn --bind :$PORT --workers 1 --threads 8 --timeout 0 app:app
docker buildx build --platform linux/amd64 -t asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev/my-proj/apps/api:$(git rev-parse --short=12 HEAD) --push .

Use the exec form (["java", …] or exec) so your process is PID 1 and receives SIGTERM.

Logging & health

# one JSON object per line on stdout → structured log entry
{"severity":"ERROR","message":"load failed","uri":"gs://…","run_id":"r-42",
 "logging.googleapis.com/trace":"projects/my-proj/traces/abc123"}

gcloud logging read 'resource.type="cloud_run_revision"
  AND resource.labels.service_name="api" AND severity>=ERROR' --freshness=1h --limit=50
gcloud logging read 'resource.type="cloud_run_job"
  AND resource.labels.job_name="loader" AND severity>=ERROR' --freshness=1d --limit=20
# service.yaml fragment: probes
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      containers:
        - image: …
          startupProbe:
            httpGet: { path: /health/started, port: 8080 }
            failureThreshold: 30
            periodSeconds: 2
          livenessProbe:
            httpGet: { path: /health/live, port: 8080 }

Without severity, every line is logged at DEFAULT, and severity-based alerts never fire.

Declarative YAML

gcloud run services describe api --region=asia-south1 --format=export > service.yaml
# edit, then:
gcloud run services replace service.yaml --region=asia-south1

gcloud run jobs describe loader --region=asia-south1 --format=export > job.yaml
gcloud run jobs replace job.yaml --region=asia-south1

The YAML follows the Knative Serving schema. In a team, keep the definition in Terraform (google_cloud_run_v2_service / _job) and use the CLI for reading and one-off executions.

Errors and what they usually mean

SymptomUsually means
"Container failed to start and listen on the port defined by PORT"App listens on the wrong port or on 127.0.0.1, crashes on boot (missing env var or secret), or the image is arm64. Read the revision's logs.
403 calling a serviceCaller lacks roles/run.invoker, sent an access token instead of an ID token, or the token's audience is not the service URL.
404 calling a serviceWrong URL or region, or ingress is internal and you called from outside the VPC.
429 / "no available instance"max-instances reached with all instances at full concurrency.
503 with "Memory limit exceeded" in logsOOM: raise --memory, or stop writing large files to the in-memory filesystem.
504 / request cancelledRequest ran past --timeout.
Permission denied on secretRuntime service account lacks secretmanager.secretAccessor on that secret.
Image pull deniedThe Cloud Run service agent cannot read the Artifact Registry repo (common across projects).
Job execution FAILED, logs emptyTask timed out, was OOM-killed, or crashed before logging. executions describe shows the reason per task.
Background work never finishesCPU is throttled after the response. Finish before responding, use --no-cpu-throttling, or hand the work to a job or Cloud Tasks.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Deploy a service and read its logs
  • Execute a job and check its result
  • Roll traffic back to a previous revision

2 Practitioner

  • Set scaling, CPU, memory, concurrency and timeouts deliberately
  • Secure services with IAM, secrets and service accounts
  • Connect to private resources with VPC egress

3 Senior

  • Design job sharding, retries and run records
  • Tune cold starts and cost
  • Design service-to-service auth and traffic management

4 Expert

  • Set the serverless platform blueprint for the organisation
  • Decide Cloud Run vs GKE vs other compute
  • Design multi-region serverless architectures

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Apigee

Google Cloud's API management platform: a gateway plus API products, developer apps, quotas, security policies, analytics and a developer portal. Background on gateways is on APIs & Integration.

Core concepts

ConceptMeaning
OrganisationTop-level Apigee container, tied to a GCP project
EnvironmentA runtime context where proxies are deployed (dev, test, prod)
Environment groupHostnames that route to one or more environments
API proxyThe façade clients call; it applies policies and forwards to the backend
Proxy endpointClient-facing side: base path, request and response flows
Target endpointBackend-facing side: the service URL, its auth and timeouts
FlowOrdered steps: PreFlow, conditional flows, PostFlow, on request and response
PolicyA reusable step: verify a key, check a token, rate-limit, transform, cache
Shared flowA reusable sequence of policies used by many proxies (security, logging)
API productA bundle of proxies and operations with quotas and scopes, offered to consumers
Developer / appA consumer and their registered app, which gets keys for products

Apigee X is the Google Cloud-hosted version; Apigee hybrid runs the runtime in your own Kubernetes clusters. Apigee Edge is the older generation.

Proxy endpoint

<!-- apiproxy/proxies/default.xml -->
<ProxyEndpoint name="default">
  <PreFlow name="PreFlow">
    <Request>
      <Step><Name>VK-VerifyKey</Name></Step>
      <Step><Name>SA-SpikeArrest</Name></Step>
      <Step><Name>Q-PerApp</Name></Step>
    </Request>
  </PreFlow>
  <Flows>
    <Flow name="search">
      <Condition>proxy.pathsuffix MatchesPath "/search" and request.verb = "GET"</Condition>
    </Flow>
  </Flows>
  <HTTPProxyConnection>
    <BasePath>/legal/v1</BasePath>
  </HTTPProxyConnection>
  <RouteRule name="default">
    <TargetEndpoint>default</TargetEndpoint>
  </RouteRule>
</ProxyEndpoint>

Target endpoint to Cloud Run

<!-- apiproxy/targets/default.xml -->
<TargetEndpoint name="default">
  <HTTPTargetConnection>
    <Authentication>
      <GoogleIDToken>
        <Audience>https://legal-api-xxxxx.a.run.app</Audience>
      </GoogleIDToken>
    </Authentication>
    <URL>https://legal-api-xxxxx.a.run.app</URL>
  </HTTPTargetConnection>
</TargetEndpoint>

Apigee calls the private Cloud Run service with a Google ID token from the proxy's service account, which needs roles/run.invoker. Clients never reach Cloud Run directly; set its ingress accordingly.

Common policies

<VerifyAPIKey name="VK-VerifyKey">
  <APIKey ref="request.header.x-api-key"/>
</VerifyAPIKey>

<OAuthV2 name="OA-VerifyToken">
  <Operation>VerifyAccessToken</Operation>
</OAuthV2>

<SpikeArrest name="SA-SpikeArrest">
  <Rate>30ps</Rate>               <!-- smooths bursts -->
</SpikeArrest>

<Quota name="Q-PerApp">
  <Interval>1</Interval>
  <TimeUnit>day</TimeUnit>
  <Allow count="10000"/>
  <Identifier ref="client_id"/>
</Quota>

<AssignMessage name="AM-RemoveKey">
  <Remove><Headers><Header name="x-api-key"/></Headers></Remove>
</AssignMessage>
CategoryPolicies
SecurityVerifyAPIKey, OAuthV2, VerifyJWT, BasicAuthentication, AccessControl, CORS
TrafficSpikeArrest, Quota, ResponseCache
MediationAssignMessage, ExtractVariables, JSONtoXML, XMLtoJSON, RaiseFault
ExtensionJavaScript, ServiceCallout, MessageLogging, FlowCallout (shared flows)

SpikeArrest protects the backend from bursts; Quota enforces each consumer's allowance. Use both.

Products, apps and keys

  1. Build a proxy and deploy it to an environment.
  2. Create an API product listing the proxy (and operations), environments, quota and scopes, e.g. "Legal Search – Standard: 10,000 calls/day".
  3. A developer registers an app and subscribes it to the product.
  4. The app gets a consumer key (and secret for OAuth). The proxy's VerifyAPIKey or OAuthV2 policy checks it and loads the product's quota.
  5. Analytics then report traffic, latency and errors per product, app and developer.

Products are how you package the same API differently: free and paid tiers, internal and partner access.

Apigee vs API Gateway vs Cloud Endpoints

ApigeeAPI Gateway
ScopeFull API managementLightweight managed gateway
FeaturesProducts, developer portal, monetisation, rich policies, analytics, hybridOpenAPI-defined routes, API keys, JWT auth, quotas
BackendsAnything reachableCloud Run, Cloud Functions, App Engine and others
CostSignificant; subscription or pay-as-you-goLow, per call
Choose whenMany APIs, external consumers, governance, analyticsA few APIs, mostly internal or simple

Cloud Endpoints is the older Google option; new designs generally pick API Gateway or Apigee.

Delivering proxies like code

  • Keep the apiproxy/ bundle and shared flows in Git; review policy changes like code.
  • Deploy from CI with the Apigee API, apigeecli or the Apigee Maven plugin, to dev → test → prod environments.
  • Manage organisations, environments and environment groups with Terraform (google_apigee_* resources).
  • Lint bundles with apigeelint before deploying.
  • Use shared flows as your blueprint: one security and logging flow every proxy calls, instead of copied policies.
  • Test with the trace/debug tool, and with automated API tests against each environment.

Security and operations

  • Validate OAuth tokens or JWTs at the edge; the backend still checks object-level permissions.
  • Strip credentials before forwarding; add a trusted identity header only from Apigee.
  • Use Advanced API Security to detect abuse and misconfigured proxies.
  • Put Cloud Armor in front of the external load balancer for WAF and DDoS protection.
  • Log to Cloud Logging with MessageLogging; never log keys or tokens.
  • Watch latency split: total vs target response time tells you if the gateway or the backend is slow.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain proxies, policies, products and apps
  • Call an API through Apigee with a key
  • Read Apigee analytics for an API

2 Practitioner

  • Build a proxy with key/token verification, spike arrest and quota
  • Use shared flows; deploy through environments from CI
  • Connect securely to Cloud Run backends

3 Senior

  • Design API products, tiers and the developer portal
  • Run Apigee across environments with Terraform and CI
  • Secure APIs at the edge with OAuth and Advanced API Security

4 Expert

  • Own the organisation's API management platform and governance
  • Design hybrid or multi-region Apigee deployments
  • Lead API monetisation and partner programmes

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

DevOps

Getting a change from a laptop to production safely and keeping it running: Git, CI/CD, releases, observability, incidents and supply-chain security.

Git you will use daily

git switch -c feat/123-short-name origin/main
git add -p                         # stage hunk by hunk
git commit -m "fix: reject unparseable SC dates"
git fetch origin && git rebase origin/main
git push -u origin feat/123-short-name
git push --force-with-lease        # after a rebase; never plain --force

git log --oneline --graph -20
git log -S "parse_date" --oneline  # commits that added/removed a string
git blame -L 40,60 file.py
git diff origin/main...HEAD        # what my branch changes
git show <sha>:path/file           # a file at a revision
git stash push -m wip; git stash pop
git restore file                   # discard local edits
git revert <sha>                   # undo a merged commit safely
git bisect start BAD GOOD          # binary-search a regression
git worktree add ../wt2 -b other origin/main   # second checkout
git reflog                         # find "lost" commits

Branching & PR habits

  • Trunk-based: short-lived branches off main, merged within a day, behind feature flags if unfinished.
  • One concern per PR. If the title needs "and", split it.
  • Protect main: PR required, checks required, no force pushes.
  • Commit messages say why, not only what. Conventional prefixes (feat:, fix:, chore:) help changelogs.
  • Review for behaviour: tests that would fail without the change, error paths, what happens when a dependency is down.
  • Promote artefacts, not branches: build once, tag the image with the commit SHA, deploy that same image to each environment.

GitHub Actions → GCP (no keys)

name: deploy
on:
  push: { branches: [main] }
permissions:
  contents: read
  id-token: write              # needed for Workload Identity Federation
concurrency: { group: deploy-${{ github.ref }}, cancel-in-progress: false }
jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-java@v4
        with: { distribution: temurin, java-version: 21, cache: gradle }
      - run: ./gradlew test
  deploy:
    needs: test
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    environment: prod          # environment secrets, protection rules
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: google-github-actions/auth@v2
        with:
          workload_identity_provider: ${{ vars.WIF_PROVIDER }}
          service_account: ${{ vars.DEPLOY_SA }}
      - uses: google-github-actions/setup-gcloud@v2
      - run: |
          IMG=asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev/${{ vars.GCP_PROJECT }}/apps/api:${GITHUB_SHA::12}
          gcloud auth configure-docker asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev --quiet
          docker build -t "$IMG" . && docker push "$IMG"
          echo "IMG=$IMG" >> "$GITHUB_ENV"
      - uses: google-github-actions/deploy-cloudrun@v2
        with: { service: api, region: asia-south1, image: "${{ env.IMG }}" }

For security, pin third-party actions to a full commit SHA rather than a tag. A workflow-level paths: filter on a required check can leave a PR waiting forever; skip inside the job instead.

Release strategies

StrategyHowWhen
RollingReplace instances a few at a timeDefault for stateless services
Blue/greenRun old and new side by side, switch traffic at onceFast rollback, needs double capacity
CanarySend a small % to the new version, watch metrics, increaseRisky changes with good metrics
Feature flagShip code dark, turn it on per user or percentageSeparating deploy from release
Expand / contractAdd the new schema, migrate, remove the old in a later releaseDatabase changes with zero downtime

Every release needs a rollback you have practised. A database migration that cannot be rolled back makes the whole release one-way.

Observability

  • Logs say what happened (structured JSON, with a request or run ID). Metrics say how much and how often. Traces say where the time went across services.
  • Golden signals: latency, traffic, errors, saturation. RED for services (rate, errors, duration); USE for resources (utilisation, saturation, errors).
  • SLI: a measured ratio, e.g. requests under 500 ms ÷ all requests. SLO: the target, e.g. 99.5% over 30 days. Error budget: the 0.5% you may spend on releases and incidents.
  • Alert on symptoms users feel (SLO burn rate), not on every CPU spike. Every alert needs an owner and a runbook.
  • Know "nothing there" from "cannot see": a check whose data source is down must report unknown, not zero or green.

Incident response

  1. Declare it early and name an incident lead. One channel, one timeline.
  2. Mitigate before you diagnose: roll back, fail over, scale up, turn the flag off.
  3. Communicate status on a schedule, even "no change".
  4. Write down what you saw and did, with timestamps, as you go.
  5. Review blamelessly afterwards: what happened, why the system allowed it, which guard would have caught it. Track the follow-ups to done.

Supply-chain & secrets

  • No long-lived keys: Workload Identity Federation for CI, service accounts for workloads.
  • Secrets in a secret manager, injected at runtime. Never in Git, images, logs or CI output.
  • Least privilege: one service account per workload, with only the roles it uses.
  • Dependency updates automated (Dependabot or Renovate), with tests gating them.
  • Scan code (SAST), dependencies (SCA), images (Trivy) and committed secrets (gitleaks, GitHub secret scanning).
  • Know what you ship: SBOMs and signed images (cosign) make "are we affected by CVE-X?" a query, not a hunt.

Measuring delivery

DORA metricQuestion
Deployment frequencyHow often do we ship to production?
Lead time for changesCommit to production: how long?
Change failure rateWhat share of deploys cause a failure?
Time to restoreHow fast do we recover?

Speed and stability rise together when changes are small, tested and easy to roll back.

Twelve-factor, short version

  • One codebase, many deploys; config in the environment, not the code.
  • Dependencies declared and pinned; backing services (DB, queue) attached by URL.
  • Build, release and run are separate steps; the same build goes to every environment.
  • Processes are stateless and disposable: fast start, graceful shutdown on SIGTERM.
  • Logs are an event stream to stdout; the platform collects them.
  • Admin tasks (migrations, backfills) run as one-off processes from the same release.
  • Dev and prod as similar as possible: same images, same database engine.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Use Git daily: branch, commit, rebase, PR
  • Read a CI run and find why it failed
  • Roll back a deploy

2 Practitioner

  • Write CI/CD workflows with caching and keyless cloud auth
  • Add structured logs, metrics and alerts
  • Take part in incident response

3 Senior

  • Design release strategies (canary, blue/green, flags)
  • Define SLOs and alerting on symptoms
  • Improve DORA metrics; lead post-incident reviews

4 Expert

  • Build an internal developer platform and golden paths
  • Set delivery and reliability standards for the organisation
  • Lead culture change toward small, safe, frequent releases

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Observability

Being able to answer new questions about a running system from the outside: what happened, where, why, and to whom. Logs, metrics, traces and profiles; OpenTelemetry; Google Cloud's tools; dashboards and alerts; and observability for data pipelines and AI features.

Signals and what each answers

SignalIsAnswersCost driver
LogsTimestamped records of events, ideally structured JSONWhat exactly happened in this request or run?Volume and retention
MetricsNumbers aggregated over time (counts, gauges, histograms)How much, how often, how fast, trending where?Number of time series (label cardinality)
TracesA request's path across services as a tree of timed spansWhere did the time go? Which dependency failed?Sampling rate
ProfilesWhere CPU and memory go inside the codeWhich function is slow or allocating?Usually low (continuous sampling)
Events / run recordsBusiness or pipeline facts (deploys, run outcomes)What changed, and did the job do its work?Low

Monitoring tells you that something is wrong from questions you planned. Observability lets you find out why, including for failures nobody predicted. You need both.

Structured logging

{"timestamp":"2026-11-12T06:15:04.120Z",
 "severity":"ERROR",
 "message":"load failed",
 "service":"odp-load", "version":"3f2a9c1", "env":"prod",
 "run_id":"r-2026-11-12", "stage":"ODP_LOAD",
 "object":"gs://landing/data/year=2024/part-3.parquet",
 "error":"400 Bad Request", "error_body":"InvalidArgument: …",
 "logging.googleapis.com/trace":"projects/p/traces/4bf92f…"}
  • One JSON object per line; a severity field so levels work.
  • Always include service, version, environment, and a request or run ID.
  • Log the evidence: the upstream error body, the input that failed, counts. A log that says only "failed" helps no one.
  • Never log secrets, tokens or unnecessary personal data.
  • Levels: ERROR needs attention; WARN is unusual but handled; INFO is a business event; DEBUG is off in production.

Metrics that matter

  • Counter: only goes up (requests, errors, rows loaded). Graph its rate.
  • Gauge: current value (queue depth, connections, lag).
  • Histogram: distribution of values (latency); gives percentiles.
  • RED for services: rate, errors, duration.
  • USE for resources: utilisation, saturation, errors.
  • Golden signals: latency, traffic, errors, saturation.
  • Cardinality: each unique label combination is a separate series. Never use user IDs, request IDs or URLs with IDs as labels.
  • Percentiles, not averages: p50 for typical, p95/p99 for the users having a bad time.

Distributed tracing

  • A trace is one request end to end; a span is one operation in it (an HTTP call, a query), with start, duration, attributes and status.
  • Context propagation: the trace ID travels in the W3C traceparent header and in message attributes across queues.
  • Sampling: keep a percentage of traces (head sampling), or keep all errors and slow ones (tail sampling in a collector).
  • Link logs to traces by writing the trace ID into every log line.
  • Batch jobs: one trace per run, a span per stage or partition.

OpenTelemetry

The vendor-neutral standard for producing logs, metrics and traces: one API in your code, an SDK that processes and exports, the OTLP protocol, and an optional Collector that receives, filters, samples and forwards to any backend.

# Java: zero-code instrumentation with the agent (HTTP, JDBC, Kafka, gRPC …)
java -javaagent:/otel/opentelemetry-javaagent.jar -jar app.jar
#   OTEL_SERVICE_NAME=legal-api
#   OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT=http://otel-collector:4318
#   OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER=parentbased_traceidratio  OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER_ARG=0.1

# Python: auto-instrumentation
pip install opentelemetry-distro opentelemetry-exporter-otlp
opentelemetry-bootstrap -a install
OTEL_SERVICE_NAME=odp-load opentelemetry-instrument python -m loader
# manual span around meaningful work (Python)
from opentelemetry import trace
tracer = trace.get_tracer("odp-load")

with tracer.start_as_current_span("load_object") as span:
    span.set_attribute("object.uri", uri)
    rows = load(uri)
    span.set_attribute("rows.loaded", rows)
# Collector: receive OTLP, batch, send to Google Cloud
receivers:
  otlp:
    protocols:
      grpc:
      http:
processors:
  batch:
exporters:
  googlecloud:
  googlemanagedprometheus:
service:
  pipelines:
    traces:  { receivers: [otlp], processors: [batch], exporters: [googlecloud] }
    logs:    { receivers: [otlp], processors: [batch], exporters: [googlecloud] }
    metrics: { receivers: [otlp], processors: [batch], exporters: [googlemanagedprometheus] }

Spring Boot and Micrometer

# application.yml
management:
  endpoints.web.exposure.include: health,prometheus
  tracing.sampling.probability: 0.1
  otlp.tracing.endpoint: http://otel-collector:4318/v1/traces
logging.structured.format.console: ecs
@Component
class LoadMetrics {
  private final Counter loaded, failed;
  private final Timer duration;
  LoadMetrics(MeterRegistry r) {
    loaded   = Counter.builder("odp.objects").tag("outcome", "loaded").register(r);
    failed   = Counter.builder("odp.objects").tag("outcome", "failed").register(r);
    duration = Timer.builder("odp.load.duration").publishPercentileHistogram().register(r);
  }
}

@Observed(name = "judgment.search")   // timer + span in one annotation
public List<Judgment> search(String q) { … }

Google Cloud Observability

ToolUse it for
Cloud LoggingAll logs from Cloud Run, GKE, BigQuery jobs and audit logs; Logs Explorer; log sinks to BigQuery or GCS; log-based metrics
Cloud MonitoringMetrics, dashboards, uptime checks, alerting policies, SLOs; queries in PromQL
Managed Service for PrometheusPrometheus metrics at scale without running Prometheus
Cloud TraceDistributed traces and latency analysis
Cloud ProfilerContinuous CPU and heap profiling in production
Error ReportingGroups exceptions from logs, shows new and recurring errors
Audit logsWho did what: Admin Activity always on; enable Data Access logs for sensitive services
# Logs Explorer query
resource.type="cloud_run_revision"
resource.labels.service_name="legal-api"
severity>=ERROR
jsonPayload.run_id="r-2026-11-12"
timestamp>="2026-11-12T00:00:00Z"

# log-based metric from a marker in the text
gcloud logging metrics create dp_incidents \
  --description="Pipeline incidents" \
  --log-filter='textPayload:"dp_incident"'

# PromQL in Cloud Monitoring: 5xx ratio and p95 latency
sum(rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count{status=~"5.."}[5m]))
  / sum(rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count[5m]))
histogram_quantile(0.95,
  sum by (le) (rate(http_server_requests_seconds_bucket[5m])))

Dashboards that get used

  • One overview per service: traffic, errors, latency percentiles, saturation, last deploy marker.
  • Top-down: user-facing SLOs first, then dependencies, then resources.
  • Same layout for every service (a dashboard template is a blueprint).
  • Show the denominator: "12 errors" means nothing without "of 1.2 million requests".
  • Annotate deploys and incidents so changes line up with effects.
  • Delete dashboards nobody opens.

Alerting

  • Page on symptoms users feel (SLO burn rate, failed runs, stale data), not on causes (CPU at 80%).
  • Every page is actionable and links to a runbook. If nobody needs to act, it is a ticket or a dashboard, not a page.
  • Alert when the signal is missing: a job that did not run, a metric that stopped reporting. Silence is not health.
  • Match on fields you control (a marker in the message, a structured field), not on a severity that may not be set.
  • Prove each alert fires once in a test environment before trusting it.
  • Review noisy alerts monthly: tune, fix the cause, or delete.

Observing data pipelines

DimensionCheck
Run outcomeEvery scheduled run recorded SUCCEEDED, FAILED or UNKNOWN; missing runs alert
FreshnessNewest data per source within its expected age
VolumeRow and object counts within expected ranges; reconciliation in = out + rejected
SchemaUnexpected new, missing or retyped columns detected
QualityTest results (nulls, keys, parse failures) tracked over time
Cost and durationBytes, slot time and runtime per run, trended

The most important rule: a check whose source is unreachable reports UNKNOWN, never zero and never green.

Observing AI features

  • Log model ID, input and output tokens, latency, stop reason and cost per call.
  • Trace each tool call and retrieval step as a span.
  • Track refusals, empty retrievals, citation failures and "not found" rates.
  • Sample real conversations for quality review and feed them into evals (with privacy controls).

Keeping observability affordable

  • Exclude noisy, low-value logs at the router (exclusion filters) or lower their level.
  • Sample traces; keep 100% of errors and slow requests with tail sampling.
  • Watch metric cardinality; drop unused labels.
  • Route long-term logs to BigQuery or GCS with retention rules instead of keeping everything hot.
  • Label resources so observability cost can be attributed to teams.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Find a request's or run's logs and errors in Cloud Logging
  • Read a service dashboard: traffic, errors, latency
  • Write structured logs with IDs and evidence

2 Practitioner

  • Instrument a service with OpenTelemetry and custom metrics
  • Write log queries, log-based metrics and PromQL
  • Build dashboards and actionable alerts with runbooks

3 Senior

  • Design observability for a system: signals, sampling, cardinality, cost
  • Trace problems across services, queues and pipelines
  • Set dashboard and alert templates for teams

4 Expert

  • Own the observability platform and its standards
  • Lead cost and data-governance strategy for telemetry
  • Drive observability-led reliability across the organisation

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Ops tooling: Prometheus, Grafana, Fluentd, Dynatrace, ServiceNow

The tools you will meet in operations teams, what each does, how they fit together, and working configuration. Concepts are on Observability and SRE.

How they fit together

ToolCategoryDoesGCP-native alternative
PrometheusMetricsScrapes and stores time-series metrics; PromQL; alert rulesManaged Service for Prometheus, Cloud Monitoring
AlertmanagerAlert routingGroups, silences and routes Prometheus alerts to people and toolsCloud Monitoring notification channels
GrafanaVisualisationDashboards and alerting over many data sources; Loki (logs), Tempo (traces), Mimir (metrics) in the same familyCloud Monitoring dashboards
Fluentd / Fluent BitLog shippingCollect, parse, enrich and forward logs to any backendThe built-in logging agents on GKE and Cloud Run
DynatraceFull-stack APM / observability platformAuto-instrumentation, topology, traces, logs, metrics, AI root-cause analysisCloud Trace, Profiler, Logging, Monitoring together
ServiceNowIT service managementIncidents, problems, changes, requests, CMDB, approvals and workflowNone: ITSM sits above monitoring

Typical flow: apps emit telemetry → Fluent Bit ships logs, Prometheus scrapes metrics (or Dynatrace's agent collects all) → Grafana or Dynatrace shows it → alerts route through Alertmanager → ServiceNow opens an incident, pages on-call, and tracks the change that fixes it.

Prometheus: model

  • Pull model: Prometheus scrapes /metrics endpoints on an interval (Spring: /actuator/prometheus).
  • Exporters expose metrics for systems that do not: node_exporter (hosts), postgres_exporter, blackbox_exporter (probes).
  • Time series = metric name + labels. Keep label values bounded.
  • Service discovery finds targets in Kubernetes, GCE and others.
  • Short-term storage locally; long-term in Thanos, Mimir or Google's managed service.
  • Recording rules precompute expensive queries; alerting rules fire when an expression holds for a duration.

Prometheus: configuration

# prometheus.yml
global:
  scrape_interval: 30s
scrape_configs:
  - job_name: legal-api
    metrics_path: /actuator/prometheus
    static_configs:
      - targets: ["legal-api:8080"]
rule_files: ["rules/*.yml"]
alerting:
  alertmanagers:
    - static_configs:
        - targets: ["alertmanager:9093"]
# rules/legal-api.yml
groups:
  - name: legal-api
    rules:
      - record: job:http_5xx_ratio:rate5m
        expr: |
          sum by (job) (rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count{status=~"5.."}[5m]))
          / sum by (job) (rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count[5m]))
      - alert: LegalApiHighErrorRate
        expr: job:http_5xx_ratio:rate5m{job="legal-api"} > 0.02
        for: 10m
        labels: { severity: page }
        annotations:
          summary: "legal-api 5xx above 2% for 10 minutes"
          runbook_url: "https://runbooks.example.com/legal-api/high-errors"
      - alert: CorpusLoadMissing
        expr: time() - corpus_last_success_timestamp_seconds > 26 * 3600
        labels: { severity: page }
        annotations: { summary: "No successful corpus load for 26 hours" }

PromQL you will use

rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count[5m])                 # per-second rate of a counter
sum by (status) (rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count[5m])) # grouped
histogram_quantile(0.95,
  sum by (le) (rate(http_server_requests_seconds_bucket[5m])))  # p95 latency
increase(odp_objects_total{outcome="failed"}[1h])             # count over a window
up == 0                                                       # targets not scraped
absent(corpus_last_success_timestamp_seconds)                 # metric missing entirely
topk(5, sum by (pod) (container_memory_working_set_bytes))    # top memory users

Always rate() a counter before summing; never sum raw counters. Alert on absent() or up == 0 so a dead exporter is not mistaken for health.

Alertmanager routing

route:
  receiver: team-ticket
  group_by: [alertname, job]
  group_wait: 30s
  repeat_interval: 4h
  routes:
    - matchers: [ 'severity="page"' ]
      receiver: oncall-page
receivers:
  - name: oncall-page
    pagerduty_configs:
      - routing_key_file: /etc/alertmanager/secrets/pagerduty_key
  - name: team-ticket
    webhook_configs:
      - url: https://integration.example.com/servicenow/alerts   # creates a ServiceNow incident

Group related alerts, silence during planned work, and inhibit child alerts when the parent (a whole cluster down) is firing.

Grafana

  • Data sources: Prometheus, Loki, Tempo, Cloud Monitoring, BigQuery, Postgres and many more, in one dashboard.
  • Dashboards as code: provision from files or the Grafana Terraform provider; keep JSON in Git and review changes.
  • Variables ($service, $env) make one dashboard serve every service: a dashboard template is a blueprint.
  • Grafana Alerting can evaluate rules across data sources; pick one alerting system per signal to avoid duplicates.
  • Explore view for ad-hoc queries, and jumping from a metric spike to logs and traces of the same time window.
# provisioning/datasources/prometheus.yaml
apiVersion: 1
datasources:
  - name: Prometheus
    type: prometheus
    access: proxy
    url: http://prometheus:9090
    isDefault: true

Fluentd and Fluent Bit

  • Fluent Bit: lightweight (C), the usual choice as a node agent or sidecar in Kubernetes.
  • Fluentd: heavier (Ruby), huge plugin ecosystem, often used as a central aggregator.
  • Pipeline: input → parser → filter (enrich, drop, mask) → buffer → output.
  • On GKE and Cloud Run logs are collected for you; add Fluent Bit when you need other destinations or self-managed clusters.
  • Mask secrets and personal data in filters before logs leave the node.
# Fluent Bit (Kubernetes node agent)
[INPUT]
    Name    tail
    Path    /var/log/containers/*.log
    multiline.parser  cri
    Tag     kube.*
[FILTER]
    Name    kubernetes
    Match   kube.*
[FILTER]
    Name    grep
    Match   kube.*
    Exclude log healthcheck
[OUTPUT]
    Name    stackdriver
    Match   *
# Fluentd
<source>
  @type tail
  path /var/log/app/*.log
  pos_file /var/log/fluentd/app.pos
  tag app
  <parse>
    @type json
  </parse>
</source>
<filter app>
  @type record_transformer
  <record>
    env prod
  </record>
</filter>
<match app>
  @type stdout
</match>

Dynatrace

  • OneAgent: installed on hosts or via the Kubernetes Operator; instruments JVM, Python, Node, databases and more without code changes.
  • Smartscape: a live topology of hosts, processes, services and their dependencies.
  • PurePath: end-to-end distributed traces, down to code-level timing.
  • Davis AI: detects anomalies and groups related events into one "problem" with a probable root cause.
  • Grail and DQL: stores logs, metrics, traces and events together and queries them with Dynatrace Query Language.
  • SLOs, synthetic monitoring, real-user monitoring and OpenTelemetry ingestion.
// DQL example: error logs per namespace in the last hour
fetch logs, from:now()-1h
| filter loglevel == "ERROR"
| summarize errors = count(), by:{k8s.namespace.name}
| sort errors desc

Commercial platforms trade cost for breadth and automation. Instrument with OpenTelemetry where you can, so changing vendor later does not mean re-instrumenting.

ServiceNow: the ITSM side

RecordIs
IncidentSomething is broken now; restore service
ProblemThe underlying cause of one or more incidents; fix it for good
Change requestA planned change to production: standard (pre-approved), normal (assessed and approved), emergency
RequestA user asking for something (access, a new environment)
CMDB CIA configuration item: a service, app, database or host and its relationships
  • Event management turns monitoring alerts into correlated alerts and incidents, linked to the affected CI.
  • Change automation: CI/CD pipelines (Harness, GitHub Actions, Jenkins) open and update change requests automatically, so low-risk deploys are approved by policy rather than a weekly board.
  • Keep the CMDB generated from real sources (cloud APIs, Terraform state, service catalogues); hand-maintained CMDBs drift.

ServiceNow Table API

# open P1 incidents
curl -s -u "$SN_USER:$SN_PASS" -H "Accept: application/json" \
  "https://$INSTANCE.service-now.com/api/now/table/incident?sysparm_query=active=true^priority=1&sysparm_limit=10&sysparm_fields=number,short_description,state"

# create an incident from an automated check
curl -s -u "$SN_USER:$SN_PASS" -X POST \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" -H "Accept: application/json" \
  "https://$INSTANCE.service-now.com/api/now/table/incident" \
  -d '{"short_description":"Corpus load missing for 26h",
       "urgency":"2","impact":"2",
       "assignment_group":"<sys_id of the Data Platform group>",
       "description":"Ledger shows no SUCCEEDED LAND since 2026-11-11 06:15 UTC. Runbook: …"}'

Use an integration user with only the roles it needs, OAuth rather than basic auth where available, and dedupe on a correlation ID so a flapping alert does not open fifty incidents.

Choosing and combining

  • All on GCP, small team: Cloud Logging, Monitoring and Trace with OpenTelemetry; little to run.
  • Kubernetes-heavy, open source: Prometheus + Grafana + Loki + Tempo + Fluent Bit; you operate them (or use managed versions).
  • Large enterprise, many stacks: Dynatrace (or similar) for automatic coverage and root cause, ServiceNow for process.
  • Whatever you choose: one alerting path per signal, runbooks on every alert, telemetry defined as code, and OpenTelemetry at the source.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Read a Grafana or Dynatrace dashboard and find a service's errors
  • Look up and update a ServiceNow incident
  • Know what Prometheus, Fluent Bit and Alertmanager each do

2 Practitioner

  • Write PromQL queries, recording and alerting rules
  • Configure log shipping and Grafana dashboards as code
  • Connect alerts to ServiceNow incidents and CI/CD to change requests

3 Senior

  • Design the monitoring stack and alert routing for a platform
  • Integrate Dynatrace or open-source tooling with OpenTelemetry
  • Automate change management while keeping control

4 Expert

  • Own observability and ITSM tooling strategy and cost
  • Lead tool consolidation or migration across the organisation
  • Set standards for telemetry, alerting and incident process

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.
