Site reliability engineering

Running production as an engineering problem: reliability targets agreed with the business, error budgets that balance speed and stability, alerting on what users feel, incident response, blameless learning, and removing toil. Signals and tools are on Observability.

Principles

  • 100% is the wrong target. Users cannot tell 99.99% from 100%, and chasing it stops all change. Pick the reliability users actually need.
  • Service level objectives make that target explicit and measurable.
  • Error budgets turn it into a decision rule: budget left, ship faster; budget spent, slow down and fix reliability.
  • Toil is reduced by engineering: automate repetitive manual work rather than staffing it.
  • Blameless postmortems: incidents are system failures to learn from, not people to blame.
  • Simplicity: every component you do not have cannot fail.
  • No person or laptop is the mechanism: if something only works while a human watches it, it is not built yet.

SLI, SLO, SLA, error budget

TermIsExample
SLIA measured ratio of good events to valid eventsSearch requests answered successfully in under 500 ms ÷ all search requests
SLOThe target for an SLI over a window99.5% over 28 days
Error budget1 − SLO: the failure you may spend0.5% of requests ≈ 3.4 hours of full outage in 28 days
SLAA contract with consequences, looser than the SLO99% monthly, or credits

Measure SLIs as close to the user as possible (load balancer or client), not from inside the server, which cannot see its own outages.

Choosing SLIs

SystemTypical SLIs
Request/response APIAvailability (non-5xx), latency (fraction under a threshold)
Data pipelineFreshness (data newer than X), completeness (rows reconciled), correctness (tests passing)
Batch jobRuns completed on time; runs succeeded
StorageDurability, read/write availability and latency
AI featureSuccessful responses, latency, grounded-answer rate from evals
Pipeline freshness SLO example
  SLI: minutes in which the newest decision_date served is < 26 h old
       ÷ minutes in the window
  SLO: 99% over 28 days
  Budget: ≈ 6.7 hours of staleness per 28 days

Error budget policy

  • Budget healthy: ship at normal speed; take calculated risks.
  • Budget at risk (e.g. 50% spent early): extra review on risky changes; reliability work moves up the backlog.
  • Budget exhausted: freeze non-urgent feature releases for the service until reliability fixes land and the budget recovers.
  • One incident ate over 20% of the budget: a postmortem with tracked actions is mandatory.
  • Agree the policy with product owners before it is needed.

Alerting on SLOs: burn rates

Burn rate = how fast you are spending the budget, relative to spending exactly all of it over the window. Burn rate 1 uses the budget in exactly 28–30 days; burn rate 14.4 uses 2% of a 30-day budget in one hour.

AlertBurn rateLong windowShort windowBudget consumedAction
Fast burn14.41 hour5 minutes2%Page
Medium burn66 hours30 minutes5%Page
Slow burn13 days6 hours10%Ticket

Both windows must exceed the threshold: the long one shows it matters, the short one shows it is still happening, so the alert stops soon after recovery. These are the starting values from Google's SRE Workbook; tune to your traffic.

On-call

  • Sustainable load: few enough pages that each one gets real attention; track pages per shift.
  • Every page has a runbook: what it means, how to check, how to mitigate, who to escalate to.
  • Primary and secondary on rotation, with handover notes.
  • Access ready in advance: break-glass roles, dashboards, tooling; never "I need permissions" at 3 a.m.
  • Reduce pages continuously: every page should lead to a fix, a tuned alert, or an automated response.

Incident response

RoleDoes
Incident commanderOwns the incident, coordinates, decides; does not debug
Operations leadInvestigates and applies mitigations
Communications leadUpdates stakeholders and users on a schedule
ScribeKeeps the timeline: what was seen, decided, done, when
  1. Declare early; set severity; open one channel.
  2. Mitigate first: roll back, fail over, disable the feature, shed load.
  3. Communicate status regularly, including "no change".
  4. Resolve, confirm with the SLIs, then hand over to the postmortem.

Severity levels

SevExampleResponse
1Product down or data loss or leak for many usersPage now, all hands, exec updates
2Major feature broken or badly degradedPage now, incident roles
3Minor feature or few users affected; workaround existsBusiness hours
4Cosmetic or internal onlyTicket

Blameless postmortem template

# Incident: <title>            Sev: 2     Date: 2026-11-12
## Summary          what happened, impact, duration (2–3 sentences)
## Impact           users affected, SLO budget spent, data affected
## Timeline (UTC)   detection, escalation, mitigation, resolution
## Root cause(s)    the conditions that allowed it, not a person
## Detection        how we found out; how we should have
## What went well
## What went badly
## Where we got lucky
## Action items     owner · due date · ticket — prevent, detect, mitigate
## Lessons          what we now know about the system

Ask "what made this possible?" five times, not "who did it?". Turn each lesson into a guard: a test, an alert, a policy, an automation.

Toil

  • Toil is manual, repetitive, automatable, reactive work that grows with the service and leaves no lasting value: restarting jobs by hand, re-running loads, copying credentials.
  • Cap it (Google aims for under 50% of an SRE's time) and spend the rest on engineering that removes it.
  • Measure it: log toil tasks and their time; automate the biggest first.
  • Automation must be durable: a scheduler and a job in the platform, not a script on someone's laptop or a session polling in a loop.

Production readiness review

  • SLOs defined, measured and on a dashboard.
  • Alerts on SLO burn and on missing runs, each with a runbook; proven to fire.
  • Logs structured, traces flowing, dashboards from the template.
  • Capacity tested at expected peak plus headroom.
  • Deploys automated, with canary or rollback tested.
  • Backups exist and a restore has been tested; RPO and RTO written down.
  • Dependencies listed with their failure behaviour.
  • Security review done; secrets in a manager; least-privilege IAM.
  • On-call rotation and escalation path in place.

Reliability practices

  • Progressive delivery: canaries, feature flags and automatic rollback.
  • Capacity planning: forecast from growth, load-test to find limits, keep headroom.
  • Graceful degradation: serve partial or cached results and say so.
  • Disaster recovery: multi-zone by default; multi-region when the SLO needs it; rehearsed restores.
  • Game days and chaos tests: break things on purpose in a controlled way to test detection and response.
  • Dependency SLOs: your SLO cannot exceed what your critical dependencies provide unless you add redundancy.

SLOs on Google Cloud

  • Cloud Monitoring service monitoring defines services and SLOs (request-based or window-based) and charts budget.
  • Alert policies can use the SLO burn rate directly.
  • Declare SLOs and alerts in Terraform (google_monitoring_slo, google_monitoring_alert_policy) so they are reviewed and versioned.
  • For pipelines, compute freshness and completeness SLIs from run records in BigQuery and export them as custom or log-based metrics.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain SLI, SLO, error budget and SLA
  • Follow a runbook and escalate during an incident
  • Contribute to a blameless postmortem

2 Practitioner

  • Define SLIs and SLOs for a service or pipeline
  • Set up burn-rate alerts and runbooks
  • Take on-call shifts and lead mitigation

3 Senior

  • Act as incident commander; run postmortems and track actions
  • Write and enforce an error budget policy with product owners
  • Run production readiness reviews and capacity plans

4 Expert

  • Set reliability strategy and SRE practice for the organisation
  • Design multi-region resilience and DR programmes
  • Build a culture of blameless learning and toil reduction

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Explore further

Study next

  • Multi-window, multi-burn-rate alerting
  • Incident command system
  • Chaos engineering and game days
  • Capacity planning and load testing
  • Disaster recovery: RPO, RTO and restore drills

  • Implementing SLOs (SRE Workbook) Choosing SLIs and targets

Harness CI/CD

Harness is a software delivery platform: CI, continuous delivery with verification and rollback, GitOps, feature flags, and more, organised as pipelines. This tab walks through Harness CI and Harness CD in depth, an end-to-end pipeline to Google Cloud, troubleshooting, and how to use Harness as a blueprint for every team.

Modules

ModuleDoes
Continuous IntegrationBuilds and tests, with caching and test intelligence (runs only tests affected by a change)
Continuous Delivery & GitOpsDeploys services to Kubernetes, serverless, VMs and more, with strategies, approvals, verification and rollback; Argo CD-based GitOps
Feature FlagsTurn features on per user or percentage, separating deploy from release
Security Testing OrchestrationRuns scanners in pipelines and gathers their results
Cloud Cost ManagementCost visibility, budgets and recommendations
Chaos EngineeringInject failures to test resilience
Infrastructure as Code ManagementRun and govern Terraform/OpenTofu workspaces
Internal Developer PortalBackstage-based service catalogue and templates

Modules and their names change over time; check the current Harness docs for what your account includes.

Building blocks

  • Account → organisation → project: the hierarchy for access control and shared resources.
  • Pipeline: stages run in order or parallel; each stage has steps.
  • Stage types: Build (CI), Deploy (CD), Approval, Custom.
  • Service: what you deploy (manifests, artefact).
  • Environment and infrastructure definition: where you deploy (dev, prod; a cluster or project).
  • Connector: a stored, permissioned link to GitHub, GCP, a registry or a cluster.
  • Delegate: an agent running in your infrastructure that executes tasks, so Harness never needs inbound access.
  • Secrets: stored in Harness or referenced from Google Secret Manager or Vault.
  • Templates: versioned step, stage and pipeline templates teams reuse.
  • Triggers: start pipelines on Git events, artefact pushes or schedules.

Pipeline as code (shape)

pipeline:
  name: legal-api
  identifier: legal_api
  orgIdentifier: default
  projectIdentifier: legal
  stages:
    - stage:
        name: Build
        identifier: build
        type: CI
        spec:
          cloneCodebase: true
          execution:
            steps:
              - step:
                  type: Run
                  name: Test
                  identifier: test
                  spec:
                    shell: Sh
                    command: ./gradlew test
    - stage:
        name: Approve prod
        identifier: approve
        type: Approval
        spec:
          execution:
            steps:
              - step:
                  type: HarnessApproval
                  name: Founder approval
                  identifier: founder
                  timeout: 1d
                  spec:
                    approvers:
                      userGroups: [prod_approvers]
                      minimumCount: 1
    - stage:
        name: Deploy prod
        identifier: deploy_prod
        type: Deployment
        spec:
          # service, environment, infrastructure and strategy
          # (rolling / canary / blue-green) go here
          ...

This shows the structure only. Build pipelines in the Harness editor first and copy its YAML into Git, since exact step specs vary by module and version.

Expressions

<+pipeline.sequenceId>           run number
<+pipeline.executionId>          unique run ID
<+codebase.commitSha>            the commit being built
<+codebase.branch>
<+artifact.tag>                  deployed image tag
<+env.name>                      target environment
<+service.name>
<+secrets.getValue("db_password")>   resolved at runtime, masked in logs
<+pipeline.variables.region>

Deploying safely

  • Strategies: rolling, canary and blue-green built in, with automatic rollback steps.
  • Continuous verification: compares metrics and logs (from Prometheus, Datadog, Cloud Monitoring and others) of the new version against the old, and fails the stage when they degrade.
  • Approvals: Harness user groups, or Jira/ServiceNow tickets.
  • Freeze windows: block deployments during set periods.
  • Policy as code: OPA policies on pipelines (e.g. "prod deploys must have an approval stage and a verification step").
  • Audit trail: who ran what, with which inputs, when.

Harness vs GitHub Actions

GitHub ActionsHarness
StrengthCI next to the code; huge action marketplaceCD: strategies, verification, approvals, governance at scale
Deployment modelYou script it in workflow stepsFirst-class services, environments and strategies
GovernanceEnvironments, rulesets, required reviewersOPA policies, templates, RBAC, freeze windows, audit
FitsSmall and medium teams; code-centric workflowsMany teams and services needing a standard, governed path to production

A common split: GitHub Actions builds and tests on every PR; Harness promotes the built artefact through environments.

End to end: commit to production on Google Cloud

  1. Trigger: a push or merged pull request on GitHub starts the pipeline (webhook trigger on main; PR trigger runs CI only).
  2. CI stage: clone, restore cache, compile and run tests (with test intelligence), run scanners, then build the image once and push it to Artifact Registry tagged with the commit SHA.
  3. Deploy to dev: a CD stage deploys that exact image to the dev environment (GKE or Cloud Run) with a rolling strategy.
  4. Verify dev: smoke tests and continuous verification against dev metrics and logs.
  5. Approval: a named group approves prod (or a policy auto-approves outside freeze windows).
  6. Deploy to prod: canary or blue-green with the same artefact; verification watches error rate and latency; automatic rollback on degradation.
  7. Notify and record: Slack/email notification, deployment recorded with who, what, which commit, and the verification result.

Build once, promote the same artefact. Rebuilding per environment means prod runs something that was never tested.

Harness CI in depth

  • Build infrastructure: Harness Cloud (hosted machines), your Kubernetes cluster (pods per build), your own VMs, or a local runner. Choose by network access, cost and isolation.
  • Codebase: the repo connector and clone settings for the pipeline; each CI stage can clone automatically.
  • Run step: any shell command in a container image you choose (eclipse-temurin:21, python:3.12).
  • Test step with test intelligence: selects only tests affected by the change, and reports results in the UI (JUnit XML).
  • Background step: a service the build needs, such as Postgres or a Kafka broker, running beside the steps.
  • Build and push steps: build an image and push it to Artifact Registry, Docker Hub or another registry.
  • Caching: Cache Intelligence for common build tools, or explicit save/restore cache steps to GCS or S3.
  • Plugins: Drone plugins and many GitHub Actions can run as steps.
  • Outputs: steps export variables for later steps and stages, e.g. the image tag.

A CI stage (shape)

- stage:
    name: Build
    identifier: build
    type: CI
    spec:
      cloneCodebase: true
      platform: { os: Linux, arch: Amd64 }
      runtime: { type: Cloud, spec: {} }       # Harness Cloud machines
      execution:
        steps:
          - step:
              type: Run
              name: Unit tests
              identifier: unit_tests
              spec:
                image: eclipse-temurin:21
                shell: Sh
                command: ./gradlew test
                reports:
                  type: JUnit
                  spec:
                    paths: ["**/build/test-results/test/*.xml"]
          - step:
              type: BuildAndPushGAR
              name: Image
              identifier: image
              spec:
                connectorRef: gcp_bld
                host: asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev
                projectID: my-proj
                imageName: apps/legal-api
                tags: [<+codebase.shortCommitSha>]

Field names follow Harness's YAML at the time of writing; generate the stage in the editor and compare before committing.

Harness CD in depth

  • Service: what you deploy: the artefact source (Artifact Registry image), manifests (Kubernetes YAML, Helm chart, Kustomize) and variables.
  • Environment: dev or prod, marked pre-production or production, with environment-level overrides (replicas, URLs, secrets).
  • Infrastructure definition: the exact target: a GKE cluster and namespace, or a GCP project and region for serverless.
  • Deployment types: Kubernetes, Native Helm, serverless targets, VMs over SSH/WinRM, Tanzu, ECS, and custom deployments for anything else. Check Harness's current list for Google Cloud targets such as Cloud Run and Cloud Functions.
  • Execution: generated from the chosen strategy, editable step by step; failure strategies say what to do on each kind of error (retry, rollback, manual intervention).
  • Rollback: a rollback section per stage, run automatically on failure or on a failed verification.
  • Input sets and overlays: saved sets of runtime inputs (which service, which tag) so one pipeline serves many services.
  • Multi-service and multi-environment deployments from a single stage.

Deployment strategies in Harness

StrategyHow Harness runs itUse when
RollingReplaces pods or instances gradually; rollback restores the previous releaseDefault for stateless services
CanaryDeploys a small canary set, verifies, then rolls out fully and deletes the canaryRisky changes with good metrics
Blue-greenDeploys the new version as a stage set, tests it, then swaps trafficInstant switch and rollback; capacity for two copies
Basic / customYour own steps (shell, HTTP, Terraform, approvals)Targets without a built-in type

Connecting Harness to Google Cloud

  • GCP connector with OIDC (workload identity federation) where your plan supports it, so no service account key is stored. Otherwise inherit credentials from a delegate running in GKE with Workload Identity.
  • One service account per environment with only the roles deploys need (Artifact Registry reader/writer, GKE developer or Cloud Run developer, and service account user on the runtime account).
  • Delegates run inside your network (for example a GKE namespace), so private clusters and services are reachable without opening inbound access. Run at least two for availability.
  • Secrets referenced from Google Secret Manager through a secret manager connector.
  • GitHub connector with a GitHub App for cloning, PR status checks and webhooks.

GitHub Actions → Harness mapping

GitHub ActionsHarness
WorkflowPipeline
JobStage
Step / actionStep / plugin
on: push, pull_requestTriggers
RunnerBuild infrastructure / delegate
Secrets and variablesSecrets, variables, connectors
Environments + required reviewersEnvironments + approval stages + OPA policies
Reusable workflowsTemplates
services: containersBackground steps
matrixLooping strategies (matrix, repeat, parallelism)

Troubleshooting

SymptomCheck
Step queued, never startsNo healthy delegate or build infrastructure matching the selector
Connector test failsDelegate network egress, credentials, or the account's IAM roles
Image pull error in deployCluster or runtime identity lacks Artifact Registry read; wrong tag expression
Deployment times outReadiness probes failing; check pod events and logs in the step output
Verification fails unexpectedlyBaseline too short, wrong metric query, or too few data points; read the analysis detail
Expression resolves to nullWrong scope or step identifier; use the expression picker in the editor
Pipeline in Git differs from UIRemote pipeline branch selection; check which branch the run used

Good practice

  • Keep pipelines, templates and input sets in Git, reviewed like code.
  • One pipeline template per deployment shape; services pass only inputs.
  • Run unit and integration tests in CI on every PR; deploy only from main.
  • Promote by artefact tag or digest; never rebuild per environment.
  • Every prod stage has verification and an automatic rollback.
  • Enforce with OPA: prod needs approval, verification, and an artefact from the trusted registry.
  • Watch delegate health and versions; alert when none are connected.
  • Track DORA metrics from Harness deployment data.

Harness as a blueprint

  • Publish account-level templates for "build a JVM service", "deploy to Cloud Run", "deploy to GKE".
  • Teams reference the template version; a fix to the template reaches every pipeline that uses it.
  • Enforce the template with OPA policies rather than reviews.
  • Keep pipelines in Git (remote pipelines) so changes are reviewed like code.
  • This is the same idea as the AI tab's blueprints card: decide once, reuse everywhere.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Run a pipeline and read its logs
  • Understand stages, steps, services and environments
  • Approve or roll back a deployment

2 Practitioner

  • Build CI and CD pipelines with connectors, secrets and triggers
  • Use canary or blue-green with verification
  • Keep pipelines in Git and use templates

3 Senior

  • Design org-wide templates and OPA policies
  • Set up delegates, RBAC and environments securely
  • Integrate verification with monitoring and feature flags

4 Expert

  • Own the delivery platform strategy and its governance
  • Lead migrations from other CI/CD tools
  • Measure and improve delivery (DORA) across teams

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Terraform on GCP

HCL with the google provider: the CLI, state, the resources a data platform needs, and how to change things safely. Version tags show when a feature arrived in Terraform.

CLI workflow

terraform init                       # providers + backend
terraform init -upgrade              # newer providers within constraints
terraform fmt -recursive
terraform validate
terraform plan -var-file=env/dev.tfvars -out=plan.tfplan
terraform show plan.tfplan           # read it before applying
terraform apply plan.tfplan          # applies exactly what you read
terraform plan -destroy

terraform plan -target=module.landing          # emergency only
terraform apply -replace=google_cloud_run_v2_job.loader
terraform output -json
terraform console                    # try expressions
terraform providers lock -platform=linux_amd64 -platform=darwin_arm64

validate checks syntax and types only. A missing optional module argument or a wrong IAM wiring shows up in a plan, not before.

Providers & backend

terraform {
  required_version = ">= 1.7"
  required_providers {
    google = { source = "hashicorp/google", version = "~> 7.0" }   # pin the major version you have tested
  }
  backend "gcs" {
    bucket = "my-proj-tfstate"      # versioning on!
    prefix = "dataplatform"
  }
}

provider "google" {
  project = var.project_id
  region  = var.region
  default_labels = { managed_by = "terraform", env = var.env }
}

# backend values cannot use variables; pass them at init:
# terraform init -backend-config="bucket=my-proj-dev-tfstate"

Variables, locals, outputs

variable "env" {
  type = string
  validation {
    condition     = contains(["dev", "prod"], var.env)
    error_message = "env must be dev or prod."
  }
}
variable "stages" {
  type = map(object({
    enabled = bool
    args    = optional(list(string), [])
  }))
}
variable "db_password" {
  type      = string
  sensitive = true
}

locals {
  name_prefix = "dp-${var.env}"
  enabled     = { for k, v in var.stages : k => v if v.enabled }
}

output "bucket_url" { value = google_storage_bucket.landing.url }

# env/dev.tfvars
env    = "dev"
stages = { land = { enabled = true, args = ["--max-objects=50"] } }

count, for_each, dynamic

# for_each over a set or map: stable addresses by key (prefer over count)
resource "google_bigquery_dataset" "layer" {
  for_each   = toset(["odp", "fdp", "cdp"])
  dataset_id = "dp_${each.key}"
  location   = var.region
}
# reference: google_bigquery_dataset.layer["fdp"].dataset_id

# count for on/off
resource "google_cloud_scheduler_job" "tick" {
  count = var.clock_enabled ? 1 : 0
  ...
}
# reference: one(google_cloud_scheduler_job.tick[*].id)

# dynamic nested blocks
dynamic "env" {
  for_each = var.env_vars
  content {
    name  = env.key
    value = env.value
  }
}

# list of objects → map for for_each
for_each = { for t in var.tables : t.name => t }

With count, removing an item in the middle renumbers everything after it and Terraform destroys and recreates those resources.

BigQuery dataset & table

resource "google_bigquery_dataset" "fdp" {
  dataset_id                 = "dp_fdp"
  location                   = "asia-south1"
  delete_contents_on_destroy = false
  labels = { layer = "fdp" }
}

resource "google_bigquery_table" "judgment" {
  dataset_id          = google_bigquery_dataset.fdp.dataset_id
  table_id            = "fdp_judgment"
  deletion_protection = true
  require_partition_filter = true

  time_partitioning {
    type  = "MONTH"
    field = "decision_date"
  }
  clustering = ["court_code", "cnr"]

  schema = jsonencode([
    { name = "judgment_key",  type = "STRING", mode = "REQUIRED" },
    { name = "court_code",    type = "STRING" },
    { name = "cnr",           type = "STRING" },
    { name = "decision_date", type = "DATE" },
  ])
}

resource "google_bigquery_dataset_iam_member" "api_reader" {
  dataset_id = google_bigquery_dataset.fdp.dataset_id
  role       = "roles/bigquery.dataViewer"
  member     = "serviceAccount:${google_service_account.api.email}"
}

Tables that dbt builds should not also be declared here; two owners fight over the schema. Declare datasets and IAM, let dbt own its tables.

GCS bucket

resource "google_storage_bucket" "landing" {
  name                        = "${var.project_id}-landing"
  location                    = "ASIA-SOUTH1"
  storage_class               = "STANDARD"
  uniform_bucket_level_access = true
  public_access_prevention    = "enforced"
  force_destroy               = false

  versioning { enabled = true }

  lifecycle_rule {
    condition {
      age            = 90
      matches_prefix = ["tmp/"]
    }
    action { type = "Delete" }
  }
  lifecycle_rule {
    condition { age = 365 }
    action {
      type          = "SetStorageClass"
      storage_class = "COLDLINE"
    }
  }
}

Cloud Run job + Cloud Scheduler

resource "google_cloud_run_v2_job" "loader" {
  name     = "${local.name_prefix}-loader"
  location = var.region
  deletion_protection = false

  template {
    task_count = 1
    template {
      service_account = google_service_account.loader.email
      timeout         = "3600s"
      max_retries     = 0
      containers {
        image = var.loader_image          # pass a tag or digest, never :latest
        args  = ["--year=2024"]
        resources { limits = { cpu = "1", memory = "2Gi" } }
        env {
          name = "DB_PASSWORD"
          value_source {
            secret_key_ref {
              secret  = google_secret_manager_secret.db.secret_id
              version = "latest"
            }
          }
        }
      }
    }
  }
}

resource "google_cloud_scheduler_job" "daily" {
  name      = "${local.name_prefix}-daily"
  region    = var.region
  schedule  = "15 6 * * *"
  time_zone = "Etc/UTC"
  paused    = var.clock_paused
  http_target {
    http_method = "POST"
    uri = "https://run.googleapis.com/v2/projects/${var.project_id}/locations/${var.region}/jobs/${google_cloud_run_v2_job.loader.name}:run"
    oauth_token {
      service_account_email = google_service_account.scheduler.email
      scope                 = "https://www.googleapis.com/auth/cloud-platform"
    }
  }
}

The scheduler's service account needs roles/run.invoker (or run.developer for overrides) on the job.

IAM: member, binding, policy

# _member: adds ONE principal to ONE role. Safe, composable. Use this.
resource "google_project_iam_member" "loader_bq" {
  project = var.project_id
  role    = "roles/bigquery.jobUser"
  member  = "serviceAccount:${google_service_account.loader.email}"
}

# _binding: OWNS the member list for one role (removes anyone else)
# _policy:  OWNS the whole policy (removes everything else, can lock you out)

resource "google_service_account" "loader" {
  account_id   = "dp-loader"
  display_name = "Corpus loader job"
}

# Workload Identity Federation for GitHub Actions: no JSON keys
resource "google_service_account_iam_member" "gh_deploy" {
  service_account_id = google_service_account.deploy.name
  role               = "roles/iam.workloadIdentityUser"
  member = "principalSet://iam.googleapis.com/${google_iam_workload_identity_pool.gh.name}/attribute.repository/org/repo"
}

Never mix _policy or _binding with _member for the same role: they overwrite each other on every apply.

Modules

modules/pipeline-job/
  main.tf  variables.tf  outputs.tf  versions.tf

module "orchestrator" {
  source        = "./modules/pipeline-job"
  name          = "orchestrator"
  image         = var.orchestrator_image
  service_account_email = google_service_account.orch.email
  args          = ["--tick"]
}

module "jobs" {                         # for_each on modules (0.13+)
  source   = "./modules/pipeline-job"
  for_each = local.enabled
  name     = each.key
  args     = each.value.args
}

output "job_names" { value = { for k, m in module.jobs : k => m.name } }

# Registry / git sources: pin a version
source  = "terraform-google-modules/cloud-storage/google"
version = "~> 9.0"
source  = "git::https://github.com/org/tf-modules.git//bq?ref=v1.4.0"

Refactor without destroying

# Bring an existing resource under management (1.5)
import {
  to = google_storage_bucket.landing
  id = "my-proj-landing"
}
# terraform plan -generate-config-out=generated.tf

# Rename / move into a module (1.1)
moved {
  from = google_cloud_run_v2_job.loader
  to   = module.jobs["land"].google_cloud_run_v2_job.this
}

# Stop managing, keep the real resource (1.7)
removed {
  from = google_bigquery_table.legacy
  lifecycle { destroy = false }
}

# CLI equivalents (no review step: prefer the blocks above)
terraform state list
terraform state show google_storage_bucket.landing
terraform state mv  'a.b' 'module.x.a.b'
terraform state rm  google_bigquery_table.legacy

lifecycle, checks, preconditions

resource "google_sql_database_instance" "corpus" {
  ...
  lifecycle {
    prevent_destroy       = true           # plan fails instead of destroying
    ignore_changes        = [settings[0].disk_size]   # autoresize owns it
    create_before_destroy = false
    precondition {
      condition     = var.env != "prod" || var.tier != "db-f1-micro"
      error_message = "prod needs a real tier."
    }
  }
}

# replace when something else changes (1.2)
lifecycle { replace_triggered_by = [terraform_data.image_rev] }

# check block: warns on every plan, never blocks (1.5)
check "landing_bucket_reachable" {
  data "google_storage_bucket" "l" { name = google_storage_bucket.landing.name }
  assert {
    condition     = data.google_storage_bucket.l.versioning[0].enabled
    error_message = "Landing bucket versioning is off."
  }
}

Functions & expressions

"${var.env}-landing"                  format("dp-%s-%03d", var.env, 7)
join(",", var.courts)                 split(",", "a,b,c")
merge(local.base_labels, var.labels)  concat(list_a, list_b)
lookup(var.tiers, var.env, "small")   try(var.cfg.timeout, "600s")
coalesce(var.override, "default")     one(resource.x[*].id)
flatten([for d in var.datasets : [for t in d.tables : "${d.name}.${t}"]])
toset(list)  keys(map)  values(map)   zipmap(keys, vals)
setproduct(var.envs, var.regions)     contains(list, "x")
can(regex("^[a-z]+$", var.name))
jsonencode(obj)  yamldecode(file("${path.module}/rows.yaml"))
templatefile("${path.module}/startup.sh.tftpl", { bucket = local.bucket })
var.enabled ? "a" : "b"
[for s in var.stages : upper(s) if s != "skip"]
{ for k, v in var.stages : k => v.args }
timestamp()   # changes every plan: avoid in resource arguments

terraform test (1.6)

# tests/stages.tftest.hcl
variables {
  env    = "dev"
  stages = { land = { enabled = true } }
}

run "bld_window_is_small" {
  command = plan
  assert {
    condition     = length(local.enabled) <= 4
    error_message = "dev enables too many stages."
  }
}

run "rejects_unknown_env" {
  command   = plan
  variables { env = "staging" }
  expect_failures = [var.env]
}

# terraform test        (mock_provider "google" {} keeps it offline, 1.7+)

Safety checklist

  • Read every plan line that says destroy or -/+ (replace). A replaced database or bucket loses its data.
  • State bucket: versioning on, uniform access, only the deploy identity can write. State holds secrets in plain text.
  • One state per environment (separate prefix or project). Workspaces suit identical copies, not dev vs prod with different shapes.
  • Pin providers with ~> and commit .terraform.lock.hcl.
  • Images by tag or digest from a variable, never :latest, so a plan shows when the image moves.
  • No secrets in tfvars. Reference Secret Manager; mark variables sensitive.
  • Apply from CI with Workload Identity Federation, from a saved plan file.
  • Config is not state: after an apply, read the live resource to confirm it.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Run init, plan and apply; read a plan
  • Change a resource's arguments safely
  • Use variables and outputs

2 Practitioner

  • Write reusable modules with validation
  • Manage state in a remote backend; use moved and import blocks
  • Run Terraform from CI with keyless auth

3 Senior

  • Design module and state layout for many environments
  • Refactor live infrastructure without destroying it
  • Write terraform tests and policy checks

4 Expert

  • Own infrastructure-as-code standards for the organisation
  • Design org-wide landing zones and guardrails
  • Lead provider upgrades and large migrations

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Kubernetes

Core objects, the kubectl you will use daily, manifests that hold up in production, and GKE specifics.

Core objects

KindUse it for
PodOne or more containers sharing network and volumes. Rarely created directly.
DeploymentStateless apps: keeps N replicas, rolling updates, rollback.
StatefulSetStable names and per-pod disks: databases, Kafka, ZooKeeper.
DaemonSetOne pod per node: log shippers, node agents.
Job / CronJobRun to completion, once or on a schedule: batch loads, migrations.
ServiceStable virtual IP and DNS name in front of matching pods.
Ingress / GatewayHTTP routing from outside into Services (Gateway API is the newer model).
ConfigMap / SecretConfiguration and credentials, as env vars or files.
PersistentVolumeClaimA request for durable storage.
ServiceAccountIdentity for pods (and, on GKE, the link to a Google service account).
HorizontalPodAutoscalerScale replicas on CPU, memory or custom metrics.
NetworkPolicyPod-level firewall rules.
NamespaceA scope for names, quotas and RBAC.

kubectl daily

kubectl config get-contexts
kubectl config use-context gke_my-proj_asia-south1_main
kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=corpus

kubectl get pods -o wide
kubectl get deploy,svc,ing
kubectl get all -A                         # every namespace
kubectl describe pod api-7d9f-abcde        # events at the bottom
kubectl logs deploy/api -f --since=10m
kubectl logs api-7d9f-abcde -c sidecar --previous   # crashed container
kubectl exec -it deploy/api -- sh
kubectl port-forward svc/api 8080:80
kubectl top pods                           # needs metrics-server

kubectl apply -f k8s/ --dry-run=server     # validate against the cluster
kubectl diff -f k8s/
kubectl apply -f k8s/
kubectl rollout status deploy/api
kubectl rollout undo deploy/api
kubectl rollout restart deploy/api         # new pods re-read env from Secrets/ConfigMaps (re-pull only with imagePullPolicy: Always)
kubectl scale deploy/api --replicas=3
kubectl get events --sort-by=.lastTimestamp
kubectl explain deployment.spec.strategy   # built-in field docs

Deployment + Service

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata: { name: api, labels: { app: api } }
spec:
  replicas: 2
  selector: { matchLabels: { app: api } }
  strategy: { rollingUpdate: { maxUnavailable: 0, maxSurge: 1 } }
  template:
    metadata: { labels: { app: api } }
    spec:
      serviceAccountName: api
      containers:
        - name: api
          image: asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev/my-proj/apps/api:3f2a9c1
          ports: [{ containerPort: 8080 }]
          envFrom: [{ configMapRef: { name: api-config } }]
          env:
            - name: DB_PASSWORD
              valueFrom: { secretKeyRef: { name: db, key: password } }
          resources:
            requests: { cpu: 250m, memory: 512Mi }
            limits:   { memory: 512Mi }
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet: { path: /actuator/health/readiness, port: 8080 }
            periodSeconds: 5
          livenessProbe:
            httpGet: { path: /actuator/health/liveness, port: 8080 }
            initialDelaySeconds: 20
          startupProbe:
            httpGet: { path: /actuator/health/liveness, port: 8080 }
            failureThreshold: 30
            periodSeconds: 2
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata: { name: api }
spec:
  selector: { app: api }
  ports: [{ port: 80, targetPort: 8080 }]

Job & CronJob

apiVersion: batch/v1
kind: CronJob
metadata: { name: corpus-load }
spec:
  schedule: "15 6 * * *"
  timeZone: "Etc/UTC"
  concurrencyPolicy: Forbid          # never two runs at once
  startingDeadlineSeconds: 600
  successfulJobsHistoryLimit: 3
  failedJobsHistoryLimit: 5
  jobTemplate:
    spec:
      backoffLimit: 0                # no automatic retries
      activeDeadlineSeconds: 3600
      ttlSecondsAfterFinished: 86400
      template:
        spec:
          restartPolicy: Never
          serviceAccountName: loader
          containers:
            - name: loader
              image: asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev/my-proj/apps/loader:3f2a9c1
              args: ["--year=2024"]
              resources: { requests: { cpu: "1", memory: 2Gi }, limits: { memory: 2Gi } }
kubectl create job --from=cronjob/corpus-load manual-20250131   # run now
kubectl get jobs; kubectl logs job/manual-20250131

Services & traffic

  • ClusterIP (default): reachable inside the cluster only.
  • NodePort: a port on every node (30000–32767). Mostly a building block.
  • LoadBalancer: provisions a cloud load balancer with an external (or internal, by annotation) IP.
  • Headless (clusterIP: None): DNS returns pod IPs directly; used with StatefulSets.
  • DNS name: <svc>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local; within the same namespace just <svc>.
  • A Service with no endpoints means its selector matches no ready pod: check labels and readiness.
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata: { name: api }
spec:
  ingressClassName: nginx          # GKE's own controller uses the annotation kubernetes.io/ingress.class: gce instead
  rules:
    - host: api.example.com
      http:
        paths:
          - path: /
            pathType: Prefix
            backend: { service: { name: api, port: { number: 80 } } }

kubectl get endpointslices -l kubernetes.io/service-name=api

Requests, limits, probes

  • Requests decide scheduling: the node must have that much free. Limits cap use.
  • Over the memory limit → the container is killed (OOMKilled, exit 137). Over the CPU limit → throttled, not killed.
  • Set memory request = limit for steady apps. Many teams leave the CPU limit off to avoid throttling.
  • JVM: size heap from the container with -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75.
  • Readiness failing removes the pod from Service traffic. Liveness failing restarts it. Startup holds the other two off until the app has booted.
  • Never make liveness depend on a database or another service: an outage then restarts every pod.
  • QoS: Guaranteed (requests = limits for all), Burstable, BestEffort (none set; evicted first).

Debug by status

StatusUsually means
PendingNo node fits the requests, a PVC is unbound, or a node selector/taint excludes all nodes. describe pod → Events.
ImagePullBackOffWrong image name or tag, or the node's identity cannot read the registry.
CrashLoopBackOffThe app starts and exits. logs --previous shows why: bad config, missing secret, failed probe.
OOMKilledMemory limit too low, or a leak. Check describe → Last State.
CreateContainerConfigErrorA referenced ConfigMap or Secret key does not exist.
Running, not ReadyReadiness probe failing: wrong path or port, or a dependency is down.
EvictedNode ran short of memory or disk.
kubectl debug -it api-7d9f-abcde --image=busybox:1.36 --target=api   # ephemeral debug container
kubectl run tmp --rm -it --image=nicolaka/netshoot -- bash          # network toolbox pod

Config, secrets, scaling, safety

kubectl create configmap api-config --from-literal=DATASET=fdp --from-file=app.yml
kubectl create secret generic db --from-literal=password='...'   # base64, NOT encrypted
kubectl get secret db -o jsonpath='{.data.password}' | base64 -d

# HPA
kubectl autoscale deploy api --cpu-percent=70 --min=2 --max=10

# PodDisruptionBudget: keep capacity during node upgrades
apiVersion: policy/v1
kind: PodDisruptionBudget
metadata: { name: api }
spec:
  minAvailable: 1
  selector: { matchLabels: { app: api } }

# NetworkPolicy: default deny ingress in the namespace
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata: { name: default-deny-ingress }
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes: [Ingress]

For real secrets use Secret Manager via the CSI driver or External Secrets, not values committed in YAML.

RBAC & GKE Workload Identity

# Pod's Kubernetes SA → Google service account (no JSON keys)
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: loader
  namespace: corpus
  annotations:
    iam.gke.io/gcp-service-account: dp-loader@my-proj.iam.gserviceaccount.com

gcloud iam service-accounts add-iam-policy-binding \
  dp-loader@my-proj.iam.gserviceaccount.com \
  --role=roles/iam.workloadIdentityUser \
  --member="serviceAccount:my-proj.svc.id.goog[corpus/loader]"

# RBAC: read-only on pods in one namespace
kubectl create role pod-reader --verb=get,list,watch --resource=pods -n corpus
kubectl create rolebinding jo-reads --role=pod-reader --user=jo@example.com -n corpus
kubectl auth can-i delete pods -n corpus --as=jo@example.com

GKE can also grant IAM roles straight to the Kubernetes principal (principal://iam.googleapis.com/projects/<number>/locations/global/workloadIdentityPools/<proj>.svc.id.goog/subject/ns/<ns>/sa/<ksa>) without a Google service account.

GKE, Helm, Kustomize

gcloud container clusters create-auto main --region=asia-south1   # Autopilot
gcloud container clusters get-credentials main --region=asia-south1
gcloud container clusters list

# Helm: packaged apps with values
helm repo add bitnami https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami
helm install redis bitnami/redis -n cache --create-namespace -f values.yaml
helm upgrade --install api ./chart --set image.tag=3f2a9c1
helm template ./chart | kubectl diff -f -
helm rollback api 1

# Kustomize: one base, overlays per env (built into kubectl)
k8s/base/kustomization.yaml       resources: [deploy.yaml, svc.yaml]
k8s/overlays/prod/kustomization.yaml
  resources: [../../base]
  images: [{ name: api, newTag: 3f2a9c1 }]
  patches: [{ path: replicas.yaml }]
kubectl apply -k k8s/overlays/prod

Autopilot: Google manages nodes and you pay per pod request. Standard: you manage node pools. For one-shot batch work, Cloud Run jobs are often simpler than a cluster.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Use kubectl to get, describe, log and exec
  • Read a Deployment and Service manifest
  • Explain why a pod is Pending or crashing

2 Practitioner

  • Write manifests with probes, resources and config
  • Use Helm or Kustomize per environment
  • Set up Workload Identity and RBAC

3 Senior

  • Design namespaces, network policies and autoscaling
  • Run production clusters: upgrades, PDBs, capacity
  • Debug networking, DNS and scheduling problems

4 Expert

  • Design multi-cluster platforms and tenancy models
  • Build operators and platform abstractions
  • Set Kubernetes strategy and security policy

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Helm charts

Helm packages Kubernetes manifests as charts: templates plus default values, versioned and installed as releases you can upgrade and roll back. Chart structure, templating, values per environment, hooks, testing, publishing to a registry, and how Helm fits with GitOps and Kustomize. Basics are on Kubernetes.

Concepts

TermMeaning
ChartA package: templates, default values and metadata
ValuesInputs that fill the templates; defaults in values.yaml, overridden per environment
ReleaseOne installed instance of a chart in a namespace, with a name
RevisionEach install, upgrade or rollback of a release; the history Helm can roll back to
Repository / registryWhere charts are published: an HTTP chart repo or an OCI registry (e.g. Artifact Registry)
Chart version vs app versionversion changes when the chart changes; appVersion names the application it deploys

Chart structure

legal-api/
  Chart.yaml            # name, versions, dependencies
  values.yaml           # defaults
  values.schema.json    # optional: validate values
  templates/
    _helpers.tpl        # named templates (labels, names)
    deployment.yaml
    service.yaml
    ingress.yaml
    configmap.yaml
    hpa.yaml
    NOTES.txt           # printed after install
    tests/
      test-connection.yaml
  charts/               # dependency charts (vendored)
# Chart.yaml
apiVersion: v2
name: legal-api
description: Legal search API
type: application          # or "library" for shared templates only
version: 0.3.0             # chart version (SemVer)
appVersion: "3f2a9c1"      # image tag it deploys by default
dependencies:
  - name: common
    version: "1.x.x"
    repository: oci://asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev/my-proj/charts

Commands

helm create legal-api                         # scaffold
helm lint ./legal-api
helm template legal ./legal-api -f values-prod.yaml    # render locally, no cluster
helm upgrade --install legal ./legal-api \
  -n legal --create-namespace \
  -f values-prod.yaml --set image.tag=3f2a9c1 \
  --atomic --timeout 5m                       # roll back automatically on failure
# Helm 4 renamed --atomic to --rollback-on-failure; check `helm version`
helm list -n legal
helm history legal -n legal
helm rollback legal 4 -n legal
helm get values legal -n legal                # what was applied
helm get manifest legal -n legal
helm uninstall legal -n legal

helm dependency update ./legal-api
helm package ./legal-api                      # → legal-api-0.3.0.tgz
helm push legal-api-0.3.0.tgz oci://asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev/my-proj/charts
helm install legal oci://asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev/my-proj/charts/legal-api --version 0.3.0

Templates

# templates/_helpers.tpl
{{- define "legal-api.fullname" -}}
{{- printf "%s-%s" .Release.Name .Chart.Name | trunc 63 | trimSuffix "-" -}}
{{- end -}}

{{- define "legal-api.selectorLabels" -}}
app.kubernetes.io/name: {{ .Chart.Name }}
app.kubernetes.io/instance: {{ .Release.Name }}
{{- end -}}
# templates/deployment.yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: {{ include "legal-api.fullname" . }}
  labels:
    {{- include "legal-api.selectorLabels" . | nindent 4 }}
    app.kubernetes.io/version: {{ .Values.image.tag | default .Chart.AppVersion | quote }}
spec:
  replicas: {{ .Values.replicaCount }}
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      {{- include "legal-api.selectorLabels" . | nindent 6 }}
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        {{- include "legal-api.selectorLabels" . | nindent 8 }}
      annotations:
        checksum/config: {{ include (print $.Template.BasePath "/configmap.yaml") . | sha256sum }}
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: app
          image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}:{{ .Values.image.tag | default .Chart.AppVersion }}"
          resources:
            {{- toYaml .Values.resources | nindent 12 }}
          {{- with .Values.env }}
          env:
            {{- toYaml . | nindent 12 }}
          {{- end }}
          envFrom:
            - secretRef:
                name: {{ required "secretName is required" .Values.secretName }}

The checksum/config annotation restarts pods when the ConfigMap changes. required fails the render with a clear message instead of deploying something broken.

Values per environment

# values.yaml (defaults: safe, small)
replicaCount: 1
image:
  repository: asia-south1-docker.pkg.dev/my-proj/apps/legal-api
  tag: ""                       # defaults to appVersion
resources:
  requests: { cpu: 250m, memory: 512Mi }
  limits:   { memory: 512Mi }
env: []
secretName: ""

# values-prod.yaml (only what differs)
replicaCount: 3
resources:
  requests: { cpu: "1", memory: 1Gi }
  limits:   { memory: 1Gi }
secretName: legal-api-secrets
  • Later -f files override earlier ones; --set overrides files. Prefer files in Git over --set.
  • Add values.schema.json so a typo in a value fails at install, not at runtime.
  • Never put secret values in values files; reference Secrets created by External Secrets or the Secret Manager CSI driver.

Hooks and tests

# templates/migrate-job.yaml: run DB migrations before an upgrade
apiVersion: batch/v1
kind: Job
metadata:
  name: {{ include "legal-api.fullname" . }}-migrate
  annotations:
    "helm.sh/hook": pre-install,pre-upgrade
    "helm.sh/hook-weight": "0"
    "helm.sh/hook-delete-policy": before-hook-creation,hook-succeeded
spec:
  backoffLimit: 0
  template:
    spec:
      restartPolicy: Never
      containers:
        - name: migrate
          image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}:{{ .Values.image.tag | default .Chart.AppVersion }}"
          args: ["migrate"]
  • Hook events: pre-install, post-install, pre-upgrade, post-upgrade, pre-rollback, pre-delete and more.
  • Migrations in a hook must be backward compatible: a rollback does not undo them.
  • helm test legal runs pods annotated "helm.sh/hook": test to smoke-test a release.
  • Unit-test templates with the helm-unittest plugin; lint and install-test charts in CI with chart-testing (ct).

Helm, Kustomize and GitOps

HelmKustomize
ApproachTemplates + valuesPlain YAML + patches per overlay
Packaging and versionsYes: charts, releases, rollbackNo: just manifests
Best forDistributing reusable apps; many similar servicesYour own manifests with small per-environment differences
RiskTemplate logic becomes hard to readMany overlays become hard to follow
  • GitOps (Argo CD, Flux): the desired chart version and values live in Git; a controller in the cluster applies them and reports drift. No one runs helm upgrade by hand in production.
  • Run helm diff upgrade (plugin) or the GitOps diff before every production change.

Chart best practices

  • Selector labels never change after the first release: Kubernetes forbids changing a Deployment's selector.
  • Standard labels (app.kubernetes.io/name, instance, version, managed-by).
  • Sensible defaults: resources, probes, security context (non-root, read-only root filesystem) on by default.
  • Keep templates boring: little logic; move complexity into values, not nested conditionals.
  • A library chart of shared helpers is your blueprint: every service chart depends on it, so a fix reaches all of them.
  • Pin dependency versions; publish charts to an OCI registry with immutable versions.
  • Document values in values.yaml comments and the schema.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Install, upgrade, roll back and inspect a release
  • Read a chart's values and override them per environment
  • Render a chart locally with helm template

2 Practitioner

  • Write charts with helpers, schema-validated values and sane defaults
  • Use hooks for migrations and helm test for smoke tests
  • Publish charts to an OCI registry

3 Senior

  • Design library charts as a shared blueprint for many services
  • Deliver charts with GitOps (Argo CD or Flux) and diffs
  • Choose between Helm and Kustomize per case

4 Expert

  • Own the organisation's deployment packaging standards
  • Run chart supply chain: signing, provenance, registries
  • Lead migrations between delivery models

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

  • Library charts

  • Argo CD ApplicationSets

Kubernetes operators

An operator extends Kubernetes with your own resource types and a controller that manages them, encoding the knowledge a human operator would apply: how to install, upgrade, back up, fail over and heal a system. The reconcile pattern, CRDs, building operators in Go, Java and Python, and the well-known operators worth using rather than writing.

The pattern

  1. A Custom Resource Definition (CRD) adds a new resource type to the Kubernetes API, such as PostgresCluster or PipelineRun.
  2. Users create custom resources stating the desired state (spec): "three Postgres replicas, version 16, nightly backups".
  3. A controller watches those resources and the objects it owns, and reconciles: observe the actual state, compare it with the spec, act to close the gap, and record what it saw in status.
  4. Reconcile runs again on every change and periodically, so the system heals itself when reality drifts.

Controllers are level-triggered: they act on the current state, not on the event that woke them. That makes them robust to missed events and restarts, as long as every reconcile is idempotent.

Operator or Helm chart?

Helm chartOperator
DoesInstalls and upgrades a set of manifestsContinuously manages a system through its whole life
Day-2 operationsNone after applyBackups, failover, scaling, upgrades, repair
FitsStateless services, most applicationsStateful systems (databases, Kafka), complex platforms
CostTemplatesA program to build, test, secure and maintain

Most teams should use well-tested operators (often installed with Helm), not write their own. Write one only when you have real operational knowledge to automate and no existing operator does it.

Operators worth knowing

  • cert-manager: issues and renews TLS certificates.
  • Prometheus Operator: Prometheus, Alertmanager and scrape targets as resources (ServiceMonitor, PrometheusRule).
  • CloudNativePG: PostgreSQL clusters with replication, failover and backups.
  • Strimzi: Kafka clusters, topics and users.
  • Spark operator: Spark applications as resources.
  • Argo CD / Flux: GitOps controllers.
  • External Secrets Operator: syncs secrets from Secret Manager into Kubernetes.
  • Config Connector / Crossplane: manage cloud resources (GCS buckets, Cloud SQL) as Kubernetes objects.

A Custom Resource Definition

apiVersion: apiextensions.k8s.io/v1
kind: CustomResourceDefinition
metadata:
  name: pipelineruns.data.example.com
spec:
  group: data.example.com
  scope: Namespaced
  names:
    plural: pipelineruns
    singular: pipelinerun
    kind: PipelineRun
    shortNames: [plr]
  versions:
    - name: v1alpha1
      served: true
      storage: true
      subresources:
        status: {}
      additionalPrinterColumns:
        - { name: Date,  type: string, jsonPath: .spec.runDate }
        - { name: Phase, type: string, jsonPath: .status.phase }
      schema:
        openAPIV3Schema:
          type: object
          properties:
            spec:
              type: object
              required: [runDate, stages]
              properties:
                runDate: { type: string, format: date }
                stages:
                  type: array
                  items: { type: string, enum: [LAND, ODP_LOAD, BUILD] }
            status:
              type: object
              x-kubernetes-preserve-unknown-fields: true
apiVersion: data.example.com/v1alpha1
kind: PipelineRun
metadata: { name: run-2026-11-12, namespace: data }
spec:
  runDate: "2026-11-12"
  stages: [LAND, ODP_LOAD, BUILD]

# kubectl get plr -n data

Go: kubebuilder and controller-runtime

kubebuilder init --domain example.com --repo github.com/example/pipeline-operator
kubebuilder create api --group data --version v1alpha1 --kind PipelineRun
make manifests      # generate CRDs and RBAC from code markers
make install run    # install CRDs, run the controller locally
func (r *PipelineRunReconciler) Reconcile(ctx context.Context, req ctrl.Request) (ctrl.Result, error) {
    var run datav1alpha1.PipelineRun
    if err := r.Get(ctx, req.NamespacedName, &run); err != nil {
        return ctrl.Result{}, client.IgnoreNotFound(err)   // deleted: nothing to do
    }

    // 1. observe: the Jobs this run owns
    // 2. compare: which stage should run next, given spec and finished Jobs?
    // 3. act: create that stage's Job with a deterministic name
    //    (creating it twice returns AlreadyExists: idempotent)
    //    and set an owner reference so it is garbage-collected with the run
    // 4. record: update status.phase and conditions via the status subresource

    return ctrl.Result{RequeueAfter: time.Minute}, nil
}

Java and Python

// Java Operator SDK
@ControllerConfiguration
public class PipelineRunReconciler implements Reconciler<PipelineRun> {
  @Override
  public UpdateControl<PipelineRun> reconcile(PipelineRun run, Context<PipelineRun> ctx) {
    // observe owned Jobs, create the next stage's Job if missing
    run.setStatus(computeStatus(run, ctx));
    return UpdateControl.patchStatus(run);
  }
}
// Quarkus has an extension for the Java Operator SDK
# Python: Kopf
import kopf

@kopf.on.create("data.example.com", "v1alpha1", "pipelineruns")
@kopf.on.update("data.example.com", "v1alpha1", "pipelineruns")
def reconcile(spec, name, namespace, status, logger, **_):
    next_stage = choose_next_stage(spec["stages"], status)
    if next_stage:
        ensure_job(name, namespace, next_stage)    # idempotent create
    return {"phase": "Running" if next_stage else "Succeeded"}   # Kopf stores it under status.reconcile

Rules for a good controller

  • Idempotent reconcile: running it ten times gives the same result as once.
  • No in-memory state: everything needed is in the cluster (spec, status, owned objects), so restarts are safe.
  • Status and conditions: report Ready, Progressing, Degraded with reasons and the observed generation.
  • Owner references so owned objects are cleaned up automatically; finalizers to clean up things outside the cluster (cloud resources) before deletion.
  • Requeue with backoff on errors; never tight-loop.
  • Leader election so only one replica acts at a time.
  • Least-privilege RBAC, generated from the code.
  • Validate early: OpenAPI schema and CEL rules (x-kubernetes-validations) reject bad specs at the API.
  • Distinguish unknown from failed: if the controller cannot read a dependency, report that, rather than marking the resource failed or healthy.

Testing and operating operators

  • Unit-test the decision logic (spec + observed state → actions) without a cluster.
  • envtest (Go) runs a real API server for integration tests; kind or k3d clusters for end-to-end tests.
  • CRD versioning: start at v1alpha1; add conversion webhooks when you introduce new versions; never break stored objects.
  • Metrics: reconcile counts, errors, duration and queue depth (controller-runtime exposes them).
  • Capability levels (Operator Framework): basic install → seamless upgrades → full lifecycle → deep insights → autopilot. Aim for the level your users need.
  • Distribution: package the operator with a Helm chart or OLM bundle.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain CRDs, custom resources, controllers and reconcile
  • Use existing operators (cert-manager, CloudNativePG, Strimzi)
  • Read a resource's status and conditions

2 Practitioner

  • Write a CRD with schema validation
  • Build a simple controller with kubebuilder, Java Operator SDK or Kopf
  • Use owner references, finalizers and status correctly

3 Senior

  • Design operators for stateful systems and day-2 operations
  • Test with envtest and real clusters; version CRDs with conversion
  • Decide operator vs Helm vs managed service

4 Expert

  • Build platform APIs on Kubernetes for the organisation
  • Maintain operators at high capability levels
  • Contribute to the operator ecosystem

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Service mesh and Istio

A service mesh moves service-to-service concerns (encryption, identity, retries, traffic splitting, telemetry, access policy) out of application code and into the platform. What a mesh does, when it is worth it, how Istio works in sidecar and ambient modes, the resources you will write, and how to debug it. Background on Microservices and Kubernetes.

What a mesh gives you

CapabilityWhat it meansWithout a mesh
Mutual TLSEvery call between services encrypted and authenticated with workload certificates, rotated automaticallyTLS and certificates managed in each service
Workload identity and authorisationPolicies such as "only the frontend's service account may call GET /api/*"Network policies (IP-based) and code checks
Traffic managementCanary weights, header-based routing, mirroring, timeouts, retriesLoad balancer rules or code
ResilienceCircuit breaking, outlier detection, connection limitsLibraries such as Resilience4j in each service
ObservabilityUniform request metrics, access logs and trace spans for every hopInstrumentation in every service
Egress controlOnly allow-listed external hosts reachableFirewall and proxy rules

North-south traffic (clients into the platform) is the gateway's job; east-west traffic (services to each other) is the mesh's. Business logic belongs in neither.

When it is worth it

Good reasons

  • Many services on Kubernetes, often in several languages.
  • A requirement for encrypted, authenticated traffic between all services (zero trust, compliance).
  • Frequent canary or progressive releases.
  • Uniform telemetry without changing every service.

Reasons to wait

  • A handful of services, or a modular monolith.
  • Serverless platforms such as Cloud Run, which already give service identity (IAM) and TLS.
  • No one to own and upgrade the mesh: it is a critical system in every request path.

Istio architecture

  • Control plane, istiod: turns your configuration into proxy configuration, issues workload certificates, and pushes both to the data plane.
  • Data plane, sidecar mode: an Envoy proxy injected into every pod intercepts its inbound and outbound traffic. Full L7 features per pod, at the cost of a proxy per pod.
  • Data plane, ambient mode: a per-node ztunnel provides L4 mTLS and identity for all pods without sidecars; optional waypoint proxies add L7 features (routing, L7 authorisation) only where needed. Lower overhead and no pod restarts to join.
  • Gateways: Envoy deployments at the edge for ingress and egress, configured with the Kubernetes Gateway API or Istio's own Gateway resource.

On Google Cloud, Cloud Service Mesh offers a managed, Istio-based mesh for GKE and other environments.

Install and enrol workloads

# sidecar mode
istioctl install --set profile=default -y
kubectl label namespace legal istio-injection=enabled
kubectl rollout restart deploy -n legal        # pods get sidecars on restart

# ambient mode
istioctl install --set profile=ambient -y
kubectl label namespace legal istio.io/dataplane-mode=ambient   # no restart needed
istioctl waypoint apply -n legal --enroll-namespace             # add L7 where needed

istioctl analyze -n legal                      # check configuration for problems

In production, install and upgrade Istio through Helm charts or your platform's managed offering, from Git, with canary upgrades of the control plane (revisions).

The resources you will write

ResourcePurpose
Gateway + HTTPRoute (Kubernetes Gateway API)Ingress and routing; the recommended API for new setups
VirtualServiceIstio routing rules: matches, weights, retries, timeouts, fault injection
DestinationRulePolicies for a destination: subsets (versions), load balancing, connection pools, outlier detection, TLS
PeerAuthenticationmTLS mode for workloads: STRICT or PERMISSIVE
AuthorizationPolicyWho may call what: by service account, namespace, path, method, JWT claims
RequestAuthenticationValidate end-user JWTs at the proxy
ServiceEntryRegister external services so they can be reached and controlled
SidecarLimit which services a sidecar knows about, to reduce memory and config size
TelemetryTune metrics, access logs and tracing

Security: mTLS and authorisation

# mesh-wide strict mTLS (after a PERMISSIVE migration period)
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1
kind: PeerAuthentication
metadata: { name: default, namespace: istio-system }
spec:
  mtls: { mode: STRICT }
---
# only the frontend may call the API, and only GET on /api/*
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1
kind: AuthorizationPolicy
metadata: { name: legal-api-allow, namespace: legal }
spec:
  selector: { matchLabels: { app: legal-api } }
  action: ALLOW
  rules:
    - from:
        - source: { principals: ["cluster.local/ns/web/sa/frontend"] }
      to:
        - operation: { methods: ["GET"], paths: ["/api/*"] }
---
# validate end-user JWTs at the proxy
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1
kind: RequestAuthentication
metadata: { name: jwt, namespace: legal }
spec:
  selector: { matchLabels: { app: legal-api } }
  jwtRules:
    - issuer: "https://auth.example.com/"
      jwksUri: "https://auth.example.com/.well-known/jwks.json"

Once any ALLOW policy selects a workload, everything not allowed is denied. Roll out in a test namespace first. The service still checks object-level permissions: the mesh knows which service is calling, not whether this user owns this case.

Traffic: canary, retries, timeouts

apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1
kind: DestinationRule
metadata: { name: legal-api, namespace: legal }
spec:
  host: legal-api
  subsets:
    - { name: v1, labels: { version: v1 } }
    - { name: v2, labels: { version: v2 } }
  trafficPolicy:
    connectionPool: { http: { http1MaxPendingRequests: 100 } }
    outlierDetection:                     # eject failing pods for a while
      consecutive5xxErrors: 5
      interval: 10s
      baseEjectionTime: 30s
---
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1
kind: VirtualService
metadata: { name: legal-api, namespace: legal }
spec:
  hosts: [legal-api]
  http:
    - timeout: 3s
      retries: { attempts: 2, perTryTimeout: 1s, retryOn: "5xx,connect-failure" }
      route:
        - { destination: { host: legal-api, subset: v1 }, weight: 90 }
        - { destination: { host: legal-api, subset: v2 }, weight: 10 }
# the same split with the Gateway API
apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: HTTPRoute
metadata: { name: legal-api, namespace: legal }
spec:
  parentRefs: [{ name: legal-gateway }]
  hostnames: ["api.example.com"]
  rules:
    - backendRefs:
        - { name: legal-api-v1, port: 8080, weight: 90 }
        - { name: legal-api-v2, port: 8080, weight: 10 }

Put retries in one place, mesh or application, not both: retries multiplied at every layer turn a slowdown into a retry storm. Retry only idempotent requests.

Egress control

# with outbound traffic policy REGISTRY_ONLY, only registered hosts are reachable
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1
kind: ServiceEntry
metadata: { name: indian-kanoon-mirror, namespace: legal }
spec:
  hosts: ["api.example-mirror.org"]
  ports: [{ number: 443, name: https, protocol: TLS }]
  resolution: DNS
  location: MESH_EXTERNAL

Route egress through an egress gateway when you need a fixed exit point, central logging, or to combine with Cloud NAT and firewall rules.

Observability with a mesh

  • Metrics for every request, such as istio_requests_total and request duration histograms, labelled by source, destination and response code.
  • Kiali draws the live service graph with health and traffic.
  • Tracing: proxies create spans, but your application must forward the trace headers (traceparent) from incoming to outgoing requests, or traces break at every service.
  • Access logs per proxy, tunable with the Telemetry resource.
# 5xx rate per destination service
sum by (destination_service) (rate(istio_requests_total{response_code=~"5.."}[5m]))
  / sum by (destination_service) (rate(istio_requests_total[5m]))

Debugging

istioctl analyze -A                                # config problems across namespaces
istioctl proxy-status                              # is every proxy in sync with istiod?
istioctl proxy-config routes   deploy/legal-api -n legal
istioctl proxy-config clusters deploy/legal-api -n legal
istioctl x describe pod <pod> -n legal             # which policies apply to this pod
kubectl logs <pod> -c istio-proxy -n legal         # sidecar access log
SymptomUsually
503 with UF / URX flagsUpstream failing or refusing; check the app and outlier ejection
503 NRNo route: VirtualService or HTTPRoute mismatch
Connection reset after enabling STRICTA client outside the mesh still sends plain text
403 RBAC: access deniedAn AuthorizationPolicy denies it (or no ALLOW rule matches)
App fails at startup calling a dependencySidecar not ready yet; hold the app until the proxy starts
Jobs never completeSidecar keeps the pod alive; use native sidecars or ambient mode
Pod has no sidecarNamespace not labelled, or pod created before the label

Meshes compared

MeshNotes
IstioMost features; sidecar or ambient; large community; more to learn and operate
Cloud Service MeshGoogle-managed, Istio-based, integrated with GKE and Cloud Monitoring
LinkerdSimpler and lightweight, with its own Rust proxy; fewer features
Cilium service mesheBPF-based networking with mesh features, often without sidecars
ConsulSpans Kubernetes and VMs, with service discovery

Adopting a mesh safely

  1. Install in a non-production cluster; enrol one namespace.
  2. Start with PERMISSIVE mTLS and observe traffic in Kiali and metrics.
  3. Move namespaces to STRICT once every client is in the mesh.
  4. Add AuthorizationPolicies service by service, tested in CI against a cluster.
  5. Move retries, timeouts and canaries into the mesh deliberately, removing duplicates from code.
  6. Keep all mesh config in Git, delivered by GitOps, with istioctl analyze in CI.
  7. Plan control-plane upgrades with revisions and canary them like any critical release.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain mTLS, workload identity and east-west vs north-south traffic
  • Read VirtualService, DestinationRule and AuthorizationPolicy resources
  • Find a pod's sidecar logs and proxy status

2 Practitioner

  • Enrol namespaces and roll out STRICT mTLS safely
  • Write authorisation policies and canary routes
  • Debug 503s and RBAC denials with istioctl

3 Senior

  • Decide whether a mesh is worth it, and sidecar vs ambient
  • Design zero-trust service-to-service security
  • Run mesh upgrades with revisions; keep config in GitOps

4 Expert

  • Own the mesh platform across clusters and environments
  • Design multi-cluster and multi-network meshes
  • Set network security strategy combining mesh, gateway and cloud controls

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.
