LLMs and their runtimes

How large language models are built and why they behave as they do, the runtimes that serve them (hosted APIs, Vertex AI, self-hosted servers), and how to build an application on a runtime: from one call, to a tool loop, to a full agent harness. Engineering practice is on AI for Engineers.

How an LLM works, briefly

  1. Tokenise: text is split into tokens (word pieces). Everything (price, limits, speed) is counted in tokens.
  2. Embed: each token becomes a vector of numbers.
  3. Transformer layers: attention lets every token look at every earlier token and mix in what is relevant; dozens of layers build up meaning.
  4. Predict: the model outputs a probability for every possible next token; one is chosen, appended, and the process repeats. Generation is one token at a time.
StageWhat happensResult
Pre-trainingPredict the next token over a huge text and code corpusBroad knowledge and language ability, up to a training cutoff
Post-trainingInstruction tuning, reinforcement learning from human and AI feedback, safety trainingFollows instructions, uses tools, refuses harmful requests
InferenceYour prompt in, tokens out, on GPUs or TPUsThe responses you build on

Consequences you design around: no memory between calls; knowledge stops at a cutoff; outputs vary run to run; fluent text can be wrong; instructions inside data can be followed.

Prompt, RAG, tools or fine-tune?

NeedReach for
Behaviour, format, toneClear instructions and examples in the prompt; structured outputs
Knowledge the model lacks (your documents, current data)RAG: retrieve and include it
Live data or actionsTools (function calling) or MCP servers
A narrow task at high volume, cheaper or fasterA smaller model, or fine-tuning one, after measuring
Consistent style the prompt cannot achieveFine-tuning (LoRA or full)

Try in that order. Fine-tuning does not add reliable facts; it changes behaviour. Facts belong in retrieval.

Runtime options

RuntimeWhat it isChoose when
Hosted model APIProvider runs the model (Anthropic API, others)Best models, no GPUs to run, pay per token
Cloud platformModels through your cloud account (Vertex AI, Bedrock, Foundry)Billing, IAM, region and data controls in your cloud
Managed open-model endpointsDeploy open-weight models to a managed endpoint (Vertex AI Model Garden)Open models without running servers yourself
Self-hosted servervLLM, Hugging Face TGI or llama.cpp server on your GPUs (GKE, Cloud Run with GPUs, VMs)Data must stay on your infrastructure; steady high volume; custom models
LocalOllama or llama.cpp on a laptopExperiments, offline development, small models

Self-hosting basics

# vLLM: OpenAI-compatible server for an open-weight model
vllm serve meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct --max-model-len 8192
# → http://localhost:8000/v1/chat/completions

# Ollama: local models with one command
ollama run llama3.1
# → API on http://localhost:11434

# llama.cpp: quantised GGUF models on CPU or GPU
llama-server -m model-Q4_K_M.gguf --port 8080
  • GPU memory ≈ parameters × bytes per parameter, plus the KV cache. An 8B model in 16-bit needs ~16 GB before the cache; 4-bit quantisation cuts that to ~5 GB at some quality cost.
  • Throughput comes from continuous batching (vLLM) and caching the prompt prefix.
  • Measure time to first token, tokens per second, and cost per thousand requests against a hosted API before committing.
  • Licences of open-weight models differ; read them before commercial use.

Building on a runtime: four levels of harness

A harness is the code around the model: the loop that sends messages, runs tools, feeds results back, manages context and stops. Pick the least you need.

LevelYou writeWho runs the loop and toolsFits
1. Single callPrompt and parsingNo loopClassify, extract, summarise
2. Manual tool loopThe while stop_reason == "tool_use" loop and the toolsYou, on your infrastructureFull control over each step
3. SDK tool runnerJust the tool functionsThe SDK runs the loop; you host itMost custom agents
4a. Agent SDKA prompt and optionsClaude Code's harness as a library, with built-in file, shell and search tools; you host itCoding and file-system agents on your own infrastructure
4b. Managed agentsAgent configuration and your tool resultsThe provider runs the loop and a sandbox per sessionLong-running hosted agents without running infrastructure

Start at level 1 and move up only when the task needs model-driven, multi-step work. Each level adds cost, latency and things to test.

Level 2: a manual tool loop

import anthropic, json
client = anthropic.Anthropic()

TOOLS = [{
  "name": "pipeline_status",
  "description": "Latest state of each pipeline stage for a date (YYYY-MM-DD).",
  "input_schema": {"type": "object",
                   "properties": {"run_date": {"type": "string"}},
                   "required": ["run_date"]},
}]

def run_tool(name, args):
    if name == "pipeline_status":
        return json.dumps(read_ledger(args["run_date"]))   # read-only
    return json.dumps({"error": f"unknown tool {name}"})

messages = [{"role": "user", "content": "Did yesterday's load finish?"}]
for _ in range(10):                                   # always bound the loop
    resp = client.messages.create(model="claude-opus-5-5", max_tokens=16000,
                                  tools=TOOLS, messages=messages)
    messages.append({"role": "assistant", "content": resp.content})
    if resp.stop_reason != "tool_use":
        break
    results = [{"type": "tool_result", "tool_use_id": b.id,
                "content": run_tool(b.name, b.input)}
               for b in resp.content if b.type == "tool_use"]
    messages.append({"role": "user", "content": results})   # all results in one message

print("".join(b.text for b in resp.content if b.type == "text"))

Append the full resp.content, not just its text. Return failed tools as results with "is_error": true rather than dropping them. Level 3 (the SDK's tool runner) does this loop for you.

Running LLM features in production

  • Timeouts, retries and fallbacks per call; handle refusals and rate limits explicitly.
  • Stream long outputs; use the batch API for offline bulk work.
  • Prompt caching: keep stable instructions and tool definitions first so repeated calls reuse them.
  • Log model, version, tokens, latency and outcome for every call; trace tool calls.
  • Pin model IDs and re-run your evals before switching models.
  • Budget: cap tokens per request and spend per day; alert on spikes.
  • Data controls: know the provider's retention terms and the region where inference runs.

Glossary

  • Context window: maximum tokens of input plus output in one call.
  • Embedding: a vector representing meaning, used for search.
  • KV cache: stored attention state that makes generating each next token fast.
  • Quantisation: storing weights with fewer bits to save memory.
  • LoRA: fine-tuning small adapter weights instead of the whole model.
  • Temperature / sampling: how random token choice is (some newer APIs fix this and expose an effort setting instead).
  • Hallucination: confident output not supported by facts.
  • Grounding: tying answers to retrieved sources.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain tokens, context windows, next-token prediction and training cutoffs
  • Make a single model call with structured output
  • Know when to prompt, retrieve, use tools or fine-tune

2 Practitioner

  • Build a tool loop or use an SDK tool runner
  • Run a local or self-hosted model and compare it with a hosted API
  • Log, budget and handle errors for LLM calls

3 Senior

  • Choose runtimes and models by quality, cost, latency and data controls
  • Design agent harnesses with bounded loops, context management and approvals
  • Plan self-hosting capacity (GPU memory, batching, quantisation)

4 Expert

  • Set the organisation's model and runtime strategy
  • Lead fine-tuning or custom model programmes where justified
  • Design platform-level AI infrastructure and governance

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Study next

  • Transformer architecture and attention
  • Inference optimisation: KV cache, batching, speculative decoding
  • Fine-tuning with LoRA
  • Agent design and context management
  • Model evaluation and benchmarking

AI for engineers

How language models behave, how to build features on them that you can test and operate, and how to work with AI assistants without lowering the bar.

Use AI like a bulldozer, not a shovel

A shovel moves one scoop at a time while you hold the handle: ask for a line, paste it, ask again. A bulldozer moves the whole job while you choose where it goes, set the boundaries and check the result. The second gets far more done, but only if you prepare the site. And only if you know enough to manage the site: see Fundamentals first on Start here.

Shovel habitBulldozer habit
"Write a function that…" with no contextHand over a whole task: the issue, the files, the conventions and what done means
Copy and paste answers from a chat windowLet an agent work in the repo: edit files, run the build and tests, read the errors, fix them
Re-explain the project in every sessionWrite the context down once (a CLAUDE.md or AGENTS.md, skills, runbooks) so every session starts informed
Check its work by reading every line as it typesDefine checks up front (tests, lint, type checks, CI) and review the finished change against them
One task at a time, waiting on eachRun independent tasks in parallel: separate branches or worktrees, subagents for research and review
Fix the same mistake by hand again next weekTurn each correction into a rule, test or hook in the repo so it cannot recur
Trust it because it sounds sureTrust it because the tests passed and the docs agree

Preparing the site

  • Context file in the repo: what the system is, how to build and test it, the rules that are not negotiable, the traps that cost time before.
  • Fast, exact gates: the commands that prove a change is done, written down so the agent runs them itself.
  • Guardrails: permission settings and hooks that stop destructive commands (force pushes, prod deploys, deletes) unless a human approves.
  • Small, clear tasks: one concern per task, with acceptance criteria. A vague brief gets a confident guess.
  • Pinned docs: links to the official docs for the versions you use, so it checks instead of recalling.
  • A reviewer that is not the author: a second agent or a person reviews the diff against the spec and the docs.

A brief that moves earth

Goal:     Reject Supreme Court dates that do not parse, instead of storing NULL.
Why:      12% of SC rows lost their decision_date silently (issue #123).
Where:    dbt/fdp/models/int_judgment_normalised.sql and its unit tests.
Rules:    Follow CLAUDE.md. Do not change the HC path. No new dependencies.
Done when:
  - a unit test with "05-05-1959" and "bad" fails before the fix, passes after
  - dbt build -s int_judgment_normalised+ is green
  - the reject table counts unparseable rows
Out of scope: backfilling old rows (separate issue).
Report:   what changed, the test output, anything you could not verify.

Goal, reason, location, rules, a checkable definition of done, scope, and what to report. The same brief works for a colleague.

Where you still drive by hand

  • Irreversible actions: production deploys, data deletion, schema changes on live data, anything sent outside the company.
  • Unclear requirements: decide what to build first; an agent will fill gaps with plausible guesses.
  • Security and money: auth, permissions, payments, secrets. Review line by line.
  • Claims about state: "prod has X" needs someone to look at prod, not a model's inference from config.
  • Judgement calls that belong to a person: priorities, trade-offs, wording that speaks for the company.

The bulldozer is only as safe as its operator. Speed comes from preparation and checks, not from skipping them.

Blueprints and frameworks, not generated boilerplate

AI makes boilerplate cost almost nothing to write. It does not make it cheap to own. Every generated copy has to be reviewed, secured, tested, upgraded and debugged, and fifty copies that are each slightly different are fifty places to fix the same bug. A blueprint (a framework, shared library, template, Terraform module or dbt package) makes each decision once, tests it once, and fixes it once for everyone.

Generated boilerplateBlueprint or framework
ConsistencyEach copy drifts: retries, logging, auth and error handling differ per serviceOne behaviour everywhere, so people and tools know what to expect
Fixing a bug or CVEFind and patch every copy, and miss somePatch once, bump the version, done
ReviewHundreds of generated lines to read in every PRReview only the part that is new; the rest is already proven
SecurityEach copy can reintroduce a hole the model learned from public codeSecure defaults built in and tested
UpgradesEvery service upgrades on its own, or neverUpgrade the blueprint; services follow
KnowledgeDecisions live in whichever prompt produced the codeDecisions are written down, versioned and reviewable
AI qualityThe model guesses your conventions from its training dataThe model follows your blueprint, so its output is smaller and fits
  • Rule of thumb: if you would ask AI to generate the same thing twice, it belongs in a blueprint.
  • Use AI to build and improve the blueprint, then to fill in only the part that is specific: the business logic, the one new query, the tests for it.
  • Point the AI at the blueprint in your context file ("new jobs use modules/pipeline-job; new services start from the service template"), so it extends instead of reinventing.
  • Generation is fine for one-off scripts, prototypes you will throw away, and filling in a blueprint's slots.

Examples in this guide: Spring Boot starters and Quarkus extensions; a Terraform module for every Cloud Run job; dbt macros, generic tests and packages; a shared library such as Culvert for storage and warehouse access; a service template in Backstage or Cookiecutter; and a written build-loop blueprint that is ported between repos instead of re-prompted in each. More on the Frameworks vs AI Boilerplate tab.

How models behave

  • Tokens: models read and write tokens (pieces of words). Cost, limits and latency are all counted in tokens.
  • Context window: everything the model can see in one call: instructions, history, retrieved documents, tool results. It remembers nothing between calls unless you send it again.
  • Non-deterministic: the same input can give different outputs. Test with sets of examples and scores, not one run.
  • Confidently wrong: a model can produce fluent, specific, false text (hallucination). Ground answers in retrieved sources and ask for citations you can check.
  • Knowledge cutoff: the model knows nothing after its training data. Current facts must come from your data or tools.
  • Instructions in data: the model cannot reliably tell your instructions from instructions hidden in a document it reads.

Calling a model (Anthropic Python SDK)

# pip install anthropic   — credentials from ANTHROPIC_API_KEY
import anthropic
client = anthropic.Anthropic()

resp = client.messages.create(
    model="claude-opus-5-5",
    max_tokens=16000,
    system="You summarise Indian court judgments for lawyers. Cite paragraph numbers.",
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": judgment_text}],
)
if resp.stop_reason == "refusal":
    handle_refusal(resp)                   # check before reading content
for block in resp.content:
    if block.type == "text":
        print(block.text)
print(resp.usage.input_tokens, resp.usage.output_tokens)   # log every call's cost

Other current IDs: claude-sonnet-5-5 (faster, cheaper) and claude-haiku-5-5 (cheapest, for bulk work). Long outputs should stream. On Vertex AI use AnthropicVertex(project_id=…, region=…) with the same calls.

Structured output, not string parsing

from pydantic import BaseModel

class Citation(BaseModel):
    case_name: str
    year: int | None
    court: str | None
    paragraph: str | None

class Extraction(BaseModel):
    citations: list[Citation]

resp = client.messages.parse(
    model="claude-opus-5-5",
    max_tokens=16000,
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": f"Extract every case cited:\n\n{text}"}],
    output_format=Extraction,
)
result: Extraction = resp.parsed_output     # validated against the schema

The schema guarantees shape, not truth. Still check values against your data: does that case exist in the corpus?

Tools: let the model call your code

from anthropic import beta_tool

@beta_tool
def search_corpus(query: str, court: str | None = None) -> str:
    """Search the judgment corpus. Returns up to 10 results as JSON.

    Args:
        query: Full-text search terms.
        court: Optional court code, e.g. "2_5".
    """
    return json.dumps(corpus.search(query, court=court, limit=10))

runner = client.beta.messages.tool_runner(
    model="claude-opus-5-5",
    max_tokens=16000,
    tools=[search_corpus],
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": "Find recent Himachal judgments on land acquisition."}],
)
for message in runner:          # the SDK runs the call → tool → call loop
    print(message)

Give tools the least privilege that works: read-only by default, writes behind human approval. MCP is the open protocol for exposing tools and data to any compatible client.

RAG: retrieval-augmented generation

StepWhat you buildWhere it goes wrong
1 IngestPipeline that loads documents with IDs, dates and sourceStale or duplicate documents; no way to tell which version was used
2 ChunkSplit into passages (by section or ~500–1,000 tokens with overlap)Chunks cut through the sentence that answers the question
3 EmbedVector per chunk; store with metadataQuery and documents embedded with different models
4 RetrieveVector search plus keyword search (hybrid), filtered by metadataExact names and citations missed by vectors alone
5 RerankScore the top 50 and keep the best 5–10Irrelevant passages crowd out the right one
6 GeneratePrompt with the passages, ask for citations to their IDsModel answers from memory when retrieval found nothing; say "not found" instead
7 EvaluateMeasure retrieval (did the right chunk come back?) and answers separatelyOnly judging the final answer hides which step failed

Vector search: Postgres & BigQuery

-- pgvector
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS vector;
CREATE TABLE chunks (id bigserial PRIMARY KEY, doc_id text, body text,
                     embedding vector(768));
CREATE INDEX ON chunks USING hnsw (embedding vector_cosine_ops);
SELECT id, doc_id, 1 - (embedding <=> $1) AS similarity
FROM chunks WHERE doc_id LIKE 'HP%'          -- HNSW filters after the index scan: can return fewer rows (pgvector 0.8+ iterative scans help)
ORDER BY embedding <=> $1 LIMIT 10;        -- <=> cosine distance
-- BigQuery: embed with a remote model, then search
CREATE OR REPLACE TABLE `p.ai.chunk_emb` AS
SELECT * FROM ML.GENERATE_EMBEDDING(
  MODEL `p.ai.embedding_model`,
  (SELECT id, body AS content FROM `p.ai.chunks`));

SELECT base.id, distance
FROM VECTOR_SEARCH(
  TABLE `p.ai.chunk_emb`, 'ml_generate_embedding_result',
  (SELECT ml_generate_embedding_result FROM ML.GENERATE_EMBEDDING(
     MODEL `p.ai.embedding_model`, (SELECT 'land acquisition compensation' AS content))),
  top_k => 10, distance_type => 'COSINE');

Evals: how you know it works

  • Build a test set of 50–200 real inputs with expected outputs or grading notes. Include the hard and adversarial cases.
  • Grade with code first: exact match, schema valid, cited ID exists, number within tolerance.
  • Model-as-judge for open-ended answers, with a written rubric, checked against human grades on a sample.
  • Run it in CI on every prompt, model or retrieval change, and track the score over time.
  • Hold out a test split you never tune against, so improvements are real.
  • Measure cost and latency beside quality; a 2% gain at 3× the cost may not be worth it.

Security

  • Prompt injection: a web page, PDF or email the model reads can say "ignore your instructions". Treat everything retrieved as untrusted data.
  • Limit the blast radius: tools get least privilege; destructive actions need human confirmation; the model never holds credentials directly.
  • Output is untrusted input: escape it before rendering, validate it before running it as SQL or shell.
  • Data leakage: do not send secrets or personal data you are not allowed to process. Check the provider's retention terms.
  • Log and audit prompts, tool calls and outputs (minus sensitive fields) so you can investigate.

Cost & latency

  • Estimate first: (input + output tokens) × price × calls per day. Log usage on every call.
  • Prompt caching: put the stable part (instructions, tool definitions, long reference text) first so repeated calls reuse it at a fraction of the price.
  • Batch API for work that can wait (bulk extraction overnight), at a lower price.
  • Right-size the model: measure whether a smaller model or lower effort holds quality on your eval before paying for the largest.
  • Trim context: retrieve 5 good passages, not 50 mediocre ones.
  • Stream long responses so users see progress, and set timeouts and retries with backoff.

Working with AI coding assistants

  • Give context: the repo's conventions, the failing test, the exact error. Vague asks get vague code.
  • Tests first: a test that fails before the change is the best spec and the best check.
  • Verify against docs: an assistant can invent a flag, method or version. Check the official docs for anything external.
  • Review every line as you would a colleague's PR: error paths, edge cases, security.
  • Keep secrets out of prompts and pasted logs.
  • Small steps: one concern per change, so a wrong turn is cheap to undo.
  • You own the result. "The AI wrote it" is not a review comment.

Agents: when and when not

  • Start with one call. Classification, extraction and summaries rarely need more.
  • Then a workflow: fixed steps in your code, a model call at each. Predictable and testable.
  • An agent (the model chooses tools in a loop) only when the steps cannot be known in advance, the value justifies the cost, and mistakes can be caught and undone.
  • Bound it: step and token budgets, timeouts, an approval gate on writes, and a full log of each tool call.

AI on Google Cloud

  • Vertex AI: hosted models (Gemini, Claude and others through Model Garden), endpoints, evaluation and pipelines in your GCP project and region.
  • BigQuery ML: call remote models from SQL (ML.GENERATE_TEXT, ML.GENERATE_EMBEDDING) and search with VECTOR_SEARCH, without moving data out.
  • Data residency: pick the region deliberately; some models run only in global or specific regions.
  • IAM as usual: service accounts with Vertex AI User, no API keys in code.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Call a model API and get structured output
  • Explain tokens, context, hallucination and prompt injection
  • Use an AI assistant with tests and review

2 Practitioner

  • Build a RAG feature with retrieval, citations and an eval set
  • Use tools and handle refusals, errors and cost
  • Brief an AI agent with context, rules and a definition of done

3 Senior

  • Design AI features with evals, guardrails, observability and fallbacks
  • Tune cost and latency (caching, batch, model choice)
  • Set up repos so AI agents work safely (context files, hooks, gates)

4 Expert

  • Set AI strategy, governance and security across the organisation
  • Design agentic systems and their safety boundaries
  • Build shared AI platforms, blueprints and evaluation infrastructure

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Frameworks and blueprints vs AI-generated boilerplate

AI can generate a new service, pipeline or Terraform stack in minutes. That makes it tempting to generate every time. This tab explains why a well-built framework usually wins, when generation is the right call, and how to use AI to build and maintain the framework instead.

The core argument

Writing code was never the main cost; owning it is. Every copy of generated boilerplate must be reviewed, secured, tested, upgraded, debugged and understood by the next person. Generation makes the cheap part cheaper and multiplies the expensive part. A framework makes each decision once, tests it once, fixes it once, and upgrades everyone together.

Generate per projectFramework / blueprint
Time to first versionMinutesMinutes, once the framework exists
ConsistencyEach copy differs subtly (retries, logging, auth, errors)Same behaviour everywhere
Fixing a bug or CVEFind and patch N copies; some get missedPatch once, release, bump versions
Review burdenHundreds of generated lines per PROnly the business-specific part
SecurityEach copy may reintroduce a known holeSecure defaults, tested once
Upgrades (Java, Spring, providers)N separate migrationsOne migration in the framework, then a version bump
OnboardingEvery repo is a new dialectLearn the framework once
KnowledgeDecisions live in prompts nobody keptDecisions in versioned, reviewed code and docs
AI output qualityModel guesses your conventions from public codeModel extends your framework; smaller, consistent output
Total cost over 2 yearsGrows with every copyMostly fixed, shared

A cost model in one line

generated:  N copies × (review + security fix + upgrade + drift) per year
framework:  build once + maintain once + N × (small version bump)

With N = 15 services, 3 framework-level fixes a year, ~1 day each:
  generated:  15 × 3 = 45 fix-days, plus drift
  framework:   1 × 3 =  3 fix-days + 15 version bumps (automatable)

The numbers are illustrative; the shape is the point. The gap grows with every new copy and every year.

When generation is the right call

  • One-off scripts and investigations you will delete.
  • Prototypes and spikes to learn whether an idea works.
  • Filling a framework's slots: the business logic, the new query, its tests.
  • Codemods: AI-written scripts that apply the same change across many repos (an upgrade, a rename), reviewed once.
  • Tests and documentation for code that follows the framework.
  • Before the third repetition: the first two times, write or generate it directly; you do not yet know what to abstract.

When not to build a framework

  • Rule of three: abstract after three real uses, not one imagined future.
  • Inner-platform effect: a framework so configurable it becomes a worse version of the language or tool it wraps.
  • Business logic in shared libraries: forces lock-step releases between teams. Share infrastructure concerns, not domain rules.
  • No owner: a framework without a maintaining team decays faster than copies.
  • Escape hatches missing: teams need a supported way to do the unusual thing without forking.

What a blueprint looks like in this stack

AreaBlueprintWhat it decides once
ServicesService template + internal Spring Boot starter or Quarkus extensionLogging format, tracing, health checks, error responses, security config, Dockerfile
JobsA job library + Terraform module for a Cloud Run jobRun records, exit codes, counters, retries, IAM, scheduling
Datadbt macros, generic tests, packages, project templateLayer conventions, key computation, date parsing that rejects, test baseline
InfrastructureTerraform modules (bucket, dataset, job, service)Encryption, labels, IAM, deletion protection, naming
DeliveryHarness or GitHub Actions templates; reusable workflowsBuild once, scan, deploy by artefact, approvals, rollback
APIsApigee shared flows; OpenAPI style guide and lintingAuth, rate limits, error format, versioning
ObservabilityDashboard and alert templates; OpenTelemetry configGolden signals, SLO alerts, runbook links
AIRepo context files (CLAUDE.md), skills, hooks, an MCP server template, eval harnessHow agents work in the repo, which checks run, what "done" means

Using AI to build the framework

  1. Collect the 3+ existing implementations; ask AI to list what they share and where they differ.
  2. Decide the framework's API and its escape hatches yourself; that is the design work.
  3. Let AI draft the framework code, tests and documentation against that design.
  4. Review it like any critical library; add contract tests.
  5. Let AI write codemods that migrate each existing project onto it, one PR per repo.
  6. Point every repo's AI context file at the framework, so future work extends it instead of reinventing it.

Keeping a framework healthy

  • An owning team with a roadmap, changelog and support channel.
  • Semantic versioning; breaking changes rare, announced, with migration guides and codemods.
  • Automated upgrades (Renovate or Dependabot) open version bumps in every consumer.
  • Golden path, not a cage: the easy way is the right way, and the unusual way is possible.
  • Scorecards (e.g. in a Backstage catalogue) show which services are on current versions.
  • Measure adoption and time to first deploy for new services.

Decision guide

  • Will this exist in more than one place for more than a few months? → blueprint.
  • Does it carry security, compliance or reliability rules? → blueprint, even for two uses.
  • Is it unique business logic? → write it (with AI help) inside the blueprint's slots.
  • Is it throwaway or exploratory? → generate, and delete it afterwards.
  • Is it the same change across many repos? → AI-written codemod, reviewed once.

AI is the bulldozer; the framework is the road. Build the road once and drive on it, rather than bulldozing a new track for every trip.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain why owning generated code costs more than writing it
  • Use the team's templates and libraries instead of generating new boilerplate
  • Fill a blueprint's slots with AI help

2 Practitioner

  • Spot repeated code and propose a blueprint after the third use
  • Write codemods with AI to apply a change across repos
  • Point AI context files at the blueprints

3 Senior

  • Design framework APIs with escape hatches
  • Own a blueprint: versioning, docs, migration guides
  • Decide build vs generate with a cost model

4 Expert

  • Run a platform team with golden paths and scorecards
  • Set the organisation's policy on AI generation vs shared frameworks
  • Measure developer productivity and adoption

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Claude Code and Claude Cowork

Claude Code is Anthropic's agentic coding tool: it reads your repository, edits files, runs commands and tests, and works through tasks with you or on its own. Claude Cowork brings the same agentic way of working to non-coding knowledge work in the Claude desktop app. How each is set up, extended and kept safe.

Where Claude Code runs

SurfaceUse it for
Terminal CLI (claude)Interactive work in a local repository
IDE extensions (VS Code, JetBrains)The same agent beside your editor, with diffs inline
Desktop appLocal and cloud sessions with a graphical interface
Claude Code on the webCloud sessions in a managed container: work continues without your laptop
Headless (claude -p)Scripts and CI: one prompt in, result out (text or JSON)
GitHub Actions (claude-code-action)Respond to issues and PRs, e.g. a label that asks Claude to build a task
Agent SDKClaude Code's harness as a library inside your own application

Everyday commands

claude                              # start in the current repo
claude "fix the failing date parser test"
claude -c                           # continue the last conversation
claude -r                           # pick an earlier session to resume
claude -p "summarise this diff" --output-format json < diff.txt   # headless

# inside a session
/init        create a CLAUDE.md for this repo
/memory      edit memory files
/agents      manage subagents
/mcp         MCP servers and their status
/permissions view rules
/compact     summarise the conversation to free context
/clear       start fresh
Shift+Tab    cycle permission modes (incl. plan mode)
Esc          interrupt

The files that shape it

FileHolds
CLAUDE.md (repo root, subfolders)Project memory: what the system is, rules, commands, traps. Loaded every session.
~/.claude/CLAUDE.mdYour personal preferences across projects
.claude/settings.jsonShared permissions, hooks, environment, model settings (committed)
.claude/settings.local.jsonYour local overrides (not committed)
.claude/agents/*.mdSubagents: specialised helpers with their own prompt, tools and model
.claude/skills/<name>/SKILL.mdSkills: packaged procedures invoked by name or when relevant
.claude/commands/*.mdCustom slash commands
.mcp.jsonMCP servers shared with the team

These files are the blueprint for how agents work in your repo. Keep them short, specific and current; a stale rule misleads every session.

Permissions and hooks

// .claude/settings.json
{
  "permissions": {
    "allow": ["Bash(./gradlew test:*)", "Bash(git status)", "Bash(git diff:*)"],
    "deny":  ["Read(./.env)", "Bash(rm -rf:*)"]
  },
  "hooks": {
    "PreToolUse": [
      { "matcher": "Bash",
        "hooks": [{ "type": "command", "command": ".claude/hooks/guard-destructive.sh" }] }
    ],
    "PostToolUse": [
      { "matcher": "Edit|Write",
        "hooks": [{ "type": "command", "command": ".claude/hooks/check-edited-file.sh" }] }
    ],
    "Stop": [
      { "hooks": [{ "type": "command", "command": ".claude/hooks/stop-gate.sh" }] }
    ]
  }
}
  • Permission modes: default (ask), accept edits, plan (read and propose only), and fully autonomous modes for sandboxed environments.
  • Hooks run your scripts at events (before/after a tool, on prompt submit, at stop, at session start). A hook that exits with code 2 blocks the action where the event allows it (PreToolUse blocks the tool call, Stop prevents stopping) and its stderr is fed back to Claude. A PostToolUse hook runs after the tool, so exit code 2 only feeds stderr back.
  • Use hooks for rules that must always hold (lint after every edit, block pushes to main); use CLAUDE.md for guidance.

Subagents and skills

<!-- simplified example of .claude/agents/reviewer.md -->
---
name: reviewer
description: Independent reviewer of a finished diff before a PR is opened.
  Use at the end of every task.
tools: Read, Grep, Glob, Bash
model: sonnet
---
Review the diff against the task, CLAUDE.md and the official docs of every
library it touches. Report bugs, missing tests and unverified API use.
Do not edit files.
<!-- simplified example of .claude/skills/<name>/SKILL.md -->
---
name: release-sync
description: Bring a project page up to date with that project's latest
  release. Use after a release or when a sync issue is open.
---
1. Read the release tag and changelog.
2. Update the version markers and the "what's new" block.
3. Run the release checker until it reports 0 findings.

Subagents keep their own context, so a reviewer is not anchored on the builder's reasoning. Skills turn a procedure that cost a debugging cycle into something every session follows.

A worked example: an autonomous build loop

  1. /new-issue turns an idea into a ready issue.
  2. /build-task builds it: verify docs, write a failing test, implement.
  3. Hooks check every edit; a stop hook runs the full check before the turn can end.
  4. A reviewer subagent checks the finished diff independently.
  5. Fix what it finds, at most three attempts, then write a blocker summary instead of looping.
  6. /compound turns what went wrong into a permanent guard (a test, hook or rule).
  7. Open a PR; a human (or a gated merge skill) merges.

Each step is a skill, agent or hook committed to the repository, so every session follows the same loop.

Claude Cowork

Cowork applies Claude's agentic loop to everyday knowledge work rather than code: it works through multi-step tasks on your files, documents and connected apps from the Claude desktop app, and checks in with you as it goes.

Claude CodeClaude Cowork
Main usersEngineersAnyone doing knowledge work: analysts, operations, legal, sales, founders
Works onRepositories: code, tests, builds, GitFolders, documents, spreadsheets, presentations, connected apps
Extended byCLAUDE.md, skills, subagents, hooks, MCP, pluginsSkills, plugins and connectors (MCP), often packaged per role
Typical tasksImplement a feature, fix a bug, review a PR, run a migrationPrepare a report from files, reconcile spreadsheets, draft documents, run a recurring brief
  • Plugins bundle skills, commands, agents and connectors for a role or team, so the organisation's way of working is installed rather than re-explained.
  • Same rules as any agent: give it a clear brief and a definition of done, limit what it can access, and review what it produces before it is sent anywhere.
  • Features and availability change frequently; check Anthropic's current product documentation.

Working well with Claude Code

  • Plan first for anything non-trivial: plan mode, read the plan, then let it build.
  • Give it a way to check itself: exact test and lint commands in CLAUDE.md.
  • One task per session; /clear between unrelated tasks to keep context clean.
  • Worktrees for parallel sessions, so they never edit the same checkout.
  • Verify claims: ask what it ran and what the output was; "done" means a green check you can see.
  • Turn every correction into a rule in CLAUDE.md, a skill or a hook.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Run Claude Code in a repo; review and accept its changes
  • Write a useful CLAUDE.md with commands and rules
  • Use plan mode and permission prompts sensibly

2 Practitioner

  • Add hooks, skills, subagents and MCP servers for the team
  • Run headless and GitHub Action workflows
  • Use Cowork with plugins and connectors for knowledge work

3 Senior

  • Design an agent workflow with gates, reviewers and attempt limits
  • Govern permissions, secrets and sandboxing
  • Turn recurring corrections into skills and hooks

4 Expert

  • Set the organisation's agentic engineering practice
  • Build shared plugins and blueprints across teams
  • Measure productivity and quality impact

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

GitHub Copilot

GitHub's AI assistant across the editor, the command line and github.com: completions, chat, agent mode, a coding agent that works on issues and opens pull requests, and code review. How to configure it for a team and how it compares with Claude Code.

Features

FeatureWhereDoes
Code completionsIDESuggests the next lines as you type
Copilot ChatIDE, github.com, mobileAnswers questions about code, explains, drafts changes
Agent modeIDE (e.g. VS Code)Plans and makes multi-file edits, runs commands and tests, iterates
Copilot coding agentgithub.comAssign it an issue; it works in a cloud environment and opens a draft PR for review
Copilot code reviewPull requestsRequested as a reviewer; leaves comments and suggestions
Copilot CLITerminalAgentic help in the shell
Model choiceChat and agentsSeveral model families, including Claude models, depending on plan and admin settings

GitHub Copilot (for developers) is a different product from Microsoft 365 Copilot (for Office documents and email). Plans and feature names change often; check GitHub's docs.

Team configuration

FilePurpose
.github/copilot-instructions.mdRepository-wide instructions: stack, conventions, commands
.github/instructions/*.instructions.mdInstructions that apply to matching paths
.github/prompts/*.prompt.mdReusable prompt files
AGENTS.mdAgent instructions read by several tools
<!-- .github/copilot-instructions.md -->
This repo is a Spring Boot 3 WebFlux service on Java 21.
- Tests: ./gradlew :api:test (unit), CI runs integration tests.
- Never call .block() on the request path.
- Errors go through GlobalExceptionHandler; no onErrorResume in controllers.
- Use JdbcClient; no new JPA entities.

Keep one source of truth: if Claude Code reads CLAUDE.md and Copilot reads its own file, generate one from the other or point both at a shared document, so the rules cannot drift apart.

Admin and governance

  • Policies at enterprise and organisation level: which features and models are allowed.
  • Content exclusion: paths or repositories Copilot must not use as context.
  • Public code filter: block suggestions matching public code, where licence risk matters.
  • MCP servers for agent mode and the coding agent, configured per repository or organisation.
  • Coding agent environment: a setup workflow, a firewall allow-list, and branch protections; its PRs go through normal review and CI.
  • Usage metrics for adoption and acceptance rates.

Copilot and Claude Code compared

GitHub CopilotClaude Code
HomeThe IDE and github.comThe terminal and repository (plus IDE, desktop, web)
StrengthsInline completions, tight GitHub integration (issues, PRs, review)Long, multi-step agentic tasks; deep customisation with hooks, skills and subagents
ConfigurationInstruction and prompt files, policiesCLAUDE.md, settings, hooks, skills, agents, plugins
Asynchronous workCoding agent on issuesCloud sessions, GitHub Action, headless mode

Many teams use both: completions while typing, an agent for whole tasks. The disciplines are the same: clear briefs, tests as the definition of done, human review, and rules written into the repo.

Using it responsibly

  • Review every suggestion; accepted code is your code.
  • Watch for invented APIs and outdated patterns; check official docs.
  • Keep secrets out of files Copilot can read; use content exclusion for sensitive paths.
  • Coding-agent PRs get the same CI, review and security checks as human PRs.
  • Prefer extending your frameworks to generating new boilerplate (Frameworks vs AI).

  • Use completions and chat; review every suggestion
  • Know the difference between GitHub Copilot and Microsoft 365 Copilot
  • Find and follow the repo's Copilot instructions

2 Practitioner

  • Write repository and path instructions and prompt files
  • Use agent mode and the coding agent on scoped issues
  • Request and act on Copilot code review

3 Senior

  • Configure policies, content exclusion and MCP for a team
  • Set up the coding agent environment and protections
  • Keep Copilot and Claude Code instructions consistent

4 Expert

  • Own AI-assisted development policy across the organisation
  • Evaluate tools and models with real task metrics
  • Lead adoption and training

Agent harness

A model on its own only produces text. The harness is everything around it that turns it into an agent: the loop, the tools, context management, permissions, checks, memory, and the record of what happened. How harnesses are built, what makes one reliable, and the options from writing your own to using a managed one. Model basics are on LLMs.

Parts of a harness

PartDoesGets it wrong when
LoopSend messages, run requested tools, return results, repeat until doneNo step or token budget: runaway cost
ToolsFunctions the model can call (files, shell, search, APIs, MCP)Too many, vague descriptions, huge outputs, no error detail
Context managementDecide what the model sees: instructions, history, retrieved files; compact or clear old contentContext fills with noise and the model loses the thread
MemoryPersist knowledge across sessions (project files, notes, memory tools)Stale memory states things that are no longer true
Permissions and sandboxLimit what tools may touch; ask a human for risky actionsBroad credentials; destructive commands unguarded
Gates and hooksDeterministic checks that run regardless of the model (lint, tests, policy)The agent decides on its own when it is "done"
Sub-agentsDelegate research or review to fresh contextsHand-offs lose detail; reviewers anchored on the builder's view
State and resumeSave progress so work survives restartsA crash loses hours of work
ObservabilityLog every model call, tool call, cost and decisionNobody can say why the agent did something
EvaluationMeasure task success, cost and interventions on a test setChanges judged by impression

Build or use?

OptionYou own
Manual loop on the model APIEverything (code on the LLMs tab)
SDK tool runnerTools and hosting; the SDK runs the loop
Claude Agent SDKPrompt, options, hosting; Claude Code's harness and built-in tools come with it
Managed agents (hosted)Agent configuration and your own tools; the provider runs loop and sandbox
Agent frameworks (e.g. LangGraph)Graph of steps and state; framework handles orchestration
Ready-made agents (Claude Code, Cowork, Copilot)Configuration: context files, skills, hooks, connectors

Configure a ready-made harness before building one. Build your own only when the task, tools or deployment need something they cannot give.

Principles for a reliable harness

  • Done is decided by checks, not by the model: tests, linters, schema validation, a reviewer.
  • Bound everything: steps, tokens, time, money, attempts at the same failure (three, then stop and report).
  • Least privilege tools; human approval for anything irreversible or external.
  • Append-only history: do not rewrite past turns; summarise forward when context is full.
  • Fresh eyes for review: a separate agent or person who sees the code, not the builder's conclusions.
  • Make lessons permanent: each failure becomes a rule, test or hook in the repo.
  • Never let the agent weaken a gate (skip a test, disable a check) to get to green.

Context engineering

  • Put stable things first (system prompt, tools) so they are cached and consistent.
  • Load just in time: give the agent tools to find files instead of pasting everything up front.
  • Keep tool outputs compact; return summaries with a way to fetch details.
  • Compact or clear old tool results in long sessions.
  • Use sub-agents to read widely and return only conclusions.
  • Write state to files (plans, progress notes) so a new context can pick up the work.

Designing tools for agents

  • Few tools, each matching a task a person would do ("search judgments", "run tests"), not every API endpoint.
  • Names and descriptions written for the model: what it does, when to use it, what it returns.
  • Typed inputs with enums and examples; strict schemas.
  • Errors that say what went wrong and how to fix it.
  • Distinguish "no results" from "could not search".
  • Read-only by default; writes need confirmation.

Failure modes to design against

  • Runaway loops repeating the same failing action.
  • Context rot: long sessions where early instructions are drowned out.
  • False completion: claiming success without running the check.
  • Gaming the check: editing tests or disabling guards to pass.
  • Prompt injection from files, web pages or tool outputs.
  • Scope creep: changing more than the task asked.
  • Credential misuse: acting with more access than the task needs.

Measuring a harness

  • Task success rate on a fixed set of real tasks, judged by checks.
  • Cost and time per completed task, not per request.
  • Intervention rate: how often a human had to step in.
  • Regression rate: changes that later had to be reverted.
  • Gate failures caught before merge vs after.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain loop, tools, context, permissions and gates
  • Configure a ready-made harness (context file, permissions)
  • Recognise runaway loops and false completion

2 Practitioner

  • Build a bounded tool loop with honest errors and logging
  • Write agent-friendly tools
  • Add deterministic gates and a separate reviewer

3 Senior

  • Design harnesses with context engineering, memory and resume
  • Evaluate harnesses on task success, cost and interventions
  • Defend against prompt injection and gate gaming

4 Expert

  • Build agent platforms and shared harness components
  • Set safety and governance policy for autonomous agents
  • Research and adopt new agent patterns with evidence

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Explore further

  • Context engineering
  • Tool design for agents
  • Multi-agent orchestration
  • Agent evaluation

Building RAG systems

Retrieval-augmented generation: find the right passages from your own data and give them to the model, so answers are current, specific and cited. The whole pipeline, each design choice, code for Postgres and BigQuery, and how to evaluate it. RAG is a data pipeline first: see Data Engineering.

Architecture

PhaseStepsRuns
Ingestion (offline)Load documents → parse (PDF, HTML, OCR) → clean → chunk → add metadata → embed → index (vector + keyword)As a pipeline: batch or on change
Retrieval (online)Query → rewrite (optional) → hybrid search with permission and metadata filters → rerank → top passagesPer question, in milliseconds
Generation (online)Prompt with passages and IDs → model answers with citations → validate citations → respond, or say "not found"Per question, in seconds
Evaluation (continuous)Golden questions → retrieval metrics + answer metrics → dashboard and CI gateOn every change, and sampled in production

Ingestion and parsing

  • Keep a stable document ID, source, version and date on everything, so each answer can name exactly what it used.
  • Parse structure, not just text: headings, paragraphs and numbered sections are the best chunk boundaries. Tables need special handling.
  • Scanned PDFs need OCR (Document AI, Tesseract); measure its error rate on your documents.
  • Clean: strip headers, footers, page numbers and boilerplate that pollute retrieval.
  • Run ingestion as an idempotent, incremental pipeline with run records: documents in = chunks indexed + rejected.
  • Handle updates and deletions: re-embed changed documents, remove withdrawn ones, and never serve a stale version silently.

Chunking

StrategyHowNotes
Fixed sizeN tokens with overlapSimple baseline; cuts through sentences
RecursiveSplit by section, then paragraph, then sentence until small enoughGood default for prose
StructuralOne chunk per section or numbered paragraphBest for legal and technical documents with structure
Parent-childRetrieve small chunks, give the model their larger parent sectionPrecise search, enough context to answer
ContextualPrepend a short summary of where the chunk sits in its document before embeddingReduces retrieval failures for chunks that make no sense alone

Typical sizes are 200–1,000 tokens. Measure: the right size is the one that maximises retrieval recall on your golden questions.

Embeddings and indexes

  • Embedding model: choose by quality on your domain and language, dimension (storage and speed), and cost. Options include Vertex AI text embeddings and other providers' embedding APIs, or open models.
  • Same model for documents and queries, and re-embed everything when you change it. Store the model name and version with each vector.
  • Distance: cosine is the usual choice; normalise vectors if the model expects it.
  • Approximate indexes (HNSW, IVF) trade a little recall for speed. Tune and measure recall against exact search on a sample.
  • Where to store: pgvector (Postgres, AlloyDB), BigQuery VECTOR_SEARCH, Vertex AI Vector Search, Elasticsearch/OpenSearch. Keep vectors next to the metadata you filter on.

Hybrid search with rank fusion (Postgres)

-- chunks(id, doc_id, body, tsv tsvector, embedding vector(768), court text)
WITH vec AS (
  SELECT id, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY embedding <=> $1) AS r
  FROM chunks WHERE court = ANY($3)            -- filter first: permissions, metadata
  ORDER BY embedding <=> $1 LIMIT 50
),
kw AS (
  SELECT id, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (ORDER BY ts_rank_cd(tsv, q) DESC) AS r
  FROM chunks, websearch_to_tsquery('english', $2) AS q
  WHERE tsv @@ q AND court = ANY($3)
  ORDER BY ts_rank_cd(tsv, q) DESC LIMIT 50
)
SELECT id, SUM(1.0 / (60 + r)) AS score       -- reciprocal rank fusion, k = 60
FROM (SELECT * FROM vec UNION ALL SELECT * FROM kw) AS both_lists
GROUP BY id
ORDER BY score DESC
LIMIT 20;                                      -- then rerank these 20

Vectors find meaning ("compensation for acquired land"); keywords find exact names, citations and section numbers that vectors miss. Fusion combines both without tuning score scales.

Reranking and query handling

  • Rerank the top 20–50 with a cross-encoder or reranking API (for example Vertex AI ranking, or a provider's rerank model); keep the best 5–10.
  • Query rewriting: turn a conversational follow-up into a standalone query ("and in Himachal?" → full question).
  • Multi-query: generate a few phrasings and fuse their results.
  • Metadata extraction: pull filters out of the question (court, year) and apply them in SQL.
  • Agentic retrieval: let the model call a search tool several times when one search is not enough, with a step budget.

Generation with citations

import anthropic
client = anthropic.Anthropic()

passages = retrieve(question, courts=user.allowed_courts)    # hybrid + rerank, top 8

content = [
    {"type": "document",
     "source": {"type": "text", "media_type": "text/plain", "data": p.body},
     "title": f"{p.case_name} ¶{p.paragraph} ({p.doc_id})",
     "citations": {"enabled": True}}
    for p in passages
]
content.append({"type": "text", "text":
    "Answer only from the documents above. If they do not contain the answer, "
    "say you could not find it. Question: " + question})

resp = client.messages.create(model="claude-opus-5-5", max_tokens=16000,
                              messages=[{"role": "user", "content": content}])
# text blocks carry a citations list pointing at the exact cited text in each document
  • Put instructions in the system prompt; treat retrieved text as data, never as instructions.
  • Check that every cited document was actually retrieved; drop or flag answers with no citations.
  • When retrieval returns nothing relevant, answer "not found". When the index is unavailable, say so: that is "cannot see", not "nothing exists".

RAG on BigQuery

-- 1. embed chunks with a remote embedding model
CREATE OR REPLACE TABLE `p.rag.chunk_emb` AS
SELECT * FROM ML.GENERATE_EMBEDDING(
  MODEL `p.rag.embedding_model`,
  (SELECT chunk_id, doc_id, court, body AS content FROM `p.rag.chunks`));

-- 2. optional vector index for large tables
CREATE VECTOR INDEX chunk_idx ON `p.rag.chunk_emb`(ml_generate_embedding_result)
OPTIONS (index_type = 'IVF', distance_type = 'COSINE');

-- 3. search
SELECT base.chunk_id, base.doc_id, base.content, distance
FROM VECTOR_SEARCH(
  (SELECT * FROM `p.rag.chunk_emb` WHERE court IN ('2_5', '14_25')),
  'ml_generate_embedding_result',
  (SELECT ml_generate_embedding_result FROM ML.GENERATE_EMBEDDING(
     MODEL `p.rag.embedding_model`,
     (SELECT 'compensation for land acquisition' AS content))),
  top_k => 20, distance_type => 'COSINE');

Good for batch RAG and analytics over large corpora. For interactive latency, serve from Postgres/AlloyDB or Vertex AI Vector Search.

Evaluating RAG

MeasureQuestion it answers
Recall@kIs the right passage in the top k results?
MRRHow high is the first right passage?
FaithfulnessIs every claim supported by the retrieved passages?
Answer correctnessDoes it match the expected answer?
Citation accuracyDo citations point to passages that support the sentence?
AbstentionDoes it say "not found" when the corpus has no answer?
  • Build 100–300 real questions with the passages that answer them, including unanswerable ones.
  • Evaluate retrieval and generation separately, so you know which step to fix.
  • Run in CI on every change to chunking, embeddings, prompts or models.

Security and access control

  • Filter by permission at retrieval time. Never retrieve documents the user may not see and hope the model ignores them.
  • Prompt injection: documents can contain instructions. Keep tools read-only, constrain outputs, and do not let retrieved text trigger actions.
  • Legal holds and suppressions apply to the index too: remove or filter suppressed documents immediately.
  • Personal data: decide what may be embedded and sent to a model; embeddings can leak content.
  • Audit: log the question, retrieved IDs and answer (with sensitive fields protected).

Failure modes and fixes

SymptomLikely cause
Right document exists, never retrievedChunking splits the answer; missing keyword search; embedding model weak for the domain
Retrieved, but answer wrongToo many passages, noisy context, prompt does not demand grounding
Exact citation or section number missedVector-only search; add keyword or exact-match lookup
Old version quotedRe-ingestion not incremental; no version filter
SlowNo ANN index, reranking too many, large context
Confident answer with no sourceNo abstention instruction or citation validation

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain the ingestion, retrieval and generation phases
  • Run a vector search over embedded chunks
  • Prompt a model to answer from passages with citations

2 Practitioner

  • Build an incremental ingestion pipeline with chunking, metadata and embeddings
  • Add hybrid search, reranking and permission filters
  • Build a golden question set and measure recall and faithfulness

3 Senior

  • Design RAG for a domain: chunking strategy, index choice, latency and cost
  • Diagnose retrieval vs generation failures from evals
  • Secure RAG: access control, prompt injection, suppressions

4 Expert

  • Own retrieval and knowledge platforms across products
  • Lead advanced retrieval (agentic, graph-based) where it measurably helps
  • Set evaluation standards for AI answers in the organisation

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

MCP: Model Context Protocol

An open protocol for giving AI applications access to tools and data through a standard interface. What it is, how to build and add an MCP server, how to call one from the API, and how to use MCP safely around a data pipeline.

What MCP is

Without MCP, every AI application needs custom integration code for every tool. With MCP, a tool or data source is wrapped once as an MCP server, and any MCP-capable client (Claude Code, Claude apps, IDEs, your own agent) can use it. Think of it as a standard port between models and systems.

ConceptMeaning
Host / clientThe AI application and its connection to a server
ServerA program exposing capabilities over MCP
ToolsFunctions the model can call (query a table, check a pipeline run)
ResourcesRead-only data the application can load into context (a file, a run record)
PromptsReusable prompt templates the server offers
Transport: stdioServer runs as a local process; messages over stdin/stdout
Transport: Streamable HTTPServer runs remotely over HTTP; authorisation with OAuth

A server in Python (FastMCP)

# pip install "mcp[cli]" google-cloud-bigquery
from mcp.server.fastmcp import FastMCP
from google.cloud import bigquery

mcp = FastMCP("pipeline-status")
bq = bigquery.Client()

@mcp.tool()
def latest_runs(run_date: str) -> list[dict]:
    """Latest state of every pipeline stage for a run date (YYYY-MM-DD)."""
    sql = """SELECT stage, state, objects_examined, objects_failed, event_ts
             FROM `my-proj.dp_control.v_pipeline_run_latest`
             WHERE run_date = @d ORDER BY stage"""
    cfg = bigquery.QueryJobConfig(query_parameters=[
        bigquery.ScalarQueryParameter("d", "DATE", run_date)])
    return [dict(r) for r in bq.query(sql, job_config=cfg).result()]

@mcp.resource("ledger://{run_date}")
def ledger(run_date: str) -> str:
    """The full event history for a run date, as text."""
    return format_events(read_events(run_date))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    mcp.run()                              # stdio by default
    # mcp.run(transport="streamable-http") # to serve over HTTP

The function name, type hints and docstring become the tool's name, schema and description. Write the docstring for the model: what it does, inputs, and what it returns. A TypeScript SDK exists with the same concepts.

Add it to Claude Code and test it

# local stdio server, shared with the team via .mcp.json in the repo
claude mcp add --scope project pipeline-status -- python mcp/pipeline_status.py

# remote server over HTTP
claude mcp add --transport http pipeline-status https://mcp.example.com/mcp

claude mcp list
# inside Claude Code: /mcp shows servers, status and tools

# interactive testing without a model
npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector python mcp/pipeline_status.py
  • Project scope writes .mcp.json, so everyone on the repo gets the same server.
  • Keep credentials out of .mcp.json; servers read Application Default Credentials or environment variables.
  • Approve which tools may run without asking in Claude Code's permission settings.

Call MCP servers from the API

resp = client.beta.messages.create(
    model="claude-opus-5-5",
    max_tokens=16000,
    betas=["mcp-client-2025-11-20"],
    mcp_servers=[{"type": "url", "name": "pipeline",
                  "url": "https://mcp.example.com/mcp"}],
    tools=[{"type": "mcp_toolset", "mcp_server_name": "pipeline"}],
    messages=[{"role": "user", "content": "Summarise yesterday's pipeline run."}],
)

The MCP connector needs both halves: the server in mcp_servers and a matching mcp_toolset in tools. It reaches remote (HTTP) servers only; check the docs for authentication options and current beta name.

MCP and a data pipeline: four patterns

PatternWhat the MCP server exposesWho uses itRule
1. Pipeline observabilityRun records, stage states, freshness per source, failures with reasonsEngineers in Claude Code: "why did ODP_LOAD fail on 19-09?"Read-only. Reports UNKNOWN when it cannot read the ledger.
2. Governed warehouse accessTable catalog, schemas, a guarded query toolAnalysts and engineers asking questions in natural languageDry-run first, byte cap, allow-listed datasets, read-only service account
3. Data catalog and lineageTable descriptions, owners, upstream and downstream models (dbt manifest, Dataplex)Anyone changing a model: "what breaks if I rename this column?"Generated from the source of truth, never hand-maintained
4. LLM step inside the pipelineReference data or lookups the model needs while extracting or classifyingA pipeline job calling a model (e.g. extracting citations from judgments)Prefer plain tool calls or batch jobs; validate outputs; never let the model write to production tables directly

MCP sits beside the pipeline, not inside its critical path. The pipeline must run, record and alert without any AI involved; MCP makes it easier to ask questions about it.

A guarded BigQuery query tool

ALLOWED = {"dp_fdp", "dp_cdp", "dp_control"}
MAX_BYTES = 5 * 1024**3                       # 5 GiB per query

@mcp.tool()
def run_query(sql: str) -> dict:
    """Run a read-only GoogleSQL SELECT over the fdp, cdp and control datasets.
    Returns at most 200 rows. Fails if the query would scan more than 5 GiB."""
    if not sql.lstrip().lower().startswith(("select", "with")):
        return {"error": "only SELECT queries are allowed"}
    dry = bq.query(sql, job_config=bigquery.QueryJobConfig(
        dry_run=True, use_query_cache=False))
    datasets = {t.dataset_id for t in dry.referenced_tables}
    if not datasets <= ALLOWED:
        return {"error": f"datasets not allowed: {sorted(datasets - ALLOWED)}"}
    if dry.total_bytes_processed > MAX_BYTES:
        return {"error": f"would scan {dry.total_bytes_processed/1e9:.1f} GB; add filters"}
    job = bq.query(sql, job_config=bigquery.QueryJobConfig(maximum_bytes_billed=MAX_BYTES))
    rows = [dict(r) for r in job.result(max_results=200)]
    return {"rows": rows, "bytes_processed": job.total_bytes_processed}

The real protection is the service account: give it only dataViewer on those datasets and jobUser. The string checks are a convenience, not a security boundary.

Deploying a remote MCP server

  • Serve with Streamable HTTP and run it on Cloud Run with its own service account.
  • Require authentication: OAuth for user-facing clients (the MCP authorisation spec builds on OAuth 2.1), or Cloud Run IAM for internal callers.
  • Rate-limit and log every tool call with the caller's identity.
  • Version tools like an API: add new ones rather than changing behaviour under the same name.
  • Ready-made servers exist for many systems, for example Google's MCP Toolbox for Databases (BigQuery, Postgres and others). Review what they expose before connecting them.

Designing good MCP tools

  • Few, task-shaped tools ("pipeline status for a date") beat many thin wrappers over every API call.
  • Clear names and descriptions: the model chooses tools from them.
  • Typed, validated inputs with enums and formats where possible.
  • Compact outputs: summarise and paginate; huge results waste context.
  • Honest errors: return what failed and how to fix it; distinguish "no data" from "could not read".
  • Read-only by default; any write needs explicit human approval in the client.

Security

  • An MCP server runs with its own credentials: give it least privilege, as you would a service.
  • Only install servers you trust and have reviewed; a malicious server can return instructions that steer the model (tool poisoning).
  • Treat tool outputs as untrusted data in the model's context.
  • Never put secrets in tool descriptions, outputs or .mcp.json.
  • Audit tool calls; alert on unusual volume or failed authorisation.

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Explain servers, clients, tools, resources and transports
  • Add an existing MCP server to Claude Code and use it
  • Know the security risks of untrusted servers

2 Practitioner

  • Build an MCP server with FastMCP and test it with the Inspector
  • Expose read-only pipeline or warehouse tools with guardrails
  • Share servers with a team via project configuration

3 Senior

  • Design task-shaped tool sets with honest errors and compact outputs
  • Deploy remote servers on Cloud Run with OAuth or IAM
  • Integrate MCP with data pipelines without putting it on the critical path

4 Expert

  • Set MCP standards, review and approval process for the organisation
  • Build shared MCP platforms over core data and systems
  • Contribute to the protocol ecosystem

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Web 3.0

"Web 3.0" means two different things. Web3 is the blockchain-based vision: decentralised ledgers, smart contracts, tokens and wallets. The Semantic Web (Tim Berners-Lee's earlier "Web 3.0") is about machine-readable, linked data. Both matter to engineers, for different reasons. The Solid tab covers Berners-Lee's current project for personal data.

Web 1.0, 2.0, 3.0

Web 1.0Web 2.0Web3 (blockchain)Semantic Web
Era1990s2000s onward2014 onward2000s onward
ShapeRead-only pagesPlatforms with user contentDecentralised apps on blockchainsLinked, machine-readable data
Who holds dataSite ownersPlatformsPublic ledgers and users' walletsPublishers, linked by URIs
Key techHTML, HTTPAPIs, cloud, mobileBlockchains, smart contracts, tokensRDF, SPARQL, JSON-LD, ontologies

Web3 building blocks

  • Blockchain: a replicated, append-only ledger agreed by consensus (proof of stake on Ethereum), without a central operator.
  • Wallet and keys: a private key controls an account; whoever holds the key controls the assets. Lost key, lost assets.
  • Smart contract: a program deployed on the chain (Solidity on Ethereum), public, and immutable unless designed to be upgradeable.
  • Tokens: fungible (ERC-20) and non-fungible (ERC-721 NFTs) assets defined by contracts.
  • Gas: fees paid for computation and storage on chain.
  • Layer 2 rollups: process transactions off the main chain and post proofs to it, for lower fees.
  • Oracles: bring outside data on chain, a trust point in themselves.
  • Decentralised storage: IPFS and similar address content by its hash.
  • DeFi and DAOs: financial services and organisations governed by contracts and token votes.

A minimal smart contract

// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.24;

/// Records a document hash and who registered it, once.
contract DocumentRegistry {
    mapping(bytes32 => address) public registeredBy;
    event Registered(bytes32 indexed docHash, address indexed by);

    function register(bytes32 docHash) external {
        require(registeredBy[docHash] == address(0), "already registered");
        registeredBy[docHash] = msg.sender;
        emit Registered(docHash, msg.sender);
    }
}

Tooling: Foundry or Hardhat to build and test, ethers.js or viem to call contracts, a testnet before mainnet, and an external audit before holding real value. Deployed bugs are public and often permanent.

Do you need a blockchain?

  1. Do several parties need to write to shared data?
  2. Do they not trust each other, or any single operator?
  3. Is there no acceptable trusted intermediary (a database run by a neutral party, a regulator)?
  4. Is public, permanent, tamper-evident history worth the cost, latency and loss of privacy?

If any answer is no, a conventional database, an append-only ledger table, signed records or a timestamping service is simpler, cheaper and easier to correct.

Also weigh regulation: rules for crypto assets differ by country and change often (India, for example, taxes crypto transfers and has evolving rules). Personal data on an immutable public chain conflicts with deletion rights.

The Semantic Web and linked data

  • RDF: data as triples (subject, predicate, object), each named by a URI, so data from different sources joins up.
  • Vocabularies and ontologies: shared meanings (schema.org, Dublin Core, FOAF; OWL for formal ontologies).
  • Formats: Turtle, JSON-LD (linked data in ordinary JSON), RDF/XML.
  • SPARQL: the query language for RDF data and knowledge graphs.
  • Knowledge graphs: entities and relationships, used in search, AI grounding and data integration.
  • For legal data: standards such as Akoma Ntoso (XML for legislation and judgments) and the European Legislation Identifier give documents stable, linkable identities and structure.
<!-- JSON-LD with schema.org, embedded in a web page -->
<script type="application/ld+json">
{ "@context": "https://schema.org",
  "@type": "Legislation",
  "name": "Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023",
  "legislationJurisdiction": "IN",
  "legislationDate": "2023-12-25" }
</script>
# SPARQL: judgments citing a given judgment
PREFIX ex: <https://example.org/legal#>
SELECT ?j ?date WHERE {
  ?j ex:cites <https://example.org/judgment/HPHC010001232024> ;
     ex:decisionDate ?date .
} ORDER BY DESC(?date)

Levels: what to expect

1 Foundation

  • Tell Web3 (blockchain) from the Semantic Web
  • Explain blockchains, wallets, smart contracts and tokens
  • Ask whether a problem really needs a blockchain

2 Practitioner

  • Write and test a simple smart contract on a testnet
  • Publish structured data with JSON-LD and schema.org
  • Query linked data with SPARQL

3 Senior

  • Assess security, cost, privacy and regulation of a Web3 design
  • Design knowledge graphs and ontologies for a domain
  • Integrate legal-document standards (Akoma Ntoso, ELI)

4 Expert

  • Set strategy on decentralised and linked-data technologies
  • Lead audits and governance for on-chain systems
  • Shape open data standards in a sector

Each level includes the ones before it. See Start here for what the levels mean.

Solid

Solid is a set of open specifications, led by Tim Berners-Lee, for putting people in control of their data. Each person keeps data in a Pod (a personal online data store) and grants apps access to it, instead of every app keeping its own copy. It uses ordinary web standards (HTTP, linked data, OpenID Connect), not a blockchain.

How Solid works

PieceIs
PodA personal data store on a Solid server, self-hosted or from a provider; organisations can run Pods for their users
WebIDA URI that identifies a person or agent, pointing to a profile document
Solid-OIDCAuthentication built on OpenID Connect: the user logs in with their identity provider; apps act on their behalf
Access controlWeb Access Control (WAC) or Access Control Policies (ACP): who may read, write, append or control each resource
ResourcesFiles and containers in the Pod, read and written over HTTP (GET, PUT, POST, PATCH, DELETE)
Linked dataStructured data stored as RDF (often Turtle) with shared vocabularies, so different apps understand the same data
NotificationsApps can subscribe to changes in resources

The key shift: apps and data are separated. A user can switch apps without losing data, and grant or revoke access per app or person.

Data in a Pod

# a public profile document (Turtle)
@prefix foaf: <http://xmlns.com/foaf/0.1/> .
@prefix solid: <http://www.w3.org/ns/solid/terms#> .

<#me> a foaf:Person ;
  foaf:name "Asha Rao" ;
  solid:oidcIssuer <https://login.example-provider.com> .
# read it over plain HTTP
curl -H "Accept: text/turtle" https://asha.pod.example/profile/card
# Web Access Control: owner has full control of a folder
@prefix acl: <http://www.w3.org/ns/auth/acl#> .
<#owner> a acl:Authorization ;
  acl:agent <https://asha.pod.example/profile/card#me> ;
  acl:accessTo <./> ; acl:default <./> ;
  acl:mode acl:Read, acl:Write, acl:Control .

An app reading from a Pod (JavaScript)

import { login, handleIncomingRedirect, getDefaultSession, fetch }
  from "@inrupt/solid-client-authn-browser";
import { getSolidDataset, getThing, getStringNoLocale }
  from "@inrupt/solid-client";

await handleIncomingRedirect();
if (!getDefaultSession().info.isLoggedIn) {
  await login({ oidcIssuer: "https://login.example-provider.com",
                redirectUrl: window.location.href, clientName: "Case Notes" });
}

const profileUrl = "https://asha.pod.example/profile/card";
const dataset = await getSolidDataset(profileUrl, { fetch });   // authenticated fetch
const me = getThing(dataset, profileUrl + "#me");
const name = getStringNoLocale(me, "http://xmlns.com/foaf/0.1/name");

Libraries: Inrupt's JavaScript client libraries, and others for Java and Python. Servers: the open-source Community Solid Server for development and research; commercial servers for production.

Where it fits

  • Personal data that many services need: health records, education credentials, identity documents, preferences.
  • Public-sector data sharing with citizen consent; some governments are piloting or running Solid-based services.
  • Portability: users switch apps without exporting and importing.
  • Consent you can audit: access is granted per resource and can be revoked.

Challenges: app ecosystem still small, linked-data skills are less common, querying across many Pods is slower than a central database, and Pod providers must be trusted to run storage well.

Solid vs Web3 vs a normal platform

PlatformWeb3Solid
Data held byThe platformA public ledgerThe user's chosen Pod
IdentityPlatform accountWallet keyWebID with OpenID Connect
PrivacyPlatform policyPublic by defaultPrivate by default, shared by grant
DeletionPlatform decidesEffectively impossible on chainUser deletes from their Pod
Built onProprietary APIsBlockchains, tokensHTTP, RDF, OIDC

  • Model data with shared RDF vocabularies
